Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | xi

Foreword
On behalf of the Ministry of Health and Medical Services, it is my great
pleasure to introduce the Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual. This manual
is a milestone in our commitment to improving the quality and accessibility
of healthcare across Kiribati, ensuring that all I-Kiribati have access to safe,
effective, and culturally appropriate care.
The development of this manual has been a collaborative effort, drawing
on local expertise and the valuable knowledge and resources shared
through partnerships with international organizations. It will serve as an
essential tool for healthcare workers across our nation, empowering them
to deliver care that meets the highest standards of clinical practice.
The Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual provides comprehensive
guidance on managing medical conditions, responding to emergencies,
promoting health and well-being, and enhancing the patient experience.
It also emphasizes the importance of community-based care, with a focus
on health promotion and prevention.
As we continue to address the health challenges faced by our
communities, this manual will play a key role in strengthening our
healthcare system, improving health outcomes, and enhancing the lives
of all those we serve. The Ministry remains committed to supporting the
health workforce and ensuring that our services meet the diverse needs of
the I-Kiribati population.
I extend my gratitude to all those who have contributed to the creation
of this valuable resource and to all healthcare workers who will use it to
provide exceptional care. Together, we are building a healthier future for
Kiribati.
Mr. Michael Bootii Nauan
Minister for Health
Republic of Kiribati

Source attribution and adaptation notice
Except where specifically identified as Kiribati Original Protocols or from
the Kiribati Health Workers Manual (Green Book)  the clinical protocols in
this Manual are adapted from the Queensland Primary Clinical Care Manual
(PCCM) published by the State of Queensland (Queensland Health) and
the Royal Flying Doctor Service (Queensland Section). The adaptations
were undertaken by the Ministry of Health and Medical Services (MHMS),
Government of Kiribati, and reviewed by MHMS clinical experts, to ensure
relevance and compliance for Kiribati. Changes include (but are not limited
to) adjustments to:
•	 reflect the unique cultural context and health-system setting of Kiribati
and its people;
•	 align with MHMS policies and procedures and applicable national
regulations;
•	 reflect local medicine supply chains, the Kiribati pharmacy formulary/
essential medicines list, and other operational constraints; and
•	 incorporate local clinical guidelines, including the Kiribati Antibiotic
Guidelines (Second edition, 2022) and any subsequent updates.

Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | xv



Introduction

Introduction
This manual has been designed to serve as a comprehensive guide for
healthcare providers, including nurses, medical assistants, and allied
health professionals, working within Kiribati’s Primary Health Care (PHC)
system. It offers essential clinical information, guidelines, protocols, and
best practices for managing common health conditions, diseases, and
emergency situations, ensuring that healthcare providers can deliver
quality care at the community level. The manual will support healthcare
workers in conducting high-level patient assessments, managing
medications, and following evidence-based protocols that align with
international and local healthcare standards.
The primary purpose of this manual is to empower healthcare providers
with the necessary knowledge and resources to deliver safe, effective,
and patient-centered care. It is intended to be a practical tool that can
be referred to on a daily basis, supporting clinical decision-making and
facilitating consistent, high-quality healthcare delivery across all health
centers and outreach services in Kiribati.
Structure of the Manual
The manual is organized into clear chapters based on the most common
health issues encountered in primary care settings in Kiribati. Each chapter
includes:
•	 Overview of the health condition or issue – A concise explanation of the
disease or condition, its prevalence, and its impact on public health in
Kiribati.
•	 Diagnosis and clinical assessment – Key diagnostic criteria, assessment
techniques, and patient history gathering to make an accurate
diagnosis.
•	 Management and treatment protocols – Evidence-based
recommendations for managing the condition, including
pharmacological and non-pharmacological treatment options.
•	 Prevention and health promotion – Guidance on how to prevent the
condition, including community-based prevention strategies and
education.
•	 Referral guidelines – When and how to refer patients for further
diagnostic testing, specialist care, or hospital-based treatment.
2 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Each chapter also highlights important considerations such as patient
safety, cultural factors, and common challenges in the Kiribati context.
Throughout the manual, clinical flow charts, decision-making algorithms,
and checklists are included to guide healthcare providers through the
diagnostic and treatment process, ensuring that no steps are overlooked.
Integration of the Manual Within the Model of Care
This manual is not an isolated resource; it is an integral part of the broader
healthcare model in Kiribati. The primary goal of the manual is to ensure
that all healthcare providers can confidently and competently deliver
essential services within the PHC framework. This manual complements
the Model of Care by guiding healthcare workers in providing services that
align with national health priorities and international standards.
The Model of Care in Kiribati is based on a comprehensive and patient-
centered approach to healthcare, which includes prevention, diagnosis,
treatment, and follow-up. The manual supports this approach by offering
evidence-based guidelines for managing health conditions at the
community level and ensuring that healthcare providers are well-equipped
to address the most common health issues. It ensures that patients receive
consistent and high-quality care, whether they are in urban or rural areas,
and helps maintain continuity of care by guiding healthcare providers on
follow-up visits and patient education.
Furthermore, the manual's integration with the regulatory and governance
frameworks, such as the Pharmaceuticals Legislation and Professional
Governance, ensures that healthcare providers adhere to the legal and
ethical standards governing medical practice in Kiribati. It also reinforces
the importance of maintaining scope of practice for nurses, medical
assistants, and doctors, ensuring that each provider works within their
professional boundaries, making appropriate referrals, and collaborating
effectively within the healthcare system.
Use of Antibiotics: All antimicrobial prescribing must align with the Kiribati
Antibiotic Guidelines (Second edition, 2022, Ministry of Health and Medical
Services, Government of Kiribati) or the most recent version. Clinicians
should use these Guidelines alongside this Manual to ensure compliance
with national policy and antimicrobial stewardship. Where there is
uncertainty or a deviation from the Guidelines is being considered, seek
advice from MHMS.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 3

The Manual’s Role in Education and Training
The manual is also a critical tool for education and training, especially for
nurses, medical assistants, and other healthcare providers who may be
less familiar with advanced medical procedures. It serves as a practical
resource during formal and informal training, allowing trainers to use it
as a reference for case studies, skills-building exercises, and continuing
education. Nurses and medical assistants can learn best practices, clinical
guidelines, and patient management techniques that will improve their
clinical competencies, helping them to undertake high-level patient
assessments and interventions with greater confidence.
For those involved in capacity building and training, the manual should
be used as a reference point in teaching clinical skills and protocols,
ensuring that PHC workers have a standard and consistent approach to
care. This also allows for continuous improvement as health conditions
and treatment guidelines evolve, ensuring that healthcare workers stay
updated with the latest evidence and standards of care.
Feedback and Continuous Improvement
To ensure that the manual remains a living document that evolves with the
needs of the healthcare system, healthcare providers are encouraged to
provide feedback on the manual’s usability, content, and clinical guidelines.
This feedback will help identify areas for improvement and updates in
future editions, ensuring that the manual remains relevant, practical,
and effective in meeting the healthcare needs of the Kiribati population.
pccmfeedback@mhms.gov.ki
By continuously updating the manual, healthcare providers can stay at the
forefront of care delivery, improving patient outcomes, and contributing to
the strength of the PHC system in Kiribati.

4 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Kiribati Patient’s Bill of Rights
1. Karinean inaomatan te aoraki
The right to be treated with Respect and Dignity
2. Te tararua ae nakoroai, rokoroi ao kororoai
The Right to receive Quality and Holistic care
3. Te tararua rinanon te katei
The Right to receive Culturally Safe and Sensitive care
4. Karinean ao Boutokaan inaomatan te aoraki
The Right to receive Informed Consent
5. Kamanoan rongorongon te aoraki
The Right to Confidentiality and Privacy
6. Te katiteboaki
The Right to be Treated Fairly
7. Butimwaen ao rinanoan ana tangtang te aoraki
The Right to Lay a Formal Complaint
8. Te tararua ae tabwannin raoi
The Right to Receive Care that is Central to the Individual, Family or
Community’s needs
9. Ongorean bwanaan te aoraki
The Right to be Heard
10. Kabobongan ao Kakororaoan raoi te tararua
The Right to Receive Quality Care
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 5

Kiribati Nurses Code of Conduct
The Nursing Council, under the I-Kiribati Medical Services Act (1996), is the
statutory authority that oversees and governs the practices of nursing in
Kiribati. The fundamental purpose of the ‘Act’ is to protect the health and
safety of the general public.
The implementation of the inaugural ‘Anuan Te Neeti’ (The Code) is a
vital feature of the Act’s commitment to the protection of the public. The
code provides a set of professional standards that nurses must adhere
to in demonstrating their being ‘fit for practice’ and maintaining their
registration.
The Code evolved during the development of the I-Kiribati Nursing
Cultural Model of Care which was informed through extensive consultation
with the public and the profession. This included a rigorous review of
Kiribati nursing consultation documents and an international literature
review of other codes.
Incorporated within the Anuan te Neeti are I-Kiribati cultural values defined
by the Nursing Council as acceptable professional conduct. To ensure public
confidence and trust, nurses are required to uphold an exceptional standard
of behaviour both in their professional and personal lives.
Anuan te Neeti code outlines the professional behaviour expected of
nurses in all practice settings as set by the Nursing Council under sections
47 & 48 of the Kiribati Medical Services Act (1996). The code is written
with the complete understanding that nursing practice is not limited to the
provision of direct clinical care delivery. Nursing practice settings extend
to all areas of nursing. This includes working in a non-clinical capacity
involving patients or members of the public, or any other role where the
integrity of the profession is upheld.
Anuan te Neeti can be utilized by:
•	 Patients or service users, families or public, employers, nurses,
professional organizations, and the statutory body (Nursing Council) as
a guide to evaluate the conduct of nurses.
6 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Failure to uphold these professional standards of behavior, may result in a
disciplinary inquiry. The term ‘patient’ has been incorporated throughout
this document to align accordingly with the Medical Services Act (1996).
Contextually, the term ‘patient’ refers to the individuals, families, groups
and communities who are the recipient of nursing care (1996 Medical
Services Act, I,p.44). While mandatory language such as ‘must’, ‘shall’ and
‘will’ is not sought throughout this code, it is an expectation that nurses
understand the foundational principles of the code and how it impacts on
their professional standing.
Please note: The terms conduct and behaviour are one and the same,
hence have been used inter-changeably throughout this document.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 7

10 principles of nursing including standards are provided in the code.
These characteristics are fundamental to I-Kiribati cultural values and are
in harmony with each other.
The term ‘trust’ has been used deliberately as an entry-point to defining
the 10 principles. This is because the first step to achieving successful
partnerships of any kind, is in the patient trusting that the nurse has their
best interests at heart. This is the Kiribati way:
Principle 1: Kateimatoaon kamanoan kakawakii aoraki
Translation: Maintain patient trust by protecting their dignity.
Principle 2: Kateimatoaen te reitaki ae tamaroa rinanon te makuri;
Translation: Maintain patient trust by working in partnership
Principle 3: Kateimatoaan ao uarokoan makuri ni kuakua rinanon te katei
ao te aroaro.
Translation: Maintain patient trust by providing culturally responsive care
Principle 4: Kateimatoaan ao kamanoan rongorongoia ma tararuakiia
aoraki.
Translation: Maintain patient trust by ensuring privacy and confidentiality
Principle 5: Kateimatoaen ao kanakoroaon te kuakua ae oioi iaoiia ke
mairouii aoraki.
Translation: Maintain patient trust by the provision of patient-centred care
Principle 6: Kateimatoaan te tararua ae manoroai, nakoroai ao n rokoroi.
Translation: Maintain patient trust by providing safe and competent care
Principle 7: Kateimatoaan onimakinan te makuri ni kuakua ao n tararua
iroiiia Neeti.
Translation: Maintain patient trust in the nursing profession
Principle 8: Kateimatoaan onimakinan karinean inaomatan te aoraki.
Translation: Maintain patient trust by providing informed consent
Principle 9: Kateimatoaan karinean te reitaki ae tamaroa imarenaia Neeti
ao raoia ni beku.
Translation: Maintain patient trust through respectful relationships with
colleagues
Principle 10: Kateimatoaen te onimakinaki rinanon kakaraon te koaua, te
eti ao te riai.
Translation: Maintain patient trust by working with integrity and honesty
8 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

The Nursing Cultural Framework
(Abridged Version)
Four key objectives underpin the Cultural Framework:
1.	 Ara Katei / Culture
2.	 Aoraki ma aia utu / Patients as consumers of Health Services and their
family
3.	 Te bonnano / Unity
4.	 Karikirakean te rabakau n te mwakuri / Professional Development
Vision
A healthy and prosperous I-Kiribati through quality and responsive nursing
workforce founded on unique cultural needs and values.
Philosophy
The cultural framework will serve as the ‘roadmap’ and also a ‘key driver’
for I-Kiribati Nurses in ensuring optimal health and safety for patients
which is an inclusive term for (individuals, families and communities) as
well as the safety for the profession.
The Philosophy is grounded on I-Kiribati cultural needs and values; to
ensure public confidence and trust, nurses and/or midwives are required
to uphold a high standard of conduct or behaviour in their personal and
professional lives.
Boua / Five posts
Symbolises the holistic nursing care delivered, incorporating contextually
the five cornerstones of health:
FIVE CORNERSTONES OF HEALTH
I-KIRIBATI TRANSLATION
Spiritual Aspects
Te Tamnei
Psychological Aspects
Te Iango
Cultural Aspects
Te Katei
Physiological Aspects
Te Rabwata
Social/other
Te Reitaki
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 9

Maneaba: I-Kiribati Nursing Model of Care
Maneaba means accommodating and welcoming: A safe place to rest, to
meet or get together or take refuge.
Historically, the maneaba was considered a meeting place for villages/
island to congregate and make important decisions. Customarily, subject
to context e.g. church gatherings/tribal affiliations or individual status,
special seating allocations within the maneaba are adhered to accordingly.
Traditional beliefs suggests that the Maneaba had been ‘purposefully’ built
with a lowly-positioned roof to serve as a constant reminder to I-Kiribati
that on entering the dwelling, they must always bow or kneel to show
respect for their Unimwane & Unaine / Elders. This was also symbolic of
acknowledging their ancestral gods, prior to the arrival of Christianity on
their shores.
In present-day, although many members of the Kiribati community have
adapted to Western ideology, traditional values and beliefs are still
maintained. Hence, the symbolic gesture in the use of the Maneaba as a
cultural framework for nursing is befitting.
This is likened to other Polynesian conceptual frameworks where
traditional thatched or flax-woven shelters have been utilised
metaphorically to depict the significance of culture in their care delivery.
10 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Kiribati Decision-Making Framework
(Abridged Version)
The Kiribati Decision-Making Framework (KDMF) provides a clear and
consistent process for nurses and medical assistants to determine whether
a clinical activity should be undertaken. This summary outlines the key
principles only. All clinicians must refer to the full KDMF for detailed
requirements, examples, and procedural steps.
Purpose
Clinical decision-making is a core professional responsibility. In Kiribati,
where many clinicians work with limited resources or in remote outer
island settings, decisions about treatment, medication administration,
delegation, and escalation must be safe, lawful, and accountable. The
KDMF supports clinicians by providing a structured approach to these
decisions, ensuring consistent and patient-centred care across all facilities.
The Framework is grounded in the ICN Definition of ‘Nursing’ and of ‘a
Nurse’ (2026).
Why the Framework Is Needed
Kiribati’s health system requires a structured decision support process
because of:
•	 Geographical isolation and limited access to higher-level services
•	 Resource constraints that affect what can be done safely in each setting
•	 Multiple cadres working together across facilities and islands
•	 Legal requirements governing the use of medicines
•	 Ongoing system development, including new PCCM interventions
The KDMF ensures that decisions are consistent, safe, and legally
defensible in all contexts.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 11

Foundations of Safe Decision-Making
The KDMF is based on three domains that must all be met before any
activity is undertaken:
1.	 Regulation and Legislation: activities must comply with the Legislation
(ie: Medicines Act 2018), MHMS policy, and professional regulation and
standards.
2.	 Professional Competence: clinicians must be ecducated / trained,
competent, and have recency of practice.
3.	 Context of Practice: decisions must reflect local resources, supervision,
staffing, and referral pathways.
Only when all three domains are satisfied is an activity considered within
safe scope of practice.
Guiding Principles
The Framework is underpinned by the following principles:
•	 Patient safety comes first in all decisions.
•	 Practice must be lawful and within authorised scope.
•	 Accountability is individual—each clinician is responsible for their own
decisions.
•	 Competence and recency are essential for safe practice.
•	 Context determines safety, particularly in remote or resource-limited
settings.
•	 Collaboration with colleagues strengthens decision-making.
•	 Alignment with professional identity, consistent with the ICN Definitions.
12 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 13

Supervision and Delegation
Supervision and delegation are integral to scope of practice in Kiribati.
Registered nurses remain accountable for decisions to delegate and must
ensure the delegatee is authorised, competent, and supported. Supervision
may be direct, indirect, or remote, depending on the complexity of the
activity and the competence of the clinician involved. These processes
support patient safety and help build workforce capacity.
Expanding Scope of Practice
Scope of practice is dynamic. Expansion must occur through a structured
and governed process, including legal review, MHMS endorsement,
education and training, supervised practice, competence assessment, and
ongoing monitoring. Any new activity added to scope must remain lawful,
supported, and in the best interests of patients.
This summary is for quick reference only. All nurses and medical assistants
must refer to the full Kiribati Decision-Making Framework for complete
guidance, detailed explanations, and examples.
Statutory Authority to Use Medicines
Medicines Act 2018, Part 4
This manual must be read together with the Kiribati Medicines Act 2018
(Part 4, ss.17–20). The manual does not create new authorities; it guides
practice within the authorities below and within each practitioner’s scope,
competence and local governance.
14 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Who may prescribe:
•	 Medical practitioners may prescribe any scheduled medicine for persons
under their care.
•	 Dentists may prescribe Level D medicines for persons under their care.
•	 No other cadre has statutory prescribing authority under the Act
(veterinary prescribers are limited to Level V for animals).
Who may supply / dispense / administer:
•	 Pharmacists may obtain, compound, prepare, store, distribute, supply or
dispense any medicine on the Register of Medicines.
•	 Medical practitioners may administer any medicine on the Register for
persons under their care.
•	 Nurses may dispense and administer Level 1 and Level 2 medicines for
persons under their care.
•	 Medical assistants, and nurses supervised by a medical assistant, may
dispense and administer Level 1, Level 2 and Level 2B medicines for
persons under their care.
Using PCCM protocols
•	 Protocols list recommended medicines and doses. You may only carry out
the parts of a protocol that fall within your statutory authority and scope.
•	 Where a protocol includes a medicine above your authorised level (e.g.,
Level 2B when you are a nurse not under medical-assistant supervision,
or any medicine requiring a prescription), a medical practitioner (or
dentist for Level D) must prescribe and, where applicable, administer.
•	 All actions must be for particular persons under your care, documented,
and undertaken with required supervision, competence and local
approvals.
Medicine schedules noted in protocols
•	 Schedules referenced in protocols (Level 1, Level 2, Level 2B, Level D,
Level V) must be applied as per ss.18–19 and the Register of Medicines.
•	 The Pharmacy team will provide the authoritative schedule status for
each medicine; the manual will reflect these once confirmed. Until then,
check the Register of Medicines or consult MHMS Pharmacy before
proceeding.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 15

Patient assessment
and transport

Rapid assessment
•	 Danger
•	 Response
•	 Send for help if unresponsive
•	 Airway - compromised
•	 Breathing - not breathing, significant respiratory distress
•	 Circulation - pulse absent, slow, rapid or profuse bleeding
•	 Disability - Alert, Voice, Pain, Unresponsive
•	 Rapid history, allergies, alerts
•	 Vital signs - RR, SpO2, HR, BP, T - use appropriate Early Warning Tool'
•	 Consider BGL
Is patient immediately at risk
Consult MO as soon as circumstances
allow
Perform immediate stabilising or l
ife-saving measures. See
Basic life support,
Advanced life support, or other topic
relevant to urgent presentation
Note: if trauma related eg fall/hit by an
object/motorvehicle accident, promptly
assess against Criteria for early
notification of trauma for interfacility
transfer (inside front cover)
If meets criteria contact 
 Contact: Tungaru Central Hospital (TCH )
Get history and do physical examination
as relevant. See History and physical
examination - adult, or History and
physical examination - child
Form a clinical impression
Select appropriate topic to guide
further assessment and management
Yes
No
Patient presentation - Adult/Child
General principles1
•	 The first priority is to assess whether the patient is seriously ill + needs
immediate management, or is less acutely sick giving time to get a full history
•	 Always ask open ended questions
•	 In children, pay particular attention to history from parent/carer where available
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 17

Adult presentation
History and physical examination - adult
Recommend
•	 If child, see History and physical examination - child
•	 Always think could it be Sepsis, if signs of infection or history/evidence of fever
or hypothermia
•	 For specific presentations, see:

–
Traumatic injuries

–
Chest pain assessment

–
Mental health emergency

–
STI/BBV assessment
•	 Ensure a culturally safe environment. As appropriate, be guided by local health
workers
•	 Document your findings clearly, concisely and in logical sequence - use this
section to assist
•	 Offer opportunistic health promotion, screening + brief intervention as
appropriate.1,2 See Chronic Conditions Manual
Background
•	 The history is the most powerful tool for identifying the likely diagnosis in most
cases1
•	 Types of history:2

–
emergency - urgent, rapid collection of crucial information compiled
concurrently with

–
life-threatening measures. Take a comprehensive history once patient is
stabilised

–
focused/problem centred - shorter and specific to the patient’s current
presenting concern

–
complete - comprehensive history of the patient's past and present health
status. Usually done at initial visit in a non-emergency situation

–
follow up - evaluation of problem from preceding visit
18 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

VITAL SIGNS - ADULT APPROXIMATE NORMAL VALUES1-4
Respiratory rate RR
12–20 breaths/minute
Heart rate HR
60–100 beats/minute
Temperature T - oral, tympanic
35.5–37.5
Blood pressure BP
Systolic &lt; 130, diastolic &lt; 85
O2 saturation SpO2
≥ 94%
Conscious level - Alert, Voice, Pain, Unresponsive
Alert
Capillary refill time CRT
≤ 2 seconds
Blood glucose level5 BGL
4.0–7.8
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 19

Step 1: Obtain history of the presenting concern/problem1,6
•	 Taking the history is the first step in making a diagnosis
•	 Use the history to guide the physical examination/further investigations
•	 An accurately acquired history will usually suggest the diagnosis. The physical
examination and investigations should be used to confirm your diagnostic
impression
HISTORY OF THE PRESENTING CONCERN/PROBLEM
Presenting concern/
problem
•	 Ask what the problem is
•	 Use open ended questioning
For each symptom
ask about
(as relevant)
•	 Ask about length of illness and details of each
symptom(s). Use SOCRATES mnemonic
•	 Site - where is the symptom - localised or diffuse
•	 Onset:

–
gradual, rapid or sudden

–
continuous or intermittent

–
what were they doing when it started
•	 Character eg if pain:

–
sharp, dull, burning, stabbing, cramp like, crushing,
tingling
•	 Radiation - (if localised) does it travel elsewhere
•	 Alleviating factors - does anything make it better eg
sitting up, medicine(s), analgesia
•	 Timing:

–
when did it first begin, duration

–
have they had it before

–
any increase in severity
•	 Exacerbating factors - does anything make it worse eg
movement, exercise
•	 Severity:

–
does it interfere with sleep or normal activities

–
if pain - mild, moderate or severe OR severity on
scale of 1–10
Associated/other
symptoms
•	 Ask specifically about fever/rigors, pain, shortness of
breath (SOB), diarrhoea, weight loss
•	 Eg nausea, vomiting, photophobia, headache, appetite,
urine, bowels, energy
Treatment ±
medicine(s) taken
during this illness
•	 What, how much, when, how often, effectiveness
•	 Ask if there are any other concerns
•	 Consider possible differential diagnosis
•	 Subsequently use closed ended questions to confirm or refute your differential
diagnoses
20 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Step 2: Ask about past history1,6
•	 Review and update past history in medical record each visit.
•	 Consider past history that may assist with differential diagnosis this visit
•	 Always ask about allergies and medicines
PAST HISTORY
Past medical
and surgical
history
•	 Significant illnesses in the past
•	 Ask about - diabetes, hypertension, angina and myocardial
infarcts, epilepsy, asthma, mood or mental health problems
•	 Previous hospital admissions, operations or injuries - where,
when, why
•	 Is patient immunocompromised eg:

–
diabetes, chronic kidney disease or hepatitis, alcoholism,
no spleen, lupus, multiple sclerosis, rheumatoid arthritis,
HIV, Down syndrome, corticosteroids, chemotherapy,
immunosuppressants7
Family history
•	 Health problems in siblings and parents eg diabetes,
hypertension, heart disease, stroke, epilepsy, asthma, cancer,
mental health
Social history
•	 Usual GP + contact details if available
•	 Job, living conditions, who else lives at home and what
responsibilities do they have in the family. Means of
communication for contact/follow up eg phone
•	 Who their partner is or if they are in a relationship
•	 Gender identification + pronouns eg her/him
•	 Smoking/ever smoked - how many a day, ever tried giving up;
vapes
•	 Alcohol - what, how much/often. Express in standard drinks/
day or week
•	 Recreational drugs
•	 Recent overseas travel (if infectious disease possible) -
where/when
•	 Diet, exercise
•	 Risk factors + indicators for Domestic and family violence
Medicines
Also see
Best possible
medication
history
•	 Regular and prn medicines - prescribed/over-the-counter,
complementary, alternative, bush medicines, vitamins, oral/
other contraceptive (females):

–
generic name, dose, route, frequency

–
explore adherence and any barriers to taking medicines as
prescribed

–
recently changed/course completed
•	 If patient has medicines with them, check against medical
record
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 21

PAST HISTORY
Allergies
Adverse
medication
reactions
•	 Allergies/reactions + type of reaction (anaphylaxis, skin
reaction, other) to:

–
medicines

–
other eg honey bee stings, sticking plaster, food

–
is adrenaline (epinephrine) autoinjector used eg EpiPen®
•	 Check medical record + document allergies/adverse
reactions8
•	 Check medical alert jewellery/accessories eg key ring, USB,
shoe tag, anklet, watch, tattoo9
Immunisations
•	 Check if up-to-date, including COVID-19 vaccination status
•	 Offer opportunistic Immunisations as appropriate
Opportunistic
health checks
(offer or
refer as
appropriate)
•	 Check if due for routine health checks eg STI/BBV check,
Cervical Screening Test, mammogram, bowel screening
•	 See Chronic Conditions Manual for adult health checks
22 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Step 3: Do physical examination1,6
•	 Most information will be gained from history taking. Use this information to guide
examination
•	 In an adult who is not sick:

–
examine the relevant system first

–
proceed to further examination if required. Be guided by your findings
•	 In a sick adult:

–
 examine the relevant system first followed by ALL other systems
•	 Use a systematic approach to physical examination
PHYSICAL EXAMINATION - ADULT
Vital signs
•	 RR, HR, BP, T, SpO2
•	 Conscious state - GCS/AVPU
•	 If indicated:

–
BGL

–
capillary refill time (≤ 2 seconds)
•	 Beware of abdominal vital signs, closely monitor and
consult for help from MA, MO.
General
appearance
•	 Do they look sick/not sick
•	 Observe:

–
posture, mobility

–
any work of breathing (WOB), breathlessness

–
conjunctiva and nail beds - are they pale

–
lips, tongue and fingers - are they blue

–
general skin colour - pale/jaundiced

–
agitation, distressed

–
sweating

–
are they well nourished
•	 Weight ± height, BMI and waist measurement
Hydration
•	 Eyes - normal or sunken
•	 Mouth and tongue - wet or dry
•	 Skin turgor - normal or reduced. Pinch skin - normal skin
returns immediately on release (normal can be reduced in
elderly)
•	 Dry axillae
•	 Recent weight loss/gain
•	 Also see Hydration assessment - adult
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 23

PHYSICAL EXAMINATION - ADULT
Skin
•	 Check the whole body in a sick patient:

–
consider removing clothing to underwear
•	 Look for:

–
rashes - vesicular, macular, papular, petechiae, purpura

–
signs of infection - redness, swelling, tenderness,
warmth

–
bruising, unexplained or unusual marks

–
general pigmentation - areas where skin is lighter or
darker
•	 Any skin lesions or sores:

–
colour, shape, size, location, distribution on body

–
non-blanching, erythema/redness

–
vesicles present

–
exudate eg clear, pus, bloody, malodorous

–
any family members/close contacts with similar lesions
•	 Palpate/feel skin:

–
temperature, dryness/moisture, clamminess

–
any tender/enlarged lymph nodes in the neck, axillae or
groin
•	 See Rash as needed
Cardiovascular
system
•	 Also see Chest pain assessment
•	 Any:

–
pain/pressure in neck, chest, arms, or radiating pain

–
SOB on exertion

–
evidence of oedema, particularly feet, hands, face or
sacrum
•	 Check skin:

–
colour - pink, white, grey, mottling. Compare trunk with
limbs

–
temperature - hot, warm, cool or cold. Compare trunk
with limbs
•	 Central perfusion - blanch skin over the sternum with your
thumb for 5 seconds. Time how long it takes the colour to
return in seconds
•	 Peripheral perfusion - blanch the skin on a finger or toe
for 5 seconds. Time how long it takes for the colour to
return in seconds
•	 Look for distended neck veins
•	 If skilled listen to heart sounds - any murmur
24 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

PHYSICAL EXAMINATION - ADULT
Respiratory
system
•	 Most information is gained from simple observation
•	 Inspect anterior/posterior chest - equal chest expansion,
abnormal chest movement, ↑WOB, use of accessory
muscles, tracheal tug
•	 Can they talk in full sentences, single words or unable to
talk at all
•	 Measure RR over 1 minute - note rhythm, depth and effort
of breathing
•	 Listen for extra noises - cough (loose, dry, muffled ±
sputum), wheeze, stridor, hoarseness
•	 Auscultate for air entry into both lung fields:

–
equal, adequate

–
any wheeze or crackles - on inspiration or expiration
•	 Percuss lung fields - dull, resonant, hyper-resonant
•	 Can they lie flat without breathlessness
Gastrointestinal/
reproductive
system
Consider cultural
safety/sensitivity
•	 Inspect abdomen for scars, distension, hernias, bruising,
masses
•	 Auscultate bowel sounds in all 4 quadrants - present or
absent
•	 Palpate abdomen:

–
soft or firm

–
obvious masses

–
tender to touch. Identify abdominal quadrant and exact
area

–
guarding or rigidity even when the patient is relaxed

–
rebound tenderness - press down and take your hand
away very quickly, is pain greater when you do this
•	 Change of bowel habits
•	 Ask women:

–
date of last normal menstrual period

–
abnormal vaginal bleeding or discharge, itch
•	 Do pregnancy test if reproductive age with abdominal
pain
•	 In men:

–
if relevant check the testes - any redness, swelling,
tenderness, itch, lesions

–
ask about penile discharge
•	 See Abdominal examination for detailed assessment
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 25

PHYSICAL EXAMINATION - ADULT
Nervous system
•	 Assess conscious state. See GCS/AVPU
•	 Any dizziness, spinning, fainting, blackouts, problems with
speech, vision, weakness in arm/leg, altered sensation,
neck stiffness
•	 Assess orientation to time, place and person. Ask:

–
name, date of birth, location, time, date, year
•	 Check:

–
pupils - size, symmetry, reaction to light

–
for asymmetry of tone and power - compare each side
of the face and limbs

–
if indicated, test touch and pain sensation - using
cotton wool and the sharp end of the percussion
hammer

–
test finger nose coordination and if possible observe
the patient walking
•	 See Dizziness/vertigo as needed
Musculoskeletal
system
•	 Ask if any painful or stiff joints or muscular pain
•	 Observe gait
•	 Inspect joints for redness, swelling, warmth or lacerations
over or near a joint
•	 See Musculoskeletal injuries as needed
Eyes
•	 Check Visual acuity of each eye as indicated
•	 Inspect:

–
eyes and surrounding structures - any redness,
discharge or swelling

–
pupils - equal in size, regular in shape, reaction to light

–
eye movements
•	 See Eye assessment for detailed assessment
26 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

PHYSICAL EXAMINATION - ADULT
Ears, nose and
throat
Ears
•	 Inspect/check:

–
pinna - any redness, swelling, nodules

–
behind the ear (mastoid) for redness, swelling, pain
•	 Any obvious swelling or redness of the ear canal. If there
is, looking with an otoscope will be painful
•	 Using otoscope, inspect:

–
canal - any redness, swelling, discharge, foreign bodies
(insects/ objects)

–
eardrum - normal, redness, dullness, bulging/retraction,
fluid, bubbles, perforations or discharge
•	 See Ear assessment for detailed assessment
Nose
•	 Feel for facial swelling (sinuses), pain
•	 Any discharge or obvious foreign body
Throat
•	 Inspect - lips, buccal mucosa, gums, palate, tongue, throat
for:

–
colour changes, swelling, bleeding, pus, fissures

–
tonsils - redness, enlargement, pus

–
teeth and gums
Urine
•	 Check urine in all sick patients and if:

–
abdominal pain (+ do pregnancy test if reproductive
age)

–
urinary symptoms

–
diabetes
•	 Do urinalysis
•	 Note colour - normal, dark or blood stained, cloudy
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 27

Step 4: Consider differential diagnosis
•	 If unsure, contact with MO
•	 Always consider Sepsis
Step 5: Select Clinical Care Guideline (CCG)
•	 To guide further assessment and management go to relevant topics on
management in this manual
Step 6: Order/collect pathology if indicated
Step 7: Collaborate with MO as needed
•	 Always consult MA or respective MO at SKH, TCH or Kiritimati, if you are unsure
•	 Use ISBAR to guide your communication
28 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Clinical consultation
CONSULTING WITH MO/RETRIEVAL COORDINATOR1
•	 Be clear and methodical
•	 Write your findings down first, time permitting
•	 Advise early if you think the patient may need retrieval
•	 Say what you think is wrong. Your assessment is important
I
Identify yourself AND identify name and spelling of receiving MO
•	 I am ... (your name and role)
•	 I am calling from ... (location)
•	 The best contact number for me is ...
S
Situation and status - why are you calling
•	 I have a patient ... (name, age and gender)
•	 Who is ... (stable/unstable/deteriorating/improving)
•	 Any immediate clinical needs eg attend the hospital, urgent retrieval
•	 I think the patient is/has ... (clinical impression/suspected diagnosis/
unsure but worried) eg I have a critically unwell man with sepsis; I have a
well female with diabetic foot ulcer
•	 Most recent vital signs and Appropriate Early Warning Tool' score, or
local EWARS
B
Background
•	 History of presenting problem, relevant past history
•	 Evaluation - physical examination, findings, investigation findings
•	 Allergies
•	 Current medicines
•	 I have …(taken the following actions eg given O2, inserted IVC, started IV
sodium chloride 0.9%)
A
Assessment and actions
•	 General appearance
•	 Weight + additional observations
•	 I am wanting … (advice, orders, retrieval)
•	 Level of urgency is ...
•	 Agree on plan of action with MO/retrieval coordinator
R
Recommendations and read back
•	 Confirm:

–
shared understanding of what needs to happen - who is doing what
and when

–
contact details - name, number to be called back on

–
follow up requirements and referrals

–
medication orders
•	 Read back critical information
•	 Identify parameters for review or escalation (any concerns - follow
normal/local escalation pathway)
•	 Identify any risks
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 29

Patient retrieval
1.
Who to contact1,2
•	 Usually the MO, or specialist if possible, will arrange retrieval if required
•	 If you think a patient may need retrieval, contact the relevant retrieval service
EARLY
•	 Notify change of clinical condition of patient if worsening or improving:

–
flight priority can always be reassessed
2.	 Retrieval preparation1,2
RETRIEVAL PREPARATION
Documentation
•	 Complete retrieval
•	 Ensure documentation goes with the patient including:

–
pre-hospital documentation

–
discharge summary - medical/surgical letter from
referring facility

–
observations forms, including all recent observations
+ current vital signs, neurological status, BGL,
neurovascular

–
current medication chart, with prn analgesia + cardiac
medications (as required)

–
IV fluid orders and fluid balance chart

–
Test result if available
Handover
location
•	 Handover location will be determined during the retrieval
coordination process
•	 If patient stabilised and prepared, handover at airport/
airstrip may occur
•	 Critical and unstable patients will be reviewed at the
referring facility by the retrieval team prior to transport
Patient escort
and baggage
Space and
weight
restrictions
apply
•	 An escort may be needed at the discretion of the pilot:

–
name + weight of escort required
•	 Medical aids/additional baggage at the pilot’s discretion
•	 Confirm no cigarette lighters, vapes, spare batteries and
aerosol cans in luggage
•	 Inform family/carers of plans/expectations
30 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

GENERAL PREPARATION
CONSIDERATION
REQUIREMENTS
RATIONALE
Allergies/
identification
•	 Apply ID bands if available
•	 Rapid correct
identification
Weight, height +
widest point
•	 If > 120 kg, measure width
at widest point eg shoulder
or hips
•	 Assists transport
planning
Analgesia
•	 Give analgesia as clinically
indicated prior to transfer
•	 See Acute pain
•	 Movement of the
patient may exacerbate
pain
Antiemetic
•	 Antiemetic essential if:
•	 head, spinal, or penetrating
eye injury
•	 Consider for history of
motion sickness or general
nausea
•	 Give 30 minutes prior to
transfer
•	 See Nausea and vomiting
•	 Vomiting may
exacerbate certain
clinical conditions
by raising ICP and
intraocular pressure
(IOP)
•	 Puts airway at risk
•	 Motion sickness
common in aeromedical
environment
IVC
•	 Ensure most patients have
at least 1 IVC
•	 IVC x 2 in critically ill/
disturbed patients
•	 IV access may be
difficult during
flight due to space
restrictions and
turbulence
CONSIDERATION
REQUIREMENTS
RATIONALE
IDC
•	 Get patients to empty their
bladder prior to transfer
•	 Insert IDC if is/may be
incontinent
•	 No toilet facilities on
aircraft
•	 Use of bedpans
is avoided due to
limitations of space and
waste disposal
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 31

GENERAL PREPARATION
Infusions
•	 Prior to transfer prepare
infusions using compatible
equipment, if possible,
when using retrieval
services
•	 Time is saved if
infusions are prepared
prior to of retrieval
team arrival
NGT/OGT
•	 Ensure NGT/OGT inserted
in:

–
all ventilated patients

–
patients with bowel
obstruction
•	 Allow for drainage of
stomach contents and
reduce risk of vomiting
and aspiration
SPECIFIC MEDICAL CONDITIONS
CONSIDERATION
REQUIREMENTS
RATIONALE
Mental illness/
disturbed
behaviour
•	 Reliable IV access. If
possible IVC x 2
•	 A risk assessment will be
completed by the retrieval
service
•	 Sedation and physical
restraint may be required.
Seek medical advice
•	 For aviation safety,
special requirements
apply to transportation
of patients showing
signs of disturbed
behaviour, or regarded
as being a danger to
themselves or others
Infectious
conditions
•	 Always notify retrieval
coordinator of:

–
infectious conditions ±
precautions

–
if immunocompromised
•	 Facilitate decision-
making regarding the
aeromedical transport
of infectious patients in
order to minimise risk
to other patients and
staff
•	 Limited ability to
isolate patients in
aircraft
Spinal injury
•	 Transport on vacuum
mattress
•	 Insert IDC
•	 Insert NGT if have altered
LOC
•	 To maintain
stabilisation
Bowel obstruction
•	 Insert NGT - leave on free
drainage or attach anti-
reflux valve (do not spigot)
•	 Give parenteral antiemetic
and adequate analgesia
prior to transfer
•	 Trapped gas will
expand in volume at
altitude and cause pain.
NGT will allow gas to
escape and reduce
vomiting
32 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

SPECIFIC MEDICAL CONDITIONS
Pneumothorax
•	 Chest drain insertion (by
MO) will done prior airlift
•	 Ensure chest drain is in
place
•	 Connect to ambulatory
chest drain system
•	 Suspected pneumothorax
should be excluded clinical
•	 Trapped gas in the
pleural cavity will
expand at altitude
and may result in
respiratory compromise
•	 Underwater seal drains
are avoided due to the
risk of retrograde flow
during transfer
Penetrating eye
injury
•	 Give antiemetic to all
patients with proven or
suspected eye injury
•	 Patients may be
transported at reduced
cabin altitude
•	 Trapped gas in the
globe will expand at
altitude and potentially
worsen the injury
•	 Vomiting may also
exacerbate injury by
raising intraocular
pressure
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 33

Pain, nausea and vomiting

Acute pain - Adult/Child
1.
May present with
•	 Acute pain
2.	 Immediate management
•	 If chest pain, go to Chest pain assessment
•	 If severe pain:

–
get rapid history + check for allergies

–
contact MO urgently

–
adult - insert IVC + give IV morphine or fentanyl
•	 If IVC delay, consider alternative routes e.g. IM/subcut
•	 If severe pain + looks sick - screen for Sepsis
•	 If sudden onset severe headache/‘worst headache of life’ - go to Headache
•	 If abdominal pain still give analgesia. Note: will not mask physical signs/hinder
diagnosis
3.	 Clinical assessment1,2
•	 The most useful diagnostic approach to acute pain is to take a detailed and
systematic history
•	 Ask about:

–
Site - where is it

–
Onset - when did it start:
	›
sudden or gradual
	›
result of trauma/activity/cold/stress

–
Characteristics eg sharp, throbbing, aching, burning, stabbing

–
Radiation - does it spread anywhere else

–
Associated symptoms eg nausea, vomiting, sweating, fever

–
Timing - duration, constant or intermittent:
	›
has anything changed the pain
	›
ever had this pain before, how often does it occur

–
Exacerbating or relieving factors:

–
eg rest, medicines, eating, position changes, ice/splinting

–
Severity - at rest, on movement:
	›
mild, moderate, severe
	›
scale from 0–10 (0 = no pain, 10 = worst pain imaginable)
	›
if child consider FLACC or FACES
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 35

•	 Ask about:

–
any pain relief already given/taken prior to presentation eg by carer, self, staff:
	›
when, what, dose, how effective

–
pain relief used in past - what worked/did not work, side effects
•	 Get past history, including:

–
current medicines, over-the-counter medicines

–
opioid use (if any)
•	 Take vital signs
•	 If child - take weight
Pain assessment scales
FLACC PAIN SCALE BIRTH–3 YEARS (OR AS REQUIRED EG NONVERBAL
PERSON)3 OBSERVE WITH LEGS + BODY UNCOVERED FOR 2–5 MINUTES
0
1
2
FACE
No particular
expression or
smile
Occasional grimace
or frown, withdrawn,
disinterested
Frequent to constant
frown, clenched jaw,
quivering chin
LEGS
Normal
position or
relaxed
Uneasy, restless, tense
Kicking or legs
drawn up
ACTIVITY
Lying quietly,
normal
position,
moves easily
Squirming, shifting,
back and forth, tense
Arched, rigid or
jerking
CRY
No cry
(awake or
asleep)
Moans or whimpers,
occasional complaint
Crying steadily,
screams, sobs,
frequent complaints
CONSOLABILITY
Content,
relaxed
Reassured by touching,
hugging or being
talked to, distractable
Difficult to console
or comfort
Calculate score: 0 = relaxed + comfortable, 1–3 = mild discomfort, 4–6 = moderate
pain, 7–10 = severe discomfort/pain
36 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

FACES 4+ years1,3
“These faces show how much something can hurt. This face [point to left-most
face] shows no pain. The faces show more and more pain [point to each from left
to right] up to this one [point to right-most face]. It shows very much pain. Point to
the face that shows how much you hurt [right now]”
Clinician to say ‘hurt’ or ‘pain’ (language child understands) - not words like ‘happy’
or ‘sad’
4.	 Management
•	 Consult MO if:

–
severe pain, even if settled after initial analgesia

–
fever, persistent tachycardia or tachypnoea, or hypotension

–
analgesia is not effective

–
unable to find cause of pain

–
recurrence of pre-existing condition

–
suspected opioid seeking
Analgesia1,4
•	 Consider medicine(s) already given
•	 Use Step wise approach + consider Non-pharmacological options
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 37

Step wise approach to acute pain management2,5
SEVERITY
ANALGESIA -
IF NOT ALLERGIC
PRACTICE POINTS
1–3
MILD
Step 1
Non-pharmacological
options AND/OR
paracetamol (L4)
•	 The paracetamol (L1) content of all
medicines must be considered. Advise
max. dosing to take at home
4–6
MODERATE
Step 2
As for step 1
AND/OR
ibuprofen* (L2)
•	 Combination of paracetamol (L1) +
ibuprofen (L2) is more effective than
the use of either alone
•	 *Always consider cautions/
contraindications, see Ibuprofen (L2)
drug box
7–10
SEVERE
Step 3
As for step 2
AND/OR
morphine (L4) or
fentanyl (L4)
•	 Check/monitor Sedation score
•	 Consider intranasal fentanyl (L4) for
child
•	 Note: IM/subcut absorption may
be impaired if poor perfusion eg
hypovolaemia, shock
•	 Preferably titrate via IV route
Non-pharmacological options1
•	 Ice, massage, heat pack, repositioning, distraction, imagery, warm/quiet room/
privacy, breathing techniques
•	 Elevation + splinting of injuries/suspected fractures - immobilisation can pain
significantly
•	 Reassurance - explain cause of pain + expected outcome to relieve anxiety
•	 If infant/young child - breastfeeding, low lighting, bubbles, cuddling carer/parent,
dummy use, swaddling
Monitor effect of analgesia
•	 Repeat pain scale:1,3

–
mild/moderate pain - 30–60 minutely as clinically indicated

–
severe pain - 10 minutely for 1st 30 minutes, then as required
•	 Do vital signs as appropriate + give antiemetic if Nausea and vomiting
•	 Note: if given subcut, monitor for at least 2 hours due to delayed absorption/
adverse effect

•	 Patient must be woken to assess sedation
SCORE

•	 Nil
1

nutrition, or if &lt; 5 kg) - max. dose to 45 mg/kg/24 hours (do not exceed 3 g/24
hours). Also see Guideline for safe paracetamol use https://www.health.qld.gov.
au/clinical-practice/ guidelines-procedures/medicines/safety

paracetamol toxicity without delay. Contact MA or MO at hospital

Note: Repeat doses until patient is more awake and breathing adequately. Patient
should improve in 1 minute. Failure to respond may indicate another cause of
unconsciousness. The duration of naloxone is short (15–30 minutes) compared
to opioids. Continue observation + monitor RR. May cause an acute withdrawal
syndrome in those with opioid dependence eg anxiety, agitation, tachycardia,
confusion
Pregnancy: Do not use in opioid dependent women, risk of withdrawal in fetus
Management of associated emergency: Consult MO. See Anaphylaxis5,18

•	 If morphine/fentanyl needed, patient will likely need retrieval for further

•	 If not retrieved, follow up as per MO + likely cause of pain
•	 If supplied with paracetamol or ibuprofen + further pain relief is needed after 48
hours, consult MO
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 Consult MO if further analgesia is needed + max. dose has been given

Nausea and vomiting - Adult/Child
Recommend
•	 Always consider life-threatening causes eg bowel obstruction, mesenteric
ischaemia, acute pancreatitis, myocardial infarction + head injury/raised
intracranial pressure1
•	 Offer antiemetic for aeromedical retrieval prophylaxis
•	 Beware-hypovolemia and hehydration may cause Nausea therefore adequate
hydration is important
1.
May present with
•	 Nausea ± vomiting
2.	 Immediate management
•	 If related to chest pain, go to Chest pain assessment
•	 Do vital signs + BGL
•	 Screen for Sepsis
ALERT suspect Button battery, in all children if vomiting blood. A button battery
lodged in the oesophagus can burn a hole through to the aorta causing catastrophic
haemorrhage
3.	 Clinical assessment
Check for red flags - if any contact MO urgently 2,3
•	 BGL may indicate DKA, BGL
•	 consider Hypoglycaemia, as cause
•	 Prolonged vomiting
•	 Looks very unwell/very drowsy
•	 Significant weight loss
•	 Abdominal distension or tenderness
•	 Rectal bleeding
•	 Green, bile or blood/coffee grounds
vomit
•	 Fever, neck stiffness, confusion
•	 Severe headache, altered LOC
•	 Isolated vomiting, lack of nausea
•	 History of head trauma/injury
•	 Bulging fontanelle - infant/young
child
•	 Child - T > 39 or 38 if &lt; 3 months
•	 Projectile vomiting if 3–6 weeks of
age. See Pyloric stenosis
44 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

•	 Always try to identify the cause of the nausea/vomiting1
•	 Get history, including:3

–
duration of illness + frequency of vomiting

–
timing in relation to eating

–
food eaten in last 24 hours - could it be food poisoning

–
similar symptoms in family members/close contacts

–
pregnancy

–
exposure to toxins/poisons/bites/stings

–
recent illicit drug use, alcohol/hangover related

–
recent travel
•	 Ask about other symptoms eg:1,2

–
chest pain, heartburn

–
headache, vertigo or dizziness

–
last bowel motion, any diarrhoea

–
related to motion/travel

–
dysuria or frequency of urine

–
fever
•	 Get past history, including:1

–
current medicines, over-the-counter medicines, previous antiemetics

–
recent initiation of a new medicine(s)

–
diabetes

–
abdominal surgery
•	 Do physical examination, including:

–
Hydration assessment - adult, or child

–
+ as determined from history eg:
	›
GCS
	›
Abdominal examination
	›
urinalysis. Note: urinalysis cannot reliably exclude UTI in infants + young
children3
	›
pregnancy test if reproductive age

–
if child, take weight
4.	 Management1
•	 Urgently contact MO if any Red flags
•	 Contact MO if:

–
child/infant, also see Child with vomiting

–
no obvious cause/unsure

–
suspected poisoning

–
re-presentation/parental concerns
•	 If pregnant - seek advice from MO - avoid antiemetic if possible:4

–
severe vomiting that starts in late pregnancy may indicate Preeclampsia

–
if hyperemesis gravidarum (extreme morning sickness) Seek MO Advice
•	 Otherwise, treat cause if known - be guided by relevant topic
•	 If probable gastroenteritis ± dehydrated, see Gastroenteritis - adult, or
Gastroenteritis child
•	 Offer antiemetic as needed + monitor effect - contact MO if not effective

46 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Emergency

Basic life support (BLS) - Adult/Child
DRSABCD resuscitation, the collapsed patient
Recommend 1-4
•	 Good quality CPR and reducing time to defibrillation are the highest priorities in
resuscitation from sudden cardiac arrest
•	 Airway takes precedent over any injury, including possibility of spinal injury -
handle gently with no twisting or bending of spinal column or neck1
•	 If pregnant, once CPR in progress put padding such as a towel under the
woman’s right hip to tilt the hips about 15–30⁰, but leave her shoulders flat
•	 Child/infant - central pulse palpation may be used to asess circulation. If a pulse
cannot be definitely identified within 10 seconds (or HR &lt; 60 with signs of poor
circulation) start CPR
Background
•	 Agonal breaths (occasional irregular gasps) are common in early stages of
cardiac arrest
1.
May present with 2
•	 Sudden collapse
•	 Unresponsive and absent or abnormal breathing ie gasping
2.	 Immediate management 1-6
•	 DRSABCD as per Basic life support flowchart
•	 Danger - check the environment/patient surroundings are safe before
proceeding. PPE as needed
•	 Response - verbal and tactile stimuli (talk and touch)
•	 Send for help - shout for help. If help not arrived in 1 minute go get help 5+
contact MO/RSQ
•	 If trauma related control catastrophic haemorrhage
•	 Airway (leave person in position found):1-5

–
open using head tilt–chin lift or jaw thrust (if neck injury, only use jaw thrust) +
	›
child - head in ‘sniffing position’
	›
infant &lt; 1 year - head in neutral position

–
if fluid/matter obstructs airway at any time:
	›
use suction if available OR if unavailable, roll on to side to clear airway 1
	›
if visible material, remove if possible eg using rescuer’s fingers 1
48 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

•	 Breathing:

–
Look (movement of chest/abdomen), Listen (for breath sounds), Feel (air on
rescuer’s cheek)

–
if unresponsive but breathing normally, see Unconscious patient
•	 If unresponsive + breathing absent or NOT normal, place on firm surface and
start CPR:

–
30 compressions: 2 ventilations (adult + child) OR

–
if paediatric BLS trained - child: 2  ventilations (first): 15 compressions 5

–
100–120 compressions per minute
•	 Defibrillation - attach automated external defibrillator (AED) as soon as available
and follow prompts:

–
adult and child > 8 use adult pads. Child 1–8 use paediatric pads (or adult size
if not available)

–
continue CPR + minimise interruptions to chest compressions

–
alternate person doing compressions when AED advises to stop
(eg to analyse rhythm/shock) or if fatigued
•	 Get ALS trained clinician(s) if available
•	 Continue CPR until responsive, normal breathing returns, or MO/SNO /MA
instructs otherwise
3.	 Clinical assessment - See Immediate management
4.	 Management
•	 Control bleeding, protect from weather + other first aid measures depending on
circumstances
5.	 Follow up
•	 According to patient’s condition/presentation
•	 Sensitive, professional debriefing of people involved in resuscitation is valuable
•	 Provide support for family members
•	 If required, see Patient death in absence of MA,
6.	 Referral/consultation
Always contact Medical Officer

52 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Intravenous (IV) cannulation
General considerations
Insertion of a cannula into a vein to administer fluids or medications.
•	 Common in emergency and elective settings.
•	 Requires good knowledge of anatomy, technique, and asepsis
•	 It is recommended to choose a straight portion of a vein to minimize the chance
of hitting valves.
•	 Use the patient’s non­ dominant arm (if possible)
•	 For prolonged courses of therapy, it is recommended to start distally and move
proximally as distal catheters are replaced.
•	 The superficial veins of the upper extremities are preferred to those of the
lower extremities for peripheral venous access as they interfere less with patient
mobility and pose a lower risk for phlebitis.
•	 DO NOT INSERT A CANNULA IN AN ARTERY – risk of limb ischemia.
•	 In a diabetic patient, avoid lower limb cannulation
Venous access allows:
•	 sampling of blood
•	 administration of IV fluids and medications
Contraindications
•	 No absolute contraindications to intravenous cannulation exist
•	 Avoid peripheral venous access in an injured, infected, or burned extremity if
possible
Equipments
•	 Non-sterile gloves,
•	 Tourniquet,
•	 Antiseptic wipes,
•	 5-ml syringe,
•	 Sterile gauze,
•	 Cannula,
•	 Saline- flush,
•	 Adhesive Plaster
Veins Commonly Used for IV Access:
•	 Cephalic vein (thumb side)
•	 Basilic vein (little finger side)
•	 Median cubital vein (elbow crease)
•	 Dorsal veins of the hand
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 53

VEIN
ARTERY

Intraosseous access - Adult/Child
Recommend
•	 Use if urgent need for fluids/medications + difficult/failed IVC access1
•	 Pain from intraosseous infusion under pressure is more severe than insertion2
•	 Check for contraindications:2

–
fracture in or adjacent to the target bone

–
excessive tissue ± absence of adequate anatomical landmarks

–
infection at site

–
previous significant orthopaedic procedure at the site, prosthetic joint

–
Intraosseous access/attempted access in targeted bone within last 48 hours
•	 Prepare equipment + site:

–
select site + needle set, as per Insertion guide (next page)1,2

–
clean site using antiseptic + aseptic technique

–
note: lidocaine (lignocaine) is not usually needed for insertion of intraosseous
needle

–
if responsive to pain, before infusion prime extension set with lidocaine
(lignocaine) 1% approx. 1 mL (10 mg)1
•	 Insert intraosseous needle (instructions next page)
•	 Once inserted:1-3

–
place stabilising dressing over the hub

–
aspirate for blood/bone marrow. The inability to withdraw/aspirate blood does
not mean

–
unsuccessful insertion, consider re-trying after the flush

–
attach primed extension set + flush with lidocaine (lignocaine) 1% OR sodium
chloride 0.9% if unresponsive

–
connect fluids. Infusion may need to be pressurised to get desired rate ie
pressure bag/ pump

INSERTION GUIDE2
Needle set
•	 Select needle set based on patient
weight, anatomy + clinical judgment
•	 45 mm needle set (yellow hub)
proximal humerus > 40 kg + patients
with excessive tissue over any
insertion site
•	 25 mm needle set (blue hub) > 3 kg
•	 15 mm needle set (pink hub) approx.
3–39 kg
Proximal humerus
•	 Place patient’s hand over the
abdomen, place your palm on the
shoulder anteriorly. The ‘ball’ under
your palm is the general target area.
Aim needle downward at approx. 45°
angle
•	 Note: abduction of arm a!er
placement will dislodge needle
Proximal tibia
•	 Extend the leg, feel for flat aspect of
tibia
•	 Approx. 3 cm (1–2 cm in infant/child)
below the patella + approx. 2 cm
medial (1 cm in infant/child) to the
tibial tuberosity along the flat aspect
of the tibia (depending on anatomy).
Aim needle at a 90° angle to the
bone
Distal tibia
•	 Approx. 3 cm (infant 1 cm/1 "nger)
above most prominent aspect of
medial malleolus. Feel for anterior
and posterior borders of tibia to
assure that insertion site is in the flat
centre of the bone. Aim needle at a
90° angle to the bone
Distal femur - child only
•	 Secure site with leg outstretched to
ensure knee does not bend. Feel for
patella, insertion site is just proximal
to superior border (max. 1–2 cm) and
approx. 1–2 cm medial
•	 Arrow EZ-IO Vascular Access System
images courtesy of Teleflex
growth plate
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 57

How to insert Arrow EZ-IO® device2
•	 Attach needle set to driver + remove safety cap
•	 Push needle tip through the skin until tip touches the bone:

–
before pressing the trigger, check at least 1 black line is visible above the skin
to confirm adequate needle set length
•	 Gently drill. Immediately stop advancing + release the trigger when you feel a
‘pop’ or ‘give’ as the needle set enters the medullary space:

–
do NOT pull/jerk back on the driver when releasing the trigger
•	 Hold the hub + pull the driver straight off needle set. Continue to hold hub in
place while twisting the stylet off with anticlockwise rotations
•	 See instructions + video at www.teleflex.com/anz/en/product-areas/emergency-
medicine/intraosseous-access/arrow-ez-io-system/clinical-resources/index.html
Manual insertion
•	 Hold the needle set with the catheter hub and stylet as 1 piece. Ensure stylet +
hub stay screwed together
•	 Rotate clockwise/anticlockwise while applying gentle, moderate steady
downward pressure without rocking the needle set. Allow rotation + pressure to
penetrate the bone cortex, not excessive force
•	 Child - stop when change of pressure or resistance felt as a ‘pop’ or ‘give’
indicating entry to medullary space
•	 Adult - advance needle set approx. 1–2 cm after entry into medullary space,
which is felt as a change in resistance; in the proximal humerus for most adults
the needle set should be advanced 2cm or until hub is flush or against the skin
After insertion
•	 Monitor closely + contact MO if:1,2

–
intraosseous lidocaine (lignocaine) is not sufficient + the patient is still in pain:
	›
MO may consider systemic analgesia

–
change in patient’s response to medications

–
flow rate/alarms on infusion pump

–
fever or signs of:
	›
extravasation/infiltration at site
	›
Compartment syndrome, in limb
58 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Diving accidents and decompression sickness
Recommend
•	 Emphasise diver safety.
•	 Advise all divers to attend certified safe diving training classes.
•	 Visitors should only dive if qualified.
•	 There is no hyperbaric chamber in the islands.
Background
Diving-related problems are increasingly common due to air travel and deeper
dives. As depth increases, pressure rises from air and water, leading to increased risk
of barotrauma and gas-related injury. Most underwater deaths are related to diver
inexperience, carelessness, or equipment failure. Key hazards include air embolism,
decompression sickness, and sinus-related injuries due to blocked passages in the
nose, ears, and sinuses.
1.
May present with
Air Embolism
•	 Symptoms appear within 2 minutes of ascent
•	 Personality changes
•	 Seizures
•	 Loss of consciousness
•	 Neurological deficits (motor or sensory)
•	 Pneumothorax
•	 Shock
Decompression Sickness
•	 Joint pain (“the bends”) – deep aching or throbbing
•	 Skin bubbles, mottling, rashes, itching, crawling sensation
•	 Neurological symptoms – mood change, altered consciousness, seizures
•	 Lung symptoms – chest pain, cough, dyspnoea, cyanosis, shock
Ear, Nose and Sinus Problems
•	 Severe ear or sinus pain
•	 Hearing difficulty
•	 Nosebleed or blood behind tympanic membrane
•	 Vertigo
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 59

2.	 Immediate management
Air Embolism
•	 Place patient on left side with head down (to keep bubbles in right side of heart)
•	 Protect cervical spine if injury suspected (use collar or stabilisation)
•	 Maintain airway
•	 Give 100% oxygen at 7–8 L/min (continue until exhausted; give air breaks only for
assessment or hydration)
•	 Insert IV line and run normal saline rapidly if hypotensive
•	 Give IV Valium 5–10 mg slowly (diluted to 10 mL with sterile water or saline)
•	 If not breathing, check central pulse — if absent or doubtful, begin BLS
•	 Apply direct pressure to stop any bleeding (do not use tourniquet)
•	 Transfer to hyperbaric chamber as soon as possible (delayed therapy can still
help)
•	 Use aircraft pressurised or fly at sea-level altitude
Decompression Sickness
•	 Manage as for air embolism:
•	 Left side, head down
•	 100% oxygen at 8–10 L/min
•	 IV fluids rapidly if BP low
•	 Valium IV slowly for seizures
•	 Transfer to hyperbaric chamber as soon as possible
Ear, Nose and Sinus Problems
•	 Beconase AQ nasal spray: 2 sprays per nostril, twice a day for 3–5 days
•	 Promethazine (Phenergan) 25 mg orally twice daily for 3–5 days
•	 If patient has had similar problems in the past:

–
Begin above therapy 1 week before diving and continue through diving period
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Full head-to-toe check for signs of gas embolism, sinus trauma, or hypoxia
•	 Neurological examination (motor, sensory, behaviour, speech)
•	 Chest and lung assessment
•	 Ear examination (including tympanic membrane)
•	 BP, HR, RR, oxygen saturation

5.	 Follow up
•	 Observe for delayed symptoms (joint pain, fatigue, confusion)
•	 Monitor vital signs closely
•	 Advise against further diving until cleared by a doctor
•	 Document dive history, including duration, depth, ascent pattern, and previous
symptoms
6.	 Referral / consultation
•	 Consult doctor all cases of:

–
Air embolism

–
Decompression sickness
•	 Also consult for:

–
Vertigo

–
Hearing loss

–
Blood in middle ear
Prevention and patient education
•	 Educate divers about:

–
Safe diving practices

–
Importance of certification

–
Lack of hyperbaric chamber in Kiribati
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 61

•	 Advise against diving for people with:

–
Lung disease or asthma

–
History of pneumothorax

–
Epilepsy or seizures

–
Migraine

–
Stroke

–
Heart disease or failure

–
Chronic ear problems or surgery

–
Vertigo

–
Pregnancy

–
Diabetes

–
Bleeding disorders
Additional diving precautions
•	 Do not dive:

–
If flown within last 24 hours

–
After non-decompression dive or dive longer than 2 hours in past 48 hours
62 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Critical emergencies
Unconscious/altered level of consciousness -
Adult/Child
Recommend
•	 Unconsciousness is a medical emergency - early stability + diagnosis are vital1
•	 Assume a serious cause until proven otherwise1
1.
May present with2
Unconscious/altered level of consciousness (LOC)
2.	 Immediate management2
•	 DRSABCD as per BLS
•	 Assist patient onto the ground/bed + position on side
•	 Do NOT leave sitting in a chair or put head between their knees
•	 Open airway - airway takes precedence over any injury:

–
handle gently, avoid twisting or forward movement of head + spine

–
use head tilt/chin lift for adult + child > 1 years

–
infant - keep head neutral
•	 Call for help + contact MO urgently
•	 Stop any bleeding
•	 Do:

–
vital signs:
	›
note pattern + regularity of breathing:1

»
deep, laboured (Kussmaul respiration) - often associated with DKA

»
shallow with extremely  RR - seen in opioid overdose

»
hyperventilation

–
give O2 if required to maintain SpO2 ≥ 94%

–
BGL - if &lt; 4 treat immediately. See Hypoglycaemia

–
insert IVC x 2

–
neurological observations ie:3
	›
GCS - if ≤ 8 requires secured airway, consider LMA, until patient can be
intubated1
	›
pupil size + reaction to light
	›
motor response in limbs
	›
fontanelle in infant
•	 If hypotensive, give rapid IV sodium chloride 0.9% 10–20 mL/kg. See Shock
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 63

3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Do not leave patient alone if possible
•	 Constantly re-check for any change, including:2,4
•	 airway for any signs of obstruction eg:
	›
laboured/noisy breathing, or no sound of breathing

›
 WOB eg intercostal recession, nasal flaring in infants
	›
abdomen moves in/out, but loss of natural rise of chest

–
GCS - if drops ≥ 2 points urgently contact MO
•	 Get rapid history from friends/relatives or bystanders:1

–
was the loss of consciousness witnessed

–
when/what happened prior/what were they doing

–
prior headache/chest pain/other symptom

–
ingestions, IV drug use

–
trauma

–
seizures/abnormal movements
•	 Look for clues/causes eg:1

–
trauma

–
signs of stroke

–
overdose eg suicide note, empty medicine packet(s), needle + syringe
	›
if suspected opioid give naloxone
	›
see Toxicology assessment

–
alcohol or substance/drug use
	›
note: if alcohol intoxication suspected, continue to look for other causes1
•	 infection, especially the elderly
•	 bruising/minor injuries
•	 indications of known allergies eg jewellery/accessory eg key ring, USB stick, shoe
tag, anklet, watch, tattoo

•	 Get past history as able (from relatives/friends/medical records):

–
known underlying illness eg epilepsy, diabetes, cancer

–
allergies

–
medications eg anticoagulants

–
recent surgery/hospitalisation

–

–
listen to chest - any wheeze, crackles

–
head to toe examination - check for:
	›
any odour eg fruity sweet smelling breath. Note: acetone - similar to paint
thinner or nail polish remover - may indicate DKA,
	›
skin eg rash, drug injection sites, puncture wounds
	›
remove all clothing as you move down, maintain privacy + keep patient
warm
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 65

4.	 Management
•	 Ongoing management as per MO, which may include:

–
evacuation + treatment according to suspected cause
5.	 Follow up
•	 According to possible cause - as per MO
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 As above
66 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Shock - Adult/Child
Background
•	 Shock is a loss of effective circulation resulting in impaired tissue oxygen +
nutrient delivery and causes life-threatening organ failure. Any seriously ill or
seriously injured patient is at risk of developing shock1
•	 Causes include - sepsis, anaphylaxis, major haemorrhage, large fluid loss + rarely
obstruction of cardiac outflow + cardiogenic failure
•	 Assume a serious cause until proven otherwise1
1.
May present with1,2
•	 Signs of shock:

–
 LOC

–
 perfusion - mottled or pale skin, cool extremities, capillary refill > 2
seconds ± clammy

–
 HR,  RR
2.	 Immediate management
•	 DRSABCD
•	 Call for help + urgently contact MO when able
•	 If signs of shock + suspected anaphylaxis:

–
give IM adrenaline (epinephrine) 1:1,000 (see Anaphylaxis, for doses)

–
repeat every 5 minutes if needed
•	 Lie the person down, if unconscious place on side1
•	 Start resuscitation
•	 Give O2 15 L/minute via non-rebreather mask 1,2
•	 Insert IVC x 2 eg 18 G or Intraosseous,  if 2 failed attempts
•	 Rapidly give bolus IV sodium chloride 0.9%:2-4

–
adult 250–500 mL, child 20 mL/kg

–
reassess + repeat if no improvement in HR, LOC or perfusion
•	 If persisting signs of shock after fluid bolus x 2 - urgently contact MO +

–
consider Sepsis, if suspected or no other cause of shock identified, initiate
sepsis pathway + give broad spectrum antibiotics2,3
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 67

•	 Monitor:
	›
vital signs, GCS, capillary refill time
•	 Continuous cardiac monitoring2
•	 Look for cause of shock:

–
haemorrhage eg:
	›
Ectopic pregnancy - do pregnancy test
	›
traumatic injuries
	›
note: if haemorrhagic shock MO may order Tranexamic acid  - give within
3hrs
	›
Upper GI bleeding, Button battery, Rectal bleeding

–
large fluid loss eg Gastroenteritis - adult, or Gastroenteritis - child

–
sepsis/septic shock, anaphylaxis, brain/spinal injuries

–
cardiac dysfunction3 eg ACS,

–
blockage of blood flow in/around the heart eg PE
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Get rapid history, ask about:

–
recent illness, nausea/vomiting, diarrhoea

–
decreased urine output

–
immunocompromised

–
underlying conditions eg cancer, heart disease, diabetes

–
any medications

–
recent drugs or alcohol
•	 Do physical assessment, including:

–
listen to chest - any wheeze, crackles

–
urinalysis

–
check skin - any rash, wounds, bruising
•	 abdomen:

–
listen to bowel sounds - present/absent - if absent, consider Bowel
obstruction

–
palpate for any tenderness, guarding, rigidity, rebound tenderness, masses
4.	 Management
•	 Ongoing management as per MO + likely cause of shock
5.	 Follow up
•	 As per MO
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 Patient might need referral to the main hospital after discussion with MO
68 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Sepsis/septic shock - Adult/Child
Recommend
•	 Sepsis is a medical emergency - early recognition + rapid treatment is imperative
for survival. Consider in every patient with fever or acute illness 1,2
•	 Initial presentation can be vague. Always have a high index of suspicion in
neonates, young infants, the elderly and immunocompromised patients
•	 If suspected sepsis – initiate treatment  + discuss with MA.
•	 Sepsis = infection + organ dysfunction 1,2
•	 Children can have sepsis with normal BP. Hypotension is a late sign of shock
•	 Neonates + infants &lt; 1 year are at highest risk as immature immune systems are
unable to ward off severe infections.
Background
•	 Septic shock = shock + infection. Sepsis = infection + organ dysfunction2
1.
May present with
•	 Consider in all patients with acute illness or deterioration who may have an
infection5
2.	 Immediate management 1,2
•	 DRSABCD
•	 Call for help + urgently contact MO when able
•	 If signs of shock + suspected anaphylaxis:

–
give IM adrenaline (epinephrine) 1:1,000 (see Anaphylaxis, for doses)

–
repeat every 5 minutes if needed
•	 Lie the person down, if unconscious place on side1
•	 Start resuscitation
•	 Give O2 15 L/minute via non-rebreather mask 1,2
•	 Insert IVC x 2 eg 18 G or Intraosseous, if 2 failed attempts
•	 Rapidly give bolus IV sodium chloride 0.9%:2-4

–
adult 250–500 mL, child 20 mL/kg

–
reassess + repeat if no improvement in HR, LOC or perfusion
•	 If persisting signs of shock after fluid bolus x 2 - urgently contact MO +

–
consider Sepsis, if suspected or no other cause of shock identified, initiate
sepsis pathway + give broad spectrum antibiotics2,3
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 69

•	 Signs of infection or history and evidence of fever or hypothermia
•	 PLUS ANY of the following:

–
looks sick or toxic

–
patient, parental, carer or
clinician concern

–
re-presentation with same illness

–
immunocompromised*

–
altered behaviour or ↓LOC

–
&lt; 3 months

–
sepsis admission within last 30
days
