Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 167

Neurological Emergencies
Headache  - Adult/Child
Background
•	 Aim to identify red flags of serious causes of headaches
•	 Also see Headache - emergency management in children
 https://www.childrens.health.qld.gov.au/qpec-statewide-guidelines/
Related Topics – Meningitis and Fitting
1.
May present with1
•	 Headache
2.	 Immediate management1
•	 Consult MO/MA urgently if any of:

–
sudden onset severe headache - ‘thunderclap headache’ or ‘worst headache
of life’:

–
suspect subarachnoid haemorrhage

–
confusion, drowsiness or neurological changes

–
severe  BP. Also see Hypertensive emergency
•	 MO/MA may advise:

–
urgent evacuation

–
insert IVC

–
analgesia
•	 closely monitor:

–
vital signs

–
conscious level. See GCS/AVPU

–
neurological observations
168 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

1.
Clinical assessment 1-3
•	 Get history of headache:

–
pain - nature, intensity, impact on normal activities

–
is this a ‘typical’ headache

–
onset - when, sudden/slow

–
provoking factors eg exercise, cough, sneeze, sexual activity

–
exacerbating factors eg erect position, exertion/valsalva, worse in mornings,
food eg caffeine

–
other symptoms:
	›
fever, vomiting
	›
aura, visual symptoms, photophobia
	›
neck pain, facial pain/tenderness

–
recent:
	›
head trauma
	›
travel
	›
infection in neck or head area
	›
sun/heat exposure

–
pregnant or recently postnatal

–
drug/alcohol use
•	 Past medical history:

–
recent medication change

–
immunocompromised, cancer

–
hypertension
•	 Do physical examination, including:

–
vital signs

–
GCS

–
check for Neck stiffness

–
Hydration assessment - adult, or child

–
pregnancy test if female of reproductive age
•	 Assess for ANY red flags
2.	 Management 1-3
•	 Consult MO/MA promptly if:

–
any red flags

–
headache is atypical or not ‘usual’ for patient

–
persistent headache, despite treatment with oral analgesia
•	 If pregnant assess for Preeclampsia
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 169

Migraine2,4
•	 If child consult MO/MA
•	 If adult says headache is typical of a migraine (that they have previously had)
offer:

–
ibuprofen with paracetamol (avoid opioids). See Acute pain

–
AND metoclopramide - even if not nauseous as improves absorption of
analgesia. See Nausea and vomiting
•	 Consider:

–
cold packs over forehead and back of skull

–
hot packs to neck and shoulders

–
neck stretches

–
rest in a dark room

–
Lifestyle habits
Tension type headache 2,5
•	 Usually bilateral, feels like pressure or tightness in head, mild to moderate
intensity (not severe enough to prevent walking or climbing stairs), ±
photophobia, sensitivity to noise, not usually nauseous:

–
offer ibuprofen ± paracetamol. See Acute pain

–
consider advice on Lifestyle habits
Lifestyle habits - to help prevent migraines and tension type headaches 4,5
•	 Regular sleep schedule
•	 Minimise variations in BGL eg eat regular meals, avoid excess sugary food/drinks
•	 Stay hydrated eg drink 1.5–2 L of water/day
•	 Limit caffeinated drinks to 1–2 cups/day
•	 Regular exercise
•	 Use relaxation techniques to manage stress eg mindfulness, yoga, breathing
techniques
•	 Avoid known triggers for patient eg alcohol, citrus fruit, chocolate, preserved
meats, perfume
•	 If posture problems or neck muscle tightness, refer to a physiotherapist
3.	 Follow up
•	 If not evacuated, advise to return if headache does not improve or if concerned
4.	 Referral/consultation
•	 Consult MO as above
•	 Might need Medical Clinic referral

170 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Dizziness/vertigo - Adult
Recommend
•	 Dizziness and vertigo are common symptoms, with many causes. Aim to identify
serious causes
Background1
•	 Dizziness common causes - postural hypotension, arrhythmias, syncope,
anaemia, hypoglycaemia, hypercalcaemia, “vitamin B12, medicine side e ect,
anxiety,2 hyperventilation3
•	 Vertigo - can be central (brain stem or cerebellum) or peripheral (inner ear)
related
1.
May present with1
•	 Dizziness, faintness or feeling like body or surroundings are moving eg spinning
2.	 Immediate management1,2,4,5
•	 Check for Red flags:

–
if any, get rapid history and contact MO urgently. Suspect Stroke

–
MO may consider retrieval for CT/MRI ± referral to neurologist
Red flags
•	 Facial weakness/numbness
•	 Arm ± leg weakness, ataxia
•	 Speech di#culty/slurred
•	 Visual eld loss, double vision
•	 Difficulty swallowing
•	 Sensory loss eg in 1 side of body
•	 Confusion
•	 Headache
•	 New sudden unilateral hearing loss with 1st onset of continuous dizziness/vertigo
3.	 Clinical assessment1,2,4,5
•	 Get history of dizziness/vertigo, including:2

–
timing - episodic or continuous; gradual or sudden onset

–
when occurred, are episodes
 in severity or frequency4

–
duration of episodes - seconds/minutes/hours/days

–
character:
	›
vertigo - spinning, rocking or tilting2
	›
dizziness - o balance, giddiness (light headed), faint (presyncope)

–
spontaneous or triggered by eg head movement, sitting, standing, rolling over,
tipping head back, lying down, loud noises, anxiety/stress
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 171

•	 Any associated symptoms eg:

–
nausea, retching/vomiting, sweating2

–
new hearing loss - 1 or both ears, fluctuating/continuous

–
tinnitus - noise or ringing in ears OR ear discharge/pain or ‘full’ sensation of
ear

–
vision issues eg bouncing, bobbing or jerking of still objects (oscillopsia)6
•	 Get past history - including migraines, Ménière’s disease, current medicines
•	 Any recent:

–
change of medicine eg for BP

–
illness eg URTI

–
ear trauma or infection

–
head trauma - even minor

–
alcohol or drug use, exposure to toxin(s)

–
diving or other pressure change (that may cause barotrauma)
•	 Do physical examination + assess:

–
vital signs + lying and standing BP - does BP drop when standing, how much

–
BGL, ECG

–
eyes - any nystagmus - quick jerky eye movements (vertical or horizontal),
either spontaneous or only when looking in a speci c direction

–
Examine ears

–
observe patient’s ability to stand and walk. Are they unsteady
If no red flags, consider additional assessments (as below) in consultation with
MO
•	 Dix-Hallpike test if:1,2,5,7

–
vertigo is episodic +

–
is only triggered by speci c head movement +

–
lasts only seconds +

–
nystagmus not occurring spontaneously +

–
no other symptoms
•	 HINTS plus exam if:

–
vertigo has continued for hours/days + nystagmus occurring spontaneously3-5,8
DIX-HALLPIKE TEST4
SEE VIDEO HTTPS://WWW.YOUTUBE.COM/WATCH?V=LXD-LGQIX-S
OFFER TO SHOW PATIENT VIDEO 1ST, SO THEY KNOW WHAT TO EXPECT.
WARN PATIENT IT CAN PROVOKE VERTIGO
Get patient to sit at the edge of the bed. Turn their head with your hands 30–45o
to 1 side
Patient to keep eyes open + focus on a stable point. Then quickly assist patient to
lie supine and hyperextend the neck 30–45o below level of bed, while still holding
head. Look for nystagmus
If no nystagmus seen, return to seated position, then test the other side
If patient gets nystagmus and reproduction of positional symptoms, test is +ve for
Benign paroxysmal positional vertigo (BPPV)1 - a common (not harmful) cause of
vertigo due to disturbance of tiny crystals in inner ear1
172 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

HINTS PLUS EXAM2,4,5
SEE VIDEO HTTPS://COLLECTIONS.LIB.UTAH.EDU/ARK:/87278/S6BP04F0
CAN ASSIST IN DETERMINING IF VERTIGO HAS A CENTRAL OR PERIPHERAL
CAUSE. DISCUSS FINDINGS WITH MO
Head
impulse test
•	 Sit opposite patient and ask them to stare at your nose
•	 Hold the patient's head with both of your hands and turn head
slowly side to side 10–20O then back to midpoint. Repeat
but rapidly rotate the head to 1 side and pause. Patient's eyes
should stay on the examiners nose with the rapid head turn
•	 Repeat again but this time rapidly rotate to the opposite side
•	 If eyes do a series of 'catch-up' movements to re-fixate on
nose, test is +ve
•	 Document the direction the head was rotated to when the
'catch-up' movement occurred - eg if catch-up movement was
seen when head was quickly rotated to the patient's le', then
document ‘+ve left head impulse test’
Nystagmus
•	 Ask patient to follow your finger as you slowly move it right to
left
•	 Observe direction of the fast phase of nystagmus - horizontal,
vertical, rotates, unidirectional or direction changing
•	 Does nystagmus stop if patient stares at object
Test of skew
•	 Ask patient to look straight ahead. Cover 1 of their eyes with
your hand. Then, quickly move your hand to cover the other
eye
•	 During this process, observe uncovered eye for any vertical/
diagonal corrective movement (if present, test is +ve)
Plus acute
hearing loss
•	 Rub fingers next to both ears and ask if new unilateral hearing
loss. If new and 1st onset of acute continuous vertigo, this is a
+ve for a central sign9
4.	 Management1,5
•	 Consider calling vertigo hotline Dial-a-Dizzy
 1300 4 DIZZY, for advice (office
hours)
•	 If BPPV likely:

–
offer to do Epley manoeuvre (see video for Dix-Hallpike test for instructions).
It may relieve vertigo immediately

–
offer advice and exercises to help, as per fact sheet eg www.safercare.vic.gov.
au/ best-practice-improvement/clinical-guidance/emergency/vertigo
•	 If already diagnosed with Ménière's disease and acute attack, offer
prochlorperazine10
•	 In all other cases of dizziness or vertigo consult MO:

–
+ if nausea/vomiting, o er prochlorperazine or promethazine
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 173

Offer CMI: May make you sleepy - avoid driving/operating machinery if affected.

Management of associated emergency: Consult MO. See Anaphylaxis,5,10,11

5.	 Follow up
•	 In all cases, if not retrieved, advise to see MO at next clinic, or to return if
symptoms worsen/ change
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 As above
174 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Transient ischaemic attack (TIA) and stroke -
Adult/Child
Recommend
•	 TIA + stroke are medical emergencies. Urgent evacuation + timing of treatment is
critical1
•	 Advise community about FAST stroke recognition message:2

–
Facial weakness, Arm ± leg weakness, Speech difficulty, Time to act fast
Background
•	 A stroke occurs when the arteries to the brain become blocked (ischaemic
stroke/cerebral infarction) or rupture (haemorrhagic stroke), disrupting blood
supply leading to brain damage1
•	 TIA - transient episode of neurological dysfunction, most are brief eg &lt; 1 hour +
resolve in 24hours with no brain infarction 1,3
•	 See Transient ischaemic attack (TIA)/stroke clinical pathway https://
clinicalexcellence.qld.gov.au/resources/clinical-pathways/tia-stroke-clinical-
pathways
1.
May present with1,3
•	 Suspect stroke if abrupt onset of ≥ 1 of:

–
asymmetric:
	›
face weakness
	›
arm weakness
	›
leg weakness

–
speech disturbance eg slurred speech

–
visual field defect/vision loss
•	 Note: any abrupt change in neurological function could represent stroke
2.	 Immediate management
•	 DRSABCD
•	 Do BGL - if &lt; 3.5 contact MO urgently + treat as per MO 1,3
•	 If persistent symptoms for &lt; 9 hours - patient may be thrombolysis candidate -
avoid delays2
•	 Get rapid history, including:3

–
symptoms + time of onset. If unknown, the last time the patient was seen to
be well

–
are symptoms resolving or persisting

–
current medicines eg anticoagulant/antiplatelet medications

–
any seizures, loss of consciousness or syncope
•	 Suspect TIA if symptoms have resolved. Ask about duration:1
•	 &lt;
 10 minutes, 10 minutes–1 hour or > 1 hour
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 175

•	 Contact MO/MA urgently in all cases of suspected stroke/TIA + urgent
evacuation
•	 Do vital signs + GCS
•	 Only give O2 if SpO2 &lt; 92% 1,4
•	 ECG only if does not cause delay. If new atrial fibrillation, send copy of ECG with
patient
•	 Continuous cardiac monitoring
•	 Insert IVC x 2
•	 Take bloods - FBC, UE, LFT, coagulation studies, group and hold for x-match
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Get past history + any TIA/stroke or similar symptoms3
•	 Do physical examination + weight (if possible)3
4.	 Management
•	 Ongoing management as per MO
•	 Brain imaging eg CT scan equired to determine cause1-3
•	 Check ability to swallow before giving oral medications:

–
if difficulty swallowing - keep nil by mouth
•	 Give paracetamol if fever
•	 If ischaemic stroke:1

–
cautious  BP if markedly raised eg > 220/110. Note: most instances resolve
spontaneously

–
do not give aspirin until brain imaging excludes intracranial haemorrhage

–
monitor BGL - avoid aggressive management/IV fluids with glucose
•	 If haemorrhagic stroke:1

–
urgent reduction of BP may be necessary to  haematoma expansion
•	 If TIA - management is similar to ischaemic stroke1
•	 Monitor closely until evacuation:

–
vital signs + GCS
5.	 Follow up
•	 As per MO
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 For medical team to review patient
176 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Delirium - Adult
GENERAL CONSIDERATION:
•	 Consider delirium where a patient has one or more of the following risks:
•	 age ≥ 65 years
•	 history of cognitive impairment or dementia
•	 severe medical illness
•	 current hip fracture
•	 cognitive concerns raised by others
•	 Delirium may be confused with dementia
•	 Medicine toxicity is a major cause of delirium
1.
May present with2,3
•	 Acute change in mental status
•	 Difficulty focusing or keeping track, easily distracted
•	 Behaviour fluctuates in severity over 24 hour period:

–
hyperactive:
	›
disorganised or incoherent thinking, rambling or illogical flow of
conversation
	›
hyperalert, startles easily
	›
restlessness, picking at clothes, tapping fingers or making frequent sudden
changes of position

–
hypoactive:
	›
lethargic, drowsy
	›
difficult or unable to rouse
	›
disoriented, unsure of where they are or what time it is
	›
inability to recall events or instructions
	›
sluggishness, staring into space, staying in one position for a long time or
moving very slowly
	›
disturbance of sleep-wake cycle, excessive daytime sleepiness and
insomnia at night
•	 Hallucinations, illusions or misinterpretations
•	 Withdrawal states eg tremors, sweating, visual hallucinations
•	 Fever
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 177

2.	 Immediate management
•	 If patient is highly agitated, threatening, aggressive or violent, see Mental health
emergency
•	 Screen for Sepsis
•	 Do not leave patient alone
3.	 Clinical assessment 2,4
•	 Assess and manage patient in quiet environment
•	 Ask family or carer about recent behavioural change eg:

–
confusion

–
concentration

–
agitation, restlessness

–
sleepiness, levels of consciousness

–
communication or response levels

–
difficulty meeting requests

–
alterations in mood
•	 Do full head to toe physical examination
•	 Identify underlying acute medical causes:2,4

–
infections eg meningitis, encephalitis, neurosyphilis, UTI, pneumonia

–
CNS conditions eg brain lesions or tumours, subdural haematoma, TIA, stroke

–
sodium, potassium and calcium abnormalities. Consider renal or liver disease

–
Hypoglycaemia, or hyperglycaemia

–
respiratory disorders leading to hypoxia or pulmonary embolism

–
myocardial infarction, arrhythmias, heart failure

–
seizures, epilepsy and postictal states

–
pain or discomfort eg urinary retention, constipation, hip fracture, dental

–
withdrawal eg alcohol, benzodiazepines
•	 Urgently identify possible medicines toxicity:2,4

–
consider polypharmacy, particularly drugs with anticholinergic effects, given
at high or. prolonged doses:
	›
anticholinergics

»
sedating antihistamines eg promethazine, doxylamine

»
drugs for bladder overactivity eg oxybutynin
	›
psychotropics

»
antipsychotics eg clozapine, olanzapine

»
lithium

»
hypnotics eg benzodiazepines

»
tricyclic antidepressants eg amitriptyline
	›
drugs for parkinsonism

»
anticholinergics eg benzatropine

»
dopamine agonists eg levodopa
178 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

›
cardiac medications

»
betablockers eg sotalol and propranolol

»
digoxin
	›
analgesics

»
opioids

»
tramadol

»
nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs)
	›
antiemetics

»
prochlorperazine

»
hyoscine hydrobromide
	›
corticosteroids (high dose)
	›
alcohol and illicit drugs eg cannabis, methamphetamine
•	 Do vital signs +

–
SpO2 and BGL to exclude hypoxia or hypoglycaemia as a cause
•	 Do:
•	 bloods/i-STAT - BGL, FBC, UECr, LFT
•	 urinalysis and MSU for MCS
•	 ECG
•	 drug screen:

–
therapeutic drug monitoring if taking digoxin, lithium, quinidine

–
illicit drugs
•	 chest x-ray
4.	 Management 2,4
•	 Manage and treat underlying medical cause as above in consultation with MO
•	 For suspected medicines or other substance use, see Poisoning and overdose
•	 Contact pharmacist for medication review
•	 Offer analgesia. See Acute pain
•	 Address hydration, nutrition, ventilation, temperature control, skin care, bladder
and bowel care
•	 Provide familiar environmental cues ie clocks, calendars, with involvement from
family
•	 Commence regular observations. Observe at all times
5.	 Follow up
•	 As per MO or specialist instructions
•	 According to identified cause of delirium
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 Consult MO and specialist
•	 If no biochemical causes or sepsis identified, patient needs Psychiatric referral.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 179

Traumatic injuries – Adult/Child
GENERAL CONSIDERATIONS:
•	 Early notification for transfer. See Criteria for early notification of trauma for
interfacility transfer
•	 (inside front cover)
•	 Assume possible spinal injury in all blunt trauma or multiple injuries.
Use caution when moving1
•	 Systematic evaluation of all trauma patients (minor and major) 2
•	 Common causes of preventable death in trauma: airway obstruction, tension
pneumothorax and haemorrhage
•	 Hard/semi-rigid cervical collars are not recommended 3,4
1.
May present with
•	 Trauma
2.	 Immediate management. Primary survey and
resuscitation DRSCABCD 1,6-9
Danger - eg body fluids, toxins, traffic, perpetrators of crime, &lt; 10 m from fallen
powerlines
•	 If there is immediate threat to a person’s life and rapid movement is needed,
make all efforts to limit spinal movement without delaying treatment.10 If you are
by yourself and patient needs moving out of danger, you may need to drag them
via ankle or arm/shoulder3
Response
Send for help - ideally 1 or more assistants are needed
Catastrophic haemorrhage - control before checking airway 1,2,7,8
•	 Do not remove any penetrating/impaled objects eg knife 10
•	 Use direct firm pressure - with hands + combine pads and crepe bandages. If not
working, check pad(s) is over bleeding point/using firmer pressure with 1 or 2
pads over smaller area
•	 If unable to control:

–
use haemostatic dressing – clean wound / compress dressing to apply: use clean
dry gause to pack the wound only, cover the wound again with dry gause and use
bandage to compress the dressing. Elevate the limp above the level of the heart
and closely monitor for signs of ongoing bleeding.
	›
If the wound still bleeding, then need to apply a tourniquet
180 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

–
arterial tourniquet (if limb): 6,10
	›
use a tourniquet rubber or a cloth
	›
apply firmly 5 to 10 cm above the injury/amputation (not over a joint/
wound)
	›
tighten until bleeding stops

»
should elevate the limb above the level of the heart

»
the tourniquet should only applied for only 1 hour to prevent risk of limb
ischaemia

»
Remove the tournique after 1 hour but if need to re-apply re-tighten the
tourniquet again after 20 – 30 minutes.

»
Limb should be always elevated when applying a tourniquet and when
re-applying it.
	›
Consult surgeon for urgent referral
•	 Airway and cervical spine (c-spine)1,3,4,7,9
•	 Look - signs of airway obstruction eg use of accessory muscles1
•	 Listen - upper airway noises + breath sounds - absent, diminished, noisy snoring,
gurgling, stridor)
•	 Open airway - chin lift/jaw thrust ±

–
airway adjuncts eg oro/nasopharyngeal (not if base of skull fracture), LMA

–
suction/remove loose foreign bodies -blood, vomit, oedema, loose teeth/
objects
•	 Maintain c-spine stabilisation:3,4,9

–
support head in a neutral position

–
consider soft collar ± sand bags to support neck
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 181

•	 If needed, roll a ‘face-down’ non-responsive person onto their back to assess
airway5

–
if excessive bleeding into the airway that suction cannot remove, roll onto side1
•	 Check for wounds/injury
•	 If motorbike helmet5 remove while assistant restricts movement of c-spine7
Breathing 1,6-9
•	 Fully expose chest. Assess:

–
RR, effort, open wounds, bruising, deformity

–
circumferential or airway burn ie singed facial hair, soot in mouth/nose

–
use of accessory muscles, symmetrical/paradoxical chest movements

–
auscultate for breath sounds/air entry (listen in axilla area)1
•	 If tension pneumothorax - do immediate Needle decompression, with 14 G needle
7,9
Signs of tension pneumothorax - life-threatening
•	 Decreased/absent breath sounds
•	 Hyper-resonance on percussion
•	 ↑ HR, ↓ BP, ↑ SOB
•	 Distended neck veins
•	 If open/sucking chest wound - apply occlusive 3 sided dressing /vented chest
seal
•	 If any abnormalities, see Chest injuries, for possible causes + immediate
management
•	 Give O₂ to maintain SpO2 ≥ 94%
•	 Start bag-valve mask ventilation if indicated
•	 Circulation + haemorrhage control 7-9

–
Control external haemorrhage by direct pressure/bandaging ± elevate +
reassess tourniquet

–
application

–
HR, central capillary refill time

–
Insert IVC x 2 large bore OR Intraosseous
•	 If shock (cool,  HR, capillary refill > 2 seconds):7,10

–
IV fluid bolus (warmed if possible) - sodium chloride 0.9% or Hartmann’s:
	›
adult 1 L (250 mL increments, aim to maintain sBP at 90). Child 10–20 mL/
kg
•	 try to find source of haemorrhage eg chest, abdomen, pelvis, long bones
Disability - basic neurological evaluation 1,6-9
•	 Alert, Voice, Pain, Unresponsive
•	 BGL
•	 Pupils - size, equal + reacting
182 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

3.	 Clinical assessment. Secondary survey - EFGHIJ
•	 Start only after lifesaving interventions/management have been initiated
•	 If ongoing bleeding/life-threatening injury - consult MO urgently
Exposure, Environment, Early transfer notification 1,7,9
•	 Assess using Criteria for early notification of trauma for interfacility transfer,
(inside front cover)
•	 Remove all clothing
•	 Keep warm/prevent hypothermia - cover with blanket/space blanket
Full vital signs, eFAST, Family
•	 Vital signs + neurological observations - GCS, motor response in limbs, fontanelle
in infant
•	 Consider eFAST if trained/available 1,6,7,9
•	 Communicate with/support family/friends
Give pain relief. Get resuscitation adjuncts5
•	 Lab/bloods - FBC, UE, lactate, troponin, BGL, LFT, group and hold, coagulation
studies, blood gases
•	 ± blood alcohol/toxin screen 1,9
•	 Monitor vital signs at least 15 minutely. Attach cardiac monitor + ECG
•	 Naso/orogastric tube (consider) - not NG if suspect base of skull fracture 1,9
•	 Oxygen/capnography as indicated 12
•	 Pain - assess + analgesia eg morphine or fentanyl. 7,9 See Acute pain,

–
if head injury suspected, do not give opioid until discussion with
MO
History from patient/witnesses 1,7,9
•	 Allergies + check for medic alert eg jewellery, key ring, USB stick, shoe tag,
anklet, watch, tattoo
•	 Medications + anticoagulation/antiplatelet therapy
•	 Past medical history + bleeding disorder, Tetanus immunisation,
•	 last menstrual period, possible pregnancy
•	 Last meal
•	 Events related to injury:5  Mechanism:
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 183

–
blunt - speed of patient/objects, seatbelt use, air bag deployment, impact
direction

–
damage to car/bike, distance ejected from vehicle, height of fall/body part
landed on

–
penetrating - type of firearm/distance from, number of shots, type/length of
blade

–
any loss of consciousness + duration

–
amnesia

–
alcohol/drug use

–
social issues eg domestic and family violence
Head to toe assessment- search everywhere for injuries as per table below 1,5,7,9
CHECK FOR - PAIN, DEFORMITIES, BRUISING, SWELLING, LACERATIONS,
ABRASIONS, BURNS, IMPALED OBJECT(S) EG KNIFE (DO NOT REMOVE
IMPALED OBJECTS)
General
appearance
•	 Position found, posture, guarding/self-protection movements
•	 Odours - alcohol, petrol, chemicals, vomit, urine, faeces
Head and
face1,7
•	 Inspect face and scalp for lacerations/bruising/penetrating
injury + check:

–
eyes - size, equal + reacting, foreign body/contact lenses,
subconjunctival haemorrhage, hyphaema, irregular iris,
penetrating injury

–
movements - ask to follow your moving finger in all
directions

–
also see Eye injury

–
nose - deformity, CSF leak, bleeding, look in nose - any
asymmetry of septum ± bluish/reddish swelling (may be
septal haematoma). Also see epistaxis

–
mouth - lacerations to gums, lips, tongue, palate. Check if
any missing/loose/ broken teeth.' Bite malocclusion - may be
Fractured jaw

–
ears - bleeding, blood behind eardrum, CSF leak - do NOT
pack to stop drainage
•	 Bruising behind ears (Tattle sign') or periorbital (raccoon's eyes)
= base of skull fracture
•	 Palpate - skull, orbit, nose and jaw, noting:

–
depression(s) in skull, boggy swelling of scalp, bony
tenderness, subcutaneous emphysema*, fontanelle in baby9
•	 If ANY trauma to head, see Head injuries
•	 Scalp lacerations can result in major blood lose7 - close with
staples or sutures prior to transfer if able (or tie hair to close) as
per Acute wounds
184 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

CHECK FOR - PAIN, DEFORMITIES, BRUISING, SWELLING, LACERATIONS,
ABRASIONS, BURNS, IMPALED OBJECT(S) EG KNIFE (DO NOT REMOVE
IMPALED OBJECTS)
Neck1,6,7
•	 Palpate neck while maintaining in-line immobilisation of the
c-spine head and neck immobilised with 2 hands by assistant.
Check if:

–
pain, deformity

–
bony/laryngeal tenderness or crepitus/step, subcutaneous
emphysema#

–
veins - distended/flattened12
•	 Assume c-spine injury until excluded. See NEXUS tool in Spinal
injuries
Chest6-9
•	 Inspect + palpate entire chest wall - including clavicles, sternum,
ribs, axilla (do NOT spring rib cage). Check:

–
expansion, paradoxical movement, accessory muscle use

–
tenderness, crepitus,

–
subcutaneous emphysema#
•	 Auscultate + percuss:

–
breath sounds/lack of, wheeze, crackles (listen in axilla area)

–
heart sounds - murmurs, friction rubs, muffled sounds

–
hyper-resonance/dullness
•	 If significant chest injury suspected - auscultate BP in both arms
and note If difference > 10–15 may indicate aortic injury
•	 If any abnormalities, see Chest injuries
•	 Haemothorax can result in major blood loss
Abdomen
and flanks
1,6-9
•	 Inspect for lacerations, seat-belt bruising/red marks
•	 Auscultate bowel sounds - present/absent (before palpating)
•	 Palpate - tenderness, guarding, rigidity, rebound tenderness,
masses:

–
especially over liver, spleen, kidneys and bladder1
•	 Assess repeatedly - injury may not be obvious. See Abdominal
injuries
Pelvis1,7
•	 Gently palpate for tenderness. Do NOT spring pelvis
•	 If fracture suspected apply pelvic binder, NOW - even if low
suspicion - do not long roll. Once binder in place, only log roll if
essential
•	 Fractured pelvis, can result in rapid major blood loss7
Perineum,
genitalia1
•	 Inspect for bruising/bleeding
•	 If bleeding from urethral opening/scrotal haematoma - suspect
pelvic fracture7
•	 Note priapism (persistent erection) - symptom of spinal injury
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 185

CHECK FOR - PAIN, DEFORMITIES, BRUISING, SWELLING, LACERATIONS,
ABRASIONS, BURNS, IMPALED OBJECT(S) EG KNIFE (DO NOT REMOVE
IMPALED OBJECTS)
Limbs1,7
•	 Inspect and palpate entire length of limbs:

–
tenderness, deformity, swelling, open fractures

–
movement of joints and strength/range of motion (passive
and active)

–
colour, distal pulses, warmth, movement, sensation, capillary
refill
•	 Apply splint(s) as per Simple fracture limbs, and do
neurovascular observations as needed
•	 If discrepancy in leg length or rotational deformity in leg
without obvious fracture, suspect Fractured pelvis
•	 Fractured femur and any open long bone fractures can result
in major blood loss7
Inspect
back1
•	 Log roll, if assistance available (may be best achieved at time of
transfer) do not roll onto side with injury
•	 Ask patient to squeeze buttocks if awake
•	 Inspect and palpate - back/chest, flanks, buttocks, perineum
and posterior thighs:

–
deformity, bruising, swelling, tenderness/deformity/step
(along spine)

–
subcutaneous emphysema#
•	 Auscultate for air entry, abnormal sounds
#Subcutaneous emphysema - air trapped under the skin (skin crackles when
palpating)
186 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Jot it down
•	 Ensure clear, accurate, concise documentation 1,7
4.	 Management1
•	 Continually reassess primary survey + monitor for signs of airway compromise
•	 Continue c-spine precautions + spinal immobilisation until evacuated:

–
for guidance on moving trauma patient see Spinal injuries.
•	 Treat wounds - remove gross contamination/irrigate + cover

–
Wound wash: give local anaesthesia around the wound

–
Flush normal saline solution mixed with betadine if available, into the wound

–
Remove all debris and foreign materials from the wound

–
Use soaked gauze with saline solution and pack in the wound

–
Dry gauze over wound dressing

–
Never close the wound : concidered dirty / contaminated wound
•	 If impaled object eg knife, VERY CAREFULLY pack around with gauze soaked in
sodium chloride
•	 0.9% and secure 10
•	 Splint fractures1
•	 MO will advise ongoing management, which may include: 1,7,9

–
IV fluids

–
tranexamic acid (within 3 hours) if haemorrhage

–
antibiotics eg if open fracture,7

–
penetrating trauma1

–
monitor vital signs + neurological observations

–
keep warm/prevent heat loss

–
nil by mouth

–
IDC + urinalysis, monitor urine output hourly

–
x-ray(s)
•	 Give Tetanus immunisation, if indicated
•	 Also consider Electrocution, Compartment syndrome, Burns.
•	 If not evacuated but affected by drugs/alcohol:

–
encourage to stay under observation until nonaffected, or if discharged, into
the care of a
•	 responsible nonaffected adult
•	 Consider non-accidental injury (if injury/presentation inconsistent with history)
eg self inflicted
•	 injury, elder abuse, Domestic and family violence, + consider Child protection.
Pregnant woman with trauma 7,13
•	 Can lose a significant amount of blood before  HR,  BP, signs of shock evident
13
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 187

•	 If > 20 weeks pregnant (uterus at level of umbilicus):

–
lie on left side, or if spinal precautions/lying on back, tilt the right side 15–300
by placing a wedge under her hip/spinal board

–
note: baby can compress the vena cava causing hypotension if lying flat
•	 Have high index of suspicion for Abdominal injuries, even with minor trauma
•	 Also see Qld Clinical Guideline Trauma in pregnancy
www.health.qld.gov.au/qcg/publications#maternity
Amputated body part 7
•	 Stump – open wound need thorough wash with saline and betadine
•	 Control bleeding with compress dressing
•	 Elevate and seek advice from MA / surgical team for secondary wound closure.

Management of associated emergency: Consult MO 2.5.6
5.	 Follow up
•	 Be guided by MO

Chest injuries - Adult
Background 1,3
•	 Life-threatening emergencies include tension pneumothorax, massive
haemothorax, open pneumothorax, flail chest, pulmonary contusions, great
vessel injury, diaphragm rupture
1.
May present with
•	 Chest Trauma
2.	 Immediate management 1-6
•	 Do primary survey DRSCABCD as per Traumatic injuries, + initiate any lifesaving
measures
•	 Assess against Criteria for early notification of trauma for interfacility transfer
(inside front cover)
•	 Use tables on following pages for possible chest injuries and for immediate
management
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 189

BLUNT TRAUMA TO CHEST EG FALL FROM HEIGHT, MOTOR VEHICLE
ACCIDENT1-4
SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS
CONSIDER
•	 Increasing SOB, ↑ HR, ↓ BP
•	 Agitation, hypoxia
•	 ↓ air entry + hyper-
resonance on percussion on
affected side
•	 ↓ chest movement
•	 Trachea deviation (late sign)
Tension
pneumothorax
•	 Immediate needle
decompression (next
page)
•	 ↓ air entry/dull percussion
on affected side
•	 Hypotension/shock
•	 Hypoxia
Haemothorax
can be massive
•	 Treat hypovolaemia - IV
sodium chloride 0.9% or
Hartmann's 1 L (20 mL/kg
for child)
•	 If respiratory distress
develops, treat as tension
pneumothorax
•	 Paradoxical movement
- segment of chest wall
moves in when the patient
breathes in and out when
patient breathes out
Flail chest
•	 Ventilation support as
needed
•	 MO may intubate prior to
evacuation
•	 Coughing up blood, crackles
•	 ↑ HR, hypoxia, ↑ WOB
Pulmonary
contusion
•	 O2 + ventilation support
•	 ↓ air entry and ↑
percussion on affected side
•	 ± unequal chest movement
Simple
pneumothorax
•	 Be alert to ↑ SOB - may
be tension pneumothorax
•	 Tender sternum
May have
underlying lung
or cardiac injury
•	 ECG
•	 MO will advise further
•	 Chest wall tenderness/
swelling
•	 Pain worse on inspiration/
cough
Broken rib(s)
•	 MO may advise review
next day if minor/no other
injury/concerns
•	 ↓ BP, asymmetric,
diminished or absent
peripheral pulses
•	 Paraplegia
Great vessel
injury3

PENETRATING TRAUMA TO CHEST EG GUNSHOT OR STAB WOUND1,4,5
SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS
CONSIDER
•	 Wound to the chest
•	 ± object sticking out
Possible
haemothorax or
pneumothorax
•	 Do NOT remove any object
sticking out of wound eg knife.
VERY CAREFULLY pack around
with gauze soaked in sodium
chloride 0.9% and secure
•	 Cover open chest wound with:

–
vented chest seal OR

–
sterile occlusive dressing large
enough to overlap the wound
edges, taped securely on 3
sides (opening at bottom) to
provide a flutter-type valve
effect - air can move out but
not in
•	 Chest wall opening
•	 Air sucking in
Open/
sucking chest
wound/open
pneumothorax
Other life-threatening complications: cardiac tamponade (can be detected with
eFAST), aortic/ tracheo-bronchial/oesophageal injury, diaphragm injury, blunt
cardiac injury2,3
Needle decompression for tension pneumothorax5,6
•	 Insert 14 G needle/cannula (use longer needle) at 2nd intercostal space, mid-
clavicular line

–
air should escape with a rush + respiratory distress should decrease
•	 If not working:

–
insert needle at 5th intercostal space, mid axillary line

–
check IVC has not kinked or tension pneumothorax has recurred/another on
the other side
•	 Note: will require definitive treatment ie chest drain to be inserted
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 191

3.	 Clinical assessment6
•	 Do secondary survey EFGHIJ as per Traumatic injuries, including examination of
chest
•	 Do eFAST if trained/available
•	 Be aware of risk factors for potential deterioration:2

–
past history - smoker, COPD, asthma, obesity

–
age > 55 years, uncontrolled painrespiratory compromise: WOB,
 RR, SpO2

–
≥ 3 fractured ribs

–
shallow breathing, unable to cough
4.	 Management 1,2,6
•	 Give O2 to maintain SpO2 ≥ 94%
•	 Ensure adequate analgesia.
•	 See Acute pain.
•	 Consult MO in all cases, who may advise:

–
urgent evacuation

–
close monitoring of vital signs + WOB

–
chest x-ray

–
ECG

–
nil by mouth

–
bloods - FBC, group and hold
•	 Note: if critical, MO may advise finger thoracostomy (if skilled). Usually only done
if patient
•	 ventilated
•	 MO may insert chest tube prior to evacuation. Attach to Heimlich Valve® or
similar ambulatory chest drainage system for evacuation
5.	 Follow up
•	 MO will advise ongoing management
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 Require Surgical Team admission

Head injuries - Adult
Recommend1
•	 Consider the possibility of non-accidental head trauma in all children presenting
with mild to
•	 moderate head injury
•	 Do not do x-ray or USS in lieu of CT scan if skull fracture suspected in child
Background
•	 If child, also see Head injury - emergency management in children https://www.
childrens.health.qld.gov.au/qpec-statewide-guidelines
•	 Head injury clinical pathways (Qld) https://clinicalexcellence.qld.gov.au/
resources/clinicalpathways/head-injury
1.
May present with
•	 Trauma to head
2.	 Immediate management 1-4
•	 Do primary survey DRSCABCD as per Traumatic injuries, + initiate any lifesaving
measures
•	 C-spine immobilisation should always be applied in all cases of traumatic brain
injury.
•	 Assess against Criteria for early notification of trauma for interfacility transfer
(inside front cover)
•	 Do GCS, + neurological observations:5

–
pupil size + reaction to light

–
motor response in limbs

–
fontanelle in infant
•	 Do vital signs + BGL if altered LOC
•	 Consult MO urgently if:2,4,6

–
GCS &lt; 15

–
seizure(s)

–
penetrating injury or suspected skull fracture (depressed/open)

–
basal skull fracture suspected - black eye with no damage around eyes,
bleeding from ear(s),

–
clear fluid (CSF) from ear(s) or nose, bruising behind ear(s)1,3

–
focal neurological deficit eg loss of sensation, paraesthesia
•	 signs of  intra cranial pressure (ICP):2
•	 GCS decreasing
•	 abnormal posture (decorticate/decerebrate)
•	 abnormal pupil responses, pupil dilation (1 or both), upward gaze
•	 BP +  HR + breathing abnormalities - ‘Cushing’s triad’ (late sign)
•	 may be just headache, vomiting, irritability, altered LOC ± Cushing’s triad
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 193

3.	 Clinical assessment 2,4
•	 Do secondary survey EFGHIJ as per Traumatic injuries, including examination of
head
•	 Get history, including: 1-3

–
time/date of injury, witnessed or not

–
mechanism of injury/how it happened

–
use of alcohol/drugs

–
bleeding disorder/taking anticoagulants 1,4
•	 Ask about symptoms. Any: 1-4

–
loss of consciousness - when, how long for. If &lt; 2 years old, was it for ≥ 5
seconds2

–
vomiting (amount), headache (severity)1

–
seizure(s) or amnesia post injury - when/duration

–
double vision, ataxia, clumsiness, gait abnormality

–
abnormal behaviour (child/infant) eg drowsiness, agitation, restlessness, not
interested in

–
things around them, not themselves (as reported by parent)2

–
weakness/tingling in arms or legs
•	 Assess for Head injury risk factors as per tables on followingg pages
194 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

ALL OF THE
FOLLOWING

OF THE FOLLOWING

≥ 1 OF THE FOLLOWING

•	 Well
appearing
child

intermediate
or high risk
features
present

(drowsiness, agitation,
repetitive questioning, slow

following vehicle accident
- high speed, ejected from

Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 195

HEAD INJURY RISK FACTORS - ADULT2
'HIGH RISK' ANY OF THE FOLLOWING
•	 Age > 65 years
•	 Multi-system trauma
•	 On anticoagulant/antiplatelet
therapy
•	 Delayed presentation or re-
presentation
•	 Known coagulopathy eg liver
disease, factor deficiency
•	 Persistent abnormal level of
alertness, behaviour and/or
cognition
•	 Known neurosurgery and/or
neurological impairment
•	 Multiple comorbidities or
combination of worrying factors
•	 Dangerous mechanism of injury
URGENT CT SCAN IF BELOW6
•	 Clinical suspicion of skull fracture
•	 Deterioration in GCS
•	 Unwitnessed head injury
•	 Focal neurological deficit
•	 Loss of consciousness > 5 minutes
•	 Post traumatic seizure
•	 Intoxicated (alcohol ± other drugs)
•	 Anterograde or retrograde amnesia
> 30 minutes
•	 Persistent vomiting/more than 1
episode3
•	 Persistent GCS &lt; 15 at 2 hours post
injury
•	 Persistent severe headache
•	 Skull fracture suspected or other
clinical concern
196 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management
•	 If drug or alcohol intoxicated at presentation, treat as if the neurological findings
are due to the head injury until proven otherwise 1,3,4
•	 If scalp laceration, control bleeding - can result in major blood loss:4

–
Treat as a bleeding wound

–
Wash and pack the wound with gause

–
Cover the wound with gause

–
Use a grip bandage to wrap over the dressing

–
This will help enforce pressure on the wound and control bleeding.
•	 Consult Surgeon / MO / MA on all cases sustaining traumatic brain injury to
stabilise and initiate treatment before referring cases to main hospital.
•	 Patient with Moderate to Severe Traumatic brain injury

–
Admit

–
monitor closely general condition: every 1 - 2 hours

–
GCS + neurological observations every 1 - 2 hours

–
vital signs – ½ hourly

–
IVC x 2 or Intraosseous

–
O2 to maintain SpO2 ≥ 95% (+ capnography if available)

–
BGL – three times a day

–
analgesia - only on MO orders
	›
Regular Paracetamol and NSAIDS injection to give
	›
Morphine is contra-indicated in cases with head injury
	›
Morphine : sedative and interfere with neuro-observation documentation

–
Start IV antibiotic : as per antibiotic guideline
	›
Cloxacillin 25mg/kg every 6 hours if there is scalp wound or no wound but
suspected fracture skull.

–
Keep patient NBM until fully conscious

–
IVF – maintenance rate

–
Discuss for urgent referral
•	 if  ICP:elevate head of bed 20–30 : Discuss with MO / MO

–
support airway/ventilation

–
keep warm/avoid hypothermia

–
keep patient NBM

–
start on IV omeprazole

–
if seizures, manage as per Fitting

–
MA may consider:
	›
IV fluid bolus 10–20 mL/kg sodium chloride 0.9% to avoid hypovolaemia
	›
Start prophylactic : Phenytoin loading dose and followed with maintenance
(discuss with surgeon / anaesthetist)
	›
Regular panadol / diclofenac

–
Discuss for urgent referral
•	 If asymptomatic, fully awake and alert, have no neurological abnormalities +
assessed as low risk
•	 If baby &lt; 6 months, consult MO promptly1
•	 If child very low risk no need to monitor ie: 1,2
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 197

•	 trivial injury (ground level fall, walking/running into stationary objects), no loss of
consciousness, GCS 15 AND no signs or symptoms of head trauma other than an
abrasion. Discharge with Head injury advice
•	 Otherwise, in collaboration with MO, monitor neurological observations for 4–6
hours: 2,6

–
1/2 hourly for 1st 2 hours post injury, then hourly for 2–6 hours/until
discharged

–
if GCS falls ≥ 1 point for 30 minutes,3 symptoms develop/persist, or any
abnormal observations, urgently consult MO
•	 Consider discharge ≥ 4–6 hours if: 1,2,6

–
GCS remains at 15

–
neurological observations remain normal + no symptoms develop

–
no concerns of non-accidental injury in child

–
no concerns of alternative/concurrent illness

–
patient has responsible adult to observe them for 24 hours + can return
promptly if needed

–
not under the influence of drugs or alcohol 1

–
Advice on RED FLAG head injury symptoms : Persistent headache, vomiting
and sudden loss of consciousness – to report to the health center ASAP
5.	 Follow up
•	 If not evacuated advise to be reviewed the next day and at the next MO clinic
(1–2 weeks)1
•	 If on anticoagulants/has bleeding disorder, advise follow up within
72 hours due to increased risk of intracranial haemorrhage6
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 Consult MO as above
•	 Needs surgical admission

198 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Head injury advice - give verbal and written advice7-9
•	 Adults and children should be observed by someone responsible for next 24
hours
•	 Give fact sheets eg:

–
adult - Concussion/minor head injury without concussion https://
clinicalexcellence.qld.gov.au/resources/emergency-department-patient-
information-sheets

–
child - Head injury care after discharge https://www.childrens.health.qld.gov.
au/chq/information-for-families/fact-sheets/
General advice
•	 Rest quietly for next 48 hours
•	 Eat a healthy diet, drink lots of
fluids
•	 Avoid activities that make
symptoms worse
•	 Use ice packs on swollen or
painful areas
•	 Use paracetamol for headache
(not ibuprofen/aspirin)
•	 Do not take sedatives/other
medicines unless instructed
•	 Do not drive for at least 24
hours and only once you can
concentrate properly
•	 Return to exercise after a few
days rest
Concussion
•	 • Symptoms may include:

–
mild headaches, which settle with rest/paracetamol

–
mild dizziness, feeling tired, difficulty paying attention or remembering
things

–
feeling emotional or moody
•	 Symptoms usually settle over time. Return to clinic if you are worried
Return to the clinic immediately if
•	 Severe headache, especially if getting
worse
•	 Dizziness or unsteady walking
•	 Nausea, vomiting or poor feeding
(child) or frequent vomiting (adult)
•	 Unexpected drowsiness or weakness
•	 Disorientation or confusion
•	 Restlessness (adult), irritability,
continued crying or unusual agitation
(child)
•	 Slurred speech or double/blurred
vision
•	 Seizure (fit) or spasms of face or limbs
•	 Difficult to wake up
•	 If you or carer(s) are worried for any
reason
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 199

Spinal injuries – Adult/Child
Recommend1
•	 Focus on minimal handling, maintaining immobilisation and preventing further
damage
Background
•	 Hard/semi-rigid cervical collars are not recommended1,2
•	 Cervical spine injury is relatively rare in children.2,3 Also see Cervical spine injury
- emergency management in children www.childrens.health.qld.gov.au/qpec-
statewide-guidelines/
1.
May present with1
•	 History of trauma, in particular:

–
motor vehicle or motor bike accident - occupant, rider or pedestrian

–
industrial accident ie workplace

–
dive or jump into shallow water or being ‘dumped’ in the surf

–
sporting accident eg rugby, falling from a horse, trampoline2

–
fall from greater than standing height eg ladder, roof

–
fall in the elderly

–
significant blow to head

–
severe penetrating wound eg gunshot
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Do primary survey DRSCABCD as per Traumatic injuries, + initiate any lifesaving
measures
•	 Maintain cervical spine (c-spine) in-line immobilisation:1
•	 Do vital signs + BGL
•	 Assess against Criteria for early notification of trauma for interfacility transfer
Cervical spine (c-spine) in-line immobilisation
•	 Support head in a neutral position in line with torso - avoid twisting/angular
movements
•	 Use soft collar (as prompt to restrict movement) + sand bags to support neck if
unconscious1,2
•	 Padding may help - adult: 2 cm under head child &lt; 8: use thoracic elevation
device (TED)1-3
•	 If conscious, ask them to stay still1
•	 Restrict spinal movement until patient can undergo appropriate examination ±
imaging4
•	 Lay flat on firm supportive surface
200 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

C-spine sand-bag support over the soft neck colar pad to immobilise
suspected C-spine injury.
3.	 Clinical assessment 1,4
•	 Do secondary survey EFGHIJ as per Traumatic injuries.

–
note mechanism of injury + check for symptoms of spinal injury eg:1

–
tingling, numbness in the limbs - where6 weakness or inability to move the
limbs (paralysis)

–
altered/absent sensation - where

–
nausea, headache or dizziness

–
head or neck in abnormal position

–
signs of head injury, altered LOC, shock

–
breathing difficulties - c-spine or thoracic injury can cause respiratory failure

–
change in muscle tone (flaccid/stiff)

–
loss of bladder or bowel control

–
priapism (persistent erection in males)
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 201

NEXUS CRITERIA TO ASSESS RISK OF C-SPINE INJURY4,5
1
Midline cervical tenderness
•	 Neck pain on palpation of the posterior midline neck area, from nuchal
ridge to the 3rd thoracic prominence, or palpation of any cervical spinous
process
2
Altered mental status
•	 GCS &lt; 15
•	 Disorientation to time, place, person or event
•	 Inability to remember 3 objects at 5 minutes
•	 Delayed or inappropriate response to external stimuli
3
Focal neurologic deficit
•	 eg motor weakness, numbness/loss of sensation, paraesthesia, other eg
priapism4
4
Intoxicated - any of
•	 Patient or observer reports recent history of  intoxication/consumption of
intoxicating substances
•	 Odour of alcohol, slurred speech, ataxia, lack of coordination, or other
cerebellar findings
•	 Behaviour consistent with intoxication
•	 Tests of bodily secretions are positive for drugs or alcohol affecting mental
alertness
5
Painful distracting injury
•	 Any non spinal related condition causing sufficient pain to distract patient
from a possible cervical spine injury eg:

–
long bone fracture, significant visceral injury

–
large laceration, crush or degloving injury, large burns

–
other injury that could impair the patient's ability to appreciate cervical
spine pain
Patient is very unlikely to have c-spine injury if NONE of the above 5 criteria are
present AND
•	 NO neck pain, bruising or deformity of neck + has full range of active neck
movement (45° rotation left and right)2,4 AND
•	 NO other high risk factors ie:2,6

–
age > 65 (higher incidence of degenerative disease)4.6

–
fall + landing head first from a height eg diving, trampolining5

–
forced neck hyperflexion (low speed, high force eg rugby scrum collapse)2

–
high risk motor vehicle/bicycle accident eg head on collision, rollover,
ejection from vehicle, death in same crash, speed > 88 km/hour2,4

–
substantial torso injury (clavicles, abdomen, flanks, back, spine, pelvis), or
thoracic/lumbar spine or face
202 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

•	 If ANY NEXUS criteria, urgently consult MO - requires evacuation ± CT scan 4,5
•	 Consult MO for clearance before stopping c-spine precautions, regardless of
NEXUS criteria

–
generally, if patient is alert, without neurological deficit, sober, does not have
additional
•	 painful injuries, and has no pain along spine or neck, it is unlikely they have a
spinal injury4
•	 Offer analgesia ± antiemetic. See Acute pain, Nausea and vomiting
•	 If breathing difficulties - monitor SpO2, capnography (if able) + VBG
•	 Continue to manage in collaboration with MO until evacuation, which may
include:

–
vital signs + neurological observations

–
monitoring respiration

–
nil by mouth

–
keep warm

–
IDC ± oro/nasogastric tube5
•	 If hypotensive consider neurogenic shock if hypovolaemia ruled out. Note:
haemorrhage is most common cause of shock in trauma5
•	 If paraplegia or a level of sensory loss on the chest or abdomen, presume spinal
instability:4

–
do not try to reduce an obvious deformity6
Moving patient with suspected spinal injury1,3
•	 Provide in-line immobilisation of the c-spine during transfer and movement of
the patient (manually support)
•	 Use scoop stretcher or log roll onto spinal board/vacuum mattress
•	 Do NOT leave on rigid spinal board - high risk of pressure ulcers/necrosis
•	 Use head blocks/sandbags to maintain cervical alignment - DO NOT strap as
significant aspiration risk
•	 If extended immobilisation, straighten bedding and remove debris from under
patient
How to log roll1.4 Min. of 3, preferably 5, people needed
•	 1 person takes the lead. They are positioned at the patient's head to provide
manual in-line stabilisation (with 2 hands) to the head and neck. This person
gives instructions to the rest of the team
•	 3 people (if available) perform the roll:

–
position along the patient's body opposite to the direction that the patient's
head is facing

–
1 at shoulders/chest; 1 at hips; and 1 in control of the legs

–
roll slowly maintaining spinal alignment, avoiding flexion, rotation, lateral
bending, sagging of chest or abdomen. Keeping the patient's nose in line
with the umbilicus at all times
•	 5th person examines back, inserts/removes spinal board etc
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 203

5.	 Follow up
•	 As advised by MO
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 Require surgical team to review

204 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Abdominal injuries - Adult/Child
General consideration
•	 Missed abdominal injuries are a major cause of avoidable death in trauma
patients1
•	 The absence of abdominal pain does not rule out the presence of significant
abdominal injury2
1.
May present with
•	 Abdominal trauma

–
Blunt abdominal injury
	›
Blunt abdominal trauma is a leading cause of morbidity and mortality
among all age groups. Identification of serious intra-abdominal pathology
is often challenging; many injuries may not manifest during the initial
assessment and treatment period.

–
Penetrating abdominal injury
	›
Penetrating abdominal trauma typically involves the violation of the
abdominal cavity by a gunshot wound or stab wound.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 205

Blunt Abdominal Injury:
•	 History taking and identifying the mechanism of abdominal injury is crucial in
establishing the severity of the abdominal injury.
•	 Two major life-threatening conditions result from blunt abdominal injury

I.
Internal bleeding from ruptured major vessels or organs

II.
Leakage from visceral content from perforated viscous – peritoneal
contamination.
Signs and symptoms
The initial clinical assessment of patients with blunt abdominal trauma is often
difficult and notably inaccurate. The most reliable signs and symptoms in alert
patients are as follows:
•	 Pain
•	 Tenderness
•	 Gastrointestinal hemorrhage
•	 Hypovolemia
•	 Evidence of peritoneal irritation (board like abdomen/rigid and positive rebound
tenderness)
If the patient had the above symptoms – warrant admission for close monitoring
and to reassess regularly
Management:
•	 Admit
•	 Do primary survey DRSCABCD as per ATLS
•	 If hypotensive and tachycardia – insert 2 large IV cannulas
•	 Resuscitate with IV fluid crytalloid fuids (give Boluses of fluid if hypotensive)
•	 Insert IDC to monitor and assess the patients response to IVF resuscitation

–
To maintain the volume of urine-output > 30 mls/hour (Normal 1-2 mls/kg/
hour)
•	 Start IV antibiotic – triple antibiotic covering gram negative, gram postive and
anaerobic bacteria :

–
Ampiciliine 1gm every 6 hours (Ampicilline 50mg/kg in paediatric age group)

–
Gentamycin 240mg daily (Gentamycin 5mg/kg daily)

–
Flagyl 500mg every 8 hourly
•	 Need regular pain relieve

–
Panadol / Diclofenac
•	 Need NGT if patient start developing abdominal distension
•	 Discuss with MA/MO for further treatment and urgent referral
206 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Pregnant woman with abdominal trauma 2,5

–
If > 20 weeks pregnant (uterus at level of umbilicus) - baby can compress the
vena cava causing hypotension if lying flat:
	›
lie on left side, or if spinal precautions/lying on back, elevate the right side
15–300 by placing a rolled towel/wedge under hip
•	 Can lose a significant amount of blood before  HR, hypotension/signs of shock
occur
•	 Blunt trauma (even minor) can cause placental abruption. See APH.
•	 Consult Obstetric opinion

–
Patient need thorough examination
	›
check for vaginal loss/bleeding
	›
fetal heart rate (if skilled) + ask about baby’s movements (normal or
decreased)
	›
monitor for contractions
•	 If ≥ 23 weeks pregnant, CTG (if available) for at least 4 hours
2.	 Follow up
•	 If not evacuated, advise to be reviewed the next day, or sooner if concerned:

–
consult MO if  pain,  HR,  T or any abdominal finding.
3.	 Referral/consultation
•	 MA to discuss with General Surgeon
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 207

Penetrating abdominal injury
Signs and symptoms
Signs and symptoms of penetrating abdominal trauma depend on various factors
including the:
•	 type of penetrating weapon or object
•	 the range from which the injury occurred
•	 which organs may be injured (abdominal anatomical land mark and correlating
structures)
•	 the location and number of wounds.
Management:
•	 Important to admit
•	 Do primary survey approach DRSCABCD

–
Airway, breathing, circulation (ABCs): Includes vital signs
	›
Need 2 big canulas size 16 and 18  IV
	›
Fluid resuscitation to start and to guide guide by BP/P and urine output
	›
Keep patient NIL BY MOUTH and to await opinions from consulting surgeon
/ MA.
	›
Start triple antibiotic – Ampicillin / Gentamycin / Flagyl : if suspect
peritoneal contamination

–
Level of consciousness (D, disability): To detect neurologic deficits

–
Location(s) of the wound(s) (E, exposure): Inspect all body surfaces, and
document all penetrating wounds
	›
All wounds need to thoroughly wash and dressed with saline gause.

–
Type of weapon or object –  Any foreign objects causing abdominal
penetrating injury are concidered DIRTY : TETANUS TOXOID injection should
always be given at the time of injury.

–
Amount of blood loss
•	 Always reassess your patient on a hourly basis especially those who are
hemodynamically unstable and report.

–
Hemodynamically unstable :
	›
Continue optimising patient and discuss with surgeon for immediate
evacuation / referral

–
Hemodynamically stable
	›
Continue with treatment
	›
To report if there are concerning issues that need medical attention
208 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Fractures, dislocations and sprains
Fractures:
General concideration:
•	 Fractures can occur in normal bones or in diseased bones.
•	 Injuries or high velocity force cause fractures in healthy bones.
•	 Pathological bone fractures occur without any injury or from very minor injuries.
•	 Pathological fractures occur because the bone is weak and can break easily
•	 Weak bones can be caused by malignancy or severe osteoporosis.
•	 Any type of fractures can cause very serious complications
2 main types of fractures
1.
Simple fracture or close fracture

–
Fracture without any open wound on the overlying skin

–
No open wound but theres obvious bone deformity
2.
Compound fracture or open fracture

–
A fracture with an open wound

–
There is an open wound with an expose bone.

–
The wound that result from a fracture bone that pierce the overlying soft
tissue and the skin creating an open wound that exposes the bone
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 209

Simple fracture of limbs - Adult/Child
Definition
•	 Close fracture
•	 Deformed or fracture limb with no open wound
Signs and symptoms
•	 History of trauma or injury

–
Important to get a mechanism of injury

–
Timing of trauma
•	 Develop :

–
Pain

–
swelling

–
bruising

–
deformity

–
range of motion (ROM) due to pain
1.
Important clinical assessment need to document in
the folder
•	  Examine the blood supply distal to the possible fracture.

–
Is the extremity warm or cold?

–
Is the extremity pale?

–
Is there a puise distal to the fracture'?

–
Check capillary filling (press the nails)-docs the pink colour return within 1-2
seconds aller releasing pressure?)
•	 Examine the nerve supply to the distal limb

–
Is there any feeling or sensation in the distal limb or hand (use a pin prick or
cotton wool)?

–
Test both sides of the extremity for feeling.

–
Have the patient perform specific movements.
•	 Assessing nerve function if forearm fracture

–
Are all finger movements distal to the fracture normal?

–
Can all the fingers move normally as if the patient is playing a piano (can the
patient spread the fingers)?

–
Can the patient make a fist and flex and extend the hand at the wrist?

–
Can the patient hyperextend the thumb?

–
Can the patient move his thumb to touch his little finger'
210 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

2.	 Immediate management
•	 Do primary + secondary survey as per Traumatic injuries
•	 Assess against Criteria for early notification of trauma for interfacility transfer
(inside front cover)
•	 Immobilise/apply splint on a fractured limb in neutral position. Severely
deformed limb from injury with highly suspicious of fracture or dislocation
•	 NEED Manupulation under sedation (Adult):

–
IV midazolam 2 - 3mg / IM Morphine 10mg  (dose 0.05mg – 0.1mg / kg / dose)

–
Reduction : Traction and counter-traction manouvre to achieve a straight and
neutral position

–
Apply a splint or a back-slap to hold the limb in neutral position

–
Elevate

–
Consult surgeon/MA for urgent referral
•	 Regular pain relief – Panadol and NSAIDs
•	 No need to give antibiotic
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 211

ALWAYS DOCUMENT NEUROVASCULAR FINDINGS BEFORE AND AFTER
MANUPULATION AND REDUCTION PROCEDURE
PLASTER BACKSLAB2,4
Positioning
of slab
•	 Discuss with MO + see Backslabs for guidance
What you
need
•	 Plaster bandages eg 7.5/10 cm - short arms/wrists, 15/20 cm -
long arm
•	 Undercast padding
•	 Bowl of cold water + trauma scissors
•	 Crepe bandages + sling
How to do
•	 Remove all rings, bracelets, watches, restrictive clothing, shoes2
•	 Apply 2-3 layers of padding + 50% overlap beyond margins of
plaster
•	 Measure a length of plaster 1 cm shorter than the padding/
stockinette at each end. Fold the roll in about ten layers to the
same length
•	 Submerge in water until bubbling stops, remove + squeeze out
excess
•	 Lightly mould to the contours of the limb in a neutral position
•	 Plaster must not fully encircle the limb
•	 Turn back padding over ends of plaster + apply crepe bandage
firmly over slab
•	 Elevate limb on pillows or sling for arm
•	 Advise patient it may take 4-6 weeks to heal + Plaster care ±
crutches if not evacuated
212 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

PLASTER BACKSLAB2,4
Plaster
care
advice4
•	 Elevate limb above level of heart when possible using a sling/
pillows, will ↓ pain + swelling
•	 Exercise fingers/toes + other joints that aren't covered by the
plaster by bending/stretching/wiggling them
•	 Avoid applying pressure + heat to the cast
•	 Use plastic bag to protect plaster while in shower + do not
scratch under the cast with a sharp object, if damaged return to
clinic
3.	 Follow up
•	 If not evacuated, admit for close monitoring and always check the injured limb
for signs of compartment syndrome.
•	 Always document and report all concern matters to MA/Orthopedic surgeon
•	 if  pain or swelling, numbness, tingling or skin colour changes to blue or white6
– urgently contact MO for urgent referral

–
treat as compartment syndrome until proven otherwise.
•	 Compartment syndrome management –
4.	 Referral/consultation
•	 MA to discuss with General Surgeon
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 213

Open (compound) fractures - Adult/Child
Defined as a fractures with a cut through the skin. The overlying skin is wounded
and the broken bone may be exposed. Compound fractures are known as open
fractures. These fractures can become infected easily and need thorough cleansing
and debriding as soon as possible after the injury.
GENERAL CONSIDERATION:
1.
Always treat as a trauma patients : Primary survey / secondary survey and
admit
2.
Consult MA/Orthopedic surgeon – for urgent referral and treatment opinion
3.
Open fracture treatment = exposed fracture bone to reduce + thorough wound
wash + IV antibiotic (Reduce the risk of osteomyelitis) + TT injection  +  splint
and elevate
1.
May present with
•	 Open wound on same limb segment as an associated fracture2
•	 Bone has broken the skin or mucous membrane ± visible bone1
•	 Wound may be small eg puncture3
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Do primary + secondary survey as per Traumatic injuries.
•	 Assess against Criteria for early notification of trauma for interfacility transfer
(inside front cover)
•	 Stop any bleeding by putting pressure on wound, not on exposed bone1
•	 If signs of neurovascular compromise contact MO urgently +

–
thoroughly irrigate with sodium chloride 0.9%

–
give antibiotics + requires urgent reduction
•	 Immobilise/apply splint as per Simple fracture of limbs.

–
irrigate with sodium chloride 0.9% if severely contaminated wound and
remove all debris and foreign objects.2

–
apply a saline-soaked dressing covered with an occlusive layer 3,4

–
apply a splint to immobilise the limb

–
elevate the limb and advice to rest on the bed (not to mobilise)
•	 Insert IVC
•	 Give analgesia. See Acute pain.
3.	 Clinical assessment 1,3
•	 Assess as per Simple fracture of limbs.

–
ask if contaminated environment eg agricultural, sewage, fresh or salt water1

–
assess neurovascular status distal to the fracture
214 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management
•	 Urgently consult MO who may advise:
•	 V Cloxacillin 1g IV Q6H
•	 IV cefazolin ± IV metronidazole1
•	 x-ray, backslab, evacuation
•	 neurovascular observations
•	 nil by mouth when time of referral or medical evacuation is confirmed
(6 – 8 hours NBM before surgical procedures carried out)
METRONIDAZOLE

mg)
Offer CMI: May cause nausea, anorexia, abdominal pain, vomiting, diarrhoea, metallic taste,
dizziness, headache or thrombophlebitis
Management of associated emergency: Consult MO. See Anaphylaxis.
5.	 Follow up
•	 As per MO advice
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 All open fractures require consultation with local orthopaedic services
•	 Surgical Admission
•	 Need to refer to main hospital for imaging and proper wound wash
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 215

Fractured pelvis - Adult/Child
GENERAL CONCIDERATION:
•	 If suspected – need to be treated urgently because the patient can bleed and
loose about 4 liters of blood and develop hypovolumic shock
•	 Mechanism of injury is important to guide treatment plan
•	 Pelvic bind = pelvic fracture to help reduce internal bleeding
1.
May present with
•	 Suspected pelvic fracture post trauma eg:1

–
tenderness of pelvic area

–
abnormal rotation of affected leg ± shock
•	 Major haemorrhage1 + likely to have other injuries
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Do primary survey DRSCABCD as per Traumatic injuries + initiate any life saving
measures
•	 Consult MA / orthopedic surgeon urgently - suspected fractured pelvis requires.
•	 Early notification of trauma for interfacility transfer (inside front cover)
•	 Do not spring the pelvis to check for stability - may dislodge clots + cause
catastrophic haemorrhage
•	 Insert IVC x 2
•	 Give IV morphine. See Acute pain
•	 Only log roll to clear airway if suction is ineffective/not available - may cause
haemodynamic Instability
•	 Apply pelvic binder urgently to risk of haemorrhage, even if low suspicion of
fracture: 3-5

–
slide under the legs + then buttocks6

–
apply over the greater trochanters + symphysis pubis region, do not apply
above iliac crests1

–
secure firmly, should be able to insert 2 fingers between patient + binder7

–
use sheet if binder not available
216 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

•	 When to use the pelvic binder:

injury suggestive of
pelvic fracture
