How to use a pelvic binder or a sheet if no binder :
A range of pelvic binders are available which aim to reduce and stabilise pelvic
fractures. Currently there is insufficient evidence to support one device over the
other. If no device is available, use a bed sheet to wrap around the pelvis. Pelvic
binders also help with:
•	 Prevention of re-injury from pelvic motion
•	 Decreasing pelvic volume
•	 Decreasing pain
•	 Controling bleeding
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 217

Application of the pelvic binder
•	 Straighten both legs
•	 Apply to skin directly
•	 At the level of greater trochanter
•	 Sam’s Sling is commonly found in most EDs – 3 sizes available (small, standard
and large. If in doubt, use the standard size which is designed to fit 95% of the
population) or a bed sheet
•	 If appropriately applied the feet should be internally rotated
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Do secondary survey EFGHIJ as per Traumatic injuries
•	 Also check:1

–
lower leg - pulses, power, sensation - any 

–
for blood at urethral opening + scrotal bruising - in males

–
urinalysis - for blood if possible, or take from IDC if inserted
•	 May also have genitourinary + Abdominal injuries
4.	 Management
•	 Gently insert IDC if delay to evacuation

–
Never attempt IDC in cases with bleeding urethra

–
Consult surgeon before inserting the IDC
•	 Important to stabilise patient before transferring to main hospital
•	 Need IVF resuscitation 2 big IV canulas to insert: 2 litres crystalloid fluid to run
fast and then reassess
•	 Need regular opiod injection for pain
•	 Transport on scoop where possible
•	 Keep warm + closely monitor until evacuation:

–
vital signs + signs of hypovolaemic shock

–
lower leg - pulses, power, sensation + pain
5.	 Follow up
•	 As per MO advice
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 MA to discuss with General Surgeon
•	 Surgical Admission
218 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Fractured jaw - Adult/Child
Recommend
•	 Be alert to airway obstruction from bleeding or swelling
1.
May present with
•	 Trauma to face + pain, swelling, bleeding, deformity, drooling
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Do primary survey DRSCABCD as per Traumatic injuries,
•	 Assess against Criteria for early notification of trauma for interfacility transfer
(inside front cover)
•	 Consider need for c-spine immobilisation,
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Ask about mechanism of injury +

–
timing + events surrounding the injury

–
any witnesses, any first aid





•	 Do vital signs + secondary survey EFGHIJ as per Traumatic injuries,
•	 Assess as per Head injuries,
•	 Do full facial examination, including:

–
check for signs of fractured jaw:
	›
clenched teeth not ‘fitting together’ as usual/misaligned
	›
limited or painful mouth opening1
	›
unable to maintain bite on tongue depressor while twisted2
	›
flattening, swelling of cheeks
	›
difficulty swallowing2
•	 any other injuries eg:

–
mouth + tongue - lacerations, swelling

–
broken teeth - keep knocked out teeth for possible replacement. See Trauma
to teeth,

–
orbit - palpate for step defect, numbness - may indicate fracture
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 219

4.	 Management
•	 Consult DO/MO who may consider:

–
IV antibiotics, x-ray, nil by mouth
•	 evacuation/hospitalisation for surgical review
•	 Offer analgesia. See Acute pain,

–
consider ice packs + soft collar for comfort1
•	 Check tetanus vaccination status and give booster if indicated.1 See Tetanus
immunisation,
•	 Consider Domestic and family violence,
•	 Closely monitor until evacuation + frequent airway checks
5.	 Follow up
•	 As per MO/DO
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 Refer to Oral Surgeon

220 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Oral facial fractures
Purpose:
•	 To assist Medical Assistants and Nurses in the primary care setting to recognize,
assess, provide initial management, and appropriately refer patients presenting
with oral facial fractures.
Background
•	 Facial fractures are increasingly common presentations in primary healthcare.
Prompt recognition and appropriate management are critical to prevent
permanent functional, cosmetic, or vision-threatening complications.
General Principles
•	 Follow Advanced Trauma Life Support (ATLS) protocols for all trauma cases.
•	 Conduct a full visual assessment in any facial trauma.
•	 Identify red flags requiring immediate referral.
•	 Provide early pain management and wound care as appropriate.
1.
May present with
•	 Nasal Fractures: Often resulting from interpersonal violence, nasal fractures
are the most common facial fractures, accounting for up to 58.6% of all facial
fractures. Symptoms include nasal deformity, epistaxis, and periorbital bruising. ​
www.racgp.org.au/afp/2012/april/maxillofacial-trauma/?utm_source=chatgpt.
com
•	 Mandibular Fractures: These may present with pain, swelling, malocclusion, and
limited mouth opening. They can result from blunt or penetrating trauma and
may destabilize the airway. www.entnet.org/wp-content/uploads/files/Trauma-
Chapter-5.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com
•	 Zygomatic Fractures: Symptoms include periorbital swelling, infraorbital nerve
paraesthesia, and restricted eye movement.​
•	 Orbital Fractures: Signs include diplopia, reduced visual acuity, and orbital rim
tenderness.​
•	 Frontal Bone Fractures: Present with forehead deformity, possible CSF
rhinorrhoea, and periorbital bruising.​
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 221

2.	 Immediate Management
•	 Airway Management: Ensure airway patency, especially in cases of mandibular
fractures that may compromise the airway. https://link.springer.com/
chapter/10.1007/978-981-15-1346-6_48?utm_source=chatgpt.com
•	 Hemostasis: Control bleeding using direct pressure, nasal packing, or hemostatic
agents as appropriate.​
•	 Analgesia: Administer appropriate pain relief, considering the severity of the
injury.​
•	 Antibiotics: Prophylactic antibiotics may be indicated in cases of open fractures
or significant soft tissue involvement. https://tsaco.bmj.com/content/9/1/
e001304?utm_source=chatgpt.com
•	 Imaging: Obtain appropriate imaging studies, such as X-rays or CT scans, to
assess the extent of the fractures.​

–
Vital Symptoms/Signs Not to Miss
These require IMMEDIATE REFERRAL:
SYMPTOM/SIGN
CONDITION
ACTION
Proptosis, chemosis,
ophthalmoplegia, vision loss
Retrobulbar
haemorrhage
Immediate emergency
referral
Sudden visual loss or
decreased pupil reflex
Traumatic optic
neuropathy
Immediate emergency
referral
Diplopia, restricted upward
gaze, nausea/vomiting
(especially in young patients)
White eye blowout
fracture
Immediate emergency
referral
Mobile maxilla, malocclusion
Maxillary/midface
fracture
Immediate referral
3.	 Clinical Assessment
•	 History: Obtain details of the mechanism of injury, time of injury, and any
associated symptoms.​
•	 Examination: Perform a thorough head and neck examination, assessing for
deformities, swelling, tenderness, and neurological deficits.​
•	 Functional Assessment: Evaluate the patient’s ability to open and close the
mouth, chew, and speak.​
•	 Neurological Examination: Assess cranial nerve function, particularly the
trigeminal nerve, for sensory deficits.​
222 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management
•	 Conservative Management: Indicated for non-displaced fractures without
significant functional impairment. Includes analgesia, soft diet, and follow-
up.​ www.rch.org.au/trauma-service/manual/maxillofacial-injury/?utm_
source=chatgpt.com
•	 Surgical Intervention: Required for displaced fractures, fractures with airway
compromise, or those causing significant functional impairment.​ www.slideshare.
net/slideshow/maxillofacial-trauma-19339123/19339123
•	 Referral: Refer to oral and maxillofacial specialists for definitive management.
https://metronorth.health.qld.gov.au/specialist_service/refer-your-patient/oral-
maxillo-facial/facial-fractures-lacerations?utm_source=chatgpt.com
5.	 Follow-Up
•	 Monitoring: Regular follow-up to assess healing, manage complications, and
address any functional issues.​
•	 Rehabilitation: May include physiotherapy for jaw mobility and speech therapy if
necessary.​
•	 Psychological Support: Provide support for patients dealing with the
psychological impact of facial trauma.​
6.	 Referral / Consultation
•	 Urgent Referral: For patients with airway compromise, significant bleeding, or
complex fractures.​
•	 Routine Referral: For non-displaced fractures without functional impairment.​
•	 Specialist Consultation: Consult with oral and maxillofacial surgeons for definitive
management. ​www.westernhealth.org.au/HealthProfessionals/Referrals/
Documents/ASC%20Access%20and%20Referral%20Guideline_Oral%20
Maxillofacial%20Surgery.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 223

Dislocations - Adult/Child
GENERAL CONSIDERATION:
•	 Pulling or pushing of a bone out of its normal position in the joint1
1.
May present with
•	 Injury + severe pain that is worse on movement + very apprehensive to move
joint2
•	 ± deformity, associated fractures
•	 Reports of popping or joint rolling out of place - mainly shoulder dislocations1
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Offer analgesia. See Acute pain
•	 Support limb using pillows as needed + apply ice packs as tolerated
•	 If simple dislocation eg patella, digit + skilled - quickly reduce to alleviate pain
•	 Contact MO if signs of neurovascular compromise distal to the site eg:

–
weakness or numbness

–
 or absent pulses, delayed capillary refill

–
changes in colour
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Ask about mechanism of injury +

–
Timing + events surrounding the injury. Any witnesses, any first aid
•	 Get past history:

–
previous dislocations, when, was it reduced, if so how/which manoeuvre1

–
any orthopaedic surgery or underlying conditions eg osteoporosis2
•	 Do physical examination, including: 1,2

–
vital signs

–
check range of motion - painful or limited + palpate joints above and below
injury – any tenderness, swelling
224 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management
•	 Consult MO who may advise:

–
x-ray to check for fracture + if can be reduced locally, or

–
evacuation/hospitalisation
•	 Collaborate with MO on reduction manoeuvres - if skilled + appropriate.
Consider:

–
nil by mouth

–
IV analgesia/sedation

–
monitor neurovascular status before + after reduction:
	›
if shoulder - check sensation over deltoid muscle, nerve can be damaged
during reduction

–
after care eg x-ray + immobilise joint/limb2
5.	 Follow up
•	 As per MA
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 MA to discuss with General Surgeon
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 225

Sprains/soft tissue injury - Adult/Child
GENERAL CONSIDERATION:
•	 Be alert to associated injuries eg fractures + dislocations1
•	 In children consider fibula fracture, often misdiagnosed as a sprain
•	 Sprain is over stretching to incomplete or complete tear of the ligament2
1.
May present with
•	 Injury + swelling, bruising + pain on movement2
•	 No fracture seen on x-ray
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Contact MO if signs of neurovascular compromise distal to the site eg:

–
weakness or numbness

–
 or absent pulses, delayed capillary refill

–
changes in colour + T
•	 Offer analgesia. See Acute pain
•	 Support limb using pillows as needed + apply ice packs
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 If child, infections of joints often present with a history of injury. See Swollen/
painful joint
•	 Ask about mechanism of injury +

–
timing + events surrounding the injury

–
any witnesses, any first aid

–
if ankle - could they weight bear immediately after injury - if not may indicate
fracture2
226 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

•	 Get past history:

–
similar injury, when, treatment - ankle sprains are often recurrent2
•	 Do physical examination, including:1
•	 vital signs
•	 check for signs of severe sprain:

–
severe pain, extensive bruising + swelling

–
complete inability to weight bear

–
loss of motion + stability + abnormal joint movement
•	 palpate joints above + below injury - any tenderness, swelling
4.	 Management
•	 If severe contact MO, who may advise:

–
x-ray ± cast for 10–14 days + non-weight bearing activity/crutches
•	 If mild or moderate sprain:

–
Rest + limit activity until pain free use is possible:2
	›
may need crutches for 2–3 days until standing is no longer painful1,3

–
Ice for 48 hours - apply for 20 minutes on/20 minutes off as needed for pain/
swelling2

–
Compression for 1st few days - bandage is better for  swelling than rigid
splint2

–
Elevate above level of heart to  pain + swelling2

–
Refer to physiotherapist if available
•	 Reassure patient pain should  gradually + most sprains heal well 1,2
5.	 Follow up
•	 Advise patient to be reviewed if: 2,3

–
changes to limb/joint - T, pallor, numbness, pins + needles or swelling

–
pain continues, increases or is not relieved by analgesia

–
contact MO + consider fracture, compartment syndrome
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 As above
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 227

Compartment syndrome - Adult/Child
GENERAL CONSIDERATION:
•	 Limb threatening emergency - urgent evacuation for surgical management 1,2
•	 Be alert in all limb injuries eg closed leg + forearm fractures, crush injuries or
treatment causing
•	 compression/constriction eg casts/splints, circumferential bandaging2
•	 Hypoxia of muscle + nerves caused by bleeding + swelling in closed muscle
cavity, leading to necrosis + amputation1
1.
May present with
•	 Limb injury + pain out of proportion to what would be expected ± not
responding to analgesia1
•	 If child - anxiety, agitation, unable to rest comfortably3
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Remove any bandaging and split the casts/splints to relieve pressure:3

–
leave a portion of cast/splint to stabilise fracture as needed

–
Elevate the limb with the splint above the level of the heart

–
Give analgesic medication – panadol / NSAIDs
•	 Rapidly assess:2

–
pain - severity, is it responding to analgesia

–
passively extend fingers/toes distal to injury, does this cause pain

–
any  sensation, numbness, pins + needles + absent pulses - compare to
opposite limb 1-4
•	 Contact MO urgently, even if low suspicion of compartment syndrome2
•	 Offer analgesia. See Acute pain
•	 Rest, apply ice packs + elevate the limb to heart level with pillows 1,4
•	 Insert IVC
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Ask about:

–
mechanism of injury, when, treatment so far

–
underlying bleeding disorders, anticoagulants2
•	 Do physical examination, including:

–
vital signs

–
check entire limb for swelling3

–
palpate muscles - firm, tight, tenderness3
228 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management 1,4
•	 Consult MO who will:

–
order x-ray to exclude displaced fracture

–
arrange urgent evacuation

–
advise further management eg splinting of limb to prevent further
 pressure
•	 Nil by mouth
•	 Closely monitor until evacuation:2

–
vital signs + neurovascular observations at least hourly

–
pain
5.	 Follow up
•	 As per MA
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 MA to discuss with General Surgeon for urgent referral
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 229

Dog Bite – all ages (Kiribati Original Protocol)
GENERAL CONSIDERATIONS:
•	 Dog bites can cause serious injury and infection, including tetanus, sepsis, and
rabies (if rabies is endemic).
•	 Early assessment and wound care are essential to reduce complications.
•	 Tetanus toxoid (TT) should be given based on immunisation history and wound
risk.
•	 In rabies-endemic areas, rabies post-exposure prophylaxis may be needed.
•	 Bitten by a dog (domestic or wild), may or may not break the skin
1.
May present with 1,3
•	 Skin puncture, laceration or crush injury from dog bite
•	 Bleeding, swelling, redness, or signs of infection (pain, pus, fever)
•	 History of animal attack or close contact with saliva through broken skin
•	 Anxiety or distress, especially in children
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Apply basic first aid: control bleeding and clean the wound with soap and
running water for at least 15 minutes
•	 Remove any foreign material if visible
•	 Cover with a clean dressing
•	 Reassure the patient and caregiver
•	 Urgently assess TT status (see table on following pages)
3.	 Clinical assessment 1,2,3
•	 Obtain history:

–
Time and location of the bite

–
Whether dog is domestic or stray, vaccinated, or known to the patient

–
Provoked or unprovoked attack

–
TT and rabies immunisation history
	›
Assess wound:

–
Site, depth, contamination, and evidence of infection

–
High-risk sites: face, hands, feet, genitals
	›
Do full vital signs
	›
Consider mental health impact (particularly in children)

230 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Consider the Dunbar Scale to assess the levl of bite severity and whether the
patient needs review by the surgical team: An assessment of the severity of biting
problems based on an objective evaluation of wound pathology
•	 Level 1. Obnoxious or aggressive behavior but no skin-contact by teeth.
•	 Level 2. Skin-contact by teeth but no skin-puncture. However, may be skin nicks
(less than one tenth of an inch deep) and slight bleeding caused by forward or
lateral movement of teeth against skin, but no vertical punctures.
•	 Level 3. One to four punctures from a single bite with no puncture deeper than
half the length of the dog’s canine teeth. Maybe lacerations in a single direction,
caused by victim pulling hand away, owner pulling dog away, or gravity (little
dog jumps, bites and drops to floor).
•	 Level 4. One to four punctures from a single bite with at least one puncture
deeper than half the length of the dog’s canine teeth. May also have deep
bruising around the wound (dog held on for N seconds and bore down) or
lacerations in both directions (dog held on and shook its head from side to side).
•	 Level 5. Multiple-bite incident with at least two Level 4 bites or multiple-attack
incident with at least one Level 4 bite in each.
•	 Level 6. Victim dead.
4.	 Management
Antibiotics
•	 All moderate or severe bites, or if patient is at high risk of infection:
•	 Amoxicillin + Clavulanate

–
Child: 20–40 mg/kg/dose (based on amoxicillin component) PO bd

–
Adult: 875 mg/125 mg PO bd

–
Duration: 5 days, extend to 7 days if signs of infection
•	 If penicillin-allergic: doxycycline + metronidazole (consult MO)
Wound care
•	 Clean thoroughly with saline after initial soap/water wash
•	 Do not suture unless wound is large and not grossly contaminated (consult MO)
•	 Update tetanus immunisation (see table)
•	 In rabies-endemic settings, follow national rabies PEP protocols
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 231

Follow up
•	 Review in 2 days to check wound healing and signs of infection
•	 Advise patient to return earlier if:

–
Fever, increasing redness/swelling

–
Pus or worsening pain

–
Reduced mobility of affected limb
	›
Continue dressing changes as needed
	›
Document TT administration and wound progression
5.	 Referral/consultation
•	 Urgent referral to MO if:

–
Deep wounds or high-risk site (face, hand, joint)

–
Clinical signs of sepsis or systemic illness

–
Suspected rabies exposure

–
Consider surgical consult if wound is not healing or needs closure
232 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Acute abdominal pain
Background
Acute abdomen refers to a sudden onset of severe abdominal pain that may
require urgent medical attention. The cause can be gastrointestinal, urological,
reproductive, respiratory, or related to trauma. A diagram classifying causes of
acute abdominal pain should be inserted here.
In females of reproductive age, always consider:
•	 Ectopic pregnancy –
particularly if pain is accompanied by vaginal bleeding
•	 Pelvic inflammatory disease (PID) – particularly if &lt;30 years and sexually active,
with new pelvic pain
In males:
•	 Testicular torsion must be considered in all males with abdominal pain
•	 In male infants with inconsolable crying, examine for testicular or scrotal
pathology
A definitive diagnosis is not always required immediately. However, a detailed and
structured history and examination are critical to guide next steps and identify red
flags.
Recommendations
Always take a comprehensive pain history and perform a full abdominal and genital
examination. Urgently consult or refer patients with red flags such as signs of
peritonitis, sepsis, shock, or suspected ectopic pregnancy or testicular torsion.
1.
May present with
•	 Sudden or gradual onset of abdominal pain
•	 Pain may be continuous or intermittent
•	 Pain may be localised or generalised
•	 Pain may radiate to the back or shoulder tip
•	 Associated symptoms may include:

–
Nausea or vomiting (bile, blood, or faecal-smelling)

–
Diarrhoea or constipation

–
Fever or shortness of breath

–
Dysuria, haematuria

–
Vaginal bleeding or discharge

–
Poor feeding, refusal to eat (children)

–
Recent trauma to abdomen, scrotum, or genital area

–
Weight loss or anorexia
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 233

2.	 Immediate management
•	 Apply DRSABCD approach
•	 If unstable: start IV access and fluids
•	 Keep nil by mouth
•	 Provide pain relief as needed
•	 If testicular torsion or ectopic pregnancy suspected: urgent referral
•	 Monitor vital signs
•	 Do not delay consultation in unstable patients or those with red flags
3.	 Clinical assessment
History of pain
Ask about:
•	 Site – ask patient to point to location
•	 Onset – sudden, gradual; what were they doing when it started
•	 Character – sharp, dull, stabbing, crampy, burning
•	 Radiation – e.g. to back, shoulder tip
•	 Timing – first onset, duration, prior episodes
•	 Exacerbating/alleviating factors – worse with movement? relieved by lying still?
•	 Severity – scale 1–10, impact on sleep or daily activity
Associated symptoms
Ask about:
•	 Vomiting – bile, blood, green, faecal odour
•	 Bowel habits – diarrhoea, constipation, black or bloody stools, last motion
•	 Urine – frequency, burning, urgency, blood
•	 Vaginal – bleeding, discharge
•	 Respiratory symptoms – shortness of breath, cough (to consider pneumonia)
•	 Weight loss, poor feeding, food refusal
•	 Foreign body ingestion or recent trauma (especially in children)
Physical examination
Perform a full abdominal examination:
•	 Look:

–
Shape and symmetry (distended, sunken)

–
Surgical scars, bruises, hernias, distended veins

–
Signs of non-accidental injury
•	 Palpate:

–
Start away from site of pain; light then deep palpation in 9 areas

–
Assess for rebound tenderness (pain when pressure is released)

–
Localised tenderness, distension, guarding (involuntary tightening)

–
Pain out of proportion to findings

–
Palpate kidneys with one hand under flank and one above
234 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

•	 Auscultate:

–
Listen for bowel sounds in all quadrants

–
Normal: low-pitched, gurgling 2–5/min

–
Hyperactive or high-pitched: consider obstruction

–
Absent: consider ileus or peritonitis
•	 Percuss:

–
Tap all four quadrants to assess for dullness or tympany
•	 Chest examination:

–
Listen for crackles or wheeze, as lower lobe pneumonia can cause abdominal
pain
•	 In males:

–
Examine testes for torsion
•	 In females:

–
Consider pelvic examination or pregnancy test if reproductive age
4.	 Management

5.	 Follow up
•	 Reassess regularly, especially if diagnosis unclear
•	 Monitor vital signs and symptoms closely
•	 Repeat abdominal examination if symptoms persist or worsen
•	 If diagnosis changes, adjust management plan and refer if needed
6.	 Referral / consultation
•	 Urgent referral to main hospital or surgical unit if:

–
Signs of peritonitis, sepsis, or shock

–
Suspected ectopic pregnancy or testicular torsion

–
Persistent pain with unclear diagnosis
•	 Consult for review if any red flag symptoms are present

236 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Management of common surgical abdominal
condition associated with acute abdominal pain
Bowel obstruction (large and small bowel)
Background
Intestinal obstruction is an important cause of acute abdomen and accounts for
approximately 10% of emergency surgical admissions. It occurs when the normal
flow of intestinal contents is interrupted, either partially or completely.
Obstruction can occur in the small or large bowel and may be classified in several
ways:
•	 Permanent or intermittent
•	 Complete or partial
•	 Acute or chronic
•	 Intrinsic or extrinsic
•	 Mechanical or functional
Mortality varies depending on the cause and associated complications. Mortality
is 100% in untreated strangulated obstructions. Morbidity and mortality are
significantly increased in patients where diagnosis is delayed or management is
inappropriate.
Common causes include:
•	 Volvulus
•	 Strangulated hernias
•	 Faecal impaction (e.g. Hirschsprung’s disease)
•	 Neoplastic causes (e.g. bowel cancer)
Recommendations
All patients with suspected bowel obstruction must be admitted for close
monitoring and stabilisation. Initiate IV fluids, insert a nasogastric tube for
decompression, commence broad-spectrum IV antibiotics, and arrange urgent
referral to the main hospital for definitive investigation and management.
1.
May present with
• Abdominal pain
• Abdominal distension or bloating
• Nausea and vomiting
• Constipation or obstipation (no passage of faeces or flatus)
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 237

2.	 Immediate management
•	 Admit patient
•	 Monitor vital signs regularly
•	 Keep nil by mouth (NBM)
•	 Insert nasogastric tube (NGT) for decompression and free drainage
•	 Begin IV fluid resuscitation as per weight
•	 Start IV broad-spectrum antibiotics (e.g. Ampicillin + Metronidazole)
•	 Organise urgent referral to main hospital for surgical review and further
management
3.	 Clinical assessment
General appearance
•	 Patient may or may not show signs of distress
•	 Blood pressure and pulse may be elevated
Abdominal examination
•	 Abdominal distension
•	 Visible or obvious bowel peristalsis
•	 Tense and tender abdomen
•	 Bowel sounds:

–
High-pitched or “tinkling” in early obstruction

–
Reduced or absent in late obstruction or paralytic ileus
4.	 Management

5.	 Follow up
•	 Monitor NG tube drainage and reassess regularly
•	 Continue IV fluids and electrolyte replacement as needed
•	 Observe for signs of deterioration (fever, tachycardia, worsening tenderness,
guarding)
•	 Ensure patient is stable for transfer
6.	 Referral / consultation
•	 Urgent referral to the main hospital for further imaging and surgical assessment
•	 If signs of strangulation or worsening clinical status, escalate immediately to
surgical team
•	 Document and hand over vital signs, resuscitation measures, and medication
given prior to transfer
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 239

Acute appendicitis
Background
Acute appendicitis is the most common abdominal surgical emergency in the
world, with a lifetime risk of 8.6% in males and 6.9% in females. It is caused by
inflammation of the appendix and requires prompt recognition and referral.
The classical history involves colicky central abdominal pain that localises to the
right iliac fossa (RIF) within 6–12 hours and becomes constant. However, this pattern
is only present in approximately half of patients, and clinical judgement must be
used to support diagnosis.
Recommendations
All suspected cases of acute appendicitis should be admitted immediately.
Keep nil by mouth (NBM), start triple IV antibiotics (Gentamicin, Ampicillin and
Metronidazole), hydrate with IV fluids, provide analgesia, and refer urgently to the
main hospital for surgical management.
1.
May present with
•	 Colicky central abdominal pain that shifts to the RIF within 6–12 hours
•	 Constant pain in the RIF
•	 Nausea or vomiting
•	 Fever
•	 Loss of appetite
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Admit patient
•	 Keep nil by mouth (NBM) – do not allow food or drink
•	 Insert IV line and commence fluids
•	 Start IV triple antibiotic therapy
•	 Provide pain relief as required
•	 Organise urgent referral to the main hospital for surgical assessment and
management
240 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

•
Haemodynamic
instability
Call
Surgery
Critical care
Oxygen
Iv fluid
resuscitation
U catheter
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 241

3.	 Clinical assessment
Abdominal examination findings of severe appendicitis:
•	 Direct tenderness: Pain on pressing in the RIF
•	 Guarding: Voluntary or involuntary tightening of abdominal muscles in the RIF
•	 Rigidity: Board-like abdomen due to reflex spasm of abdominal wall
•	 Rebound tenderness: Pain when pressing firmly for 30 seconds in the RIF and
releasing suddenly
•	 Rovsing’s sign: Rebound pain in the RIF when pressing and releasing in the LIF
•	 Psoas sign: Pain in the RIF when patient lifts flexed thigh against resistance
•	 Fever or tachycardia may also be present
4.	 Management

5.	 Follow up
•	 Monitor vital signs and pain severity
•	 Reassess abdominal tenderness
•	 Continue IV antibiotics and fluids

•	 Refer urgently to the main hospital surgical team
•	 Escalate immediately if worsening symptoms or generalised peritonitis
•	 Handover medications given, timing of symptoms, and observations during transfer
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 243

Cholecystitis
Background
Cholecystitis is inflammation of the gallbladder, most commonly caused by
gallstones. Gallstone disease is one of the most frequent abdominal conditions
requiring hospital admission in the developed world. In the UK alone, 5.5
million people are estimated to have gallstones, and approximately 50,000
cholecystectomies are performed annually.
Gallstones are found 2–3 times more frequently in women than in Obesity is the
strongest independent risk factor for gallstone development in women. This is likely
due to the effect of oestrogen, which increases the movement of cholesterol from
the bloodstream into the liver, contributing to gallstone formation.
Recommendations
If acute cholecystitis is suspected, admit the patient immediately. Resuscitate if
there are signs of sepsis or dehydration. Keep the patient nil by mouth (NBM),
initiate IV fluids and broad-spectrum antibiotics, and provide opiate analgesia. Refer
to the main hospital or surgical team, especially if pain persists or the patient is
unstable.
1.
May present with
•	 Constant right upper quadrant (RUQ) abdominal pain (not colicky)
•	 Pain lasting 2–6 hours, may persist up to 24 hours
•	 Nausea and vomiting – may occur but are not dominant features
•	 Murphy’s sign positive (pain on inspiration during RUQ palpation)
•	 Chronic cholecystitis may present with vague symptoms such as:

–
Intolerance of fatty foods

–
Early satiety

–
Recurrent similar episodes
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Admit patient
•	 Apply DRSABCD and initiate resuscitation if signs of dehydration or sepsis
•	 Insert two large bore IV cannulae
•	 Insert urinary catheter (IDC) and measure urine output if unstable
•	 Keep nil by mouth (NBM)
•	 Start IV fluids and broad-spectrum antibiotics
•	 Provide opiate analgesia
•	 Urgently refer to the surgical team or main hospital if instability or pain persists
244 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Gall stone lodges in infundibulum of gall bladder/cystic duct/common bile duct
Increased intraluminal pressure causes hollow viscus distension and pain, nausea
and vomiting, with contraction of the whole biliary tree
Stone not passed -continued rise in
intraluminal pressure and viscus distension
causes mucosal ischaemia. Inflammatory
mediator release and bacterial infection
cause additional damage to mucosa -
acute cholecystitis

Stone is passed and symptoms
of obstruction are relieved
- biliary colic/ chronic
cholecystitis
Complications of continued gall stone obstruction*/- perforation
Infection with obstruction - gallbladder empyema (obstruction at cystic duct)
and ascending cholangitis (complete obstruction at common bile duct, with
bacterial infection)
Gallstone pancreatitis - gallstone obstruction of pancreatic duct
Chronic cholecystitis - recurrent episodes of pain and non specific symptoms
secondary to the presence of gallstones in the biliary tree
Gallstone Ileus - fistula between the inflamed gallbladder and the adjacent
duodenum or jejunum. Gallstone passes to Gl tract. Intestinal obstruction results.
Continued mucosal damage - gangrene of the gallbladder wall
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 245

3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Check vital signs: temperature, pulse, blood pressure, respiratory rate
•	 Abdominal examination:

–
RUQ tenderness

–
Murphy’s sign positive

–
Guarding may be present
•	 Look for signs of systemic sepsis (tachycardia, hypotension, low urine output)
•	 Assess hydration and urine output
4.	 Management

5.	 Follow up
•	 Monitor temperature, pulse, blood pressure, and urine output
•	 Reassess pain control and clinical response to antibiotics
•	 If pain resolves and sepsis is controlled, continue supportive care and plan for
surgical referral
•	 If pain or signs of instability persist, escalate care
6.	 Referral / consultation
•	 Consult surgeon for all patients with suspected acute cholecystitis
•	 Urgent referral to senior surgeon and/or Critical Care if instability persists despite
resuscitation
•	 Refer to the main hospital if surgical care or imaging is not available on site
246 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Rectal bleeding (Anorectal disease)
Background
Rectal bleeding may be minor spotting or significant blood loss. It may appear as
blood mixed with stool, blood coating the stool, or blood leaking from the rectum.
The cause may be benign or serious, so a careful history and examination are
essential.
Common benign causes include:
•	 Haemorrhoids
•	 Anal fissure
•	 Anorectal abscesses (including pilonidal disease)
•	 Rectal prolapse
Other causes and important differentials include:
•	 Sexually transmitted infections
•	 Inflammatory bowel disease (IBD)
•	 Malignancy (colorectal or anal cancer)
•	 Proctalgia fugax
•	 Proctitis
•	 Anorectal foreign bodies
•	 Local trauma (including sexual trauma)
Recommendations
Take a detailed history and examine the perianal region carefully. Rectal bleeding
should never be assumed to be benign without excluding serious pathology. Refer if
signs of systemic illness, large volume bleeding, or failure to respond to conservative
management.
1.
May present with
•	 Bright red blood mixed with stool
•	 Blood coating stool or leaking from the anus
•	 Perianal pain, swelling or itching
•	 Difficulty passing stool
•	 Fever, anorexia (if abscess)
•	 Recent weight loss, change in bowel habit
•	 Mucous discharge or tenesmus (urge to pass stool)
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 247

2.	 Immediate management
•	 Assess haemodynamic stability (pulse, BP)
•	 If heavy bleeding or signs of anaemia, lie
patient flat and monitor closely
•	 Provide analgesia as needed
•	 Begin IV fluids if unstable or signs of
dehydration
•	 Do not delay rectal examination if serious
pathology suspected
3.	 Clinical assessment
Detailed history should include:
•	 Onset, duration, and amount of bleeding
•	 Colour of blood – bright red vs dark or maroon
•	 Relation of blood to stool (mixed in, coating, separate)
•	 Straining or hard stools
•	 Past history of haemorrhoids
•	 Change in bowel habits – constipation, diarrhoea, mucous
•	 Associated symptoms: rectal pain, itch, abdominal pain, weight loss
•	 History of IBD, cancer, recent polypectomy
•	 History of rectal or sexual trauma
•	 Use of aspirin, NSAIDs, anticoagulants or antiplatelet agents
Examination:
•	 Inspect perianal area for:
•	 External haemorrhoids
•	 Skin tags
•	 Anal fissures
•	 Haematomas
•	 Perform rectal examination where safe
and appropriate to check for masses,
tenderness, or stool colour
248 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management
If haemorrhoids:
•	 Advise patient to:
•	 Avoid straining
•	 Maintain fibre-rich diet and good hydration
•	 Respond to urge to defecate—don’t delay bowel motion
•	 Avoid attempting to force bowel movement without urge

•	 Continue antibiotics until patient is afebrile and clinically improving
5.	 Follow up
•	 Reassess after 5–7 days or earlier if worsening
•	 Monitor for recurrence or signs of infection
•	 If bleeding persists or is associated with systemic symptoms, refer for specialist
assessment
•	 Encourage lifestyle changes to reduce straining and constipation
250 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

6.	 Referral / consultation
•	 Refer to doctor if:

–
Bleeding is persistent, recurrent, or unexplained

–
Suspicion of colorectal cancer or inflammatory bowel disease

–
Large perianal abscess requiring incision and drainage

–
Anal fissure not healing or very painful
•	 Withhold anticoagulants or antiplatelets temporarily if appropriate—consult
doctor first
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 251

Abdominal wall hernias
Background
A hernia is defined as the protrusion of a viscus or its contents through a weakness
in the abdominal wall. These are a common cause of abdominal pain and must be
assessed during abdominal examination.
Hernias can be classified as follows:
1.
Anatomical type:

–
Inguinal hernia

–
Umbilical hernia
2.
Reducibility:

–
Reducible hernia

–
Non-reducible (incarcerated or strangulated) hernia
Hernias may present without symptoms or may become painful or obstructed.
Bowel herniation into the hernia sac may lead to obstruction, strangulation, or
ischaemia.
Recommendations
All patients presenting with abdominal pain or swelling should be examined
for hernia. Refer urgently if the hernia is irreducible, painful, or associated with
symptoms of bowel obstruction. Avoid attempts to manually reduce the hernia.
1.
May present with
•	 Abnormal swelling, especially in the umbilical or groin area
•	 Swelling may reduce when lying flat (if reducible)
•	 Pain or tenderness over the swelling
•	 Nausea, vomiting, and abdominal distension if bowel obstruction present
•	 Incarcerated or strangulated hernias may present with:

–
Severe abdominal pain

–
Non-reducible tender mass

–
Constipation or no passage of flatus

–
Fever or signs of sepsis
252 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

2.	 Immediate management
If signs of intestinal involvement are present (severe abdominal pain, vomiting,
distension):
•	 Admit the patient immediately
•	 Keep nil by mouth (NBM)
•	 Insert nasogastric tube for decompression and free drainage
•	 Start IV fluid resuscitation
•	 Start broad-spectrum antibiotics
•	 Provide analgesia
•	 Arrange urgent referral to the main hospital
•	 Surgical consultation must occur within 6 hours to avoid strangulation and
ischaemic bowel

If no signs of intestinal involvement:
•	 Provide pain relief
•	 Advise rest and avoid lifting or strenuous activity
•	 Avoid massage or manual reduction (may risk ischaemia)
•	 Refer to surgeon for elective hernia repair
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Inspect for visible abdominal or groin swelling
•	 Palpate swelling: assess for tenderness and reducibility
•	 Ask the patient to cough or stand to assess impulse
•	 Check for signs of bowel obstruction:

–
Distension

–
Bowel sounds (high-pitched or absent)

–
Vomiting or failure to pass stool/gas
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 253

4.	 Management
For complicated hernia (with signs of obstruction or strangulation):

•	 Counsel patient to avoid straining, lifting heavy objects, or manipulating the
hernia
•	 Educate patient to return immediately if pain increases or hernia becomes
irreducible
5.	 Follow up
•	 Monitor for pain, recurrence, or worsening signs
•	 Reassess hernia regularly for changes in size, tenderness, or reducibility
•	 For reducible hernias, refer for elective surgical repair
•	 For non-reducible or symptomatic hernias, urgent referral is required
254 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

6.	 Referral / consultation
•	 Urgent referral to surgical team if:

–
Painful, tender, or non-reducible hernia

–
Signs of bowel obstruction or sepsis
•	 Elective referral for reducible hernias without complications
•	 Do not attempt manual reduction of incarcerated or strangulated hernias
outside hospital setting
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 255

Common medical
conditions

Urticaria, allergic rhinitis - Adult
Hives, hay fever
1.
May present with
Hives (urticaria)
•	 Itchy rash - blotches or raised red lumps:1

–
vary in size from pinhead to dinner plate. When first appear, can look like
mosquito bites

–
usually disappear within minutes to hours in one spot, but may come and go
•	 ± angio-oedema - deeper swellings mostly affects face and lips:1,2

–
often bigger, last longer and may itch less ± hurt or burn
•	 ± symptoms of viral infection - most common cause of hives in children1,2
•	 Note: if hives occur within 1–2 hours of exposure (eg food, medicines, stings) and
disappear within
•	 6–8 hours, is likely an allergic reaction. Localised hives may be a contact allergy1
Hay fever (allergic rhinitis)
•	 Clear rhinorrhoea, sneezing, nasal blockage, nasal itch3,4
•	 Darkened circles around eyes ± watery, red eyes3
•	 Itchy throat, frequent need to clear throat3
•	 Note: symptoms can be confused with recent URTI
2.	 Immediate management
•	 If hives, check for ANY signs of anaphylaxis. If any treat urgently as per
Anaphylaxis
Signs of Anaphylaxis - any  ONE of the following:
•	 Difficult/noisy breathing
•	 Swelling of tongue
•	 Swelling/tightness in throat
•	 Difficulty talking ± hoarse voice
•	 Wheeze or persistent cough
•	 Persistent dizziness or collapse
•	 Pale and floppy (young children)
•	 Abdominal pain, vomiting
•	 Suspect Foreign body in nose, in child with any nasal occlusion, facial swelling ±
pain, or smelly, purulent or blood-stained nasal discharge3,6
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 257

3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Get history, including:

–
onset/duration of symptoms

–
known allergies/triggers eg food, exercise, pollen, dust mites, mould

–
previous episodes, treatment, was it effective3

–
eczema, asthma6

–
if hives:
	›
foods and medications consumed several hours before the reaction6
	›
possible stings or bites6
	›
symptoms of a viral infection eg fever, malais

–
if hay fever:
	›
are symptoms there year round, seasonal or come and go
	›
is it troublesome for patient/impacting on sleep, sport, work etc
•	 Do physical examination, including:

–
vital signs

–
if hives - check skin, describe lesions, localised/widespread
4.	 Management
•	 If angio-oedema (see above), consult MO
•	 Advise patient to avoid/minimise exposure allergens (if known)4
•	 Give antihistamine eg cetirizine (effective for hives and hay fever)
•	 If hives advise:1

–
keep cool, wear loose clothing

–
avoid aggravating factors eg alcohol, excessive heat, spicy foods7

–
do not take aspirin or other NSAIDs - can make symptoms worse

–
most hives will resolve within a couple of weeks without treatment
•	 If hay fever:7

–
if watery, red eyes, also see Allergic conjunctivitis, for eye drops

–
advise to see MO at next clinic if:
	›
severe/troublesome for patient, or has pre-existing asthma3
	›
note: hay fever can co-exist with asthma

–
if allergic to pollens, advise may be at risk of thunderstorm asthma. Stay
indoors in thunderstorms. Also see https://www.allergy.org.au/patients/
asthma-and-allergy/ thunderstorm-asthma
258 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 259

Upper respiratory tract infection (URTI) - Adult
Common cold, influenza, bronchitis, sore throat
1.
May present with1
Common cold (acute viral rhinosinusitis)
•	 Blocked nose
•	 Runny nose or posterior nasal drip (can trigger cough)
•	 Facial pain/pressure
•	  sense of smell
•	 If fever, usually mild
Influenza
•	 As per common cold:

–
+ sudden onset of fever, headache, muscle + joint pain, severe malaise, sore
throat2,3
Bronchitis - inflammation of bronchi that usually follows an URTI4,5
•	 Cough ±

–
purulent or coloured sputum

–
SOB, wheeze

–
chest discomfort/pain due to frequent coughing

–
nasal congestion

–
headache, fever
•	 If cough not prominent feature, consider likelihood of other URTI eg common
cold, tonsillitis, pharyngitis. See Sore throat
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Use appropriate PPE
260 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Get history, including:1

–
onset, duration, severity of symptoms

–
fever

–
SOB, cough, any sputum - colour

–
facial pain, chest discomfort/pain

–
asthma, COPD, immunocompromised1,6

–
recent international travel7
•	 Do physical examination, including:

–
vital signs

–
check/listen for:
	›
WOB

›
 air entry, wheeze, crackles
	›
ENT - red throat, enlarged tonsils, bulging red eardrums
	›
enlarged head/neck lymph glands
4.	 Management
•	 Most respiratory tract infections are viral + get better without antibiotics8
•	 If severe symptoms + patient unwell consult MO
•	 Paracetamol or ibuprofen if needed. See Acute pain
•	 Advise to treat symptoms eg:

–
gargle warm salty water

–
suck on an ice cube or lozenges

–
have a soothing drink eg honey and lemon

–
use saline nasal drops/spray

–
wash hands, cover mouth when sneezing/coughing to help stop others
catching it
Common cold likely
•	 Symptoms usually clear within 7 days1
•	 dvise to return if develops features of bronchitis and/or Bacterial sinusitis
Influenza likely
•	 Consider nose and throat swab PCR to confirm (or as per Public Health Unit
advice)
•	 Consult MO if at risk of complications (antiviral medicine(s) may be indicated),
including:2
 –
≥ 65 years

–
immunocompromised, chronic conditions

–
pregnant woman

–
obesity

–
homeless person
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 261

Bronchitis likely4
•	 Advise usually resolves without antibiotics within 2–3 weeks. Cough can persist
longer, sometimes up to 8 weeks
•	 Consider other causes of cough eg:

–
Pneumonia - adult

–
Pertussis

–
influenza

–
heart failure
5.	 Follow up
•	 Advise to be reviewed if not improving in a few days, symptoms worsen, new
symptoms, or concerned. Consult MO
•	 If cough continues > 3 weeks, advise to see MO at next clinic - may need further
investigations
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 Laboratory confirmed influenza is notifiable 
262 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Acutely swollen/painful joint - Adult
Recommend1,2
•	 The causes can be difficult to diagnose. Be suspicious of septic arthritis
(an orthopaedic emergency), acute oste0myelitis + ARF
1.
May present with1-3
•	 Painful, red + swollen joint(s) ± fever, malaise
2.	 Immediate management1
•	 Vital signs
•	 Screen for Sepsis
3.	 Clinical assessment1,2
Red flags1,2
•	 Acute swelling, redness and marked  in range of motion of joint
•	 Systemic symptoms eg  HR, fever, malaise, night sweats, weight loss3
•	 Recent joint surgery
•	 Recent injury/wound (can be minor)
•	 Get history, including:

–
pain - sudden or gradual onset

–
past episodes ± diagnosis

–
ARF/RHD diagnosis or family history of1

–
other symptoms eg fatigue, skin infection, sore throat3

–
IV drug use2
•	 Examine joints:

–
swelling, tenderness, warmth + mobility

–
if the pain seems out of proportion to the joint signs - consider ARF
•	 Check for swollen lymph nodes
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 263

4.	 Management1,2
•	 Be suspicious of septic arthritis, acute oste0myelitis and ARF.
•	 If highly suggestive of gout, see Gout
•	 If suspected sprain/soft tissue injury, see Sprains/soft tissue injury
•	 Offer analgesia. See Acute pain
•	 Contact MO if any Red flags
5.	 Follow up
•	 As per MO
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 As above
264 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Gout - Adult
Background
•	 Gout occurs from deposits of urate crystals in the body. Causes joint pain +
swelling1
1.
May present with1,2
•	 Painful, red + swollen joint(s) eg big toe, knee
•	 Attack of gout
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Not applicable
3.	 Clinical assessment1,2
•	 Get history, including:

–
previous diagnosis,  past episodes of gout + treatment eg urate lowering
medicines
•	 Vital signs
•	 Examine joint(s) for swelling, tenderness, warmth and mobility:2

–
hard nodules over elbows, knees + feet may indicate chronic gout1
4.	 Management1,2
•	 Always consider another cause of the pain. See Swollen/painful joint
•	 Consult MO if:

–
systemic symptoms eg ↑ HR, fever, malaise, night sweats, weight loss

–
first attack of gout (can mimic septic arthritis, an orthopaedic emergency1)

–
re-presentation for this attack
•	 If acute attack of previously diagnosed gout, offer short-term pain relief:1,3

–
NSAID eg ibuprofen or indomethacin for 3–5 days. See Acute pain

–
OR colchicine as a single 1 day course (start as soon as possible)3
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 265

COLCHICINE
L3
FORM
STRENGTH
ROUTE
DOSE
DURATION
Tablet
500 microg
Oral
1 mg then 500 microg
1 hour later
stat
Offer CMI: May take up to 48 hours for inflammation to subside. May cause
diarrhoea, nausea, abdominal discomfort, vomiting, sore throat, rash or GI
bleeding. If already taking colchicine wait at least 12 hours before next dose
Note: If renal or hepatic impairment, seek MO advice
Contraindication: Blood dyscrasias,  severe GI disease, corneal wounds or ulcers
Management of associated emergency: Consult MO. See Anaphylaxis1,3
5.	 Follow up
•	 Advise to be seen at next MO clinic for ongoing management
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 As above
266 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Secondary prophylaxis for acute rheumatic
fever (ARF) - Adult/Child
Recommend1
•	 Strict regular long-term benzathine benzylpenicillin  (Bicillin LA®) prophylaxis
(every 21–28 days) is critical to prevent recurrent Group A Strep infections
causing ARF
•	 Every day of non-treatment over 28 days puts the person at high risk of
recurrence of ARF
Background1
•	 Recurrent ARF can lead to a chronic condition called rheumatic heart disease
(RHD) which involves deformity and dysfunction of the heart valves
•	 The RHD Register and Control Program TCH + is available for clinical support and
education  74028100 Ext 302
•	 Recommended resources. See https://www.rhdaustralia.org.au/resources

–
The 2020 Australian guideline for prevention, diagnosis  and management of
acute rheumatic fever and rheumatic heart disease (3rd edition)
1.
May present with
•	 Common age group 5-25 years old
•	 History of recurrent sore throat
•	 recurrent joint pains, may or may not be migratory
•	 Fever especially with acute attacks of joint pains
•	 Involuntary movements (syndeham chorea)
•	 Non-blanching rash which is painless and not itchy. Commonly located on the
trunk and extremeties.
•	 Easily fatigued
•	 SOB if advanced to RHD
•	 +/- Murmur on auscultation
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Pain relief: ibrufen 400mg tds no longer than a weak
•	 Paracetamol for fever
•	 Bedrest
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 267

3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Check and complete care items on the patient's ARF/RHD injection booklet

–
Fill in registration form if newly diagnosed

–
Check injection compliance

–
Last echo at least every two years, annually for RHD.
•	 Ask:

–
if any problems after previous injection(s)

–
where patient wants injection:1

–
ventrogluteal preferred site; lateral thigh is acceptable

–
note: upper outer quadrant of the buttock is associated with sciatic nerve
damage and must be used with caution
4.	 Management1
•	 The duration of secondary prophylaxis is a specialist decision based on a number
of individual and environmental factors. Prophylaxis can only be ceased by a
specialist MO
•	 Injections can be given anytime between 21–28 days:

–
a specialist may recommend a 21 day regimen (rather than 21–28 days) for
patients who have breakthrough ARF despite complete adherence to a 28 day
regimen OR have Moderate RHD .
•	 Give benzathine benzylpenicillin (Bicillin LA®):

–
consider strategies for Managing injection pain

–
if allergic to penicillin, specialist will order oral erythromycin

–
Mix: 1ml lidnocaine + 3mls water for injection.
•	 If patient has bleeding problems after injections or consistently declines Bicillin
LA® despite attempts to identify and address any barriers to injections:

–
specialist may order oral phenoxymethylpencillin
•	 If oral antibiotics given:

–
only after discussion with MO

–
emphasise consequence of missed doses + encourage to consider returning to
injections

–
 monitoring for Group A Strep infections eg Impetigo, Sore throat and
recurrence of ARF
268 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

•	 Use strategies to support patient to return for next injection eg:

–
reschedule next appointment before they leave the clinic

–
update recall for 21 days time

270 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Anemia - Adult/Child
Recommend
Anaemia is a condition where the number of red blood cells or the amount
of haemoglobin is below normal. It is not a diagnosis in itself but a sign of an
underlying cause that must be identified and treated.
Normal Haemoglobin Ranges
•	 Adults

–
Males: 13–18 g/dL

–
Females: 12–16 g/dL
•	 Children

–
Birth: 13.5 g/dL

–
6 weeks: 9.5 g/dL

–
3 months: 10 g/dL

–
6 months – 1.5 years: 11 g/dL

–
1.5 – 7 years: 10.5 g/dL

–
7 – 12 years: 11.5 g/dL
Types of Anaemia
Anaemia is not a diagnosis in itself—there is always an underlying cause which must
be determined before treatment. The main types of anaemia include:
Deficiency Anaemia
Caused by a lack of substances essential for blood production:
•	 Iron deficiency (most
common):

–
Caused by blood loss (e.g. trauma, ectopic pregnancy, childbirth, excessive
menstruation, bleeding gastric ulcer)

–
Medications such as aspirin, ibuprofen, and indomethacin may cause gastric
ulcers and bleeding

–
Inadequate dietary intake of iron (common in malnutrition)

–
 Chronic helminth infection and colon cancer can also cause chronic bleeding
•	 Folic acid deficiency:

–
Causes megaloblastic anaemia (red blood cells are larger than normal)

–
Common in pregnancy, infancy, liver disease, and malabsorption disorders
•	 Vitamin B12 deficiency:

–
Results in pernicious anaemia due to impaired absorption

–
Causes include gastrectomy, absence of intrinsic factor, or malabsorption (e.g.
non-tropical sprue with villous atrophy)
•	 Vitamin C deficiency:

–
Leads to scurvy, which can be associated with anaemia
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 271

Haemolytic Anaemia
•	 Due to excessive destruction of red blood cells
•	 May occur in conditions like sickle cell disease
Anaemia due to Bone Marrow Disorders
•	 Aplastic anaemia: failure of the bone marrow to produce red cells
•	 Leukaemia: cancer of the blood-forming tissues
•	 Drug-induced: some antibiotics (e.g. chloramphenicol), anti-epileptics, and oral
diabetic medications can suppress the bone marrow
•	 Exposure to poisons may also impair red cell production
Anaemia of Chronic Disease
•	 Seen in chronic conditions such as:

–
Renal failure

–
Tuberculosis

–
Chronic liver disease

–
Thyroid disorders

–
Rheumatoid arthritis

–
Malignancies (cancer)
1.
May present with
•	 Fatigue, pallor, weakness
•	 Shortness of breath, palpitations
•	 Dizziness, fainting, headache
•	 Poor feeding or growth (children)
•	 Irritability or behavioural changes
•	 Delayed development or school problems• Heavy or prolonged menstruation
(women)
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Check haemoglobin using lab or haemoglobinometer if available
•	 Assess and triage – check vital signs
•	 If signs of severe anaemia (extreme pallor, fainting, rapid breathing), call for help
and consult MO urgently
•	 Stop contributing medications (e.g. NSAIDs, aspirin)
•	 Deworm all patients:

–
Albendazole 400 mg stat, repeat after 1 week

–
OR Mebendazole 100 mg twice daily for 3 days, repeat after 1 week
272 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 History: recent or chronic bleeding, nutrition, medication, chronic illness
•	 Full physical exam:

–
Skin, conjunctiva, palms for pallor

–
Liver, spleen, lymph nodes

–
Rectal/pelvic exam in adults if bleeding suspected

–
Growth chart and milestones in children
•	 Ask about:

–
Menorrhagia, post-partum bleeding

–
Abdominal pain or past surgeries

–
Diet and malnutrition

–
Chronic infections (TB, liver or kidney disease)
4.	 Management
•	 If Hb low and likely cause is known (e.g. iron deficiency), treat accordingly
•	 If due to deficiency of iron, folate or vitamin B12 – give supplementation and treat
cause
•	 The underlying cause must be identified (e.g. bleeding, malabsorption, chronic
disease)
Iron therapy (first-line)
•	 Adults:

–
Ferrous sulphate 300 mg orally twice daily after meals

–
Once Hb is normal, reduce to 1 tablet daily × 6 months
•	 Children:

–
Dose as per age and weight – typically 3–6 mg/kg/day of elemental iron
divided twice daily

–
Include folic acid and Vitamin C
•	 Pregnancy:

–
Ferrous sulphate with folic acid (200 mg iron + 350 mcg folate) – 1 tablet daily
throughout pregnancy
Add-on treatments
•	 Vitamin C – 100 mg orally 3
times daily
•	 Vitamin A – 200,000 IU orally
stat in malnourished children
•	 Folic acid – Add if poor diet,
pregnancy, or megaloblastic anaemia suspected
•	 Vitamin B12 – Only on doctor
advice, for confirmed B12 deficiency
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 273

Dietary advice
•	 Encourage iron-rich foods: liver, meat, fish, leafy greens, legumes, eggs
•	 Give small, frequent meals in children
If no improvement
•	 Hb should rise ~1g/dL per week
•	 If no improvement or relapse after treatment, refer to hospital
•	 If patient becomes symptomatic or has unclear cause, refer to MO
5.	 Follow up
•	 Weekly review for 1 month
•	 Monitor clinical improvement and weight gain (children)
•	 Reinforce adherence to iron and diet
•	 Continue therapy for 3–6 months to rebuild iron stores
6.	 Referral/Consultation
•	 If severe anaemia, no improvement after 2–3 weeks, or cause unclear
•	 If symptoms worsen (e.g. fainting, breathlessness)
•	 Suspected bone marrow disorders, leukaemia, or chronic disease
•	 Children with delayed development or failure to thrive despite treatment
274 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Oedema – Adult/Child
Background
Oedema is the accumulation of fluid in the tissues, usually seen as swelling. It may
be generalised or localised. Oedema can be a sign of underlying systemic disease
and must be evaluated carefully. Bilateral oedema often suggests systemic causes
such as cardiac, renal, or hepatic disease, while unilateral oedema may point to local
causes such as infection or thrombosis.
Recommend
•	 Consider serious causes such as heart failure, nephrotic syndrome, renal failure,
liver disease, or malnutrition.
•	 In pregnant women, assess for pre-eclampsia.
•	 Swelling of one leg may be due to local causes including infection, injury, or vein
thrombosis.
•	 Refer significant oedema for review at central hospital.
1.
May present with
•	 Swelling of both legs, face, abdomen, or eyelids
•	 Shortness of breath, fatigue, distended neck veins (suggesting heart failure)
•	 Jaundice or ascites (suggesting liver disease)
•	 Pallor, tiredness, dizziness (suggesting anaemia)
•	 Swelling of arms and legs with skin changes in children (suggesting Kwashiorkor)
•	 Ankle swelling in pregnancy (physiological or pre-eclampsia)
•	 Swelling of only one leg – may be due to cellulitis, deep vein thrombosis (DVT),
or injury
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Check vital signs and oxygen saturation
•	 Triage and prioritise if patient is breathless, confused, or acutely unwell
•	 In pregnant women with oedema + raised BP or proteinuria, refer urgently for
suspected pre-eclampsia
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 275

3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Full history including:

–
Onset, location, progression of swelling

–
Symptoms of heart, kidney or liver disease

–
Medication history

–
Dietary status

–
Pregnancy status
•	 Physical examination:

–
Assess site and extent of oedema

–
Look for signs of heart failure (e.g. crepitations, elevated JVP)

–
Examine liver and abdomen for signs of liver disease or ascites

–
Check BP and urine (for protein) in pregnant women

–
Look for local signs of infection or thrombosis in one leg
4.	 Management
•	 Manage according to likely cause:

–
Heart failure: consider diuretics if advised by MO

–
Nephrotic syndrome or renal failure: consult MO; may require fluid/salt
restriction

–
Liver disease: supportive care; refer

–
Kwashiorkor: nutritional rehabilitation

–
Pregnancy: assess for pre-eclampsia

–
Infection/DVT in one leg: initiate antibiotics if cellulitis; refer if thrombosis
suspected
•	 Advise rest with leg elevation where appropriate
•	 Monitor urine output and vital signs
5.	 Follow up
•	 Monitor oedema and underlying cause
•	 Adjust treatment based on clinical course
•	 Reassess nutritional status in children or malnourished adults
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 Refer to central hospital if:

–
Generalised oedema with signs of systemic illness

–
No improvement

–
Pregnant woman with high BP or proteinuria

–
Unexplained or worsening one-leg oedema
276 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Generalised weakness – Adult/Child
Background
Generalised weakness is a common complaint and may result from physical illness,
poor nutrition, infection, mental health disorders, or chronic disease. It is important
to distinguish between true muscular weakness and fatigue. Chronic or progressive
symptoms must be evaluated thoroughly to identify the underlying cause.
Recommend
•	 Assess for systemic diseases such as anaemia, diabetes, heart failure, infection, or
neurological disorders.
•	 Rule out psychiatric causes such as depression or grief.
•	 Review nutritional status and medication use.
•	 Chronic or unexplained weakness should be referred to a medical officer.
1.
May present with
•	 Tiredness, reduced strength, inability to perform usual tasks
•	 Difficulty walking, climbing stairs, combing hair
•	 Associated symptoms: fever, palpitations, tremors, polyuria, night breathlessness
•	 History of chronic illness or recent infection
•	 Poor appetite or weight loss
•	 Emotional distress, recent bereavement, anxiety
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Not applicable
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Take a full history:

–
Onset, duration, pattern (general or local)

–
Triggers or relieving factors

–
Impact on work/daily life

–
Recent illnesses, sleep, diet, stress

–
Medication or substance use
•	 Physical exam including:

–
Vitals and general appearance

–
Neurological assessment

–
Musculoskeletal exam

–
Signs of anaemia, infection, or thyroid disease
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 277

4.	 Management
•	 Address reversible causes:

–
Poor sleep or nutrition: improve rest and diet

–
Overwork: advise rest and gradual return to activity

–
Mild infection: symptomatic treatment
•	 Treat specific conditions if identified:

–
Anaemia: treat per anaemia protocol

–
Diabetes or hypothyroidism: refer to MO

–
Cardiac or respiratory issues: assess and refer as needed

–
Mental health issues: provide support, consider MO review
•	 Reassure if no serious cause found and symptoms are mild
•	 Encourage gradual increase in activity and good sleep hygiene
5.	 Follow up
•	 Reassess within 1–2 weeks or sooner if deterioration
•	 Monitor weight, activity tolerance, mood and energy levels
•	 Repeat full assessment if new symptoms emerge
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 Refer to MO if:

–
Weakness persists or worsens without clear cause

–
Associated with serious signs (fever, weight loss, breathlessness, neurological
symptoms)

–
Suspected depression or anxiety requiring more support
278 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Fever – Adult
Recommend
•	 Fever in adults may have a wide range of causes. A careful clinical history and
examination can help narrow the differential diagnosis.
•	 If fever persists for >3 weeks without diagnosis, and evaluation has failed to
identify a cause, the condition may be classified as “fever of unknown origin”
(FUO).
•	 Refer to medical officer if patient is clinically unwell, deteriorating, or the cause
of fever remains unclear.
Background
•	 Fever is a common symptom in adults and may reflect infections, malignancies,
autoimmune conditions, or other systemic disorders. Persistent or unexplained
fever, particularly when accompanied by weight loss, night sweats, or
localising symptoms, warrants systematic evaluation. Clinical features may help
distinguish between infectious and non-infectious causes, such as malignancy or
autoimmune disease.
1.
May present with (see table on following pages)
•	 Fever (subjective or documented ≥38°C)
•	 Chills, night sweats, weight loss, anorexia
•	 Localised pain (headache, abdominal pain, back pain, sore throat)
•	 Fatigue, generalised weakness
•	 Cough, hemoptysis
•	 Rash, myalgias, joint pain
•	 Urinary symptoms
•	 Neurological symptoms (confusion, headache, visual changes)
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Assess and stabilise airway, breathing, circulation (ABC) if acutely unwell
•	 Monitor vital signs: temperature, heart rate, respiratory rate, blood pressure,
oxygen saturation
•	 Commence IV fluids if hypotensive or dehydrated
•	 Administer antipyretics (e.g. paracetamol 1g orally every 6 hours if needed)
•	 Isolate patient if suspicion of a contagious infection (e.g. tuberculosis, typhoid,
viral hemorrhagic fever)
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 279

3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Obtain detailed history:

–
Onset and duration of fever

–
Associated symptoms (see above)

–
Recent travel, exposures (e.g. TB contacts, animals, unsafe food or water)

–
Immunocompromised status (HIV, diabetes, steroid use)

–
Vaccination history

–
Medication history

–
Menstrual and sexual history in women
•	 Perform physical examination:

–
Look for lymphadenopathy, jaundice, rashes, hepatosplenomegaly

–
Cardiac exam: new murmurs (suggestive of endocarditis)

–
Respiratory exam: crackles, reduced air entry (TB or pneumonia)

–
Neurological exam: meningism, confusion

–
Abdominal exam: tenderness, guarding, rebound, ascites

–
Genital and rectal exam as appropriate
4.	 Management
•	 Treat empirically only if a likely diagnosis is made or patient is severely ill
•	 Common syndromes and actions:

–
Suspected malaria: Refer and commence antimalarials if rapid test or thick
smear is positive

–
Suspected pneumonia: Consider antibiotics (e.g. amoxicillin or doxycycline)

–
Suspected TB: Refer for sputum testing and evaluation

–
Suspected UTI: Treat with appropriate antibiotics (e.g. nitrofurantoin or
trimethoprim)

–
Suspected typhoid: Ceftriaxone IV or azithromycin orally

–
Suspected endocarditis: Refer urgently for medical review and blood cultures
•	 Provide supportive care: fluids, nutrition, rest
•	 Monitor progress daily
5.	 Follow up
•	 Review in 2–3 days or sooner if symptoms worsen
•	 If no improvement after 5–7 days of empirical treatment, consult medical officer
•	 Investigate further if fever continues beyond 2 weeks
6.	 Referral/Consultation
Refer to a doctor or hospital if:
•	 Fever >7 days with no clear cause
•	 Severe or rapidly worsening symptoms
•	 Suspected endocarditis, meningitis, or TB
•	 Known malignancy or immunocompromised patient
•	 Need for further laboratory investigations or imaging

Non-Communicable Disease (NCD)
Assessment and management
Recommend
•	 Assess all adult patients for common NCDs: obesity, hypertension, and diabetes.
•	 Use risk-based approach for prevention and treatment.
•	 Counsel patients on lifestyle changes and adherence to medications.
•	 Refer to hospital or physician when urgent criteria are met.
Background
•	 Non-communicable diseases (NCDs) are a major public health concern in
Kiribati. The most prevalent NCDs include cardiovascular diseases, diabetes,
and associated metabolic conditions such as obesity and dyslipidemia.
Effective management requires a comprehensive approach including lifestyle
interventions, medication adherence, regular screening, and timely referral.
1.
May present with
•	 General fatigue or weakness
•	 Excess weight or central obesity
•	 Headaches, dizziness
•	 Blurred vision or visual changes
•	 Excessive thirst, hunger, urination
•	 Sores on feet or infections that heal slowly
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Check vital signs: BP, HR, weight, height (calculate BMI)
•	 Measure random or fasting blood glucose
•	 Ask about lifestyle, symptoms, family history
•	 If symptoms suggest urgent referral (e.g. BP >200/120 or blood glucose >30
mmol/L), refer immediately
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 BMI:

–
Overweight: 25.0–29.9

–
Obesity: ≥30
284 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

How to measure BMI and screen for obesity
MEASURE BODY MASS INDEX (BMI):
•	 Using the adult height board, take the client’s height in centimetres.
•	 Using the beam balance (weighing scale), take the client’s weight in kilograms.
•	 Use the BMI chart or the formula below to compute for the BMI
BMI = weight (kg) ÷ height (m)2     or
         = weight (kg) ÷ height (cm) ÷ height (cm) x 10,000
CLASSIFY
If the client’s BMI is equal to or above 30, the client is obese and is AT RISK.
Classification
BMI
Underweight
≤ 18.5
Healthy weight
18.5 – 24.9
Overweight
25.0 – 29.9
Obese
30.0 – 39.9
Severely Obese
≥ 40.0
•	 BP classification:
If the client’s average blood pressure is below 120/80 mm Hg, classify as NOT AT
RISK.  Otherwise, refer below.

–
Normal: &lt;120/80

–
Prehypertension: 120–139/80–89

–
Stage 1 HTN: 140–159/90–99

–
Stage 2 HTN: ≥160/≥100
•	 Blood glucose:

–
FBS ≥6.1 mmol/L or RBS ≥11.1 mmol/L = raised

–
Confirm DM if FBS >7.0 mmol/L or PPBS >11.1 mmol/L twice
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 285

4.	 Obesity
•	 Counsel on dietary changes, portion control, physical activity
•	 Monitor BMI and waist circumference
5.	 Management of Hypertension
•	 Start treatment within 4 weeks of diagnosis
•	 &lt;65 yrs: HCTZ 12.5–25 mg/day or Enalapril 5–20 mg/day
• ≥65 yrs: Amlodipine 2.5–10 mg/day or HCTZ
•	 Use combinations as needed (e.g. Enalapril + HCTZ)
•	 Avoid Atenolol in asthma; avoid HCTZ in gout

6.	 Management of Diabetes
•	 Metformin 500 mg bd with meals (max 1 g tid)
•	 Add Glibenclamide or Glipizide if uncontrolled
•	 If still uncontrolled, add insulin (Isophane 10U at night)
•	 Monitor fasting BGL daily, titrate as needed
Supportive Management
•	 Statins (Simvastatin/Atorvastatin) for Type 2 DM ≥40 yrs or TC ≥8 mmol/L
•	 Aspirin 75–100 mg if CVD risk ≥20% (no contraindications)
•	 Inspect feet every visit; perform microfilament test every 6 months
•	 Eye exam yearly; refer for vision changes
•	 Check urine protein yearly
288 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

preferred test for monitoring blood
glucose control.

with each patient, contrasting likely
benefit of long-term better glycaemic
control with health risk and quality of

achieve target.
•	 Consider relaxing target HbA1c
level if side effects of achieving
this put the patient at high risk of

drivers, people operating machinery),
if advanced complications or other

MANAGEMENT OF HYPOGLYCAEMIA (EMERGENCY)
(PG. 48 OF PEN GUIDELINE.)
•	 Hypoglycaemia is blood glucose level of 4mmol/L or less
•	 The signs and symptoms of hypoglycaemia are:

–
Cold sweat

–
Tremor

–
Tingling in the lips or mouth

–
Hunger

–
Palpitations

–
Pounding headache

–
Feeling faint or hungry

–
Light headedness

–
Irritability

–
Confusion

–
Decreased or loss of consciousness
•	 If the patient is unconscious do not administer anything orally.  Seek medical
attention immediately.
•	 If the patient experiences symptoms of hypoglycaemia or a blood glucose level
of 4mmol/L or less and is conscious:
•	 Administer quick-acting sugar immediately.   Examples of quick-acting sugar
include:

–
2 teaspoons of sugar

–
2 teaspoons of honey or jam

–
1 glass of fruit juice

–
 ½ can of soft drink

–
6 jelly beans or soft lollies

–
1 ripe banana
•	 Recheck blood glucose level in 15 minutes, if available.
•	 If blood glucose level is ≤4mmol/L or the patient is not feeling better, give
quick-acting sugar again or give long acting carbohydrate or let the patient
have meal if it is due. Example of long acting carbohydrates include:

–
1 piece of fruit

–
Sandwich

–
Biscuits
•	 Let the patient rest.
•	 Identify the cause of hypoglycaemia, which may include:

–
Missing a meal, delayed meal or no carbohydrate in previous meal

–
Physical activity or exercise

–
Too much oral hypoglycaemic or insulin

–
Too much intake of alcohol or intake of alcohol on an empty stomach
•	 Advise the patient to carry quick-acting sugar with them at all times.
*Improving Diabetes Care, A Training Program for the Health Workers in Nauru.
World Diabetes Foundation Project, 2007.
290 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

MANAGEMENT OF DIABETIC KETOACIDOSIS (EMERGENCY)
(PG. 47 OF PEN GUIDELINE.)
•	 Diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) is characterized by ketosis, hyperglycaemia and
acidosis. DKA occurs predominantly in Type 1 diabetic patients but can occur
in Type 2 diabetes.
•	 The common precipitating factors of DKA are omission of insulin, infection
or sepsis, drugs such as corticosteroids and recent trauma or acute coronary
event.
•	 Symptoms include:

–
Vomiting

–
Abdominal pain

–
Kussmaul’s breathing (initially rapid and shallow but as acidosis worsens,
breathing becomes deep and laboured)

–
Mental confusion progressing to coma

–
Dehydration

–
Ketotic breath
•	 Patients also notice rapid weight loss which can be a marker for dehydration. It
is necessary to test urine for moderate to large ketone bodies.
•	 Management should be undertaken urgently, and the most important
intervention is
FLUID REPLACEMENT
•	 Administer intravenous normal saline – first litre for 30 minutes, second later
for one hour, third litre for 2 hours and fourth later for 4 hours.
•	 Transfer patient to the hospital once condition is stabilized.
•	 In case that an IV access cannot be established, give a short-acting insulin e.g.
Actrapid IM at 8 units/hour. Insulin therapy can be started subsequently.
*RMI National Diabetes Guidelines 2016
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 291

THE RECOMMENDED GUIDELINES ON ANTI-PLATELET DRUG
(PG.43 OF THE PEN GUIDELINE)
All individuals with established CVD or CVD risk ≥20% should be given
low-dose Aspirin (start at 75mg – 100mg) if there are no contraindications
(e.g. hypersensitivity to aspirin, bleeding GI ulcers, haemorrhagic diathesis,
haemorrhoids, thrombocytopenia, ulcerative colitis etc.)

RECOMMENDED GUIDELINES ON LIPID-LOWERING DRUGS (STATINS)
(PG.42 OF THE PEN GUIDELINE)
All individuals with total cholesterol at or above 8 mmol/L (320 mg/dl) should
be advised to follow a lipid-lowering diet and given a statin (Simvastatin/
Atorvastatin, start at 10mg – 40mg, nocte) to lower the risk of cardiovascular
disease.
All other individuals need to be managed according to the cardiovascular risk (10-
year risk of cardiovascular event &lt;5%, 5 to &lt;10%, 10 to &lt;20%, ≥20%)
