7.	 Follow up
•	 Monthly for BP or glucose control
•	 Annually for lipid profile, proteinuria, foot and eye checks
•	 Reinforce lifestyle advice at each visit
8.	 Referral/Consultation Urgent referral to hospital if:
•	 BP >200/120 mmHg after rest
•	 Blood glucose >30 mmol/L or >20 mmol/L with symptoms
•	 Diabetic foot ulcers, severe infection, or vision loss
•	 New chest pain, stroke/TIA symptoms
Refer to physician if:
•	 Persistent BP >140/90 in people &lt;40 yrs
•	 Target organ damage or known CVD
•	 Poor glucose control despite oral meds
•	 Newly diagnosed DM with urine ketones 2+
•	 Total cholesterol >8 mmol/L
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 293

Cardiovascular and
respiratory disorders

Congestive cardiac failure - Adult
Recommend
•	 CHF is a clinical syndrome that may develop slowly over months or years and is
due to the heart's inability to pump enough blood to meet the body’s needs.
•	 It is vital to identify and manage both the underlying cause and any precipitating
factors.
•	 Early diagnosis and appropriate treatment can improve quality of life and reduce
morbidity and mortality.
Background
Congestive Heart Failure (CHF) is a condition caused by structural or functional
abnormalities of the heart leading to impaired cardiac output. It is not a disease
itself, but a syndrome resulting from other cardiac or systemic conditions.
CHF is commonly due to conditions such as ischaemic heart disease, valvular heart
disease, hypertension, hyperthyroidism, or cardiomyopathy. Less common causes
include congenital heart disease, infective endocarditis, myocarditis, cor pulmonale,
and pericardial disease. In many cases, CHF is in a compensated state until
decompensated by precipitating factors.
1.
May present with
•	 Shortness of breath on exertion or at rest
•	 Orthopnoea or paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnoea
•	 Fatigue or weakness
•	 Peripheral oedema
•	 Elevated jugular venous pressure
•	 Hepatomegaly or ascites
•	 Weight gain from fluid retention
2.	 Immediate management If severe cardiac failure:
•	 Sit patient upright
•	 Give oxygen (1–3 L/min if available)
•	 Administer Frusemide 40 mg IM or IV slowly over 10 minutes
•	 Contact medical officer urgently
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 295

3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Take history of cardiac disease, hypertension, medications, salt/fluid intake,
adherence
•	 Examine for signs of fluid overload, arrhythmia, murmurs, infection
•	 Investigations (if available):

–
CBC

–
Electrolytes and urea

–
ECG

–
Chest X-ray (if accessible)
4.	 Management
Non-pharmacological:
•	 Salt restriction (no added salt)
•	 Weight reduction if overweight
•	 Fluid restriction only if dilutional hyponatraemia (limit to 1.5 L/day)
•	 Daily weights before breakfast with a target weight loss of 05.-1kg/day. If target
weight loss not achieved it indicates inadequate diuresis and may push up
frusemide dose if BP >90/60.
•	 Educate on medication adherence and monitoring symptoms
Pharmacological (mild to moderate heart failure):
•	 Frusemide 40–80 mg orally daily
•	 Enalapril 2.5 mg daily, increasing gradually to a max of 20 mg daily

–
Monitor for hypotension, especially in elderly, dehydrated, or hyponatraemic
patients
•	 If still symptomatic, add:

–
Digoxin 0.0625–0.5 mg orally daily (adjust dose based on age and renal
function)
Other medications (if available):
•	 Spironolactone, thiazide diuretics
•	 Beta-blockers (carvedilol, metoprolol, bisoprolol)
•	 Isosorbide dinitrate
•	 Hydralazine
5. Precipitating factors
•	 Poor medication adherence
•	 Excess salt or fluid intake
•	 NSAIDs, steroids, antidepressants, verapamil
•	 Infections, especially respiratory
•	 Anaemia, hyperthyroidism, uncontrolled hypertension
•	 Arrhythmias (especially atrial fibrillation)
296 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

•	 Acute myocardial infarction
•	 Pregnancy
•	 Overexertion
5.	 Follow up
•	 Monitor clinical status, weight, adherence, side effects
•	 Adjust medications as needed
•	 Reinforce lifestyle modifications
•	 Ensure follow-up with medical officer
6.	 Referral / Consultation
•	 Always consult a medical officer when managing CHF
•	 Refer urgently if:

–
No response to treatment

–
Worsening symptoms

–
Suspected underlying cause needing further investigation
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 297

Pneumonia - Adult
Recommend
•	 Pneumonia is a common and potentially serious lung infection requiring timely
recognition and appropriate management.
•	 Antibiotic treatment should be initiated early in suspected cases.
•	 Patients with moderate or severe pneumonia may require referral or
hospitalisation.
Background
Pneumonia is an acute infection of the lung parenchyma, often presenting with
cough, fever, pleuritic chest pain, and shortness of breath. It is caused by a range of
pathogens, including bacteria, viruses, and fungi, and its severity can vary widely.
Risk factors include chronic illness, advanced age, recent travel, hazardous alcohol
use, and exposure to environmental sources (e.g. muddy soil/water).
1.
May present with
•	 Pleuritic chest pain
•	 Fever, rigors
•	 Cough with sputum production, shortness of breath
•	 Fatigue, muscle aches or generalised body pain
2.	 Immediate management Clinical assessment
•	 •
Take detailed history, including:

–
Chest pain, fever, rigors, night sweats

–
Cough and sputum characteristics (purulent or coloured)

–
Shortness of breath, increased respiratory rate

–
Existing chronic illnesses (e.g. diabetes, CKD, CLD), immunocompromised
status

–
Hazardous alcohol intake

–
Recent travel or exposure to muddy soil or water
•	 Physical examination:

–
Observe work of breathing

–
Listen for decreased air entry, wheeze, crackles (do they clear with cough?)

–
Assess for dullness on percussion

–
Inspect skin for ulcers, abscesses, non-healing sores
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Contact MO in all cases for further advice

–
May recommend antibiotics, sputum for MCS ± PCR, IV fluids, or evacuation
298 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management Evacuation/hospitalisation is needed
if any of the following:
•	 RR ≥ 25/min, HR ≥ 100 bpm, sBP &lt; 90 mmHg
•	 SpO2 &lt; 92% on room air (or lower than normal in COPD)
•	 Acute confusion
•	 Comorbidities, social circumstances, or age ≥ 65 years
CORB-65 Severity Score
•	 C: acute Confusion
•	 O: Oxygen saturation ≤ 90%
•	 R: Respiratory rate ≥ 30/min
•	 B: low Blood pressure (systolic &lt; 90 mmHg or diastolic ≤ 60 mmHg)
•	 Score ≥ 2 suggests severe CAP and need for inpatient management
Antibiotic Treatment
•	 Outpatient (if mild):

–
Oral amoxicillin or daily IM procaine penicillin

–
If penicillin allergy: doxycycline orally
•	 Inpatient (severe or moderate with CORB-65 ≥ 2):

–
Benzylpenicillin 2MU IV every 6 hours

–
PLUS doxycycline 100 mg orally twice daily

–
Discontinue IV once clinically improving and switch to oral antibiotics to
complete course
Advise:
•	 Fever should reduce within a few days
•	 Appetite and general wellbeing should improve progressively
•	 Other symptoms may take weeks to fully resolve
5.	 Follow up
•	 Daily review until improvement, or earlier if symptoms worsen
•	 Consult MO if no improvement or worsening
•	 Continue follow-up at next scheduled clinic visit
6.	 Referral/Consultation
•	 Refer if:

–
No improvement after 3 days

–
Severe pneumonia suspected
References:
•	 Adapted from current clinical guidelines on pneumonia assessment and
management.
•	 CORB-65 tool adapted from Australian pneumonia study cohort.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 299

Pleural effusion - Adult
Recommend
Pleural effusion is a potentially serious condition that may indicate infection,
heart failure, or malignancy. Evaluation of the underlying cause and referral for
definitive management is essential. If an underlying lung infection is suspected, treat
empirically for pneumonia. Provide oxygen support if SpO₂ is less than 94%.
Background
Pleural effusion is the collection of fluid in the pleural space surrounding the lungs.
It commonly occurs due to tuberculosis, congestive heart failure, malignancy, or
bacterial infections. Symptoms can be subtle but may include cough, shortness of
breath, and chest discomfort. Diagnosis requires clinical suspicion and confirmation
via chest examination or imaging.
1.
May present with
•	 Shortness of breath, especially on exertion
•	 Chest heaviness or discomfort
•	 Cough, which may be dry or productive
•	 Fever or night sweats, especially if due to infection or TB
•	 General fatigue
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Assess airway and breathing
•	 Support the patient in a comfortable sitting position
•	 Administer oxygen if SpO₂ is less than 94%
•	 Take history:
	›
Duration of symptoms
	›
History of TB, heart failure, or cancer
	›
Night sweats, weight loss, recent illness
•	 Physical examination:
	›
Reduced chest movement on affected side
	›
Dullness to percussion over fluid
	›
Decreased or absent breath sounds
	›
Reduced vocal fremitus
	›
Possible tracheal deviation in large effusions
•	 Check for associated signs:
	›
Lymphadenopathy
	›
Peripheral oedema (suggestive of heart failure)
	›
Signs of systemic disease
300 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Perform full respiratory and cardiovascular exam
•	 Assess vital signs
•	 If signs of infection are present (e.g. fever, purulent sputum, crackles), treat
empirically for pneumonia while awaiting referral
•	 Consider differential diagnoses: pneumonia, TB, cancer, heart failure
4.	 Management
•	 Consult MO in all suspected cases
•	 Refer to hospital or doctor for further assessment and possible thoracocentesis
•	 Do not attempt aspiration or drainage in PHC setting without training and
supervision
•	 Provide oxygen therapy if SpO₂ &lt; 94%
•	 If pneumonia is suspected, start empiric antibiotic treatment as per pneumonia
protocol
•	 Supportive care: monitoring and symptom relief
5.	 Follow up
•	 Review at next clinic visit if already referred
•	 Monitor for worsening breathlessness or new symptoms
•	 Support adherence to TB or heart failure treatment if initiated
5.	 Referral/Consultation
•	 Refer urgently if:

–
Moderate to large effusion suspected

–
Respiratory distress

–
Suspected TB or malignancy

–
No clear cause found

–
Patient fails to respond to initial antibiotic treatment
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 301

Dyspnoea – Adult
Recommend
Dyspnoea is a serious clinical symptom that may be caused by cardiac or
respiratory conditions. Early identification of the underlying cause is important
for appropriate management. Sudden onset dyspnoea, especially in children, may
indicate foreign body aspiration and requires urgent attention.
Background
Dyspnoea is defined as an uncomfortable awareness of breathing or difficulty in
breathing. It can result from disease of the lungs, heart, or nervous system. Common
causes include asthma, pneumonia, congestive heart failure, pulmonary embolism,
anaemia, or pleural effusion. It is essential to distinguish between cardiac and
respiratory origins of breathlessness for appropriate management.
1.
May present with
•	 Shortness of breath during activity or at rest
•	 Chest tightness (may be mistaken for pain)
•	 Waking at night gasping for breath (paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnoea)
•	 Needing multiple pillows to sleep (orthopnoea)
•	 Wheeze, cough (dry or productive)
•	 Fatigue, reduced exercise tolerance
•	 Anxiety, rapid breathing, fast heartbeat
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Assess airway, breathing, and circulation
•	 Provide oxygen if available (1–3 litres per minute)
•	 Support patient in a sitting position
•	 Ask about and document key symptoms and history:

–
Onset, duration, triggers (e.g. exercise, allergens)

–
History of heart disease or respiratory illness

–
Smoking or occupational exposures

–
Presence of cough, sputum (colour, amount)

–
Orthopnoea or paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnoea
•	 Do full physical examination:

–
Vital signs: RR, SpO₂, HR, BP, temperature

–
Chest exam: wheeze, crackles, reduced breath sounds, dullness to percussion

–
Cardiac exam: murmur, elevated JVP, pedal oedema

–
General appearance and work of breathing
•	 Consult MO urgently if:

–
Acute severe distress

–
Hypoxia or cyanosis
	›
Suspected cardiac failure or pulmonary embolism
	›
No clear diagnosis after assessment
302 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Distinguish between likely cardiac and respiratory causes:

–
Cardiac: history of heart failure, dry cough, white frothy sputum, orthopnoea,
peripheral oedema, crepitations, improved with diuretics

–
Respiratory: history of asthma or chronic lung disease, purulent sputum,
wheeze, improved with bronchodilators

–
PE: sudden onset, tachypnoea, tachycardia, immobility history
•	 Identify red flags:

–
Confusion, cyanosis, very low SpO₂, inability to speak full sentences
4.	 Management
•	 Start initial treatment based on suspected cause (e.g. salbutamol for asthma,
frusemide for heart failure)
•	 Provide oxygen if desaturation is present
•	 Consult MO for further management or referral
•	 Prepare for evacuation if severe symptoms or uncertain diagnosis
5.	 Follow up
•	 Monitor vital signs regularly
•	 Reassess respiratory status every 2–4 hours
•	 Review in clinic once stable to confirm underlying diagnosis and ongoing
treatment
6.	 Referral/Consultation
•	 Refer to medical officer for all moderate to severe cases or if diagnosis unclear
•	 Evacuate if respiratory distress does not improve with initial treatment or if
hypoxia persists
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 303

Ear, Nose and Throat
(ENT)

Ear assessment - Adult/Child
Recommendations
•	 Check every child’s ears at each clinic visit
•	 Encourage families to bring their child to the clinic if they notice any ear
symptoms or have concerns about hearing or speech development
•	 If hearing loss is suspected, liaise with the school or daycare to support the
child with classroom strategies such as sound field amplification or seating
arrangements
Background
•	 Otitis media (OM) is inflammation or infection of the middle ear. In children,
OM may develop within the first months of life and often becomes recurrent or
chronic, potentially causing hearing loss
•	 Hearing loss can significantly affect speech and language development and
educational outcomes
Resources
•	 Care for Kids’ Ears – Australian Government resources for families and carers
Website: www.careforkidsears.health.gov.au/internet/cfke/publishing.nsf/
Content/Home
•	 Otitis Media Guidelines and App – Clinical guidance and mobile app for
diagnosis and management
Website: https://otitismediaguidelines.com/#/start-main
Preventing Ear Infections
•	 Practise regular handwashing, especially after nose blowing or coughing
•	 Keep hands and face free of nasal discharge
•	 Ensure children are up to date with pneumococcal and influenza vaccinations
•	 Teach and model ‘Blow, Breathe, Cough’ technique
•	 Breastfeed if possible; if bottle feeding, use an upright feeding position (avoid
feeding while lying flat)
•	 Avoid the use of dummies for children over 6 months of age
•	 Keep sick children away from babies
•	 Avoid exposing children to tobacco smoke
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 305

1.
May present with1
•	 No symptoms - routine check of ears
•	 Sore, itchy, or discharging ear(s) or hearing loss
•	 Young child - pulling/rubbing/holding ear(s), unsettled/irritable, ↓feeding
•	 Concerns about ears, hearing or language/talking
2.	 Immediate management1
•	 If unwell child with fever, admit and start antibiotic
•	 Monitor vital sign
•	 Consultation or refer.
3.	 Clinical assessment1
•	 If ear symptoms, ask about:

–
onset, severity, duration

–
pain/itchy

–
discharge - colour, amount

–
deafness, fullness

–
dizziness/tinnitus (ringing in the ear)

–
recent swimming/water sports

–
trauma/blow to ear eg slap, diving, object poked in3

–
Any other symptoms eg:
	›
runny nose, sore throat, cough
	›
hearing loss
	›
concerns about language/talking, learning or behavioural problems (could
mean ↓ hearing)

–
Get past history:
	›
prior ear infection(s), perforation(s), operation(s) to ear, ENT review - what,
when, treatment
	›
prior serious illnesses eg meningitis
	›
last hearing test/audiology (date), results
	›
family history of ear/hearing concerns
Examine ears 1,4,6
•	 Examine both ears. Start with normal ear
•	 Check pinna (outer ear). Any:

–
discharge, flaky or scaly skin

–
pain on moving pinna or tragus (flap of skin in front of ear canal)

–
is ear pushed out/forwards
•	 Check behind ear for redness or swelling
•	 Palpate for swollen/tender lymph glands - around ears and neck
306 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

•	 Look inside ear with otoscope - if pain levels allow:

–
pull pinna to straighten canal: adult - up, out and back; child - down and back

–
if pus/discharge - gently clean with Tissue spears first

–
if impacted wax prevents examination consider syringing with warm water (if
skilled):7

–
note: do NOT irrigate if peforation likely, diabetes, immunocompromised or on
anticoagulants7
•	 Ear canal:

–
swelling, redness, lump(s), flakes/scales

–
discharge, foreign body, debris, dislodged grommet (ventilation tube)
•	 Tympanic membrane (TM) (eardrum):1,6

–
colour - pearly grey, yellow/amber, pink/red, white (pus),white chalky patches
(scarring)

–
translucency - shiny/translucent (normal), opaque, dull/cloudy, fluid/bubbles

–
position - neutral, bulging or retracted (sucked in):

–
handle of malleus is prominent if retracted, or not seen well if bulging6

–
perforation (hole) - position, duration, size (a readily visible hole is ‘moderate’
to ‘large’ size):

–
a severely retracted eardrum, retraction pocket or healed hole might be
mistaken for a perforation

–
grommet
Eardrum Red Flags
If any of the following signs are observed, further assessment is required:
•	 Hole or retraction in the upper (attic) part of the eardrum
•	 Crusting, granulation tissue, or discharge in the attic region
•	 Eardrum appears severely retracted (sucked in)
•	 Presence of a dull white mass behind the eardrum
•	 Perforation located near the edge of the eardrum
 Possible cholesteatoma
Consult a MA or Medical Assistance urgently for ENT referral.
•	 Consider looking for movement of intact eardrum(s) if not too painful:1

–
if eardrum moves in and out easily you can exclude a perforation + fluid in the
middle ear (ie

–
exclude otitis media and acute otitis media)

–
check for movement by: gentle valsalva (hold nose and blow), pneumatic
otoscopy or

–
tympanometry (Type A is normal)
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 307

•	 Hearing Test:
“The Audiometer” is the machine measures the sound levels that people can hear
in units called decibels in hearing level (dB HL). For example, people with normal
hearing may be able to hear a sound at a level of 0 decibel (0 dB HL). A person
with hearing loss may only be able to hear a sound at a level of 50 decibels
which is in the range for moderate hearing impairment. This test available now at
the central hospital (TCH-Nawerewere).
•	 Weber’s and Rinne’s tests:
These tests assess air conduction (AC) and bone conduction (BC) and are used
to help delineate whether hearing loss is sensorineural (SNHL)or conductive
(CHL) in origin
Check nose, throat, chest + other systems as needed.
308 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Nose bleed/epistaxis - adult/child
Background
•	 95% of all nose bleeds are anterior and are usually managed by simple
compression of the mose.
1.
May present with
•	 Nose bleed
2.	 Immediate management1
ALERT suspect Button battery insertion or ingestion in child with blood around
nostrils. If lodged in the eosophagus, it can burn a hole through to the aorta causing
catastrophic haemorrhage.
•	 DRSABCD
•	 If active bleeding + SHOCKED2 (major obvious epistaxis in process)
(cool, ↑HR , CRT > 2 seconds):

–
Admit

–
urgently consult MA/NP + urgent evacuation

–
insert Anterior nasal pack (use cotton or ribbon gauge) soaked with
adrenaline 0.5mg (5mls of 1:10,000).

–
Insert 2 large IV cannula

–
start IVF resuscitation with sodium chloride 0.9% 10–20 mL/kg

–
If not controlled,
	›
Then apply Posterior nasal pack,  (take out anterior pack first)
	›
Patient positioning sits upright, nose succion.
	›
Insert a lubrication Foley cateter (10-14 Fr) gently passing along the floor of
the nose until t appear at the back of the oropharyngx.
	›
Inflate with a small amount of saline (7-10mls)
	›
Gently pulled forward until the inflated balloon seats firmly against the
posterior nasal opening then insert anterior packing (Cotton/ribbon gauge)
to fill up the front part of the nasal cavity.
	›
Then clamp the catheter infront of the nostril entrance to maintain tension
and prevent dislodgement.

–
Antibiotic: Augumentin or Septrin  (when applying posterior nasal packing).
•	 If active bleeding + NOT shocked:2

–
apply continuous pressure on the lower third of nose with thumb and
forefinger for 5–10 minutes

–
sit upright, lean forward

–
breathe through mouth + spit blood out rather than swallowing
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 309

–
if not stopped after 10–15 minutes:1,2
	›
reapply pressure and reassess (incorrect pressure most common cause of
continued bleeding) if still not stop
	›
insert the anterior nasal pack with adrenaline and continue applying
pressure

–
if still bleeding:

–
Then apply posterior nasal pack

–
continue to manage as per ‘if active bleeding + shocked’ above

–
urgently consult MA/NP + urgent evacuation
3.	 Clinical assessment 1,2
•	 Do vital signs
•	 Get history, including:

–
onset, duration and severity of bleeding

–
prior nose bleeds - when, how often, severity, treatment

–
red flags for tumour e.g, nasal blockage, facial pain/swelling, headaches,
deep toothache, teenage male1
•	 risk factors for nose bleed:

–
nose or facial trauma, nose picking, CPAP use

–
prior nasal surgery

–
intranasal medicine or drug use

–
bleeding disorders (or family history of), easy bruising

–
kidney/liver disease, hypertension

–
taking anticoagulants/antiplatelets e.g, NSAIDs
4.	 Management
•	 If bleeding stops with compression alone, patient can go home with first aid
advice:

–
return if bleeding re-starts and unable to control at home with compression

–
if risk factors/red flags present or recurrent bleed(s), advise to see MA/NP at
next clinic
•	 If anterior nasal pack inserted, MA/NP may advise:1,2

–
evacuation/hospitalisation OR home with pack in situ + give Nasal pack
discharge advice (remove in 24–72 hours)

–
± antibiotics

310 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Nasal pack discharge advice1
•	 May have symptoms of a cold while in place eg blocked nose, ↓ smell, facial
pressure, headaches, nasal drainage, tearing from eyes
•	 Try not to blow nose. Sneeze with open mouth
•	 To avoid ↑ blood flow to the nose and risk of further bleeding - avoid straining,
lifting > 5kg, strenuous exercise, bending over+ sleep with head slightly elevated
•	 Avoid aspirin and ibuprofen (may ↑ bleeding). Take paracetamol if needed for pain
•	 Keep pack moist by spraying with sodium chloride 0.9%
•	 Return immediately if bleeding starts (nose or mouth), fever, ↑ pain, vision
changes, SOB, loss of colour of skin around nose, swelling of face, skin rash

5.	 Follow up
•	 If nasal pack in situ and not evacuated, review next day or sooner if any concerns:

–
remove pack as advised by MA/NP
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 MA/NP may consider ENT referral as appropriate
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 311

Traumatic rupture of eardrum - adult/child
1.
May present with1,2
•	 Hearing loss, tinnitus (ringing in ears), earache
•	 + history of:

–
blow to ear e.g, slap, fall from water skis, diving

–
rapid change in ear pressure e.g, air travel, scuba diving, blast/explosion

–
object poked in ear e.g, cotton tipped applicator
2.	 Immediate management Not applicable
3.	 Clinical assessment1
•	 Ask about:

–
circumstances + mechanism of injury e.g, any water exposure

–
date/time of injury + when first noticed symptoms

–
vertigo/dizziness, hearing loss, tinnitus, light-headedness, other symptoms/
injuries

–
prior history of ear infections/problems
•	 If blow to head, assess for Head injuries,  including base of skull fracture
•	 Do vital signs
•	 Do otoscopy - note location + size of perforation, any discharge/pus:

–
do NOT syringe ears with water or remove any blood in canal - can delay healing
•	 If hearing loss, use tuning fork test to determine type (if able):1,3

–
activate fork by striking firmly on your bent elbow. Place vibrating fork firmly
on the midpoint of the patient’s skull, or central forehead. Is sound heard
better in normal ear (sensorineural hearing loss), or ear with ruptured eardrum
(conductive hearing loss)
4.	 Management
•	 If vertigo/dizziness seek urgent medical advice
•	 Consult MA/NP who may advise:

–
chloramphenicol ear drops if ‘dirty’ injury (e.g, in water) or discharge/infection
•	 Advise to keep ear dry until healed or cotton wool in ear with Vaseline® over top
while showering.
•	 Avoid swimming in the sea or in a contamination water.
•	 Most perforations will heal without treatment within 2 months4
•	 If related to assault/Domestic and family violence,  offer support/referral as per
local policies. Also consider Child protection,
312 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

5.	 Follow up
•	 Advise to be reviewed in 2–3 days, see MA/NP at next clinic + review 2 weekly
until healed if asymptomatic, or sooner if concerned e.g, infection
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 MA/NP may refer to ENT specialist if not healed after 3 months
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 313

Foreign body in ear or nose - adult/child
1.
May present with 1,2
•	 Foreign body (FB) in ear or nose e.g, insect, bead, pebble, plastic toy, food

SYMPTOMS SUGGESTING POSSIBLE FB IN EAR/NOSE OF CHILD
EAR
NOSE
•	 Ear pain, hearing loss, discharge
(pus or blood)
•	 Ear fullness, hiccups or cough that
won't stop
•	 Tinnitus (ringing in ear)
•	 Nasal occlusion or bleeding nose
•	 Malodorous, pus or blood stained
discharge
•	 Facial swelling or pain
2.	 Immediate management 1,2
•	 Urgently consult MA/NP for ENT advice.

–
suspected Button battery,  or paired magnets (in nose) - time critical
emergency:
	›
urgent removal needed. If suspected, but not visualised, x-ray needed to
rule out. If no on-site x-ray arrange urgent evacuation

–
bleeding or airway issues if FB to nose
ALERT button batteries require urgent removal to prevent necrosis of surrounding
tissue
3.	 Clinical assessment 1,2
•	 Get history and examine ears/nose
•	 Identify exact location, shape and composition of FB
•	 If possible, get assistant to help, especially if child (in addition to caregiver)
4.	 Management 1,2
•	 Foreign body in the Ear (external auditory canal.)

–
Examen ear canal with otoscope or any available light source

–
If there’s a foreign body

–
Do ear Flushing (you can try flushing at maximum of 4-5)

–
If the FB successfully removed then check the other ear.

–
If the FB not successfully remove then consult the MA/NP
Note: if the INSECT make sure to kill it first by using some drops of oil or Lignocaine,
then can flush.

314 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

REMOVING FB FROM NOSE2
SMALL, SMOOTH
OR SPHERICAL
OBJECTS
•	 Self blow/exhale - if child is old enough to cooperate eg
> 3 years, encourage to blow nose while occluding the
nostril opposite to the FB
•	 Parents' kiss - ask parent/caregiver to seal the child's
mouth with their mouth, while occluding the unaffected
nostril. Then give a short sharp puff of air
•	 Suction - only try if FB visible and not 'lodged'. Use micro
suction tube. Form a solid seal between tube and object.
Be careful not to push FB up nose
•	 Magnet device - (if available) for metal object - insert
gently until clicks onto object. Be careful not to push FB
up nose
SOFT,
IRREGULAR,
SMALL OBJECT
•	 Forceps - only use if FB visible and looks easy to grasp.
Avoid repeated grasping attempts as may push FB in
further
OTHER OBJECTS
•	 Consult MO/NP
REMOVING FB FROM EAR1
SOFT,
IRREGULAR,
SMALL OBJECT
•	 Forceps - only use if FB visible and looks easy to grasp.
Avoid repeated grasping attempts as may push FB in
further
INSECT OR SMALL
INORGANIC
OBJECT
•	 Gentle irrigation - If insect, put 2-3 drops of cooking oil
into ear to kill first. Irrigate with warm water if using a
cut 14/16 G cannula (needle removed) with a 30 or 60mL
syringe. Direct stream along upper back area of canal.
Repeat as needed
•	 Do NOT irrigate if suspected perforated eardrum,
grommets, button battery, vegetable matter or
expandable FB
SMOOTH/
SPHERICAL
MOBILE OBJECTS
•	 Gentle suction - only try if FB visible and not 'lodged'.
Use micro suction tube to form a solid seal between tube
and object
•	 Magnet device - (if available) for metal object - insert
gently until clicks onto object
OTHER OBJECTS
•	 Consult MO/NP
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 315

How to do Ear Flushing:
Method
•	 The patient must sit with the ear
facing the light and you must be
able to see the entrance to the
ear canal clearly.
•	 Fill the syringe with warm water
and squirt it hard a few times
back into the water container to
check that it works well and the
tip does not come off.
•	 Gently pull the pinna backward
away from the head.
•	 Place the tip of the syringe just inside the canal and squirt the water into the
ear canal.
•	 Water should be directed around the foreign body. The water will then be behind
the foreign body and should push it out.
•	 Catch the water that comes out in a bowl. Check to see what has come out of
the ear canal.
•	 Examine the ear canal with an otoscope after every five syringes.
•	 Once the foreign body has come out of the ear canal you should be able to see
the ear drum.
•	 Check both ears.
NOTE
•	 Remember: the water used to syringe the ear MUST be the same as body
temperature — 37°C, warm but comfortable on the hand.

–
o	 If you use cold or hot water for ear flushing, the patient may feel dizzy,
nausea or vomiting.

–
o	 If the patient complains of dizziness, stop syringing and make the patient
comfortable with support.
•	 DO NOT direct the water straight onto the foreign body.

–
o	 This will push the foreign body further into the ear canal.
•	 DO NOT SYRINGE if the ear is dry and if you know or suspect there is a
perforation in the ear drum. Refer these patients.

–
o	 Syringing a dry perforated ear may lead to suppurative otitis media.
•	 If you see any trauma to the ear canal after removal of the foreign body:

–
o	 Fill the ear canal with eardrops.

–
o	 Give the patient eardrops to take home and use four times each day.

–
o	 Check the ear again after two days.
Attention
•	 Do not syringe ear if there is pain in the ear or recent trauma.
•	 Always look in the ear before syringing. If any pain — stop and look again.
•	 If CSOM — syringe with diluted povidone-iodine 10%.
316 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

5.	 Follow up
•	 Ear FB removed - advise to be reviewed:1

–
if any symptoms arise ie hearing loss, discharge, marked pain, facial nerve
paralysis or dizziness/feeling off balance. Consult MA/NP ± ENT referral
•	 Nose FB removed - advise to be reviewed:2

–
if any symptoms arise ie fever, purulent discharge, nosebleed, facial pain or
swelling. Consult MO/NP ± ENT referral
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 If FB not successfully removed
•	 As above.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 317

Epiglottitis - Adult/Child
 Background1
•	 A life-threatening condition caused by infection of the epiglottis + surrounding
area
•	 Since the Haemophilus influenzae type b (Hib) vaccine, epiglottitis is rare
1.
May present with
•	 High fever, looks sick
•	 Hyperextension of neck, may not move neck
•	 Drooling saliva, difficulty swallowing
•	 Stridor, muffled voice
•	 Restless, unsettled
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Secure airway

–
Anticipate any airway obstruction but avoid distressing a child as may
exacerbate symptoms:

–
Avoid examining the oral cavity and throat

–
allow the child to settle in the position most comfortable.
•	 The patient should be kept in an upright position as supine positioning can
aggravate airway obstruction
•	 Start urgent IV antibiotic chloramphenicol 25mg/kg up 1g IV 6hrly

–
Oral antibiotic if can’t assess to iv: amoxicillin clavulanate 500+125 mg (child
15 mg/kg amoxicillin component up to 500 mg) orally, 8-hourly.
•	 Corticosteroid:

–
Dexamethasone 10 mg IV, as a single dose; repeat at 24 hours if required (not
recommended for children)
•	 IV Fluid to maintain hydration, especially if the patient is unable to swallow.
3.	 Clinical assessment1
•	 Assess for any upper airway obstruction (stridor, muffle voice, difficulty
breathing)
•	 Also looking for drooling, and dysphagia.
•	 Sore throat which is progressive rapidly.
•	 High grade fever, sick looking.
318 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management
•	 Continue with Oxygen support, antibiotic and IVF Maintenance.
•	 Urgent refer if shows sign of airway obstruction.
5.	 Referral/Consultation
•	 Consult every case diagnose with epiglottitis
•	 Refer if a patient shows airway compromise.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 319

Mastoiditis - Adult/Child
Recommend
•	 Refer all suspected mastoiditis cases urgently.
•	 Treat with IV antibiotics if available. If IV access is not possible, give oral
antibiotics while arranging urgent referral.
•	 Consult MO for further assessment and referral.
Background
•	 Mastoiditis occurs when an ear infection spreads to the mastoid bone (behind
the ear), causing swelling, redness, and a possible collection of pus under the
skin behind the pinna (outer ear).
•	 This condition can become serious and requires urgent treatment to prevent
complications.
1.
May present with
•	 Pain behind the ear
•	 Fever
•	 Possible discharge from the ear
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Refer urgently to a health centre for prompt care
•	 Administer IV antibiotics if available
•	 If IV access is not possible, give oral antibiotics
•	 Consult MO urgently
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 History of ear discharge
•	 Swelling and redness behind the pinna
•	 Pain on pressing the bone behind the pinna (post-auricular pain)
•	 Fluctuant swelling (pus under the skin may feel like a fluid-filled sac)
•	 Fever
•	 Otoscopy may show pus in the ear canal
320 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management
•	 Admit and start IV antibiotics:

–
Cloxacillin 25–50 mg/kg IV every 6 hours

–
Metronidazole (Flagyl) 500 mg IV every 8 hours

–
Gentamicin 5 mg/kg IV once daily for 5 days
•	 Ear drops: ciprofloxacin or chloramphenicol 4–5 drops twice daily for 7 days
•	 Give pain relief
•	 Educate on ear hygiene
5.	 Referral / consultation
•	 Refer for surgical review (mastoidectomy) where indicated

Acute otitis media (AOM) - Adult/Child
Acute middle ear infection ± perforation
Recommend1,2
•	 A red eardrum alone is not diagnostic of AOM. It can also be caused by crying,
fever, URTI
•	 Always consider other causes of illness in a sick child with fever
Background1
•	 Perforation of the eardrum is common, resulting in ear discharge and relief of
pain2
1.
May present with1-3
•	 Acute onset of:

–
bulging eardrum (can look red or white/yellow)

–
fluid in the middle ear OR ear discharge for &lt; 2 weeks
•	 ± symptoms eg:

–
pain, fever, lethargy

–
URTI symptoms eg cough, runny nose

–
pulling/rubbing/holding ear(s)

–
unsettled/irritable (young child)

–
infant may present with feeding difficulties
2.	 Immediate management1,2
•	 Do vital signs
•	 Advise Ear Hygiene.
3.	 Clinical assessment1
•	 Get history, including:

–
pain - location, onset

–
discharge from ear - duration1

–
previous ear infections - when, how treated

–
grommets

–
other symptoms - eg fever, cough, runny nose, rash, nausea/vomiting,
diarrhoea

–
feeding - normal or ↓ intake

–
times passed urine/number of wet nappies in last 24 hours

–
immunocompromised
322 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

•	 Do physical examination, including:

–
full examination if unwell child

–
ears as per Ear aAsessment
	›
eardrum - look for bulging OR perforation with discharge
	›
it may be difficult to see eardrum if profuse discharge, especially in infants4

–
nose, throat, chest
•	 If ear discharge, take swab for MCS
4.	 Management1-4
•	 Start Antibiotic: Amoxycillin 25mg/kg (adult 1g) oral BD x 7days,

–
if completed but no changes (still bulging) then can increase to 50mg/kg
(adult 2g) BD x 7days,

–
if no change after 4-7 days, then give Augmentin: child amoxycillin component
45mg/kg/dose up to 1.5g BD. Adult 1500mg + 375mg BD

–
If still bulging, then consult or refer.

–
Painkiller: (Panadol/ibuprofen)

–
Ear hygiene.
•	 If any of the following. (Admit, start IV antibiotic and consult).

–
looks sick, lethargic, pale, irritable, not alert or interactive when T reduced

–
rash, ↑ RR, or respiratory distress

–
immunocompromised
•	 If antibiotics NOT indicated:4

–
reassure AOM will often resolve by itself

–
advise to be reviewed in 2–3 days or sooner if becomes more unwell

–
if after 2–3 days there is no improvement, offer antibiotics
•	 In all ear cases, advise Ear Hygiene.

5.	 Follow up1,3
•	 Follow up results of MCS. Check if sensitive to current antibiotics (discuss with
MO if not)
•	 Check taking antibiotics correctly at each review. Offer support as needed
•	 If unwell advice daily review until improving, or sooner if concerned (consult MO
if concerned):

–
then advise review after 7 days + weekly until resolved
•	 If acute perforation, advise review again at 6 weeks. If still perforated, refer to
MO clinic
•	 If eardrum intact, advise review again at 3 months:

–
check for persistent fluid behind the eardrum. If present, see OME.

–
note: it is common for fluid to be present for about a month after AOM but it
is mostly gone by 3 months

•	 If AOM resolved, but recurrent ie ≥ 3 episodes in prior 6 months OR ≥ 4 episodes
in prior year:

–
if ‘child at high risk of complications’ refer to next MO clinic:
	›
may need 3–6 months of prophylactic antibiotics

–
review monthly for 3 months:
	›
if further episodes of AOM in the 3 month period, arrange ENT referral +
hearing assessment
	›
if no further AOM advise parent/carer to return if any concerns
•	 Continue routine screening as per Ears and hearing checks in the Chronic
conditions manual www.health.qld.gov.au/rrcsu/clinical-manuals/chronic-
conditions-manual-ccm
6.	 Referral/consultation1
•	 If there are concerns about child’s hearing, speech development, behaviour or
school progress, refer for formal hearing assessment if not done recently.

Fluid behind the intact eardrum(s), glue ear

•	 If fluid persisting > 3 months in both ears, treatment may be needed to help

May present with1,2

–
detected by loss of movement of eardrum - shown by Type B tympanometry,

dull eardrum/light reflex missing ± eardrum retracted

grey-white fluid or bubbles behind eardrum - can be hard to see

–
± hearing loss - may be evident through speech delay/learning or behavioural
issues

prior OME/ear infections, when, treatment, is child under ENT specialist

–
any concerns about hearing, speech and learning/behavioural problems,
development

–
hearing test(s) - when, results

–
do vital signs

–
if possible, do valsalva, tympanometry or pneumatic otoscopy to confirm
eardrum not moving.
•	 See Ear assessment.

•	 Consider antibiotics for otitis media ONLY if symptoms of acute infection present

Chronic suppurative otitis media (CSOM) –
Adult/Child
Recommend1,2
•	 Chronic discharging ear(s) can cause hearing impairment and disability.
Occasionally serious complications can occur eg intracranial infection or
mastoiditis1
1.
May present with1,3
•	 Ear discharge present for ≥ 2 weeks + eardrum perforation (hole)
2.	 Immediate management Not applicable
3.	 Clinical assessment3
•	 Get history as per Ear assessment, including:

–
amount + duration of ear discharge

–
any concerns about hearing, speech and learning/behavioural problems,
development
•	 Examine ears:

–
take swab for MCS first

–
use Tissue spears, to clean

–
document size and location of hole in eardrum eg draw picture in notes

–
if grommet - note position (still in eardrum); any granulation tissue around
base
•	 If pus thick and you are unable to view eardrum, consider initial irrigation of ear.
Use diluted Betadine® (1 part Betadine® to 20 parts warm water) followed by
Tissue spears,: or suctioning.
4.	 Management1,3
•	 If discharge &lt; 2 weeks, treat as AOM, with perforation. Unless child has history of
dry perforation with intermittent discharge, in which case continue as below
•	 If discharge ≥ 2 weeks3

–
Give ciprofloxacin ear drops 4-5 drops BD for 7 days.

–
If hole in eardrum is not visible or very small (pin hole size), advice Ear
Hygiene

–
amoxicillin or Augmentin as per AOM.
328 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

•	 Advise:

–
Dry mopping (Using a tissue wick/toilet paper or Cotton  with a Wooden stick
applicator). BEFORE ear drops.

–
importance of continuing tissue spears + ear drops until ears dry (may be > 2
weeks/long-term)

–
water precautions (when swimming/bathing) - use cotton wool in ear with
Vaseline® over top,

–
hearing loss likely while discharging
•	 if discharge > than 2 weeks with:

–
T > 38.5 or systemically unwell

–
redness/swelling behind the ear

–
perforation in Attic area.

–
crusting or granulation tissue - in attic area or around base of grommet
Then admit, start IV Antibiotic, eardrop (ciprofloxacin/chloramphenicol) Ear
hygiene and consult or refer to ENT.
Tissue spears
•	 Wash hands thoroughly before and after the procedure
•	 Use a clean tissue. Tear strips 1–1.5 cm wide along the length of the tissue and
fold each strip in half
•	 Gently pull the ear to straighten the ear canal:

–
Pull ‘back’ for young children

–
Pull ‘back and up’ for older children
•	 Gently insert the tissue spear into the ear canal. Slowly twist it in until resistance
is felt, or the child cries, coughs, or blinks
•	 Leave in place for approximately 30 seconds to absorb discharge
•	 Remove and discard. Repeat with a fresh spear until the ear is dry

Dry perforation - Adult/Child
Hole in eardrum with no discharge
1.
May present with1
•	 Perforated eardrum (hole) without any discharge
2.	 Immediate management Not applicable

–
Get history and examine ears as per Ear assessment, including:

–
duration of perforation (if known)

–
recent trauma to ear/head eg slap

–
prior ear infections, when, treatment, is child under ENT specialist

–
concerns about hearing, speech and learning/behavioural problems,
development

–
hearing test(s) - when, results
•	 Examine ears:

–
document size and position of perforation eg draw picture in notes

–
do valsalva, tympanometry or pneumatic otoscopy to confirm eardrum not
moving to exclude healed perforation.

–
note any crusting/scab or granulation on the eardrum/near hole

•	 If related to trauma, see Traumatic rupture of eardrum.
•	 Advice Ear Hygiene
•	 Consult ENT team if hole could indicate Cholesteatoma,  eg:2

–
is in Attic area, (upper area) of eardrum OR

–
boarders rim of the eardrum OR

–
crusting/scab or granulation on the eardrum/near the hole
•	 Otherwise, advise parent/carer:

–
if a child, is at risk of developing chronic discharging ears

–
will often heal by itself in time, but need to monitor

–
water precautions (when swimming/bathing).1Use cotton wool in ear with
Vaseline® over top, or earplugs with swimming cap
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 331

•	 Advise to return if ear starts discharging
•	 Otherwise, review monthly until healed
6.	 Referral/consultation1
•	 If perforation persists for > 3 months, refer for hearing test/audiology review +
ENT review
•	 If language, learning or behavioural problems, refer to speech pathology

Otitis externa (acute) - Adult/Child
Ear canal infection, swimmer’s ear, tropical ear
1.
 May present with1
•	 Ear pain (can be severe), itch
•	 Tenderness on moving outer ear
•	 ± discharge
2.	 Immediate management Not applicable
3.	 Clinical assessment1
•	 Get history including:

–
onset, duration

–
swimming/water exposure or trauma to ear canal eg vigorous cleaning/
scratching, use of hearing aids, cotton bud.

–
prior otitis externa - when, treatment

–
immunocompromised, diabetes, recent radiotherapy
•	 Do vital signs
•	 Examine ears - often very painful, approach gently:

–
if discharge, take swab for MCS first

–
dry mop using Tissue spears, to clean any discharge/debris from canal. Do not
syringe with water2
•	 using otoscope, look for:

–
widespread redness/swelling of ear canal

–
discharge/debris/foreign body

–
eardrum - intact or perforated - may be too swollen to see. If no discharge,
consider

–
Tympanometry. Type A means the eardrum is intact1
•	 Is cause likely bacterial or fungal3
Comparison of Bacterial and Fungal Otitis Externa
BACTERIAL
FUNGAL
•	 Very painful when pulling the pinna,
pressing the tragus, or on otoscopy
•	 Itching is more prominent than pain;
manipulations are generally well
tolerated
•	 Ear canal is narrow and swollen
•	 Ear canal is usually wide
•	 Discharge is smelly, creamy or dry
pus, yellow in colour; canal may be
scaly or flaky
•	 Debris appears thick like ‘wet
newspaper’ (hyphae) and ‘salt and
pepper’ (spores)
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 333

4.	 Management1
•	 Offer Painkiller:

–
Paracetamol: children: 10-15 mg/kg orally/ rectally every 4-6 hours when
required.
adults: 500-1000 mg orally every 4-6 hours when required.

–
Ibuprofen: children: 5-10 mg/kg orally every 6-8 hours when required,
adults: 200-400 mg orally every 4-6 hours when required.
•	 Start Oral antibiotic + ear drop if any of:

–
fever

–
cellulitis/redness extends outside the ear canal

–
immunocompromised, diabetes.
•	 Antibiotic: Amoxycillin

–
children ≤3 months of age: 30 mg/kg/day orally given in 2 divided doses.

–
children >3 months of age: 20-40 mg/kg/day orally given in 3 divided doses.

–
adults: 250-500 mg orally three times daily, or 500-875 mg orally twice daily.
Amoxicillin/clavulanate:

–
children ≤3 months of age: 30 mg/kg/day orally given in 2 divided doses

–
children >3 months of age: 20-40 mg/kg/day orally given in 3 divided doses

–
adults: 250-500 mg orally three times daily, or 500-875 mg orally twice daily
Dose refers to amoxicillin component.
Ciprofloxacin:

–
children: consult specialist for guidance on dose; adults: 500-750 mg orally
twice daily.
•	 Ear Drops:

–
Chloramphenicol 4-5 drops BD for 7 days

–
ciprofloxacin 4-5 drops BD for 7 days
•	 Advise:1,2

–
symptoms should improve after 2–3 days of treatment, with full resolution up
to 2 weeks4

–
keep ear dry + for 2 weeks after resolved. Use cotton wool balls with Vaseline®
over top while showering; avoid swimming
Ear Drops Advice – Otitis Externa
•	 If possible, ask someone else to help administer the drops
•	 Clean the ear first using Tissue Spears (As above) Ensure thorough cleaning,
especially in cases of fungal infection, as leftover spores may cause recurrence
•	 Warm the bottle of ear drops in your hand for 5–10 minutes before use
•	 Lie down with the affected ear facing up. Instill the drops, then gently press the
tragus (the small flap at the front of the ear) for 30 seconds. Remain lying with
the ear up for at least 3–5 minutes
•	 Stop using the drops if the person develops ringing in the ears, hearing loss, or
problems with balance
334 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

5.	 Follow up
•	 review in 1 week (or sooner if worried/worsening):
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 If no improvement with treatment.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 335

Sore throat – (Pharyngitis, tonsillitis) -
Adult/Child
Recommendation
•	 Treat sore throats with antibiotics promptly in people living in rural + remote
settings and in others who are at high risk of Acute rheumatic Fever and Acute
post streptococcal glomerulonephritis.
1.
May present with
•	 Sore throat ± fever
•	 Difficulty swallowing, not eating/drinking as much, croaky voice
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Look for symptoms of airway obstruction/compromise or deep neck space
infection eg peritonsillar abscess (quinsy), including:
•	 muffled voice, stridor, trismus (unable to open mouth)
•	 drooling, neck swelling/twisting, posturing ie sitting forward, neck extension
•	 severe neck pain, respiratory distress
•	 If any of above, contact MA, admit to start iv antibiotic, secure airway and for
urgent retrieval
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Get history, including:

–
cough, fever, rash

–
feeding - normal or reduced intake, difficulty swallowing

–
number of times passed urine/wet nappies in last 24 hours

–
other symptoms eg diarrhoea, vomiting, headache, malaise, runny nose, red
eyes, hoarse voice, joint pains

–
past history - ARF or RHD, immunocompromised, immunisation status
•	 Do physical examination, including:

–
chest - listen for crackles/wheeze, air entry

–
throat/mouth:

–
redness, swelling/pus on tonsils

–
ulcers/vesicles, strawberry tongue

–
greyish or green membrane - may indicate diphtheria

–
ears

–
palpate neck for enlarged/tender lymph nodes

–
look for rash + signs of APSGN, and ARF
•	 A runny nose or a hoarse voice is suggestive of a viral infection and does not
require antibiotic therapy.
•	 Sore throat in the absence of a runny nose or a hoarse voice is suggestive of a
bacterial infection may need antibiotics treatment.
336 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

•	 Use table below for differential diagnosis of sore throat

Hand, foot and

in family/school

OTHER PRESENTATIONS THAT MAY INCLUDE A SORE THROAT
SIGNS AND SYMPTOMS
POSSIBLE CAUSE
MANAGEMENT
•	 Fever > 38.5°C,
malaise

mouth and throat

in neck
•	 Headache,
abdominal pain,
vomiting, ↓ appetite
Herpangina (mouth
blisters)
More common in
children &lt; 5 years
•	 If suspected, consult MO
•	 Usually resolves within 7 days
•	 Advise to return for review if
not improving

Non-infectious causes may include:
Allergic rhinitis, smoke exposure, dry air, cancer, or reflux.
4.	 Management
•	 Supportive care

–
paracetamol: children: 10-15 mg/kg orally every 4-6 hours when required,
maximum 75 mg/kg/day; adults: 500-1000 mg orally every 4-6 hours when
required, maximum 4000 mg/ day

–
Get plenty rest.

–
Drink a lot of water

–
Collect throat swab.
•	 antibiotics are not usually indicated but if throat swab
•	 confirmed group A Streptococcus (GAS) or history of rheumatic fever
•	 Consult admit and start IV antibiotic if:

–
&lt; 12 month

–
unable to drink/signs of dehydration

–
T ≥ 39

–
looks sick, not alert/interactive when ↓T

–
rash or signs of ARF or APSGN

–
immunocompromised

–
suspected diphtheria

–
severe throat pain
•	 Use flowchart on following page to determine if antibiotics are indicated or not
for ‘sore throat’
338 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Sore Throat: Is the Patient at High Risk of ARF (Acute Rheumatic Fever)?
Consider patient at high risk of ARF
if ANY of the following apply:
•	 Living in an ARF-endemic area
•	 Māori or Pacific Islander person
•	 Existing RHD, or history of ARF/
RHD and aged &lt; 40 years
Important Notes
•	 If on secondary prophylaxis for ARF and needs more antibiotics, confirm last
dose.
•	 If &lt; 7 days since last dose, do not give extra dose.
•	 The next secondary dose is due in 28 days unless otherwise reset.
Symptomatic Treatment
•	 Use paracetamol or ibuprofen for relief of pain and fever
•	 Throat lozenges may be helpful for adolescents and adults
•	 Encourage adequate fluid intake – prefer water, avoid sugary drinks
•	 Advise to rest and avoid exposure to smoke
Also Consider High Risk If:
•	 Family or household has recent
ARF/RHD Overcrowding (≥
2 people/bedroom) or low
socioeconomic status
•	 Migrant/refugee from a low- or
middle-income country
•	 Give 1 dose of IM benzathine
benzylpenicillin (Bicillin L-A®)
•	 If IM not possible, give oral
phenoxymethylpenicillin and
explain the importance of
completing the full course
•	 Mild allergy (e.g. rash): Give
cefalexin
•	 Severe allergy (e.g. anaphylaxis):
Give azithromycin
•	 Do a throat swab (MCS) first
•	 Give antibiotics immediately
Antibiotic Selection
Do NOT give antibiotics routinely
•	 If symptoms suggest Strep A sore
throat/tonsillitis:

–
Take throat swab (MCS or
PoCT)

–
Review next day or earlier if
concerned (e.g. new symptoms,
rigors, dehydration)

–
If not improving in 3–7 days and
swab confirms Strep A, consider
phenoxymethylpenicillin

Adult: 500 mg twice daily
Child: 15 mg/kg (max 500
mg) twice daily
10 days
Oral
liquid
125 mg/5
mL, 250
mg/5 mL
Oral
Same as above
10 days
Offer CMI: May cause diarrhoea, nausea, pain at injection site
Food has little effect on absorption
Contraindication: Severe/immediate allergy to penicillin
Cross-reactivity: Possible with cephalosporins and carbapenems
Management of associated emergency: Consult MO. See Anaphylaxis
340 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

5.	 Follow up
•	 Check results of throat MCS if taken
•	 Advice to be reviewed the next day, or earlier if worried, symptoms worsen or
new symptoms develop eg vomiting, dehydration, fever, rigors:2
•	 If at high risk of ARF, or previous ARF or RHD:1

–
provide education on signs + symptoms of ARF and APSGN, which can occur
about 10–14 days after sore throat or skin sores
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 If patient is not responding to treatment.
•	 If shows any signs of airway obstruction.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 341

Acute sinusitis - Adult/Child
Also commonly known as acute rhinosinusitis is a symptomatic inflammation of the
mucosal lining of the nasal cavity and paranasal sinuses.
1.
May present with1
•	 with purulent nasal drainage
•	 accompanied with nasal obstruction, facial pain/pressure/fullness.
2.	 Immediate management1
•	 Suspect Foreign body in child with:

–
any nasal occlusion, facial swelling ± pain, or smelly, purulent or blood-stained
nasal discharge
•	 Consult MA urgently if there are any signs of spreading infection:

–
acute onset confusion

–
Double or ↓vision

–
neck stiffness, severe headache, photophobia

–
swelling or cellulitis around eyes, forehead or face

–
bulging eyes, painful eye movements
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Get history, including:

–
onset, duration, severity of symptoms

–
facial pain/tenderness

–
fever, cough, nasal discharge - colour

–
immunocompromised
•	 Do physical examination, including:

–
any facial swelling + tenderness with gentle palpation

–
ENT
342 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management
•	 Suspected Acute viral Sinusitis.

–
Adequate rest and hydration and warm facial packs may be useful.

–
paracetamol or ibuprofen if needed
•	 Suspected Acute Bacterial Sinusitis.

–
it is usually self-limiting, and antibiotics are not usually indicated

–
consider symptomatic treatment as per URTI.

–
Paracetamol or ibuprofen.
•	 May consider antibiotics, if:

–
severe symptoms persisting > 3–4 days, or worsening after initial
improvement, or persist for > 7–10 days without improvement
•	 Amoxicillin/clavulanate:

–
children: 90 mg/kg/ day orally given in 2 divided doses; adults: 2000 mg
orally (extended-release) twice daily Dose refers to amoxicillin component.
5.	 Follow up
•	 Advise to be reviewed in 1 week.
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 if symptoms worsen, do not improve or signs of spreading infection:
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 343

Allergic rhinitis - Adult/Child
Is an immunoglobulin E (IgE)-mediated inflammatory nasal condition resulting from
allergen exposure in a sensitised individual, often associated with atopic dermatitis
(eczema) and asthma often begin in childhood and young adult.
1.
May present with
•	 Nasal pruritus, sneezing, rhinorrhoea, and nasal congestion.
•	 Frequently, there is associated palate, throat, ear, and eye itching as well as eye
redness, puffiness, and watery discharge.
2.	 Clinical assessment1
•	 Get history, including:
Identifying triggers, noting the onset and duration of symptoms, and assessing
for seasonal variations. It's also important to ask about other allergic conditions
(asthma, eczema), family history of allergies, and response to previous
treatments.
•	 Physical examination of the nose is necessary. Findings may include swelling of
the turbinate and nasal mucosa, the presence of clear nasal mucus and/or a pale
nasal mucosa, and nasal crease (a transverse crease resulting from repetitive
nose rubbing and manipulation).
3.	 Management
•	 Give antihistamine e.g. Cetirizine, children ≥6 months of age: 2.5 to 5 mg orally
once daily when required; children ≥6 years of age and adults: 5-10 mg orally
once daily when required or Promethazine 10-25mg oral 2-3 times a day.
•	 If mild to moderate symptoms: intranasal corticosteroid nasal spray:
beclomethasone nasal spray: (50 micrograms/ spray aqueous) children ≥6 years
of age and adults: 50-100 micrograms (1-2 sprays) in each nostril twice daily.
•	 Allergen avoidance: reducing exposure to relevant allergens such as dander, dust
mite, and pollen
•	 Nasal saline irrigation twice a day or can use sterile water 500mls add with 1
teaspoon of salt and ½ teaspoon of baking soda…can use the syringe to flush
each nostril. (Note to keep your solution clean and safe avoid contamination.)
4.	 Follow up
•	 In 2-4 weeks and find out how well the symptoms are controlled.
5.	 Referral/Consultation
•	 If patient symptoms are severe and not respond to drug therapy.
344 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Problems of Thyroid Grand - Adult
Thyroid gland problems encompass range of disorders that affect the thyroid's
ability to produce hormones, leading to either an overactive (hyperthyroidism)
or underactive (hypothyroidism) state. These conditions can manifest in various
symptoms and may result from various causes, including autoimmune diseases,
iodine deficiency, or thyroiditis.

hormone.

different types,
including
papillary,
follicular,
medullary, and
anaplastic.
Lumps or
abnormal
masses within
the thyroid
gland.

Can be caused
by autoimmune
diseases like
Hashimoto's
thyroiditis,
iodine
deficiency, or
thyroid surgery.
Often caused
by Graves'
disease, thyroid
nodules, or
inflammation of
the thyroid.
Often
unknown, but
some factors
can increase
risk.
Can be benign
cysts, tumours,
or cancerous.

Slowed
metabolism,
leading to
fatigue,
weight gain,
feeling cold,
constipation,
and depression.
Increased
metabolism,
resulting in
weight loss,
anxiety, rapid
heartbeat,

and heat
intolerance.
Can include
a lump in the

swallowing or

pain.
Often
asymptomatic,
but large

cause swelling
and difficulty
breathing or
swallowing.
Treatment:
Usually involves
hormone
replacement
therapy with
levothyroxine.
Can include
anti-thyroid
medications,
radioactive
iodine, or
surgery.
Varies
depending
on the type
and stage of
cancer, and
may involve
surgery,
radioactive
iodine, or other
therapies
Varies
depending
on the
nature of the
nodule, and
may include
monitoring,
medication, or

Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 345

1.
May Present with
•	 Can manifest in various ways, but common symptoms include changes in heart
rate, weight, and body temperature, as well as fatigue, mood changes.
2.	 Immediate management Not applicable
3.	 Clinical assessment1
•	 Thorough history-taking is vital, and the presence of any red flag symptoms or
risk factors should be documented.
•	 Red flag symptoms for head and neck cancer include:

–
a mass that has been present for >2 weeks

–
a recent voice change

–
dysphagia or odynophagia

–
ipsilateral otalgia, nasal obstruction or epistaxis

–
unexplained weight loss or loss of appetite.
•	 Risk factors for head and neck cancer include:

–
smoking

–
alcohol use

–
age >40 years

–
past history of previous head and neck malignancy

–
past history of head and neck cutaneous lesions.
4.	 Management
•	 Refer suspicion cases for hyper and hypothyroidism for further investigation and
treatment.
•	 Consult for patient suspicious of neck malignancy (may need FNA, Biopsy to
confirm a diagnosis.)
5.	 Follow up
•	 Patient on thyroid treatment need to evaluate how the thyroid gland functioning.
6.	 Referral/Consultation
•	 If compromise airway.
•	 If suspicious of neck malignancy

Oral health
Note that protocols for Fractured Jaw and Oral Facial Fractures are in the
Emergency Chapter.

Trauma to teeth - Adult/Child
Knocked out tooth, displaced tooth,
broken tooth
Recommend
•	 Offer education on how to manage a knocked out ‘adult’ tooth. Prompt Frst aid
may end up saving a tooth
1.
May present with
•	 Knocked out, displaced, broken tooth/teeth
•	 Bleeding in mouth
•	 Injury/swelling to lips, tongue/face
2.	 Immediate management1
•	 If a knocked out adult tooth:

–
replant within 15 minutes if possible - see how to do this under Management.3
3.	 Clinical assessment1
•	 If practical, find all missing teeth/tooth fragments
•	 Ask about:

–
circumstances of injury

–
current medications
•	 Do vital signs
•	 Inspect mouth, teeth, soft tissues/gums.
•	 Assess for bleeding +

–
bite - suspect jaw or facial fracture if bite is abnormal. See Fractured jaw.

–
Check for other injuries, especially head and neck1

–
If a tooth looks like it is missing, but not found at site of the accident, assess if:
	›
patient has inhaled the tooth, OR
	›
tooth has been forced up into the socket completely, appearing to be
missing (covered in a clot)
	›
if not sure, consult MO/dentist who may consider x-ray1
348 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management
•	 Control any bleeding with gentle pressure:

–
if bleeding continues, see Post tooth extraction bleeding.
•	 Ofer analgesia - ibuprofen preferred.2 See Acute pain.

–
add paracetamol if needed

–
advise to take regularly, rather than as required, for continuous pain relief
Knocked out tooth1
•	 Check if it is an adult or baby tooth:

–
baby teeth - smaller, lighter in colour

–
a child > 5 years may have a mixture of adult and baby teeth, can be hard to tell
•	 Do NOT replant a knocked out baby tooth - can damage the developing adult
tooth
•	 Replant permanent (adult) tooth within 15 minutes if possible - more likely to heal:

–
if done > 1 hour, unlikely to succeed
•	 Check tetanus vaccination status and give booster if indicated. See Tetanus
immunisation.
How to replant a knocked out adult tooth1
•	 Hold tooth carefully by the top (crown), not the root
•	 Check the tooth is intact - sometimes the root may have broken off:

–
if suspect it is not intact, consider urgent dental review
•	 If dirty, rinse tooth briefly with dairy milk or sodium chloride o.9%:

–
do NOT use water. Do not rub or scrub
•	 Replace tooth in the socket with firm finger pressure:

–
be careful to ensure the tooth is placed the right way around. Take extra care
if multiple teeth lost
•	 Use Temporary splint to hold in place (see below)
•	 Advise:

–
soft diet

–
use chlorhexidine gluconate 0.2% mouthwash. See Gum disease
•	 ALL re-implanted teeth need urgent review and treatment by dentist:

–
arrange evacuation as needed
Temporary splint1
•	 Fix the replanted tooth to the adjacent teeth:

–
use a small piece of folded aluminium foil or malleable material eg Blu Tack®,
orthodontic wax

–
put firmly over replanted tooth and teeth either side, so adjacent teeth act as
a splint. Ask the patient to bite down to hold in position
•	 Start antibiotics after replanting the tooth:1

–
doxycycline if not allergic and 8 years of age OR

–
amoxicillin if not allergic for child &lt; 8 years, or if doxycycline allergy in adult:
	›
if allergic to amoxicillin, consult MO/dentist
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 349

If unable to replant a knocked out tooth1
•	 Do not let the tooth dry out
•	 Store in dairy milk - cool or room temperature. Tooth ligament cells can survive in
milk up to 6 hours
•	 If milk not available, store in sodium chloride 0.9% or saliva + plastic wrap - get
patient to spit
•	 saliva into wrap first. Ligament cells can survive up to 1 hour if stored this way
•	 Do NOT store in water
•	 Consult dentist urgently
Displaced tooth1 - tooth is still in the socket, but moved position
•	 Do not reposition a baby tooth
•	 Reposition adult tooth to original position with !rm !nger pressure
•	 Put Temporary splint on tooth
•	 Consult dentist urgently - arrange evacuation as needed
Broken tooth/teeth3
•	 Keep tooth fragments, as dentist may be able to be re-bond onto the broken
tooth
•	 If no pain from broken tooth, treatment is not urgent - refer to the next dental
clinic
•	 If pain, dentine or pulp may have been exposed
•	 Exposure of dentine:

–
intermittent pain when exposed to stimulus - hot, cold or sweet food or drinks

–
cover any obvious cavity with orthodontic wax or other inert material eg Blu
Tack® or temporary

–
filling eg Cavit®2- note: will not last very long

–
analgesia not normally needed

–
advise to see dentist as soon as possible

–
avoid stimulus eg hot/cold drinks
•	 Exposure of pulp:

–
red soft tissue is seen in the area of break or cavity

–
severe pain persisting as a dull throbbing ache - even after removal of the
stimulus

–
very sensitive to touch, may bleed or have a blood clot over it
•	 needs urgent dental treatment:

–
within 24 hours to avoid infection/more damage

–
arrange evacuation as needed
•	 if delay to treatment expected, consult dentist who may advise:

–
orthodontic wax or other inert material eg Blu Tack®, to cover the broken
tooth to reduce pain

–
OR temporary filling eg Cavit®

–
note: these measures may still not be successful in reducing pain adequately.
Consult dentist

5.	 Follow up
•	 As per dentist’s advice. Refer for next dentist clinic visit

352 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Toothache - Adult/Child
Background1
•	 There is insuffcient evidence to support the use of Oil of Cloves for toothache.
Ingestion can cause life-threatening adverse reactions in children, + safety has
not been established in pregnant + lactating women
1.
May present with2
•	 Toothache/dental pain
•	 Tooth/teeth sensitive to hot/cold
•	 With or without:

–
bad breath (halitosis) ± bad taste in mouth

–
tooth decay - hole in tooth, broken down tooth, darkened tooth

–
facial swelling ± dental abscess (gum boil)
2.	 Immediate management Not applicable
3.	 Clinical assessment2
•	 Get history - use Common causes of dental pain. table for prompts:

–
pain

–
associated symptoms eg bad breath, facial swelling, fever

–
dental history as appropriate
•	 Do vital signs
•	 Inspect oral cavity, teeth, soft tissues, lymph nodes, ears
4.	 Management2
•	 Use Common causes of dental pain table to guide treatment
•	 Refer anyone with toothache to a dentist - dental treatment is the most
effective means of reducing pain2
•	 Offer analgesia.2 See Acute pain.

–
ibuprofen preferred

–
add paracetamol if needed

–
Dental extraction by Medical Assistants if carious and symptomatic

–
advise to take regularly, rather than as required, to achieve continuous pain
relief

–
if not effective, consider (adult)
•	 Give analgesia for shortest duration possible, no more than 5 days without
review2
•	 If severe pain consult MO/dentist
•	 Reinforce good oral hygiene. See the Chronic conditions manual
www.health.qld.gov.au/ rrcsu/clinical-manuals/chronic-conditions-manual-ccm
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 353

•	 Pain worsens

throughout the

–

± bad breath

is not quite right

6.	 Referral/consultation

Dental caries - Adult/Child
Tooth decay
Background
•	 People from rural and remote areas are at high risk of dental caries1
•	 Application of fluoride varnish 2–4 times a year to primary (baby) + permanent
(adult) teeth is associated with a substantial reduction in the extent of caries
experienced2
1.
May present with2
•	 Tooth/teeth sensitive to hot/cold, biting or pressure
•	 Dental caries (tooth decay) - hole in tooth, broken down tooth, darkened tooth
•	 Risk factors for ' risk of dental caries1 - see the Chronic conditions manual
www.health.qld.gov.au/rrcsu/clinical-manuals/chronic-conditions-manual-ccm
2.	 Immediate management Not applicable
3.	 Clinical assessment3
•	 Ask when last dental visit was
•	 Assess for risk factors for dental caries
•	 Examine teeth for dental caries:

–
holes/cavities or structural damage which can be brown or black in
appearance

–
tooth/teeth surfaces with a white or frosty appearance may indicate early
stages of decay

–
pain or sensitivity

–
bad breath or a bad taste in the mouth
356 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management3
•	 If toothache, see Toothache.
•	 If symptomatic and caries- Dental Extraction by MA
•	 Ask about any adverse experience associated with previous fluoride varnish
application
•	 Offer to apply fluoride varnish to teeth if:

–
there is evidence of dental caries or person is at high risk of dental caries +

–
regular brushing with fluoride toothpaste is likely to be ine ective +

–
person is > 18 months old +

–
there are no contraindications
•	 Refer for dentist review
•	 If patient has been recalled for re-application of fluoride varnish:

–
assess for any changes in risk status

–
check if patient has had fluoride varnish applied anywhere else during the
recall period eg by a dentist

–
reapply as indicated
•	 Reinforce good oral hygiene. See the Chronic conditions manual
www.health.qld.gov.au/rrcsu/clinical-manuals/chronic-conditions-manual-ccm
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 357

Offer CMI: Teeth may appear discoloured temporarily following application. Do
not brush teeth on day of application - resume brushing the next morning. Eat
soft foods for the rest of the day to minimise disruption of the varnish
Note: Do not apply if ulcerative gingivitis or stomatitis to avoid discomfort for
patient (contains alcohol). Do not leave fluoride varnish unattended when in use.
Fluoride varnish is an S4 when used by clinicians other than dental practitioners
Contraindication: Allergy to colophony (natural rosin) or sticking plaster; any
episode of severe allergy or bronchial asthma that has required hospitalisation
Management of associated emergency: Adverse reactions extremely rare. If
occurs contact MO or dentist.
5.	 Follow up
•	 Arrange re-call for review of oral health status and reapplication of fluoride
varnish:

–
if low risk - every 6 months, if high risk - every 3 months
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 Refer high risk and patients with obvious dental caries to dentist
358 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Mouth ulcers - Adult/Child
Background
•	 Ulcers persisting > 2 weeks are potentially serious1
•	 Ulcers are common in teenagers and young adults. The most common mouth
ulcer is recurrent aphthous stomatitis (unknown cause), which aects 5–60% of
the general population1,2
1.
May present with2
•	 Ulcer(s) in mouth
2.	 Immediate management Not applicable
3.	 Clinical assessment1,2
•	 Ask about:

–
when did ulcer(s) appear, duration

–
is it recurring

–
does patient suspect the cause eg trauma from:3
	›
biting tongue/cheek
	›
thermal burn from eating or drinking food that is too hot/cold
	›
poor fitting dentures/sharp or broken teeth
	›
use of topical agents in mouth, oral rinses

–
pain or pins and needles/tingling sensation(s) in mouth or face

–
dry mouth - is cause known eg medicine side effect

–
fever

–
ulcers anywhere else on body
•	 Past history:

–
previous ulcer(s)

–
immunocompromised eg chemotherapy, malnutrition, HIV

–
STI history. See STI/BBV assessment. - consider syphilis and gonorrhoea:2

–
screen if appropriate

–
smoking, alcohol use

–
anaemia

–
diet, recent weight loss
•	 Do:

–
vital signs

–
 inspect mouth, lips and tongue:
	›
1 or more ulcers
	›
size, location, shape - oval/round or irregular
	›
any pigmented lesions on the ulcer
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 359

4.	 Management2
•	 Most ulcers are self-limiting and heal within a few days
•	 If patient unwell/has other symptoms, consult MO/Dentist
•	 If poor fitting dentures or broken/sharp teeth, advise to see dentist at next clinic
•	 If ulcer(s) recurring, or has persisted for > 2 weeks:2,4

–
 refer to next MO/Dentist clinic for investigations ± biopsy for less common
causes eg:metabolic, dermatological, allergic, immunological, infectious or
cancer
•	 Symptom relief options:2

–
chlorhexidine gluconate 0.2% mouthwash. See Gum disease. for instructions

–
 topical anaesthetic5 eg Seda lotion®

–
 salt water mouth rinses3

–
avoid acidic drinks (eg fruit juice or soft drink) and very spicy/sharp food

–
clean teeth properly

–
if needed, paracetamol or ibuprofen may help. See Acute pain.

•	 Advise to be reviewed in 2 weeks. If not healed, advise to see MO/Dentist at next

•	 Consult MO/dentist as above. Consider telehealth consult with dentist

Tooth abcess - Adult/Child
Recommend1
•	 Antibiotics are not a substitute for dental treatment of a tooth abscess. The
source of infection must be treated eg extraction of tooth, root canal treatment
or surgical intervention
1.
May present with1,2
•	 Toothache
•	 Localised swelling (abscess) on gum (¬± pus) ¬±
•	 facial swelling/pain
•	 fever
•	 bad breath
•	 systemically unwell
2.	 Immediate management1

Dry Socket - Adult/Child
Background1
•	 Dry socket (alveolar osteitis) is when a blood clot disintegrates prematurely after
a tooth/teeth extraction resulting in inflammation of the bone below
1.
May present with1
•	 Severe pain ± bad breath 1-4 days post tooth extraction
2.	 Immediate management Not applicable
3.	 Clinical assessment1
•	 Ask about:

–
when tooth extraction occurred, pain
•	 Do vital signs
•	 Inspect tooth extraction site:

–
typically the socket appears grey, non healing and is o#en !lled with debris

•	 Offer analgesia - ibuprofen preferred.2 See Acute pain.

–
add paracetamol if needed

–
advise to take regularly, rather than as required, to achieve continuous pain relief
•	 Irrigate socket gently with warm sodium chloride 0.9% to remove debris
•	 Place Alvogyl (antiseptic and analgesic) dressing loosely into socket:

–
does not require removal later. Note: do not use if allergic to iodine

if not available contact MO/dentist for further advice

•	 If pain persists > 3 weeks, or signs (eg pain, redness) outside of the tooth socket,
consult MO/Dentist

Gum disease - Adult/Child
Gingivitis, periodontitis, necrotising gingivitis
Background1,2
•	 Gingvitis is inflammation of the gums caused by th epresence of undisturbed
plaque.  If not managed appropritaley, can lead to peridontitis (loss of bone/
tissue that supports the teeth).
1.
May present with1
Gingivitis1
•	 Red, swollen gums that bleed easily
•	 Rarely painful
Periodontitis2
•	 Bad breath, bad taste
•	 Gum recession
•	 Bleeding ± swollen gums
•	 If severe, may have:
•	 loose teeth, spaces between teeth
•	 pain
•	 abscess. See Tooth abscess for management
•	 Rarely seen in children
Necrotising gingivitis3
•	 Bleeding gums, ulcers - may have grey membrane
•	 Signifcant pain
•	 Bad breath
•	 ± swollen lymph nodes, fever
•	 Most common in young adult smokers. Rarely seen in children
2.	 Immediate management Not applicable
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Get history, including:

–
smoking

–
diabetes

–
teeth brushing/oral hygiene/dental history

366 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management1-3
•	 Offer analgesia as needed. See Toothache for dental pain recommendations
•	 Consult MO/dentist if:

–
severe ¬± systemic signs and symptoms eg fever, malaise

–
patient has an underlying medical condition eg poorly controlled diabetes,
immunocompromised
In all cases1,2
•	 Offer education on good oral hygiene. See the Chronic conditions manual https://
www.health.qld.gov.au/rrcsu/clinical-manuals/chronic-conditions-manual-ccm
Gingivitis1
•	 Refer to dentist for check-up and clean - removal of plaque and calculus
•	 If pain limits ability to brush teeth/floss well:
•	 consider short-term use of chlorhexidine gluconate 0.2% mouthwash
•	 Should resolve within 1 month
Periodontitis2
•	 If child consult MO/Dentist - needs urgent paediatrician review to investigate
underlying cause2
•	 Refer to next dental clinic for removal of plaque and calculus + ongoing care
•	 Support patient to modify risk factors eg smoking, diabetes management2
•	 Antibiotics are rarely needed
Necrotising gingivitis3
•	 Refer to dentist as soon as possible - for debridement of plaque and calculus +
ongoing care
•	 Give metronidazole + advise to use chlorhexidine gluconate 0.2% mouthwash
until pain reduces
•	 Advise to stop smoking
•	 Note: antibiotics alone, without debridement by dentist + improvement of oral
hygiene will usually lead to recurrence
