Oral facial fractures (Kiribati Original Protocol)
Purpose:
To assist Medical Assistants and Nurses in the primary care setting to recognize,
assess, provide initial management, and appropriately refer patients presenting with
oral facial fractures.
Background
Facial fractures are increasingly common presentations in primary healthcare.
Prompt recognition and appropriate management are critical to prevent permanent
functional, cosmetic, or vision-threatening complications.
General Principles
•	 Follow Advanced Trauma Life Support (ATLS) protocols for all trauma cases.
•	 Conduct a full visual assessment in any facial trauma.
•	 Identify red flags requiring immediate referral.
•	 Provide early pain management and wound care as appropriate.
1.
May present with
•	 Nasal Fractures: Often resulting from interpersonal violence, nasal fractures
are the most common facial fractures, accounting for up to 58.6% of all facial
fractures. Symptoms include nasal deformity, epistaxis, and periorbital bruising.
www.racgp.org.au/afp/2012/april/maxillofacial-trauma/?utm_source=chatgpt.
com
•	 Mandibular Fractures: These may present with pain, swelling, malocclusion, and
limited mouth opening. They can result from blunt or penetrating trauma and
may destabilize the airway. www.entnet.org/wp-content/uploads/files/Trauma-
Chapter-5.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com
•	 Zygomatic Fractures: Symptoms include periorbital swelling, infraorbital nerve
paraesthesia, and restricted eye movement.
•	 Orbital Fractures: Signs include diplopia, reduced visual acuity, and orbital rim
tenderness.
•	 Frontal Bone Fractures: Present with forehead deformity, possible CSF
rhinorrhoea, and periorbital bruising.
372 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

2.	 Immediate management
•	 Airway Management: Ensure airway patency, especially in cases of mandibular
fractures that may compromise the airway. https://link.springer.com/
chapter/10.1007/978-981-15-1346-6_48?utm_source=chatgpt.com
•	 Hemostasis: Control bleeding using direct pressure, nasal packing, or hemostatic
agents as appropriate.
•	 Analgesia: Administer appropriate pain relief, considering the severity of the
injury.
•	 Antibiotics: Prophylactic antibiotics may be indicated in cases of open fractures
or significant soft tissue involvement. https://tsaco.bmj.com/content/9/1/
e001304?utm_source=chatgpt.com
•	 Imaging: Obtain appropriate imaging studies, such as X-rays or CT scans, to
assess the extent of the fractures.
Vital Symptoms/Signs Not to Miss
These require IMMEDIATE REFERRAL:
SYMPTOM/SIGN
CONDITION
ACTION
Proptosis, chemosis,
ophthalmoplegia, vision loss
Retrobulbar
haemorrhage
Immediate emergency
referral
Sudden visual loss or
decreased pupil reflex
Traumatic optic
neuropathy
Immediate emergency
referral
Diplopia, restricted upward
gaze, nausea/vomiting
(especially in young patients)
White eye blowout
fracture
Immediate emergency
referral
Mobile maxilla, malocclusion
Maxillary/midface
fracture
Immediate referral
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 History: Obtain details of the mechanism of injury, time of injury, and any
associated symptoms.
•	 Examination: Perform a thorough head and neck examination, assessing for
deformities, swelling, tenderness, and neurological deficits.
•	 Functional Assessment: Evaluate the patient's ability to open and close the
mouth, chew, and speak.
•	 Neurological Examination: Assess cranial nerve function, particularly the
trigeminal nerve, for sensory deficits.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 373

4.	 Management
•	 Conservative Management: Indicated for non-displaced fractures without
significant functional impairment. Includes analgesia, soft diet, and follow-up.
https://www.rch.org.au/trauma-service/manual/maxillofacial-injury/?utm_
source=chatgpt.com
•	 Surgical Intervention: Required for displaced fractures, fractures with
airway compromise, or those causing significant functional impairment.
https://www.slideshare.net/slideshow/maxillofacial-trauma-19339123/19339123
•	 Referral: Refer to oral and maxillofacial specialists for definitive management.
https://metronorth.health.qld.gov.au/specialist_service/refer-your-patient/oral-
maxillo-facial/facial-fractures-lacerations?utm_source=chatgpt.com
5.	 Follow up
•	 Monitoring: Regular follow-up to assess healing, manage complications, and
address any functional issues.
•	 Rehabilitation: May include physiotherapy for jaw mobility and speech therapy if
necessary.
•	 Psychological Support: Provide support for patients dealing with the
psychological impact of facial trauma.
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 Urgent Referral: For patients with airway compromise, significant bleeding, or
complex fractures.
•	 Routine Referral: For non-displaced fractures without functional impairment.
•	 Specialist Consultation: Consult with oral and maxillofacial surgeons for
definitive management. https://www.westernhealth.org.au/HealthProfessionals/
Referrals/Documents/ASC%20Access%20and%20Referral%20Guideline_Oral%20
Maxillofacial%20Surgery.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com
374 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Odontogenic infection and spread to neck
spaces (Kiribati Original Protocol)
Background
Odontogenic infections arise from dental or oral pathology and can lead to serious
systemic complications. If left untreated, these infections can spread to adjacent
tissues, including the neck spaces, leading to severe conditions such as Ludwig’s
Angina. Early detection and prompt management are essential to prevent airway
compromise, sepsis, and other life-threatening consequences.
General Principles
•	 Follow standard infection control protocols to prevent cross-contamination and
ensure patient safety.
•	 Recognize red flags and symptoms that require urgent referral.
•	 Provide early pain management and antibiotics as indicated.
•	 Always ensure airway management is prioritized, especially if neck space
involvement is suspected.
•	 Conduct a full assessment and monitor for any signs of systemic infection or
airway compromise.
1.
May present with
Odontogenic infections may present with the following symptoms, which could
indicate spread to the neck spaces:
•	 Swelling and erythema of the lower face, neck, or jaw.
•	 Severe pain in the affected area.
•	 Difficulty swallowing (dysphagia) or painful swallowing.
•	 Trismus (inability to open the mouth fully).
•	 Fever and chills suggesting systemic infection.
•	 Difficulty breathing or hoarseness (suggesting airway compromise).
•	 Swelling of the tongue or floor of the mouth.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 375

2.	 Immediate management
•	 Airway Management: Always assess the airway first. In cases of Ludwig’s Angina,
where airway obstruction is a risk, early intervention to secure the airway is
critical. Consider intubation or emergency surgical intervention if necessary.
•	 Analgesia: Administer appropriate pain relief, considering the severity of the
infection and the patient’s overall condition. Opioid analgesics may be required
for severe pain.
•	 Antibiotics: Broad-spectrum intravenous antibiotics should be administered
early. Common regimens include a combination of penicillin, metronidazole, or
clindamycin to cover both aerobic and anaerobic bacteria.
•	 Hydration and Fluid Management: Administer IV fluids as necessary to maintain
hydration and support systemic circulation.
•	 Monitoring: Close monitoring of vital signs, including temperature, pulse, and
oxygen saturation, is essential to detect signs of sepsis or airway compromise
early.
Vital Symptoms/Signs Not to Miss
These symptoms require IMMEDIATE REFERRAL to a higher level of care or
emergency intervention:
SYMPTOM/SIGN
CONDITION
ACTION
Difficulty breathing, stridor,
or hoarseness
Airway compromise
Immediate emergency
referral
Severe swelling of the neck,
dysphagia
Ludwig’s Angina
Immediate referral for
airway management
Fever with worsening
symptoms
Sepsis or systemic
infection
Immediate referral
for antibiotics and
management
Increased pain and swelling
unresponsive to analgesics
Spread of infection to
deep spaces
Immediate referral for
surgical consultation
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 History: Obtain a detailed history of the symptoms, including the onset and
progression of pain, fever, and swelling. Ask about recent dental procedures or
infections.
•	 Examination: Perform a thorough examination of the oral cavity, including
palpation of the floor of the mouth, submandibular regions, and neck. Assess for
signs of swelling, erythema, and tenderness.
•	 Neurological Examination: Check for signs of infection affecting the cranial
nerves, particularly the glossopharyngeal and vagus nerves, which could signal
involvement of deep neck spaces.
•	 Functional Assessment: Evaluate the patient's ability to open the mouth,
swallow, and breathe comfortably. Difficulty opening the mouth or swallowing
could be an indication of severe infection or airway compromise.
376 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management
•	 Conservative Management: For mild odontogenic infections, oral antibiotics and
analgesics may be sufficient. Follow up with dental or oral health specialists as
necessary.
•	 Surgical Intervention: Surgical drainage may be necessary for deep infections or
when there is abscess formation. In severe cases, ludwig's angina or deep neck
infections may require neck exploration or debridement.
•	 Referral:

–
Refer patients with signs of airway compromise or severe systemic infection to
the emergency department for immediate intervention.

–
Refer to an oral and maxillofacial specialist or ENT surgeon for definitive
management, especially if the infection has spread to deep neck spaces.
5.	 Follow up
•	 Monitoring: Patients should be closely monitored for any signs of worsening
infection or airway obstruction. If the patient requires hospitalization, frequent
reassessment is necessary.
•	 Rehabilitation: In severe cases, follow-up may include physical therapy for jaw
mobility or speech therapy, depending on the extent of the infection and its
impact on the oral and pharyngeal structures.
•	 Psychological Support: Provide psychological support for patients dealing with
the stress or anxiety of managing a potentially life-threatening condition.
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 Urgent Referral: For patients with airway compromise, signs of sepsis, or those
who require surgical drainage or debridement.
•	 Routine Referral: For patients with early-stage odontogenic infections or those
not showing signs of severe systemic involvement.
•	 Specialist Consultation: Consult with an oral and maxillofacial surgeon, ENT
specialist, or infectious disease expert as necessary.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 377

Trigeminal neuralgia – Adult/Child
(Kiribati Original Protocol)
Background
Trigeminal neuralgia is a chronic pain condition involving the trigeminal nerve (5th
cranial nerve).
•	 It causes sudden, severe, sharp, or electric shock-like pain on one side of the face.
•	 Often triggered by light touch, chewing, talking, wind or brushing teeth.
•	 May be idiopathic or due to compression, multiple sclerosis (MS), post-herpetic
neuralgia, or rarely a tumour.
•	 In resource-limited settings, clinical diagnosis and first-line treatment should be
initiated before considering imaging.
•	 See also:

–
Better Health Channel – Trigeminal Neuralgia www.betterhealth.vic.gov.au/
health/conditionsandtreatments/trigeminal-neuralgia

–
https://www.ninds.nih.gov/health-information/disorders/trigeminal-neuralgia
Recommend
•	 Always discuss with Medical Officer (MO) if trigeminal neuralgia is suspected.
This condition is rare in children – consider alternative diagnoses in young
patients.
•	 Early treatment improves quality of life and reduces the risk of persistent pain
and weight loss.
•	 If recurrent or resistant to treatment, refer to visiting neurologist or dental
surgeon if available.
1.
May present with
•	 Severe, sharp, stabbing or electric-shock pain in the cheek, jaw, teeth, or
forehead.
•	 Pain occurs on one side only.
•	 Attacks last from a few seconds to 2 minutes, often in clusters.
•	 Pain may be triggered by:

–
Chewing, swallowing, brushing teeth

–
Talking, touching the face, cold wind
•	 No fever, swelling, rash or pus
•	 No facial numbness or weakness (if present → consider another diagnosis)
•	 Atypical or persistent pain, numbness or bilateral symptoms may suggest
another condition (e.g. dental abscess, shingles, sinusitis, MS, tumour).
378 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

2.	 Immediate management
•	 Reassure patient and explain condition.
•	 Start first-line medication only after MO review.
•	 Avoid known triggers (e.g. cold wind, chewing on affected side).
•	 Advise to return for review if:

–
Symptoms worsen or do not improve in 1–2 weeks

–
Facial numbness, weakness, or persistent pain occurs
•	 Do not attempt dental extraction unless clear cause identified.
•	 If available, refer to dental or visiting specialist clinic.
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 History:

–
When did pain start? How long does each episode last?

–
Which part of the face is affected?

–
Any known triggers?

–
Pattern: how often, how intense?

–
Previous similar episodes?

–
Recent dental problems or facial infections?
•	 Examination:

–
Check vital signs

–
Inspect skin for rash or swelling (exclude herpes zoster)

–
Check cranial nerves: any weakness, droop, numbness?

–
Examine teeth and gums

–
Palpate face for tenderness or masses
•	 If neurological signs or persistent pain → MO may refer for imaging (if available)
4.	 Management
•	 First-line medication (after MO review):

–
Carbamazepine
	›
Form: Tablet (100 mg)
	›
Strength: 100 mg
	›
Route: Oral
	›
Dose: Start 100 mg twice daily; increase slowly every 3–7 days
	›
Maximum: Usually 400–1200 mg/day in 2–3 divided doses
	›
Duration: Review after 2 weeks
	›
Contraindications: Hypersensitivity, bone marrow suppression
	›
Safety notes: Monitor for drowsiness, dizziness, rash, low sodium, low white
cell count. Do not stop suddenly.
	›
Blood tests (if available): FBC, electrolytes, LFTs prior and during treatment
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 379

•	 If carbamazepine is not tolerated: MO may consider, or amitriptyline if available
•	 Non-medication care

–
Soft diet, avoid cold wind, cover face in wind

–
Offer counselling for chronic pain or mental health impact

–
Monitor for weight loss or sleep disruption
5.	 Follow up
•	 Review within 1–2 weeks to assess symptom control and side effects
•	 If pain is well controlled, continue same dose
•	 If no improvement or poor tolerance, discuss with MO
•	 Ongoing review if long-term therapy is needed
•	 Encourage return for reassessment if:

–
Pain worsens

–
New neurological symptoms develop

–
Unable to eat or sleep

–
Facial numbness or weakness appears
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 Refer to MO or specialist if:

–
Diagnosis uncertain or persistent pain

–
Numbness or facial weakness present

–
Recurrent episodes not responding to treatment
•	 If available, refer to:

–
Dental services if suspect dental origin

–
ENT or neurology specialist (if visiting or via outreach)

–
Consider telehealth if imaging or specialist opinion needed
•	 If suspected secondary cause (MS, tumour, shingles) → refer to hospital
380 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Jaw dislocation – Adult/Child
Background12
Jaw dislocation most commonly involves anterior displacement of the mandible
at the temporomandibular joint (TMJ), typically caused by trauma or excessive
mouth opening (e.g. yawning, dental procedures). It may be unilateral or bilateral.
Prompt recognition and reduction are essential to relieve pain and restore function.
Inappropriate handling can worsen the injury or delay recovery. Dislocations may
recur in individuals with underlying joint laxity or structural abnormalities.
Recommend12
•	 Jaw dislocation is typically an anterior displacement of the mandible.
•	 Prompt reduction is important to relieve pain, restore function, and prevent
complications.
•	 Avoid repeated manipulation – refer early if uncertain.
1.
May present with12
•	 History of trauma, yawning, laughing, or wide mouth opening
•	 Pain in front of ear(s) or jaw
•	 Inability to close mouth
•	 Jaw appears displaced forward, difficulty speaking
•	 Drooling, anxiety, or distress
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Reassure patient and support jaw with gentle pressure
•	 Avoid forcing jaw movement
•	 Place patient in sitting or semi-reclined position
•	 Provide analgesia and refer promptly if in distress or reduction not possible
3.	 Clinical assessment123
•	 Take history: mechanism of injury, previous dislocations, recent dental procedures
•	 Check for:

–
Symmetry of jaw

–
Palpable depression in preauricular area

–
Inability to occlude teeth

–
Associated injuries (e.g. head, cervical spine)
•	 Assess for signs of fracture or nerve injury (numbness, weakness)
•	 Examine ears and oral cavity
•	 Assess airway and ability to swallow/speak
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 381

4.	 Management
a. Analgesia & Sedation (if available and trained)

b. Reduction (if trained)45
Only attempt if trained and confident; otherwise refer.
•	 Wear gloves, sit or stand facing patient.
•	 Patient seated upright, mouth slightly open.
•	 Wrap gauze around both thumbs and place on lower molars inside mouth.
•	 Rest fingers under chin.
•	 Apply downward and backward pressure until you feel the jaw ‘pop’ into place.
•	 Immediately support jaw with a bandage (e.g. Barton bandage) or instruct
patient to keep mouth closed.
382 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

5.	 Follow up
•	 Avoid wide mouth opening for 2 weeks (e.g. yawning, large bites)
•	 Soft diet for several days
•	 Educate patient on recurrence risk
•	 Monitor for signs of fracture, nerve damage, or joint dysfunction
•	 Refer for imaging or specialist if:

–
Recurrent dislocations

–
Suspected fracture

–
Reduction unsuccessful
6.	 Referral/consultation
Refer urgently if:
•	 Unable to reduce or unsure of diagnosis
•	 Suspected mandibular fracture
•	 Associated head injury or airway compromise
•	 Dislocation complicated by seizure, trauma, or high-risk comorbidity
MHMS to advise if ongoing instability or access to imaging/specialist is required.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 383

384 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Eye problems

Eye assessment – Adult/Child
Understanding eye anatomy is crucial for public health nurses as it enables them
to assess, identify, and address potential eye and vision problems contributing to
overall patient well-being and preventing vision loss.
EYE TIPS1
DO - WHEN INDICATED
DON'T
•	 Check visual acuity (VA) and record
it
•	 Test pupil light reactions
•	 Evert the eyelid
•	 Stain with fluorescein to help
identify a corneal defect, unless
a ruptured eyeball or penetrating
injury is obvious
•	 Advise not to drive with eye padded
as depth perception may be altered
•	 Give local anaesthetic eye drops to
take home
•	 Try to remove protruding foreign
body (FB)
•	 Put drops or ointment in an eye that
has suffered an obvious rupture or
penetrating injury
•	 Use steroid eye drops or double
pad an eye, unless advised by MO/
ophthalmologist
•	 Routinely apply eye pad
1.
May present with1
•	 Red, painful eye(s)
•	 Changes to vision, photophobia, blurred vision
•	 Discharge, tearing
•	 Feeling like something in eye (Foreign body sensation)
386 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

2.	 Immediate management
•	 If history of substance in eye(s):

–
irrigate eye(s) with 1–2 L of sodium chloride 0.9% for ≥ 30 minutes:1
	›
put in few drops of anaesthetic eyedrop (may use Lignocaine 1%) wait for
10-15 minutes then rinse
	›
tap water/shower if nothing else available2,3
	›
go to Chemical burn to eye.
•	 If protruding foreign body (FB) - do not remove:4

–
consult MO urgently Ophthalmologist
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 If severe pain, See MO Advice
•	 If injury/trauma, ask about:

–
time and mechanism of injury eg sharp object, flash burn, exposure to laser

–
could there be a foreign body (FB):
	›
ask about all activities in preceding 24 hours
	›
especially activities that can cause high velocity projectiles eg hammering
metal, angle
	›
grinding, operation of high speed machinery
	›
possible contamination with plant material/soil

–
use of protective eyewear

–
if forceful blunt injury, suspect eyeball rupture ± ‘blow out’ fracture of the orbit
•	 If child always suspect injury as cause of red, painful eye:

–
were there any witnesses, is the injury consistent with explanation9

–
if non-accidental injury is suspected, also see Child protection.
•	 Ask about symptoms:

–
one or both eyes affected

–
rate of onset - sudden or over several hours6,7

–
duration - is it getting better or worse

–
loss of vision, double or blurred vision6-8

–
photophobia, pain or grittiness, redness, discharge6-8

–
other eg flashing lights, floaters, headaches, halos around lights, fever
•	 Get past history:

–
diabetes, cold sores (herpes simplex virus), rheumatoid arthritis, ↑ BP

–
glasses, contact lenses (sleeping in, swimming in, prolonged use)6,8

–
eye problems eg injury, cataracts, surgery

–
family history eg glaucoma
•	 Do vital signs

–
Examine eye
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 387

Eye examination
•	 If a red, swollen, tender eyelid + febrile assume Orbital/periorbital cellulitis.
•	 Examine systematically
•	 Measure VA
•	 Do visual acuity (VA)

–
note: 3/3 or 6/6 VA does not exclude a serious condition. ↓VA may be long-
standing or due to uncorrected refractive error

–
repeat VA using a multiple-pinhole occluder if available. If vision improves it
likely means a VA issue that can be corrected with glasses, and less likely an
acute issue
•	 Use ophthalmoscope set to +10D and held at 10 cm from eye or bright pen light 5
•	 Check both eyes without putting pressure on the eye or forcing the lids open,
including:4

–
eyelashes - any in-turned, crusted discharge

–
eyelids - do they fully close, any lacerations, swelling, redness, bruising

–
lower eyelid - colour of conjunctiva

–
sclera - bleeding, FB

–
cornea - clear or hazy, FB, white spot1

–
anterior chamber - any blood (hyphaema) or pus (hypopyon)

–
 pupils:
	›
shape + reaction to light (do they react equally)
	›
red reflex for any dimming, any cloudiness
	›
any asymmetry in the position of and brightness of the corneal reflex

–
eye movements:
	›
ask patient to look up, down, side to side - any pain
	›
any restriction. If restricted upward movement consider orbital fracture in
blunt injury
	›
any double vision. To confirm if it is true double vision (and not just blur/
ghosting), cover eye. If double vision disappears, it is not true double vision
•	 If eye swollen closed, gently lift lid to check for injuries + pupil size and reaction
to light
388 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

•	 Always evert upper eyelid if red eye, sensation of FB, or grittiness:

–
check for/exclude retained FB

–
+ look for redness or small round bumps - may indicate conjunctivitis
(bacterial, viral, allergic)
•	 IF SKILLED ONLY

–
Stain with fluorescein to help identify a FB or corneal defect eg abrasion

–
Measure intraocular pressure (IOP) if tonometer available and skilled
Visual acuity (VA)
•	 Use Snellen or tumbling E chart positioned at the specified distance eg 3 or 6
metres, from the patient in a well lit area:10

–
test one eye at a time

–
ask patient to cover one eye with their palm and read or point in the direction
of the E's, from the top of the chart left to right

–
record VA as 2 numbers. Top number is the distance from the chart, bottom
number is the smallest line of letters/Es that can be read without mistakes eg
3/3, 6/9
•	 If cannot read the top letter at 3 or 6 metres:10

–
hold up your fingers at varying distances eg 5 metres, 4 metres etc and record
vision as counting fingers (CF) (the maximum distance they can see between
5 and 1 metre eg VA CF 5 m)
•	 If cannot see to count fingers, check if they can see hand movements or have
perception of light by shining a torch into the eye at 10 cm5
Fluorescein examination of cornea (eye drops or strips)
•	 Avoid if obvious or high likelihood of globe rupture or penetrating injury
•	 If red eye(s) use separate strips/tissues for each eye1
•	 Fluorescein eye drops - use smallest amount possible, too much will flood the
eye and a defect may be missed.4 Gently dab the closed eye with a tissue to
remove excess
•	 Fluorescein strips - gently touch the strip to the inside of the lower eyelid,
pre-moisten with saline if dry eye. Repeat if more dye is needed
•	 Ask the patient to blink to spread the dye
•	 Dye will pool or 'uptake' (stain) in damaged areas of cornea
•	 In normal light uptake will look yellow. With blue light of ophthalmoscope
uptake will be green:11

–
multiple pinpoint uptake - may be dry eye

–
sharp, well-defined border - may be abrasion(s)

–
round with fuzzy edges, branching (dendritic) pattern - may be herpes
simplex virus

–
vertical lines on upper cornea - may be retained FB in eyelid
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 389

How to event an eyelid1

Image 2

5 mm from lid edge) and apply very
light pressure downward with the bud
(images 1 & 2)
Evert the eyelid by using the eyelashes
to gently pull the lid upwards over the
bud. Remove the bud (image 3)

Conjunctivitis,

If acute glaucoma likely, MO may order:13

eye drops to ↓ IOP eg 0.5% timolol, 2% pilocarpine + IV/oral acetazolamide
•

–
corticosteroid eye drops eg dexamethasone AND mydriatic/cycloplegic eye
drops eg atropine
•
Ongoing management as per MO/ophthalmologist

Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 391

Foreign body in eye - Adult/Child
1.
May present with1-3
•	 Report of something in the eye - (Foreign Body sensation)

–
Pain, photophobia- more if corneal FB

–
Redness, epiphora

–
may be worse when blinking or with eye movements

–
Poor vision
•	 ± red, watery eye(s), swollen eyelid(s), photophobia
2.	 Immediate management
•	 See Eye injury. if:4

–
obvious penetrating injury, or caused from high speed and force (high
velocity)

–
protruding foreign body (FB) - do not remove
•	 Advise to not rub the eye
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Ask about:

–
time + how FB happened eg:4
	›
hammering metal, angle grinding, operation of high speed machinery - high
velocity
	›
sand, dust, eyelash 1,3 - low velocity
	›
if unsure, ask about all activities in preceding 24 hours, especially activities
that can cause

–
high velocity projectiles

–
use of protective eyewear 1,3

–
pain, changes in vision, photophobia 1,3

–
contact lenses - get patient to remove 1

–
previous eye conditions
•	 Do vital signs?
•	 Examine both eyes as per Eye assessment.

–
instil (or Plain Lignocaine 1%)if needed for pain1

–
visual acuity (VA) - test unaffected eye first (Always Right Eye first)

–
evert eyelids: 1,2
	›
if FB seen under eyelid, use moistened cotton tip with sodium chloride
0.9% to sweep away 1,2

–
stain with fluorescein - may help to see FB + show any Corneal abrasion.
•	 If unable to tolerate examination, contact MO3
392 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management
•	 If no FB visible, but fluorescein shows abrasion on cornea, see Corneal abrasion.
•	 Consult MO if:

–
high velocity projectile - x-ray may be needed

–
event happened a few days prior + there is ↑ pain, worsening vision + diffuse
redness2
	–
↓VA, pupils irregular or non-reactive

–
blood in anterior chamber (hyphaema) - suggests perforation of eyeball

–
FB ‘lodged’ in the cornea
•	 Only try to remove the FB if it:

–
looks superficial/not ‘lodged’ in the eye

–
is at least 4 mm from the pupil
Removal of foreign body2,3
•	 Lie patient down, with affected eye closest to you
•	 Ask patient to look up, approach from side to ↓ blinking. Hold eyelid open if
needed2
•	 First try to irrigate with sodium chloride 0.9% (use a syringe without needle) FB
may wash off2
•	 Or, try to dislodge with a moistened cotton tip applicator using ‘dab' or 'nudging'
motion2
•	 If still not dislodged, MO may consider removal from cornea with 25 G needle
(best done with slit lamp)4
•	 Consider instilling oxybuprocaine in both eyes to stop blinking to aid removal
•	 If unable to remove the FB, or there is a 'rust ring' after removal - consult MO
•	 If FB removed from cornea give chloramphenicol as per Corneal abrasion.
•	 Advise to wear protective eyewear during risky activities1
OXYBUPROCAINE

Eye
Drops
0.4%
Eye
Adult and child 1
drop

to 6 drops may be
used for foreign body
removal

CMI: May sting for a few seconds. Close eyes after instillation. Dab away tears, do
not rub
Contraindication: Ruptured eyeball or penetrating eye injury
Management of associated emergency: Consult MO.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 393

5.	 Follow up
•	 If FB removed from cornea, advise to return the next day for review + stain with
fluorescein:

–
consult MO if VA ↓, pain or redness ↑, or defect on fluorescein staining
•	 For any other FB, advise to return if the sensation of FB in eye worsens or
persists overnight:

–
consult MO
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 As above
394 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Corneal abrasion - Adult/Child
1.
May present with1,2
•	 Sudden pain + foreign body sensation - even if none is present ±
•	 tearing, photophobia, blurred vision, difficulty opening eye, history of eye trauma
2.	 Immediate management Not applicable
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Get history and examine eyes as per Eye assessment.
•	 Do vital signs?
4.	 Management
•	 Consult MO urgently if:

–
suspected Corneal ulcer.

–
significant pain, photophobia, ↓ vision or worsening symptoms

–
wears contact lenses
•	 MO may advise urgent evacuation for ophthalmology review ± topical antibiotics
•	 If abrasion &lt; 4 mm + normal vision and resolving symptoms:3

–
give chloramphenicol1

–
advise most heal in 1–2 days1-3

–
do not patch or pad the eye4
•	 Offer ibuprofen.4
•	 See Acute pain.
CHLORAMPHENICOL

mL)

(advice as big as

Contraindication: Ruptured eyeball or penetrating injury
Management of associated emergency: Consult MO.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 395

5.	 Follow up
•	 Advise to be reviewed daily until healed or sooner if symptoms worsen:

–
repeat VA, stain with fluorescein - consult MO if worsening symptoms/no
improvement
•	 Advise to see MO at next clinic
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 As above
396 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Flash burn to eye - Adult/Child
Background1,2
•	 Damage to the cornea from excessive exposure to bright ultra violet (UV) light.1
If left untreated
•	 infection can start, which can lead to loss of vision2
1.
May present with2
•	 Intense pain/feeling that there is something in eye (usually both eyes)
•	 Exposure to UV light around 5–12 hours prior eg:1

–
welding, direct sunlight, reflection off snow/water, sunlamp, lightning,
explosion, solar eclipse
•	 Red, watery eye(s), closed eyelid(s)
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Offer analgesia. See Acute pain.
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Ask about:3

–
recent welding/other exposure to UV light

–
onset/duration of pain/symptoms1

–
photophobia, blurred/changed vision

–
one eye or both

–
contact lenses - get patient to remove

–
has this happened before - ↑ risk of infection after previous injury/burn3
•	 Do vital signs
•	 Examine both eyes as per Eye assessment.

–
MO to advise on pain relief

–
VA

–
stain with fluorescein - may show widespread dots (superficial defects) across
the cornea,

–
particularly where not protected by eyelids in normal position, often both
eyes5

–
if from welding check for FB in eye.
•	 Check face/skin for burns4
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 397

4.	 Management1,5
•	 Consult MO if:

–
large or central (over pupil) corneal abrasion

–
VA ↓
•	 Treat as per Corneal abrasion.
•	 Advise the pain and discomfort should start improving in a few hours
•	 Usually heals in 1–2 days
5.	 Follow up
•	 As per Corneal abrasion.
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 As above
398 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Chemical burn to eye - Adult/Child
Recommend
•	 Immediate irrigation is critical to avoiding permanent damage + blindness. 1-3
1.
May present with1
•	 History of substance in eye(s) ±

–
Pain

–
weeping, swelling of the lids or conjunctiva
	–
↓ vision

–
Photophobia

–
Foreign body sensation

–
Red eye
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Irrigate eye(s) with 1–2 L of sodium chloride 0.9% for ≥ 30 minutes. Use:2

–
IV bag with giving set fully open or2

–
tap water/shower if nothing else available 1,3

–
do not examine eye(s) until this has been done
•	 Lie patient down, hold tubing 3–5 cm above eye surface, ask patient to:2

–
open eyelids as wide as possible - may need to hold eyelid open

–
look left, right, up + down while irrigating

–
if only one eye, tilt head away from unaffected eye

–
remove any foreign bodies - evert upper/lower eyelids + sweep with a soaked
cotton tip 1,3
•	 Seek MO advice re pain relief
•	 Offer analgesia if eye drops do not provide adequate pain relief. See Acute pain.
•	 Urgently contact MO
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 399

3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Get rapid history while irrigating:

–
when/how did it happen - if explosion may have other injuries1

–
what was the chemical and active ingredients if possible. Note: alkalis burn
deeper + require

–
more irrigation eg lime, oven cleaner 1-3

–
any first aid, how soon after 1,2

–
photophobia

–
pain in or around the eye

–
vision changes
•	 Do vital signs?
•	 After 30 minutes of irrigation:2

–
use litmus paper to check pH, touch the paper on inside lower eyelid

–
pH should be between 6.5–7.4

–
if not - keep irrigating + contact MO

–
use Morgan Lens® if skilled
•	 If pH 6.5–7.4 check:

–
VA + stain with fluorescein

–
outer aspects of eye + lids for abnormalities: 1,2

–
cornea clear or cloudy, can you see iris details2 cloudy may indicate severe
burn1

–
evert lids again - any retained chemical 2,3
•	 check surrounding skin
4.	 Management
•	 Consult MO urgently, who will advise:

–
further management ± evacuation for ophthalmology review
5.	 Follow up
•	 As per MO/ophthalmologist
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 As above
400 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Eye injury - Adult/Child
Blunt, penetrating
1.
May present with1-3
•	 Trauma ±
•	 Blunt Penetrating

–
Pain

–
Possible bleeding

–
loss of vision, double vision, photophobia

–
excessive tearing - if globe perforated, aqueous will leak out + look like
excessive tearing
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Assess + treat life-threatening injuries4 eg Head injuries. Traumatic injuries.
•	 If obvious penetrating injury or protruding FB do not remove - consult MO
urgently 2,5
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Get history + do examination as per Eye assessment, including:

–
if blow to the eye/blunt injury eg fist, falls, sports - check for orbital fracture:3,4

–
feel the bony rim above and below the eye - any deformity, pain

–
any numbness of the lower lid, cheek, side of nose, upper lip, teeth3

–
if the eye looks displaced lower than other eye or is turning out - may indicate
a blow out fracture

–
note: if using fluorescein, also look for the Seidel sign - where the aqueous
leaking out of a perforating injury causes an expanding dark patch to form in
the fluorescein pattern

–
May have Iris prolapse
•	 If suspected penetrating injury, check tetanus status2
•	 Do vital signs
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 401

4.	 Management
•	 If ruptured eyeball or penetrating injury cannot be ruled out:2,5

–
consult MO urgently

–
Apply Eye shield:
	›
protect by taping rigid eye shield over eye:2
	›
if no eye shield use cut down Styrofoam® cup taped securely to the brow +
cheek
	›
make sure the shield/cup is not pressing on the eye
	›
bed rest on back, with head elevated if preferred, in dim lighting
	›
Don’t remove anything attached or sticking out of the Eye.
	›
keep nil by mouth 2,5
•	 Consult MO in all cases, who may advise:

–
Antibiotics: Oral or IV antibiotics
	›
Note: Do not put any creams/ointment or preservative-contain into the
eye as they toxic to internal structures.

–
Consider Tetanus toxoid as needed.

–
urgent evacuation for ophthalmic review ± x-ray, CT scan/MRI if suspected
intraocular FB:
	›
if risk of trapped air from penetrating injury, sea level cabin pressure is
required for flight
•	 Offer analgesia ± antiemetic. Nausea and vomiting can ↑injury.2,5 See Acute pain.
•	 Nausea and vomiting
•	 Prognosis: guarded
5.	 Follow up
•	 If not evacuated advise follow up as per MO/ophthalmologist
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 As above
402 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Sudden, painless loss of vision - Adult/Child
Background1
•	 This is an emergency. Causes can include - stroke, TIA, blockage in eye vessel,
retinal detachment
1.
May present with
•	 Sudden loss of vision - partial or complete in 1 or both eyes
2.	 Immediate management
•	 If loss of vision + signs of stroke eg one sided weakness, slurred speech:1,2

–
contact MO urgently

–
see Stroke
3.	 Clinical assessment1-3
•	 Get rapid history/assessment as per Eye assessment
•	 Also ask:

–
how quick did the vision go - sudden, over several minutes or hours

–
1 or both eyes - 1 usually means ocular cause; both usually systemic disease

–
has vision returned - may indicate TIA/vascular cause

–
other symptoms - any:
	›
preceding flashes ± floaters, or recent facial trauma - may indicate retinal
detachment
	›
double vision, ‘dark shadow’ in vision of affected eye
	›
jaw pain on chewing - may indicate temporal arteritis

–
prior episodes

–
contact lenses/glasses

–
hypertension, diabetes, cataract surgery

–
current medicines, eye drops
•	 Vital signs + BGL
•	 Examine eyes, including:

–
VA (distance + near) + pupil reaction to light

–
visual field, eye movements

–
red reflex - loss of reflex may indicate retinal detachment
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 403

4.	 Management
•	 Consult MO urgently for all cases:

–
ongoing management ± urgent evacuation for specialist review/management
•	 Minimise activity eg bed rest3
5.	 Follow up
•	 As per MO
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 As above
404 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Orbital/periorbital cellulitis - Adult/Child
Background
•	 Orbital cellulitis is an infection of the eye socket + surrounding tissue. It is an
emergency + can cause blindness + intracranial infection1
•	 Periorbital cellulitis is an infection of the eyelid1
•	 Overlapping clinical features makes it difficult to differentiate2
•	 Children &lt; 4 years are at ↑risk of both due to a undeveloped orbital septum1
1.
May present with
•	 Single eye with:2

–
swelling + redness of eyelid

–
eye pain or tenderness

–
Low grade fever

–
Ptosis (Drooping eyelid)

–
Usually follows trauma around the eye or
	›
Facial Infection
	›
Hordeolum
	›
Following upper-respiratory tract infection
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Do vital signs
•	 Screen for Sepsis
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Get rapid history:1,2

–
pain - eye, ear, facial

–
fever, malaise

–
headache - severe or persistent
	–
↓ vision, double vision

–
recent stye, trauma or insect bite to eye/eyelid

–
Bacterial sinusitis, tooth or ear infection3

–
Hib immunisation history
•	 Check head + neck, look for obvious signs of infection eg:

–
insect bite to the eye, wound, stye, infected tooth, enlarged lymph nodes,
tender sinuses3
•	 Examine both eyes. Check for:1-3
	–
↓ VA, pupil reaction to light not equal

–
degree of swelling, reduced eye opening - does this limit your ability to
examine the eye

–
painful/limited eye movements - unable to look in different directions

–
protrusion of the eyeball
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 405

4.	 Management
•	 If both eyes are swollen, or non-tender (painless) swelling in a well looking
patient, more likely to
•	 be an allergic reaction.2 Consult MO
•	 Contact MO urgently in all other cases, who may advise:
•	 if periorbital cellulitis likely AND patient otherwise well eg no fever, malaise:

–
oral antibiotics
•	 if orbital cellulitis OR severe periorbital cellulitis suspected:

–
insert IVC

–
blood cultures, FBC 1,2

–
Pus swab (For culture)

–
IV antibiotics

–
urgent evacuation ± CT scan1

–
urgent ENT + ophthalmology advice
•	 Offer analgesia. See Acute pain
Antibiotics1 MO may order:

–
oral trimethoprim + sulfamethoxazole (PLUS IV cefotaxime if severe)
•	 If non remote community and NO prior history of MRSA infection:

–
oral flucloxacillin (cefalexin if mild allergy, clindamycin if immediate sensitivity)
OR

–
oral amoxicillin + clavulanic acid (if features of sinusitis, cefalexin if mild
allergy)

or pale stools
Note: If renal impairment, taking ACE inhibitor or potassium, HIV or SLE seek MO
advice
Pregnancy: Do not use in the 1st trimester or in late pregnancy
Contraindication: Severe or immediate allergic reaction to sulfonamides,
megaloblastic anaemia, severe hepatic impairment, elderly
Management of associated emergency: Consult MO. See Anaphylaxis

Note: Rapid injection &lt; 1 minute can cause life-threatening arrhythmias. If renal

cephalosporins. Do not mix with aminoglycosides eg gentamicin
Management of associated emergency: Consult MO. See Anaphylaxis
5.	 Follow up
•	 If not evacuated, advise to be reviewed daily until improved or sooner if
symptoms worsen:

–
if not improving after 24 hours, contact MO
•	 Advise to see MO at next clinic
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 As above
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 407

Conjunctivitis - Adult/Child/Newborn
Related topics - Eye differential diagnosis
1.
May present with1,2
•	 Newborn:

–
Starts within a few days or weeks after birth

–
Usually Bilateral

–
Red eyes

–
Pus discharge (mucoprulent)

–
Swollen eyelids
•	 Itchy/irritated ± red eye(s)
•	 Discharge - watery, pus, mucous
•	 Crusting of eyelids/eye lashes
•	 Pain
•	 swoll
2.	 Immediate management Not applicable
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Ask about:1,2

–
pain, photophobia or changes in vision

–
onset/duration of symptoms

–
one eye or both

–
contact with person with red eyes/conjunctivitis

–
recent URTI, sinusitis, flu-like symptoms

–
hay fever, known allergies

–
contact lenses

–
vesicular rash, cold sores, shingles

–
exposure to irritants eg smoke or chlorine - usually self-limiting. If chemical
exposure, also see
•	 Chemical burn to eye
•	 Do vital signs
•	 Check for swollen glands in neck - may indicate viral conjunctivitis
•	 Examine both eyes as per Eye assessment, including:

–
VA + stain with fluorescein

–
evert eyelids - small round bumps under eyelid may indicate allergic
conjunctivitis
•	 Use flow chart for further assessment
408 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

•	 Rule of Thumb:

–
If it started in first 5 days after birth probably Gonorrhoeal

–
If it started after 5 days probably Chlamydia

–
If it started after 3 weeks it is not urgent
4.	 Management
•	 Contact MO urgently if:2

–
pain, photophobia or reduced vision, or worsening symptoms

–
fluorescein uptake shows Corneal abrasion

–
wears contact lenses, or has herpes/shingles infection
Newborn and young infant
•	 2–12 months:

–
if sticky/watery discharge is the ONLY symptom (no redness):

–
likely blocked tear duct rather than conjunctivitis2

–
usually gets better without treatment

–
seek advice from midwife/child health nurse/MO as needed
•	 if other signs of conjunctivitis eg red eye(s):

–
consult MO

–
if suspected bacterial conjunctivitis give chloramphenicol2
Suspected conjuctivitis

Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 409

Neonate:
•	 consult MO
•	 do swabs for MCS + gonorrhoea and chlamydia PCR:

–
if gonorrhoea - ophthalmic emergency2 consult MO urgently

–
if chlamydia - MO may order azithromycin2
	›
treat mother + contact tracing.

–
if suspected bacterial conjunctivitis, MO will order chloramphenicol

–
note: chlamydia/gonorrhoea in neonate may reflect mother-to-child
transmission, accidental transmission or sexual abuse.2 See Child protection if
concerns
Child and adult
•	 Viral conjunctivitis:

–
usually in one eye, but often spreads to both

–
reassure patient usually self-limiting but may take weeks to resolve

–
do not give chloramphenicol2

–
lubricating eye drops may provide symptomatic relief
•	 Allergic conjunctivitis:

–
if patient has Allergic rhinitis, oral antihistamines may help2

–
antihistamine eye drops eg ketotifen, may help reduce symptoms and
lubricating eye drops may help to remove allergen1
•	 Bacterial conjunctivitis:

–
most cases will resolve in 7 days without treatment2

–
if marked symptoms eg purulent discharge, give chloramphenicol2
•	 Chlamydial conjunctivitis:

–
consider if sexually active with one red eye + mucopurulent discharge, or
chronic

–
conjunctivitis:1
	›
do STI/BBV tests, + swab eye for chlamydia PCR (write ‘eye’ on pathology
form)
	›
give oral azithromycin
•	 In all cases:

–
symptomatic treatment may help eg:
	›
cold compresses several times/day or ice packs1

–
clean eye(s) as needed with clean water to remove crusting and discharge.
Gently wipe from

–
inside to outside to avoid spreading infection to other eye3

–
frequent hand washing

–
avoid sharing towels, pillows

–
keep children away from school/child care until no discharge4
410 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 411

5.	 Follow up
•	 Advise to return if worsening or new symptoms. Contact MO who may:

–
order bacterial + viral swabs

–
arrange ophthalmic referral to exclude alternative diagnoses
•	 If bacterial and not resolved in 5 days, patient should have immediate ophthalmic
review1
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 As above
412 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Trachoma - Adult/Child
Background
•	 Repeated infection by the bacterium Chlamydia trachomatis causing
Conjunctivitis and possibly leading to trichiasis
•	 Different strains from the STI chlamydia.
•	 can lead to eyelid contraction and in-turned eyelashes that rub on the eyeball,
causing painful
1.
May present with1,2
•	 Child - repeated conjunctivitis
•	 Adult - in-turned eyelashes, cloudy cornea

–
Painful eyes

–
White or watery discharge

–
Photophobia

–
Irritated Red eye

–
Nearly always bilateral
2.	 Immediate management Not applicable
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Get history, including:1,2

–
previous episodes for patient and family

–
time spent in known area of outbreak

–
contact with person(s) with similar symptoms
•	 Do vital signs
•	 Examine eyes as per Eye assessment,  including:

–
evert the upper eyelids, look for:2
	›
≥ 5 pale round spots (follicles)
	›
intense redness, swelling - can you see normal blood vessels
	›
note: follicles ± intense redness are indications of active trachoma - take
eyelid swab for chlamydia PCR
	›
visible scarring, in-turned eyelashes, signs of pulling out eyelashes

–
check cornea - clear or cloudy
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 413

4.	 Management
•	 Surgery: For those who have entropion or Trichiasis

–
Epilation of in-turned lashes if surgery is not available
•	 Antibiotics:

–
Treats the Infected but also helps prevent the spread

–
Azithromycin (Zithromax)
	›
Now the main treatment for active Trachoma
	›
Single dose
	›
PO once per year
	›
Available in tablets and liquid (Paediatric Oral Suspension) for 6months old
- 7 years old plus children who are unable to swallow tablets.

–
note: aim to treat all members of relevant household(s) within 1 week of
starting treatment2

–
Tetracycline eye ointment
	›
Old Treatment
	›
Apply BID for 6 weeks  for &lt;6months old.
	›
Treats eye infection only
•	 Facial Cleanliness:

–
Facial washing is an important component
•	 Environmental Improvement:

–
Remove the environmental risk factors for trachoma

–
Includes bringing better water supply and toilets to a community

–
May have to convince people to change their habits:
	›
People must use the clean water made available to clean their hands and
faces
	›
People must not leave human and animal waste uncovered as it attracts
flies
	›
It is often very difficult for people to change their behaviours.
AZITHROMYCIN

dosing stick)

200 mg/5 ml
Offer CMI: May cause rash, diarrhoea, nausea, abdominal cramps or thrush
Management of associated emergency: Consult MO. See Anaphylaxis
414 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

5.	 Follow up
•	 Check swab result:

–
if Chlamydia trachomatis is identified notify Public Health Unit1
•	 Advise to return if worsening or new symptoms, contact MO who may:

–
order swabs for MCS and Chlamydia trachomatis PCR

–
arrange ophthalmic referral if repeated infections, in-turned eyelashes or
corneal changes
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 As above
•	 Chlamydia trachomatis is a notifiable disease by pathological diagnosis
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 415

Diabetic retinopathy
Background
•	 Damage to the retina and blood vessels due to diabetic mellitus (DM)
•	 Can affect any patient with DM (type 1 or 2)- risk increases with years since
diagnosis and poor or unstable blood sugar control.
•	 Progresses over months/years from non-proliferative to proliferative retinopathy

–
Blindness from damage to the macula and/or retinal detachment and scarring
•	 Damage is usually progressive and irreversible
1.
May present with
•	 Asymptomatic at the beginning of retinopathy
•	 May have decreased VA
•	 Sudden or Gradual
•	 Flashes of light
•	 Floaters
•	 History of DM, but can be in patients without diagnosis of DM (presenting feature
for DM)

–
Blood sugar levels are often poorly controlled (high or fluctuating)
2.	 Immediate management Not applicable
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Get rapid history/assessment as per Eye assessment plus Red Reflex
•	 Also ask:
	›
how quick did the vision go - sudden, over several minutes or hours
	›
1 or both eyes
	›
other symptoms - any:

–
preceding flashes ± floaters- may indicate retinal detachment

–
double vision, ‘dark shadow’ in vision of affected eyes
	›
prior episodes
•	 Check Blood sugar levels and blood sugar
•	 Careful, dilated ophthalmoscopy in both eyes with red-free light
•	 Assess, if possible, grade of retinopathy (if skilled)
416 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management
•	 Consult/Refer with Ophthalmologist
•	 Treatments aims to stop progression of the disease- it cannot reverse the loss of
vision

–
Laser treatment

–
Monthly Intravitreal Injection
•	 All Diabetic patients should have dilated fundus exam annually or as per advice
accordingly.
•	 Strict control of blood of sugar levels and blood pressure
•	 Prognosis:

–
Good if early stage and good glycaemic/blood sugar control.

–
Prognosis decreases with level of retinopathy, glycaemic/blood sugar control
and lack of treatment options.
5.	 Follow up
•	 As per Eye Nurses/ Ophthalmologist
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 As above
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 417

Gastrointestinal

Gastrointestinal emergencies
Acute abdominal pain - Adult/Child
Recommend
•	 Always consider:

–
Ectopic pregnancy, in all females of reproductive age with abdominal pain ±
bleeding1

–
PID, in sexually active females, particularly if &lt; 30 years, with new onset of
pelvic pain1

–
Testicular torsion, and check testes in males with abdominal pain + male
infants with inconsolable crying2
Background

–
If child, also see QPEC guidelines Acute abdominal pain - emergency
management in children
1.
May present with
•	 Abdominal pain
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Do vital signs
•	 If shock - pale, sweaty, HR, capillary refill > 2 seconds ± LOC:

–
contact MO urgently

–
start treatment for Shock

–
Screen for Sepsis

–
If severe pain:
	›
insert IVC x 2, do rapid assessment

–
Offer analgesia ± antiemetic. See Acute pain. Nausea and vomiting
	›
note: analgesia does not mask physical signs/hinder diagnosis3-5
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 419

3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 It is not necessary to make a definitive diagnosis. It is more important to
recognise cases which are significant
•	 If child, also see Child with abdominal pain
•	 Ask about the pain:4

–
Site - where is it, ask patient to pinpoint location

–
Onset:
	›
gradual, rapid or sudden
	›
continuous or intermittent
	›
what were they doing when it started

–
Character - sharp, dull, burning, stabbing, cramp like, crushing, tingling

–
Radiate - anywhere eg shoulder tip, back

–
Alleviating factors eg sitting up, medicine, relief by moving about eg renal
colic, or from lying very still eg peritonitis

–
Timing:
	›
when did it first begin, duration
	›
have they had it before, when, what happened
	›
any increase in severity

–
Exacerbating factors - does anything make it worse eg movement

–
Severity - mild, moderate or severe (scale 1–10):
	›
does it interfere with sleep or normal activities
•	 Ask about:4

–
associated symptoms eg fever, SOB, headache, nausea, vomiting - any blood,
bile or green vomit - does it look/smell like faeces

–
recent weight loss, changes in appetite, food refusal/poor feeding6

–
bowels - any diarrhoea, constipation, blood, black stools/melaena, time of last
motion, passing wind1

–
urine - dysuria, frequency, urgency, haematuria1

–
vaginal - bleeding, discharge. Note: abnormal discharge may indicate PID

–
possible causes of pain, including:
	›
foreign body ingestion + consider Button battery6
	›
poisoning eg venoms, paracetamol, lead, iron5
	›
recent trauma eg abdominal, scrotal2
•	 Get past history, including:7,8

–
medical + surgical

–
smoking, alcohol + other drugs

–
medications + family

–
menstrual - last period, was it normal, are periods regular, any contraception1
420 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Do physical examination, including:
Red flags from abdominal examination - if any contact MO urgently
•	 Pain radiating to the back + palpable pulsatile mass - could be AAA4
•	 Localised tenderness + distension
•	 Guarding -↑muscle tightness in response to palpation or board like rigidity
•	 Rebound tenderness - pain when pressure is applied + then released suddenly
•	 Pain disproportionate to presentation/working diagnosis
•	 Abdominal examination:3-5,8

–
look for:

–
symmetry, shape eg distended or sunken
	›
surgical scars, bruises, distended veins, hernias
	›
signs of non-accidental injury

–
listen for bowel sounds - absent, decreased or hyperactive:
	›
normal are low pitched + gurgling 2–5/minute. Hyperactive/high pitched
suggests Bowel obstruction

–
palpate - start lightly, away from site of pain + move through the 9 areas.
Follow with deep palpation:
	›
palpate kidneys - with 1 hand underneath + 1 hand above (below rib line),
gently push down
	›
check testes/groin in males2

–
percuss (tap) all 4 quadrants to check for dullness
•	 Listen to chest - any wheeze, crackles. Note: pneumonia + ACS, can present with
abdominal pain
•	 ECG
•	 Urinalysis + pregnancy test if reproductive age - if +ve go to Ectopic pregnancy
•	 BGL - if > 10 check for ketones + consider DKA
•	 Bloods/i-STAT including lactate, bladder scan if available
•	 Any jaundice, enlarged lymph nodes
•	 Hydration assessment - adult, or child
•	 Rectal examination - may be indicated if suspected Upper GI bleeding
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 421

Abdominal quadrants
4.	 Management8
•	 Consult MO in all cases if:

–
infant/child, pregnant woman or > 60 years

–
moderate to severe pain

–
cause of pain not known
•	 MO may order:

–
chest + abdominal x-ray

–
urgent retrieval/hospitalisation

–
antibiotics, IDC, NGT, nil by mouth
5.	 Follow up4,5
•	 If not retrieved, advise patient/carer to be reviewed next day, or sooner if:

–
pain persists > 24 hours, worsens or changes from generalised to localised, or

–
worsening symptoms or new symptoms develop eg vomiting, fever

–
if any of the above, contact MO
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 As above
422 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Abdominal pain causes1-8
Right hypochondriac
•	 Gall bladder - biliary
colic or cholecystitis
•	 Hepatitis
•	 Pneumonia
•	 Liver abscess/tumour
- rare
Epigastric
•	 Myocardial infarction
•	 Gastritis
•	 Gastric/duodenal ulcer
•	 Pancreatitis
•	 Ruptured aortic
aneurysm
•	 Button battery
ingestion
Left hypochondriac
•	 Pneumonia
•	 Pancreatitis
•	 Ruptured spleen
Right iliac
•	 Appendicitis
•	 Ectopic pregnancy
(unilateral)
•	 Ovarian cyst
•	 PID (bilateral)
•	 Strangulated hernia
(usually men)
•	 Testicular torsion
Umbilical
•	 Strangulated umbilical
hernia
•	 Ruptured aortic
aneurysm
•	 Gastroenteritis
•	 Small bowel
obstruction
•	 Inflammatory bowel
disease
•	 Early appendicitis
(then moves to right
iliac)
Left iliac
•	 Diverticulitis
•	 Ectopic pregnancy
(unilateral)
•	 Ovarian cyst7
•	 PID (bilateral)
•	 Strangulated hernia
(usually men)
•	 Testicular torsion
Suprapubic
•	 Ectopic pregnancy
•	 Testicular torsion
•	 Ovarian torsion
•	 Miscarriage/
pregnancy related/
RPOC
•	 UTI
•	 Large bowel
obstruction
•	 Acute retention of
urine
•	 Uterine "broid
complication
•	 PID
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 423

Gastroenteritis/dehydration - Adult
Diarrhoea ± vomiting
Background1
•	 Most acute diarrhoea is viral and resolves within 24–48 hours without specific
treatment
•	 Antibiotics are of no benefit in most cases and may exacerbate diarrhoea
1.
May present with1
•	 Sudden onset of diarrhoea ± vomiting, fever, abdominal pain, nausea, chills
•	 Lethargy, dehydration
2.	 Immediate management1
•	 Do vital signs + Hydration assessment
•	 If severe dehydration:

–
contact MO urgently

–
continue to manage as per Shock
•	 Screen for Sepsis

Urine output
normal or 
moderate 
significant  or no
output
424 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Get history, including:1

–
gastrointestinal symptoms:
	›
date/time of onset, frequency + amounts, is there blood ± mucous in stools,
bile stained or green vomit, location and severity of abdominal pain
	›
note: if > 24 hours of vomiting without diarrhoea, consider other causes.
See Nausea and vomiting

–
fever, rash, headache, light headedness/dizziness, mobility

–
known illness in contacts

–
recent overseas travel, antibiotic use or hospital admission

–
immunocompromised

–
medications eg aperients
•	 Do physical examination, including:

–
BGL + ketones - if  consider DKA

–
i-STAT - UE, lactate

–
urinalysis - ketones

–
Abdominal examination
•	 Do stool MCS + multiplex PCR + C.difficile if:1

–
diarrhoea > 7 days

–
severe symptoms eg blood in stools, high fever, HR, abdominal tenderness
or severe pain

–
immunocompromised

–
recent overseas travel (C.difficile not required)
4.	 Management1
•	 Rehydration is the most important aspect of management
•	 Consult MO if:

–
vomiting and unable to tolerate oral fluids - IV fluids may be required2

–
severe symptoms

–
pregnant, immunocompromised or elderly
•	 If minimal or no dehydration:

–
advise to continue eating + drinking as tolerated
•	 If mild to moderate dehydration:

–
give ORS, even if intermittent vomiting

–
note: if ORS is not available, use 1/2 level teaspoon of salt + 6 level teaspoons
of sugar in 1 L of clean drinking water

–
aim for 2–3 L over 24 hours. Frequent small volumes may be better tolerated
eg 50 mL every 15–30 minutes

–
avoid fatty or sugary foods and excessive intake of soft drinks, sports/energy
drinks, cordials and fruit juice. If used they should be diluted

–
once tolerating/responding to ORS, advise patient to return to normal diet1
•	 Offer antiemetic if needed. See Nausea and vomiting
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 425

•	 Control the spread advice:

–
if prepares/serves food - avoid handling food until no diarrhoea or vomiting
for 48 hours2

–
if healthcare worker - do not return to work until no symptoms for 48 hours3

–
otherwise, if single case - avoid others until no symptoms, feeling well + no
loose bowel motions for at least 24 hours2
5.	 Follow up1
•	 Advise patient to return + contact MO if:

–
symptoms persist > 48 hours, or worsen

–
develops severe vomiting, abdominal cramps, or blood in stools
•	 Review stool pathology results
6.	 Referral/consultation2
•	 Notify Public Health Unit if ≥ 2 cases of diarrhoea ± vomiting in the same location
or single case in a food handler 
426 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Gastritis - Adult
Background1
•	 Inflammation of the gastric mucosa commonly caused from Helicobacter pylori
infection, use of NSAID, or alcohol
1.
May present with1,2
•	 Epigastric pain or discomfort ± nausea, vomiting, heartburn, reflux
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Do vital signs
•	 Insert IVC x 2
•	 If shock - pale, sweaty, HR, capillary refill > 2 seconds ± LOC:

–
contact MO urgently + see Shock
•	 Assess as per Chest pain assessment, + see ACS
•	 Offer analgesia ± antiemetic. See Acute pain, Nausea and vomiting
3.	 Clinical assessment1,2
•	 Do not jump to conclusions as to the cause of the epigastric pain in a person
who drinks alcohol
•	 Do NOT use medication cocktails eg pink lady, to assist in ruling out a cardiac
cause3
•	 Ask about:

–
pain - onset + duration

–
is it accompanied by nausea, vomiting or heartburn

–
any blood in vomit - fresh or coffee grounds, ± melaena, go to Upper GI
bleeding

–
recent heavy alcohol intake

–
are they taking NSAID, aspirin, antacid

–
previous episodes - when, any treatment

–
recent weight loss, difficulty or painful swallowing

–
other symptoms eg SOB, dizziness, syncope/collapse

–
recent food intake
•	 Get past history - heart disease, GORD, peptic ulcer disease, abdominal surgery
•	 Do physical examination, including Abdominal examination +

–
ECG, BGL + bloods/i-STAT - lactate
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 427

4.	 Management1,2
•	 Contact MO urgently if intense boring pain that radiates to the back
•	 Consult MO, who may advise:

–
IV pantoprazole ± thiamine if alcohol related

–
bloods eg lipase, LFTs

–
retrieval/hospitalisation ± referral for endoscopy
5.	 Follow up
•	 Advise to return if symptoms  or new symptoms eg blood in vomit - contact
MO urgently. Otherwise advise to see MO at next clinic
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 As above
428 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Upper gastrointestinal bleeding - Adult/Child
Vomiting blood
Background1
•	 Usually caused secondary to peptic ulcer disease, erosions, oesophagitis or
oesophageal varices
1.
May present with1
•	 Vomiting blood or dark vomit with ‘coffee grounds’ ± melaena, pain
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Do vital signs
•	 Insert IVC x 2
•	 If shock - pale, sweaty, HR, capillary refill > 2 seconds ± LOC, or large active
bleed:

–
contact MO urgently + see Shock
ALERT suspect Button battery, in all children with upper GI bleed. A button battery
lodged in the oesophagus can burn a hole through to the aorta2
3.	 Clinical assessment3,4
•	 Get rapid history, including:

–
is blood dark, bright or coffee grounds, amount + duration

–
melaena or any fresh blood in stools

–
pain

–
repeated retching/vomiting prior to bleeding - may indicate oesophageal tear1

–
previous episodes

–
history of liver failure, ischaemic heart disease, renal failure, peptic ulcer
disease, oesophageal varices

–
alcohol misuse

–
are they taking aspirin, NSAID, anticoagulants, corticosteriods, iron
supplements
•	 Do physical examination, including Abdominal examination +

–
ECG

–
i-STAT - lactate, baseline Hb (HemoCue if available)

–
bloods - FBC, UE, INR, LFT, cross match
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 429

4.	 Management3,4
•	 Consult MO urgently, who may advise:

–
IV fluids

–
IV antibiotics ± octreotide if oesophageal varices

–
IV pantoprazole ± thiamine if alcohol misuse

–
retrieval/hospitalisation for urgent endoscopy/surgery
•	 Monitor vital signs closely until retrieval, keep nil by mouth
5.	 Follow up
•	 As advised by MO
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 As above
430 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Complications of liver cirrhosis – Adult
Cirrhosis of the liver can lead to a range of serious and potentially life-threatening
complications. The four protocols below addresses common complications of liver
cirrhosis encountered in primary care: ascites, variceal haemorrhage, spontaneous
bacterial peritonitis (SBP), and hepatic encephalopathy. Early recognition, basic
supportive care, and timely referral are critical.
Background
Liver cirrhosis is a chronic condition caused by long-term damage to the liver,
commonly due to hepatitis B or alcohol use. As cirrhosis progresses, it leads to
portal hypertension and impaired liver function, which in turn result in complications
such as:
•	 Ascites – accumulation of fluid in the abdomen
•	 Variceal haemorrhage – bleeding from dilated veins in the oesophagus or
stomach
•	 Spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (SBP) – infection of ascitic fluid
•	 Hepatic encephalopathy – brain dysfunction caused by toxins normally cleared
by the liver
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 431

Ascites – Adult
Recommend
Ascites is the accumulation of fluid in the peritoneal cavity and is the most common
complication of liver cirrhosis, regardless of the cause (e.g. hepatitis B, alcohol).
Ascites is primarily caused by portal hypertension with contributing circulatory,
vascular, functional, and biochemical abnormalities that lead to fluid retention. It is
typically managed through dietary sodium restriction and the use of diuretics.
Background
The primary goals of therapy are to:
•	 Minimise ascitic fluid volume
•	 Decrease peripheral oedema
•	 Improve quality of life (e.g. improve appetite, ease of movement, reduce
discomfort and shortness of breath)
•	 Complications of ascites include spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (SBP) and
hepatorenal syndrome. Early diagnosis and effective volume control can prevent
complications and reduce the need for hospitalisation.
1.
May present with
•	 Abdominal distension
•	 Fullness in the flanks
•	 Weight gain
•	 Shortness of breath
•	 Reduced appetite or early satiety
On physical examination:
•	 Distended abdomen
•	 Shifting dullness (suggests >500 mL fluid)
•	 Fluid thrill (suggests >1 L fluid)
•	 Signs of chronic liver disease (e.g. jaundice, spider naevi, palmar erythema)
•	 +/- Ultrasound scan (if available) may be used to confirm diagnosis or monitor
treatment response
432 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

2.	 Immediate management
•	 Call for help – inform MO urgently
•	 Triage patient and assess vital signs
•	 If available and trained, consider abdominal paracentesis (therapeutic and/or
diagnostic)
•	 Discontinue alcohol use
•	 Discontinue medications that may reduce renal perfusion – e.g. NSAIDs, beta-
blockers, ACE inhibitors (discuss with MO)
•	 Treat underlying liver disease – if HBsAg-positive, initiate Tenofovir and link
patient to Hepatitis Program
•	 Begin dietary education with sodium restriction &lt;2g/day
Clinical assessment
•	 Confirm ascites through physical signs and/or ultrasound if available
•	 Evaluate for signs of liver cirrhosis and complications
•	 Assess for signs of SBP (fever, abdominal tenderness, altered mental status)
•	 Assess nutritional status, mobility, and overall wellbeing
•	 Consider other comorbidities or medications contributing to fluid retention
•	 Monitor daily weight for baseline
3.	 Management
•	 Dietary management:

–
Sodium restriction to &lt;2g/day (low-salt diet)

–
Encourage adequate nutrition (small frequent meals)
•	 Diuretic therapy:
•	 Start with:

–
Spironolactone 100 mg PO OD

–
Furosemide 40 mg PO OD

–
For small patients, consider starting at half doses:

–
Spironolactone 50 mg PO OD

–
Furosemide 20 mg PO OD
•	 Titration:

–
Titrate doses upwards only after ≥3–5 days

–
Maintain a 100:40 mg spironolactone:furosemide ratio

–
Maximum doses:
	›
Spironolactone 400 mg/day
	›
Furosemide 160 mg/day
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 433

•	 Monitoring:

–
Aim for daily weight loss of 500–1000 g/day

–
Monitor blood pressure, urine output, and signs of dehydration or electrolyte
disturbance
•	 Monitor for muscle cramps, dizziness, or confusion
•	 Adjust doses in consultation with MO
•	 Monitor for signs of complications such as SBP or renal dysfunction
•	 If patient is not improving, refer to tertiary hospital for further care (e.g. liver
function tests, electrolytes, endoscopy)
4.	 Follow up
•	 Monitor weight daily where possible
•	 Review diuretic response after 3–5 days
•	 Continue dietary counselling and monitor adherence to sodium restriction
•	 Educate patient and family on signs of complications (e.g. fever, confusion,
abdominal pain)
•	 Ensure linkage to Hepatitis Program if HBsAg positive
•	 Consider referral for ongoing
5.	 Referral / consultation
Refer to medical officer or hospital if:
•	 Ascites is severe or refractory to diuretics
•	 Symptoms of spontaneous bacterial peritonitis are present
•	 Signs of renal impairment or electrolyte imbalance
•	 Paracentesis is required but cannot be safely performed locally
•	 Specialist review needed for liver disease or complications (e.g. hepatology,
endoscopy)

Variceal hemorrhage – Adult
Recommend
Variceal hemorrhage is a life-threatening complication of portal hypertension,
commonly seen in patients with liver cirrhosis from any cause (e.g. chronic hepatitis
B infection, alcohol use). Screening for hepatitis B (HBsAg) is important if the
patient’s status is unknown. Prompt resuscitation, bleeding control, and infection
prevention are essential.
Background
Oesophageal or gastric varices are dilated veins that develop due to portal
hypertension. When these rupture, they can cause massive upper gastrointestinal
bleeding. Early identification and urgent management are critical to reduce
mortality and prevent complications.
Goals of acute therapy are to:
•	 Restore and maintain haemodynamic stability
•	 Restore and maintain adequate oxygenation
•	 Control bleeding
•	 Prevent complications including infection and rebleeding
1.
May present with
•	 Haematemesis (vomiting blood)
•	 Melena (black, tarry stools)
•	 Dizziness or syncope
•	 Signs of shock (e.g. hypotension, tachycardia)
•	 History of liver disease or known cirrhosis
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Call for help – inform MO immediately
•	 Triage patient and assess vital signs
•	 Insert large bore IV access (16G cannula) and begin fluid resuscitation
•	 Aim to maintain BP >90/60 mmHg
•	 Provide oxygen by nasal cannula – maintain oxygen saturation >95%
•	 Consider nasogastric tube (NGT) to clear blood and clots if preparing for
endoscopy
•	 If available, give erythromycin (as a prokinetic) to improve endoscopy visibility
•	 Begin broad-spectrum antibiotics:

–
Ceftriaxone 1g IV OD ± Ciprofloxacin 500 mg PO BD

–
Complete 7 days total antibiotic therapy
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 435

3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Assess ABCs (airway, breathing, circulation) and haemodynamic status
•	 Confirm GI bleeding symptoms and history of liver disease
•	 Perform physical examination for signs of cirrhosis (jaundice, spider naevi,
ascites)
•	 Screen for HBsAg if status unknown
•	 Monitor vital signs continuously
•	 Record baseline haemoglobin if available
4.	 Management
Pharmacologic therapy (if available):
•	 Octreotide 50 mcg IV bolus followed by 50 mcg/hour IV infusion
•	 Continue for 2–5 days following cessation of bleeding
Blood transfusion:
•	 If hypovolaemic or anaemic, transfuse to maintain Hb ≥7 g/dL and &lt;9 g/dL
Endoscopy:
•	 Arrange urgent endoscopy after stabilisation
•	 Should be done within 12 hours of hospital admission
•	 May include variceal band ligation to control bleeding
Nutritional support:
•	 Once haemodynamically stable, wait 24–48 hours post-bleed before resuming
oral or enteral feeds
Chronic management:
•	 If patient is HBsAg-positive, discuss initiation of beta-blocker therapy (e.g.
propranolol) with MO
•	 Refer to Hepatitis Program for antiviral therapy and long-term follow-up
•	 Plan for repeat endoscopy for ongoing variceal surveillance
5.	 Follow up
•	 Monitor for signs of rebleeding (e.g. new haematemesis or melena)
•	 Reassess haemodynamic stability and nutritional intake
•	 Link patient with Hepatitis Program if HBsAg positive
•	 Arrange for long-term follow-up and surveillance endoscopy if available
•	 Educate patient and family on avoiding alcohol and recognising signs of bleeding
436 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

6.	 Referral / consultation
Refer urgently to hospital or medical officer if:
•	 Ongoing bleeding or haemodynamic instability
•	 Need for blood transfusion or urgent endoscopy
•	 HBsAg-positive patients requiring further assessment and antiviral therapy
•	 First presentation of variceal bleed requiring comprehensive care

Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 437

Spontaneous Bacterial Peritonitis (SBP) –
Adult
Recommend
Spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (SBP) is an infection of preexisting ascitic fluid in
the absence of any secondary intra-abdominal source (e.g. perforated viscus). SBP
typically occurs in patients with end-stage liver disease. Without a high index of
suspicion and early antibiotic treatment, mortality is high.
Background
SBP is caused by translocation of intestinal bacteria into ascitic fluid, most
commonly Escherichia coli, Klebsiella spp., and streptococci. Diagnosis is clinical
and confirmed by ascitic fluid analysis with a PMN leukocyte count ≥250 cells/
microlitre. Patients may present with non-specific or no symptoms, so clinical
suspicion is essential.
1.
May present with
•	 Asymptomatic
•	 Fever
•	 Hypotension
•	 Abdominal pain
•	 Abdominal tenderness
•	 Altered mental status
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Call for help – inform MO
•	 Triage patient and assess vital signs
•	 Start empiric antibiotic therapy to cover intestinal bacteria:

–
Ceftriaxone 2g IV OD, if available

–
OR Ampicillin 1g IV q6h + Gentamicin 240mg IV OD
•	 Continue antibiotics for 5–10 days depending on clinical response
•	 Treat underlying liver disease – if HBsAg-positive, initiate Tenofovir once the
patient is clinically improving
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Assess for fever, abdominal tenderness, or changes in mental status in any
patient with known cirrhosis and ascites
•	 Perform abdominal examination for signs of peritonitis
•	 If trained and resources permit, perform diagnostic paracentesis

–
Diagnosis confirmed if PMN count ≥250 cells/microlitre
•	 Exclude secondary causes of infection (e.g. perforation)
438 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management
Empiric antibiotic therapy:
•	 Ceftriaxone 2g IV OD
•	 OR Ampicillin 1g IV q6h + Gentamicin 240mg IV OD
Supportive care:
•	 Monitor blood pressure, temperature, fluid status, and mental status
•	 Continue routine cirrhosis management, including dietary sodium restriction and
diuretics unless contraindicated
•	 If HBV positive, initiate Tenofovir once stable and link to Hepatitis Program
Prophylaxis (in discussion with MO):
Patients at increased risk of SBP (e.g. previous SBP, variceal bleeding, or ascitic fluid
protein &lt;1 g/dL) may benefit from antibiotic prophylaxis with:
•	 Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole (Septrin) 960mg PO OD
•	 OR Ciprofloxacin 500mg PO OD
5.	 Follow up
•	 Monitor temperature, vitals, abdominal tenderness, and mental status daily while
on treatment
•	 Review antibiotic response after 48–72 hours
•	 Educate patient and family on signs of recurrence (fever, confusion, worsening
abdominal pain)
•	 Ensure linkage to Hepatitis Program if HBsAg-positive
•	 Assess need for long-term prophylaxis with MO
6.	 Referral / consultation
Refer to medical officer or hospital if:
•	 Paracentesis cannot be safely performed
•	 Patient is septic, hypotensive, or deteriorating despite treatment
•	 No clinical improvement within 72 hours
•	 HBV-positive patient needing antiviral therapy or specialist liver care
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 439

440 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Hepatic encephalopathy – Adult
Recommend
•	 Hepatic encephalopathy (HE) is a spectrum of potentially reversible
neuropsychiatric abnormalities seen in patients with liver dysfunction, particularly
those with cirrhosis. It results from accumulation of toxins, such as ammonia, that
are normally cleared by the liver.
Background
Common features of hepatic encephalopathy include:
•	 Disturbance of diurnal sleep pattern (insomnia or hypersomnia)
•	 Loss of balance and falls
•	 Advanced neurologic dysfunctions such as asterixis (flapping tremor) and
hyperactive reflexes

Grading hepatic encephalopathy:
•	 Grade I: changes in behaviour, disordered sleep
•	 Grade II: lethargy, moderate confusion
•	 Grade III: marked confusion (stupor), incoherent speech, sleeping but arousable
•	 Grade IV: coma, unresponsive to pain
Precipitating factors to evaluate for include:
•	 Gastrointestinal bleeding (e.g. varices)
•	 Infection (e.g. SBP, UTI)
•	 Kidney failure
•	 Electrolyte imbalance (especially hypokalaemia)
•	 Hypoxia
•	 Metabolic abnormalities (e.g. alkalosis, hypoglycaemia)
•	 Constipation
1.
May present with
•	 Sleep disturbance
•	 Personality or behaviour changes
•	 Difficulty concentrating
•	 Confusion or disorientation
•	 Slurred speech or tremor
•	 Drowsiness or coma
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 441

2.	 Immediate management
•	 Call for help – inform MO
•	 Triage patient, determine vital signs and assess severity using grading system

–
Grade I may be managed as an outpatient

–
Grades II–IV require admission for treatment
•	 Identify and treat any precipitating factors such as sepsis, bleeding, or
dehydration
•	 Determine the underlying cause of cirrhosis (e.g. hepatitis B, alcohol)
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Assess mental status and grade severity of encephalopathy
•	 Check for signs of infection, bleeding, or constipation
•	 Review medications and fluid balance
•	 Screen for hepatitis B if not already known
•	 Examine for signs of chronic liver disease
4.	 Management
Supportive care:
•	 Provide appropriate nutritional support – no need to restrict protein intake
•	 Avoid dehydration; ensure adequate fluid intake
•	 Maintain a safe environment – implement fall precautions
•	 If agitated, consider non-pharmacological restraint options before sedation
Nutritional support:
•	 Maintain energy and protein intake
•	 Recommend small meals throughout the day
•	 Offer a late-night snack of complex carbohydrates (e.g. bananas, potatoes, nuts)
Drug therapy:
•	 Start lactulose 30–45 mL PO every 1–2 hours until 2–3 soft stools/day
•	 Once improving, reduce to 30–45 mL PO every 6–8 hours to maintain 2–3 soft
stools/day
•	 Continue lactulose for maintenance if recurrent encephalopathy occurs
•	 If sepsis is suspected (e.g. fever, suspected SBP or UTI):
•	 Start Ampicillin 1g IV q6h + Gentamicin 240 mg IV OD for 5–7 days
•	 Adjust duration based on clinical response
If HBsAg-positive:
•	 Facilitate linkage to care with Hepatitis Program
•	 Initiate antiviral therapy with Tenofovir once the patient is clinically stable
442 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

5.	 Follow up
•	 Monitor stool output and mental status daily
•	 Reassess for any recurrent precipitating factors
•	 Educate patient and family about early signs of encephalopathy and importance
of adherence to therapy
•	 Encourage long-term follow-up with Hepatitis Program for HBV-positive patients
•	 Consider long-term use of lactulose in recurrent cases
6.	 Referral / consultation
Refer to medical officer or hospital if:
•	 Encephalopathy is Grade II or above
•	 Sepsis or gastrointestinal bleeding is suspected
•	 Mental status does not improve with therapy
•	 Patient is HBsAg-positive and requires evaluation for antiviral treatment
