Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 443

Hepatitis A – Adult/Child
Recommend1
•	 Acute hepatitis A is a notifiable condition
•	 Vaccinate according to the National Immunisation Program schedule, and
advise on avoidance of risk factors (e.g. untreated water, poor hand hygiene,
unsafe food preparation)
•	 Perform contact tracing to identify and monitor exposed individuals
Background1
•	 Hepatitis A virus (HAV) is common in Kiribati
•	 HAV is transmitted by the faecal–oral route, typically from contaminated food or
water, and less commonly via sexual contact (oral–anal practices)
•	 Incubation period: 2–7 weeks (average 28–30 days)
•	 HAV infection is self-limiting, with illness usually resolving within 6 months. Does
not cause chronic disease
•	 HAV is excreted in stools 2 weeks before symptoms appear, and up to 1 week
after the onset of jaundice
•	 Two cases in a household or community setting constitute an outbreak
•	 Clinical severity varies:

–
Mild illness (1–2 weeks)

–
Severe disabling illness (lasting several months)
•	 Relapsing hepatitis A (up to one year) can occur; severity increases with age
•	 Mother-to-child transmission of HAV is not typical, but all pregnant women
should be screened and managed for hepatitis B, HIV, and syphilis, regardless of
suspected hepatitis A
May present with1,2
•	 Often asymptomatic, especially in infants and young children
•	 If symptomatic:

–
Fever, malaise, nausea, abdominal discomfort

–
Loss of appetite

–
Jaundice (yellow skin/eyes)

–
Dark urine, pale stools

–
Itchy skin (pruritis)
•	 Laboratory findings:

–
Detection of anti-hepatitis A IgM, in the absence of recent vaccination (if
available)

–
Detection of HAV by nucleic acid testing (if available)
444 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

1.
Immediate management
•	 Acute infection is self-limiting and can be managed on an outpatient-basis
•	 If in-patient care is required, it may involve supportive therapy
•	 Counselling of patient and immediate family must be provided to ensure
awareness, support and prevention of transmission
2.	 Clinical assessment1
•	 Obtain comprehensive history, including:

–
Contact with known hepatitis A case

–
Water and sanitation conditions at home or workplace

–
Travel history

–
Medications and occupation (especially food handling, healthcare, teaching)

–
Immunisation history
•	 Perform standard observations (Q-ADDS/CEWT or local early warning tools)
•	 Physical examination – look for jaundice, dehydration, abdominal tenderness
•	 Take bloods for:

–
Hepatitis A, B, and C serology

–
Liver function tests (LFTs)

–
INR
•	 For antenatal patients:

–
Conduct point-of-care screening for hepatitis B, HIV, and syphilis using
WHO-approved tests (e.g. Determine™ HBsAg2)
3.	 Management1,2
•	 Consult Medical Officer (MO) on all occasions when hepatitis A is suspected or
jaundice is present
•	 Consult urgently if INR ≥ 1.5, or if signs of liver failure (e.g. confusion, bleeding,
prolonged prothrombin time)
•	 Treatment of hepatitis A is supportive and symptomatic:

–
Hospitalisation rarely required

–
Bed rest advised if jaundiced
•	 During the infectious period (7 days after jaundice or dark urine begins, or
2 weeks after initial symptoms), advise the patient not to:

–
Donate blood

–
Drink alcohol or take paracetamol

–
Prepare or handle food for others

–
Have sex

–
Attend school, childcare, work

–
Provide personal care to others

–
Share food, drinks, cigarettes, drug-injection equipment
•	 Patient and household education:

–
Emphasise handwashing and hygiene

–
Avoid shared food preparation
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 445

•	 If pregnant and HBsAg positive:

–
Start antiviral prophylaxis at 28 weeks gestation, or earlier if clinically
indicated (see Hepatitis B section)

–
Women from outer islands should begin treatment during antenatal contact
and remain on treatment after delivery

–
Continue treatment for at least 6 weeks postpartum, to reduce risk of flares –
use is safe during breastfeeding

–
Ensure timely hepatitis B birth dose vaccine is provided – give within 24
hours of delivery
4.	 Follow up1
•	 Advise patient to return within 24 hours for clinical review
•	 Ensure Public Health notification has occurred
•	 For pregnant women, confirm linkage to antenatal follow-up and treatment
continuation postpartum if on hepatitis B therapy
5.	 Referral/consultation
•	 Consult MO for all confirmed or suspected cases
•	 Notify Public Health – hepatitis A is a notifiable disease
•	 Liaise with Public Health Unit for:

–
Contact tracing

–
Immunisation of close household contacts

–
Monitoring of antenatal screening coverage and treatment compliance in
pregnant women

–
Outbreak response in community settings
446 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Hepatitis B – Adult/Child
Recommend1
•	 Acute hepatitis B is a notifiable condition and a vaccine-preventable disease
•	 Screen all patients who test positive for an STI for hepatitis B virus (HBV)
•	 Screening for HBV, HIV, and syphilis should be offered to all antenatal mothers
during initial antenatal care (ANC) clinic
•	 Use WHO pre-qualified point-of-care rapid diagnostic tests for screening, e.g.,
Determine™ HBsAg2 test
•	 HBV-positive individuals must be referred to local clinics and linked to hepatitis
care services
•	 All neonates must be given timely HBV birth dose vaccination within 24 hours of
birth
•	 Advise not to share razors, toothbrushes, unsafe tattooing tools or any blood-
contaminated personal items
Background1,2
•	 Hepatitis B is endemic in Kiribati
•	 Acute HBV infection is often asymptomatic:

–
50–70% of adults and up to 90% of children have subclinical infection
•	 Risk of chronic infection decreases with age:

–
~90% of infants infected at birth develop chronic HBV

–
&lt;10% of adults do
•	 Up to 25% of people with chronic HBV may develop cirrhosis, liver failure, or
hepatocellular carcinoma
•	 Transmission routes:

–
Perinatal (mother-to-child) during child-birth or obstetric interventions

–
Percutaneous (e.g. injecting drug use)

–
Sexual contact
•	 Patients with acute HBV infection require follow-up HbsAg testing after 6
months
1.
May present with1,3
•	 No symptoms
•	 If present, general symptoms are:

–
Fatigue, nausea, vomiting, abdominal pain

–
Loss of appetite

–
Rash, low-grade fever

–
Jaundice (yellow skin/eyes), dark urine, pale stools
•	 Laboratory findings:

–
HBsAg positive (in a person previously negative)
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 447

2.	 Immediate management
•	 HBV is a notifiable disease and should be reported to the National Hepatitis
Program
•	 Acute infection is usually self-limiting and can be managed as an out-patient
case
•	 Counselling and education of patient and immediate family of condition,
transmission, treatment options and prevention strategies
3.	 Clinical assessment2,3
•	 History:

–
Mode of exposure: unsafe sex, injecting drugs, shared equipment, tattoos

–
Family history of liver disease or HBV

–
Vaccination history

–
In pregnant patients: gestation, antenatal care attendance
•	 Examination:

–
Vitals (Q-ADDS/CEWT)

–
Check for liver tenderness, hepatomegaly, ascites, or history of haematemesis/
melena
•	 Investigations:

–
HBsAg

–
+/- Hepatitis A, C, D serology (discuss with Hepatitis Program)

–
LFTs, INR – calculate APRI-score
•	 In antenatal patients:

–
Initiate antiviral prophylaxis at 24 weeks (or earlier if advised) for all HBsAg-
positive mothers

–
Outer island mothers should begin treatment early and remain on therapy
post-delivery

–
Continue antiviral treatment until at least 6 weeks postpartum
448 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management3
•	 Consult Medical Officer (MO) for all cases where hepatitis B is suspected or
jaundice is detected
•	 Urgent referral if INR ≥ 1.5 or signs of liver failure
•	 Treatment is supportive:

–
Bed rest if symptomatic

–
Avoid alcohol and paracetamol
•	 If HbsAg is positive:

–
Test for HBeAg and consider HBV DNA (if available)
•	 If anti-HBs negative, consider vaccination
•	 Educate patient and household:

–
Modes of transmission, safe practices, importance of hygiene

–
Do not share personal items
•	 Perform contact tracing (exposure window: up to 180 days prior)
•	 Offer HBV vaccine to unvaccinated contacts – review vaccination records for
children and offer catch-up doses
•	 For pregnant women:

–
Link to hepatitis care services (clinic)

–
Prioritize timely (&lt;24hrs) provision of hepatitis B birth dose vaccination to
newborns of HBV-positive mothers – including home births

–
Monitor treatment continuation postnatally
5.	 Follow up1
•	 Review within 24 hours
•	 Reinforce adherence, education, contact tracing, and preventative strategies
(e.g., childhood vaccinations)
•	 Ensure ongoing antenatal follow-up if patient is pregnant
6.	 Referral / consultation
•	 Consult MO as required
•	 Notify Public Health Unit / National Hepatitis Program
•	 Refer to specialist/hepatitis clinic at Tungaru Central Hospital (especially for
chronic HBV or liver failure)
•	 For antenatal patients, ensure coordination between MCH and clinical services
for PMTCT compliance
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 449

Hepatitis C – Adult/Child
Recommend1
•	 Acute hepatitis C is a notifiable condition
•	 For all patients with anti-HCV antibodies: perform HCV PCR (viral load) to
confirm active infection.
•	 Screen all at-risk patients (STI-positive, IV drug users, sex workers) for hepatitis
B, C, HIV and syphilis
•	 Include universal antenatal screening for hepatitis B, HIV, and syphilis using
WHO pre-qualified point-of-care tests in all health facilities
Background2
•	 Hepatitis C virus (HCV) is uncommon in Kiribati
•	 Acute HCV = first 6 months after infection
•	 Most cases are asymptomatic; spontaneous clearance in 20–25% of cases
•	 Causes chronic infection and should be treated with antivirals
•	 WHO-recommends treatment initiation for chronic HCV.
•	 Chronic HCV is curable
•	 ≥2 cases = outbreak3
1.
May present with3
•	 No symptoms (most cases)
•	 If present:

–
Jaundice, fatigue, anorexia, nausea

–
Vomiting, upper abdominal pain
•	 Laboratory findings:

–
Positive anti-HCV

–
Positive HCV RNA

–
Elevated ALT
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Not applicable
450 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

3.	 Clinical assessment4
•	 History:

–
IV drug use, tattooing, blood exposure, shared razors

–
Contact with HCV-positive individuals

–
Alcohol intake
•	 Observations:

–
Full vitals (Q-ADDS/CEWT)

–
Urinalysis for bilirubin/urobilinogen
•	 Examination:

–
Jaundice, hepatomegaly, RUQ tenderness
•	 Blood tests:

–
LFTs, INR, UEC – calculate APRI-score

–
HCV RNA (viral load)

–
HIV, HBV serology
•	 In pregnant women:

–
Perform routine hepatitis C screening

–
Coordinate with Hepatitis Clinic/ANC for follow-up

–
While PMTCT risk for HCV is lower than HBV, screening is essential for
maternal counselling and postnatal infant follow-up
4.	 Management2,3
•	 Consult MO for all suspected cases
•	 Urgent referral if INR ≥ 1.5 or if systemically unwell
•	 Supportive treatment only for acute phase
•	 Bed rest advised if jaundiced
•	 Educate patient and contacts:

–
Avoid sharing injecting equipment, razors, toothbrushes

–
Do not donate blood

–
Avoid paracetamol and address alcohol intake
•	 Vaccinate against hepatitis A and B – if unvaccinated
•	 Contact tracing (up to 180 days exposure) in consultation with MO or Public
Health
•	 Chronic HCV infection: requires treatment with antivirals (Sofosbuvir &
Daclatasvir) – consult MO/Hepatitis Program
•	 Pregnant women:

–
Provide education and linkage to early infant screening

–
Ensure no delay in treatment planning if chronic HCV confirmed postpartum
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 451

5.	 Follow up3
•	 Review in 24 hours, reinforce education (i.e. alcohol use, adherence)
•	 Repeat HCV RNA after 6 months to confirm clearance or chronicity
•	 Monitor LFTs and INR
•	 Annual HCV RNA testing if risk factors persist
6.	 Referral / consultation
•	 Consult MO as required
•	 Notify Public Health Unit / Hepatitis Program
•	 Refer to hepatitis specialist/clinic if chronic HCV or if treatment is indicated
•	 Coordinate care with Hepatitis services for pregnant patients and infant testing
plans
452 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Hepatitis D – Adult/Child (Kiribati Original Protocol)
Recommend
•	 All patients with advanced liver disease should be assessed for HDV
•	 All patients who are HBV-positive should be screened for HDV (Anti-HDV test)
and linked to care
•	 Patients with an acute flare of hepatitis should be considered for screening for
anti-HDV
Background
•	 Hepatitis D (delta) virus (HDV) is endemic in Kiribati
•	 HDV is an enveloped RNA virus
•	 It is an incomplete (satellite) virus that depends on Hepatitis B virus (HBV) for its
survival – causing HBV/HDV co-infection
•	 Elimination of HDV is closely associated tied with HBV elimination – via birth
dose vaccination and preventative practices
•	 Treatment for HDV exists but is currently unavailable in Kiribati
•	 All HBV/HDV co-infected patients should be offered antiviral therapy for HBV
1.
May present with
•	 Acute HDV infection of an HBV-positive patient can be asymptomatic
•	 If present:

–
Jaundice, fatigue, anorexia, nausea

–
Vomiting, upper abdominal pain
•	 Laboratory findings:

–
Positive HBsAg

–
Positive anti-HDV

–
Positive HDV RNA

–
Elevated ALT
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Not applicable
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 453

3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 History:

–
HbsAg-positive, tattooing, blood exposure, shared razors

–
Contact with HBV-positive individuals / mother
•	 Observations:

–
Full vitals (Q-ADDS/CEWT)

–
Urinalysis for bilirubin/urobilinogen
•	 Examination:

–
Jaundice, hepatomegaly, RUQ tenderness
•	 Blood tests:

–
LFTs, INR, UEC – calculate APRI-score

–
HDV RNA (viral load)

–
HIV, HBV serology
•	 In pregnant women:

–
Perform routine hepatitis B screening

–
Coordinate with Hepatitis Clinic / ANC for follow-up

–
Due to MTCT risk for HDV is believed to be lower in children, screening is
essential for maternal counselling and postnatal infant follow-up
4.	 Management
•	 Consult MO for all suspected cases
•	 Urgent referral if INR ≥ 1.5 or if systemically unwell
•	 Supportive treatment only for acute phase
•	 Bed rest advised if jaundiced
•	 Educate patient and contacts:

–
Avoid sharing injecting equipment, razors, toothbrushes

–
Do not donate blood

–
Avoid paracetamol
•	 Vaccinate against hepatitis A – if unvaccinated
•	 Contact tracing with HbsAg-screening and linkage to care
•	 HDV infection requires treatment with immunoglobulins (pegylated interferon)
and antivirals (Bulevirtide) – consult MO/Hepatitis Program
•	 Pregnant women:

–
Ensure HBV-positive pregnant mothers are treated with prophylaxis antivirals
(Tenofovir) from 24 weeks gestation onwards – see Hepatitis B

–
Provide education and linkage to early infant screening

–
Ensure all HBV-positive mothers are linked to care with Hepatitis Program
454 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

5.	 Follow up
•	 Review in 24 hours, reinforce education, adherence
•	 Monitor LFTs and INR
6.	 Referral / consultation
•	 Consult MO as required
•	 Notify Public Health Unit / Hepatitis Program
•	 Refer to Hepatitis specialist/clinic if chronic HCV or if treatment is indicated
•	 Coordinate care with Hepatitis services for pregnant patients and infant testing
plans
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 455

Obstetric and
Gynecological
Note: The following guidelines are to be supplemented by
Kiribati Obstetrics and Gynaecological: Clincial Practice Guildelines’ 2021

Pelvic Inflammatory Disease (PID)
(Kiribati Original Protocol)
Recommend
•	 PID is a potentially serious reproductive tract infection that can lead to infertility,
ectopic pregnancy, or chronic pelvic pain. Early recognition and treatment are
essential to reduce complications.
Background
•	 Pelvic Inflammatory Disease (PID) is an infection of the upper female genital
tract, including the uterus, fallopian tubes, and ovaries. It is usually caused by
ascending sexually transmitted infections (STIs), most commonly Neisseria
gonorrhoeae, Chlamydia trachomatis, and Mycoplasma genitalium.
1.
May present with
•	 Pelvic or lower abdominal pain with no other identifiable cause
•	 Cervical motion tenderness
•	 Uterine tenderness
•	 Adnexal tenderness
Additional supportive features (not required but supportive):
•	 Fever >38.3°C
•	 Abnormal cervical or vaginal discharge
•	 Elevated ESR or CRP
•	 NAAT positive for Chlamydia or Gonorrhoea
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Begin empiric treatment if clinical suspicion is high do not delay for test results
Rule out ectopic pregnancy with urine pregnancy test
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 History: sexual activity, contraception use, past STIs or PID
•	 Examination: abdominal exam, pelvic exam if trained Investigations:

–
Urine pregnancy test

–
NAAT for Chlamydia, Gonorrhoea, and M. genitalium

–
HIV and syphilis serology

–
Vaginal swab for microscopy and culture (if available)

–
CBC and CRP

–
Pelvic ultrasound if tubo-ovarian abscess suspected
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 457

4.	 Management
A. Outpatient (mild to moderate PID):
•	 Start treatment immediately:

–
Ceftriaxone 500 mg IM single dose (1 g if >150 kg body weight)
PLUS

–
Doxycycline 100 mg orally twice daily for 14 days
PLUS

–
Metronidazole 500 mg orally twice daily for 14 days
(Metronidazole covers anaerobes and should be included unless
contraindicated)
B. Inpatient (severe PID or complications): Admit if:
•	 Surgical emergency not excluded (e.g. appendicitis)
•	 Tubo-ovarian abscess
•	 Severe illness (fever, vomiting, significant pain)
•	 Pregnancy
•	 No improvement after 48–72 hours of outpatient therapy
Recommended regimen:
•	 IV Ceftriaxone 1–2 g daily
•	 PLUS
•	 Doxycycline 100 mg orally or IV every 12 hours
•	 ± Metronidazole 500 mg IV every 8 hours
Continue IV antibiotics until 24–48 hours after improvement, then complete 14 days
with oral doxycycline ± metronidazole.
5.	 Follow up
•	 Reassess clinically within 48–72 hours to confirm response to treatment
•	 Counsel patient to abstain from sex until treatment completed and partner(s)
treated
•	 Test of cure not routinely needed unless symptoms persist or M. genitalium
involved
•	 Retest for STIs in 3 months
6.	 Referral/Consultation
•	 Refer to doctor or hospital if suspected abscess, pregnancy, or no response to
treatment
7.	 Partner Notification and Treatment
•	 All sexual partners from the last 60 days should be evaluated and treated for
Chlamydia and Gonorrhoea, regardless of symptoms
•	 Advise abstinence until all partners have been treated
458 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Group B Streptococcus (GBS) Screening in
Pregnancy (Kiribati Original Protocol)
Recommend
•	 PID is a potentially serious reproductive tract infection that can lead to infertility,
ectopic pregnancy, or chronic pelvic pain. Early recognition and treatment are
essential to reduce complications.
Background
•	 Pelvic Inflammatory Disease (PID) is an infection of the upper female genital
tract, including the uterus, fallopian tubes, and ovaries. It is usually caused by
ascending sexually transmitted infections (STIs), most commonly Neisseria
gonorrhoeae, Chlamydia trachomatis, and Mycoplasma genitalium.
1.
May present with
•	 Pelvic or lower abdominal pain with no other identifiable cause
•	 Cervical motion tenderness
•	 Uterine tenderness
•	 Adnexal tenderness
Additional supportive features (not required but supportive):
•	 Fever >38.3°C
•	 Abnormal cervical or vaginal discharge
•	 Elevated ESR or CRP
•	 NAAT positive for Chlamydia or Gonorrhoea
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Begin empiric treatment if clinical suspicion is high do not delay for test results
•	 Rule out ectopic pregnancy with urine pregnancy test
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 History: sexual activity, contraception use, past STIs or PID
•	 Examination: abdominal exam, pelvic exam if trained
•	 Investigations:
•	 Urine pregnancy test
•	 NAAT for Chlamydia, Gonorrhoea, and M. genitalium
•	 HIV and syphilis serology
•	 Vaginal swab for microscopy and culture (if available)
•	 CBC and CRP
•	 Pelvic ultrasound if tubo-ovarian abscess suspected
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 459

4.	 Management
A. Outpatient (mild to moderate PID):
•	 Start treatment immediately:

–
Ceftriaxone 500 mg IM single dose (1 g if >150 kg body weight)
PLUS

–
Doxycycline 100 mg orally twice daily for 14 days
PLUS

–
Metronidazole 500 mg orally twice daily for 14 days
(Metronidazole covers anaerobes and should be included unless
contraindicated)
B. Inpatient (severe PID or complications): Admit if:
•	 Surgical emergency not excluded (e.g. appendicitis)
•	 Tubo-ovarian abscess
•	 Severe illness (fever, vomiting, significant pain)
•	 Pregnancy
•	 No improvement after 48–72 hours of outpatient therapy
Recommended regimen:
•	 IV Ceftriaxone 1–2 g daily
PLUS
•	 Doxycycline 100 mg orally or IV every 12 hours
•	 ± Metronidazole 500 mg IV every 8 hours
•	 Continue IV antibiotics until 24–48 hours after improvement, then complete 14
days with oral doxycycline ± metronidazole.
5.	 Follow up
•	 Reassess clinically within 48–72 hours to confirm response to treatment
•	 Counsel patient to abstain from sex until treatment completed and partner(s)
treated
•	 Test of cure not routinely needed unless symptoms persist or M. genitalium
involved
•	 Retest for STIs in 3 months
6.	 Referral/Consultation
•	 Refer to doctor or hospital if suspected abscess, pregnancy, or no response to
treatment
7.	 Partner Notification and Treatment
•	 All sexual partners from the last 60 days should be evaluated and treated for
Chlamydia and Gonorrhoea, regardless of symptoms
•	 Advise abstinence until all partners have been treated
460 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Cervical cancer screening adult female
(Kiribati Original Protocol)
Recommend 1–5
•	 Cervical cancer is preventable through regular screening and early treatment of
precancerous lesions
•	 Screen all eligible women aged 30–49 years, or earlier if HIV-positive
•	 Visual Inspection with Acetic Acid (VIA) is the recommended screening method
•	 HPV vaccination and safe sexual practices reduce cervical cancer risk
•	 Encourage community education and reduce stigma around screening
Background  1–5
•	 Cervical cancer is the fourth most common cancer among women globally, but
highly preventable
•	 In Kiribati, cervical cancer is a leading cause of cancer-related deaths in women
•	 Most cases are linked to persistent infection with high-risk human papillomavirus
(HPV) types
•	 Screening allows early detection of precancerous changes and timely treatment
•	 VIA is a simple, low-cost screening method suitable for use in low-resource
settings
1.
May present with
(Screening may be initiated even if asymptomatic)
•	 Woman aged 30–49 years, or sexually active woman requesting screening
•	 History of multiple sexual partners, early sexual debut, or STI
•	 HIV-positive status (start screening earlier and repeat more frequently)
•	 No prior cervical cancer screening
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Ensure privacy and informed consent
•	 Offer VIA screening if trained personnel and supplies are available
•	 Refer if VIA not available or provider not trained
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Confirm age and sexual history
•	 Ask about HIV status (if known), previous screening, and HPV vaccination
•	 Assess for symptoms of invasive cancer (e.g. abnormal bleeding, discharge)
•	 Conduct a pelvic exam and VIA (if trained and available)
•	 Document findings on screening record
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 461

4.	 Management
If VIA negative:
•	 Reassure patient
•	 Advise next VIA in 3–5 years (or sooner if HIV-positive)
•	 Provide HPV vaccination advice if eligible
If VIA positive (precancerous lesion suspected):
•	 Offer cryotherapy (if available and eligible)
•	 Refer to gynaecology if not eligible for cryotherapy or no cryotherapy available
•	 Counsel on follow-up and possible further treatment
If invasive cancer suspected:
•	 Refer urgently to gynaecology or surgical/oncology care
•	 Provide emotional support and ensure follow-up
5.	 Follow up
•	 VIA-negative: repeat screening in 3–5 years (or every 3 years if HIV-positive)
•	 VIA-positive and treated: follow-up in 1 year
•	 VIA-positive and referred: ensure referral completed and tracked
•	 Monitor program performance and coverage rates
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 Refer to:
•	 Gynaecology or surgical specialist for:

–
Suspected invasive cancer

–
VIA-positive and not suitable for cryotherapy

–
Recurrence or treatment failure

–
HIV clinic if not already in care
•	 MHMS to advise on referral pathways and eligibility for public sector HPV
vaccination programs
462 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Abnormal Uterine Bleeding (AUB) adult
female (Kiribati Original Protocol)
Recommend  1–5
•	 AUB is bleeding from the uterus that is irregular in volume, duration, or timing
and not related to pregnancy
•	 Identify and manage life-threatening bleeding promptly
•	 Consider pregnancy, infection, fibroids, malignancy, and hormonal causes
•	 Take a thorough history and conduct a pelvic exam
•	 Refer persistent, heavy, or unexplained bleeding
Background  1–5
•	 AUB may be caused by pregnancy-related conditions, hormonal imbalances,
uterine fibroids, infections, or malignancy
•	 Women may present with heavy, prolonged, frequent, or irregular periods, or
postmenopausal bleeding
•	 In low-resource settings, early identification and referral are key to preventing
complications such as anaemia or undiagnosed cancer
•	 PALM-COEIN is a helpful classification for causes of AUB:

–
PALM (structural): Polyp, Adenomyosis, Leiomyoma (fibroid), Malignancy

–
COEIN (non-structural): Coagulopathy, Ovulatory dysfunction, Endometrial,
Iatrogenic, Not yet classified
1.
May present with
•	 Heavy menstrual bleeding (soaking >1 pad/hour)
•	 Bleeding between periods or after sex
•	 Bleeding after menopause
•	 Irregular or prolonged periods
•	 Dizziness, fatigue, signs of anaemia
•	 History of miscarriage, pregnancy, or contraceptive use
•	 Vaginal discharge, fever, or pain (if infection)
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Assess and manage heavy bleeding and haemodynamic stability
•	 Insert IV line, check vital signs, consider fluid resuscitation if signs of shock
•	 Rule out pregnancy with urine pregnancy test
•	 Refer urgently if:

–
Heavy bleeding and unstable vital signs

–
Suspected miscarriage or ectopic pregnancy

–
Postmenopausal bleeding

–
Bleeding with uterine mass or suspected malignancy
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 463

3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Ask about:

–
Menstrual history (cycle, duration, flow, pain)

–
Last menstrual period (LMP), pregnancy risk

–
Contraceptive use or recent childbirth/miscarriage

–
Sexual history and STI symptoms

–
Past gynaecological conditions or procedures
•	 Perform physical and pelvic exam if trained:

–
Check for uterine enlargement, cervical lesions, discharge
•	 Do basic tests if available:

–
Urine pregnancy test

–
Haemoglobin (if possible)

–
STI screening if indicated
4.	 Management
If haemodynamically stable and not pregnant:
•	 Start oral iron if anaemic
•	 If due to hormonal imbalance:

–
Consider Norethisterone 5 mg orally twice daily for 10–14 days (if available)
•	 If infection suspected:

–
Treat for PID or cervicitis per STI protocol
•	 Advise keeping a menstrual diary
•	 Reassess in 2–4 weeks
If bleeding persists, is heavy, or cause is unclear:
•	 Refer for gynaecological assessment and ultrasound if available
•	 Avoid NSAIDs in heavy bleeding unless known to be helpful and anaemia is
excluded
5.	 Follow up
•	 Review in 2–4 weeks
•	 Monitor bleeding pattern and haemoglobin if available
•	 If improved, plan ongoing management (e.g. contraception, iron, repeat review)
•	 If no improvement or bleeding worsens, refer
464 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

6.	 Referral/consultation
Refer to:
•	 Gynaecology or hospital for:

–
Postmenopausal bleeding

–
No improvement with initial management

–
Heavy bleeding with anaemia

–
Suspicion of cancer (e.g. cervical mass, persistent intermenstrual bleeding)
•	 MHMS to advise on referral locations and follow-up pathway
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 465

Vaginal itchiness (Kiribati Original Protocol)
Recommend
•	 Vaginal itchiness is a common complaint and may be caused by infectious,
irritant, or allergic conditions. Accurate diagnosis is important to ensure
appropriate treatment and avoid unnecessary antibiotics.
•	 If suspicious cervical or vulval lesions are seen, refer for visual inspection with
acetic acid (VIA), colposcopy, or treatment such as LLETZ where appropriate.
Background
•	 Vaginal itchiness may be caused by infections such as candidiasis, bacterial
vaginosis (BV), or trichomoniasis, as well as irritant contact dermatitis, atrophic
vaginitis (in post-menopausal women), or rarely cervical or vulval intraepithelial
neoplasia. Diagnosis is largely clinical in low-resource settings, supported by pH
testing and microscopy where available.
1.
May present with
•	 Vaginal or vulval itching, burning, irritation
•	 Vaginal discharge (may be thin, thick, white, grey, green, or yellow)
•	 Odour (especially fishy, suggestive of BV)
•	 Dysuria or superficial dyspareunia
•	 Redness, swelling, or visible lesions on vulva or cervix
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Assess and reassure
•	 Provide a private space for examination
•	 Always consider and exclude pregnancy if relevant
•	 Examine vulva and vagina with clean gloves and light source
•	 Look for:

–
Discharge characteristics (colour, consistency, odour)

–
Vulval or vaginal redness or swelling

–
Ulcers, sores, or white plaques

–
Cervical appearance (contact bleeding, lesions, ectopy)
466 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Ask about:

–
Onset, duration, and severity of itching

–
Changes in discharge or odour

–
Sexual history and contraception

–
Recent antibiotic use or new products (e.g. soaps, sprays)

–
Menstrual status or menopause
•	 Consider vaginal pH testing if available:

–
pH >4.5 suggests BV or trichomoniasis

–
pH ≤4.5 suggests candidiasis
Common Causes and Features
CONDITION
TYPICAL
SYMPTOMS
DISCHARGE
NOTES
Candidiasis
Intense itching,
redness, soreness
Thick, white,
“cottage cheese”
Normal pH; not
an STI
Bacterial vaginosis
Mild itch, strong
fishy odour
Thin, grey/white,
homogeneous
pH >4.5, clue cells
on microscopy
Trichomoniasis
Itch, irritation,
frothy discharge,
odour
Green/yellow,
frothy
Often STI, treat
both partners
Irritant dermatitis
External itching,
burning
None or clear
History of new
soaps, pads,
douching
Atrophic vaginitis
Postmenopausal
dryness, itch, pain
Scant, clear or
bloody
Consider topical
oestrogen for
symptom relief
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 467

4.	 Management
Empiric treatment based on clinical suspicion if diagnostic testing not available.
•	 Suspected Candidiasis (no odour, thick white discharge, intense itching):

–
Clotrimazole pessary 500 mg single dose at night
OR

–
Miconazole 1200 mg single dose vaginal suppository
OR

–
Fluconazole 150 mg oral single dose (if available)
•	 Suspected Bacterial Vaginosis (fishy odour, greyish discharge, mild irritation):

–
Metronidazole 400 mg orally twice daily for 7 days
OR

–
Metronidazole 2 g orally as a single dose (less preferred if recurrent)
•	 Suspected Trichomoniasis (frothy discharge, STI risk):

–
Metronidazole 2 g orally single dose

–
Treat sexual partner(s) as well

–
Advise to abstain from sex until both partners treated
•	 Irritant dermatitis:

–
Avoid irritants (soap, douches, sprays)

–
Apply hydrocortisone cream 1% thinly to affected area twice daily for up to 7
days
•	 Atrophic vaginitis (postmenopausal women):

–
Consider topical oestrogen pessary (e.g. estriol 0.5 mg vaginal pessary at
night for 2–4 weeks then twice weekly) if recurrent or persistent symptoms

–
Discuss with MO before prescribing
•	 If unclear diagnosis:

–
Consider combined treatment:

–
Metronidazole 400 mg bd x 7 days

–
Clotrimazole 500 mg pessary single dose

–
Review response in 7 days
5.	 Follow up
•	 Review in 7 days if symptoms persist
•	 Reinforce STI prevention and safe sex practices
•	 Advise not to douche or use irritants in the genital area
•	 Counsel on recurrence prevention for candidiasis (avoid tight clothing, stay dry)
468 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

6.	 Referral/Consultation
•	 Case discussed for management with medical assistant first
•	 Medical assistant needs to discuss further with the consultant oncall if unable
to manage the case then to refer if not manageable on the outer islands for all
gynae patients
•	 Refer if:

–
Ulcers, lesions, or cervical abnormalities are seen

–
Persistent symptoms despite treatment

–
Suspicion of cervical or vulval neoplasia

–
Postmenopausal women with bleeding or persistent irritation
Visual inspection with acetic acid (VIA)
•	 VIA involves applying 3–5% acetic acid to the cervix and observing for
acetowhite changes that may indicate cervical intraepithelial neoplasia
•	 If suspicious lesions seen, refer for colposcopy or LLETZ treatment if available
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 469

Cord Presentation and Cord Prolapse
(Kiribati Protocol)
Cord presentation and cord prolapse
Background
•	 Umbilical cord complications occur when the cord lies below or alongside
the presenting part. This can lead to compression of the cord, reducing or
stopping blood flow to the fetus. Cord prolapse, in particular, is a life-threatening
emergency and requires immediate intervention to prevent fetal hypoxia and
death. These events most commonly occur after rupture of membranes and are
more likely when the presenting part is not well engaged.
Recommended:
•	 Umbilical cord complications are obstetric emergencies due to risk of fetal
hypoxia and death from cord compression. Immediate action is required to
relieve pressure on the cord and arrange urgent delivery.
Definition (important clinical distinctions)
•	 Overt cord presentation – the umbilical cord lies below the presenting part but
the membranes are still intact
•	 Occult cord presentation (occult prolapse) – the cord lies alongside the
presenting part and is not visible or felt, but may cause fetal distress
•	 Cord prolapse – the cord passes through the cervix below the presenting part
after rupture of membranes and may be felt or seen in the vagina or outside the
introitus

–
Cord prolapse is an immediate emergency requiring urgent delivery
Figure: Cord presentation and prolapse types (occult, overt, complete prolapse)
Source: PSRH, 2015. The Pacific Emergency Maternal and Newborn Training Manual.
470 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

1.
May present with
•	 Cord felt during vaginal examination
•	 Cord visible at introitus or protruding from vagina
•	 Sudden fetal distress (abnormal or bradycardic fetal heart rate)
•	 Change after rupture of membranes
•	 In occult cases: fetal distress without visible cord
•	 Reduced or absent fetal movements (late sign)
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Call for help immediately
•	 Always call the hospital and discuss the case with the obstetrician immediately
•	 Explain the situation clearly to the woman and obtain verbal consent where
possible
•	 Position the woman to relieve cord compression:

–
Knee-chest position (preferred if feasible)

–
OR left lateral (exaggerated Sims position) with pillows under hips

Figure 2: Knee-chest position and Sims (left lateral) position
•	 Insert a gloved hand into the vagina and:

–
Gently lift the presenting part off the cord

–
Maintain this position continuously until delivery or handover
•	 Do NOT handle, pull, or compress the cord
•	 If cord is outside the vagina:

–
Cover with sterile gauze soaked in warm saline

–
Prevent drying and vasospasm
•	 Administer oxygen if available
•	 Stop oxytocin infusion immediately if running
•	 Prepare for urgent delivery or transfer
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 471

3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Check fetal heart rate (continuous if possible)
•	 Assess if cord is pulsating (indicates fetal viability)
•	 Confirm rupture of membranes
•	 Assess cervical dilation if skilled to do so
•	 Estimate time to delivery vs transfer
•	 Assess maternal condition
4.	 Management
•	 Maintain continuous manual elevation of presenting part
•	 Keep woman in position that reduces cord compression
•	 Minimise handling and vaginal examinations
•	 If delivery is imminent:

–
Prepare for assisted vaginal delivery
•	 If delivery is NOT imminent:

–
Arrange urgent transfer to hospital

–
Ensure continuous decompression of cord during transfer
•	 If delay expected:

–
Insert urinary catheter

–
Fill bladder with 500–700 mL normal saline to elevate presenting part
•	 Continue oxygen and monitoring
5.	 Follow up
•	 Continuous monitoring of fetal condition
•	 Monitor maternal vital signs
•	 Document:

–
Time of diagnosis

–
Fetal heart rate

–
Interventions performed

–
Time of referral
6.	 Referral and consultation
•	 This is an obstetric emergency – immediate referral required
•	 Always contact hospital and obstetrician immediately
•	 Arrange urgent transport
•	 Maintain all interventions during transfer
•	 Ensure skilled escort if available
472 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Shoulder Dystocia (Kiribati Protocol)
Background
•	 Shoulder dystocia occurs when, after delivery of the fetal head, one or both
shoulders become impacted against the maternal pelvis, preventing delivery.
This leads to compression of the umbilical cord and interruption of fetal oxygen
supply. It is an unpredictable emergency that can occur in any birth but is
more common in large babies and in mothers with certain risk factors. Rapid
recognition and coordinated manoeuvres are essential to reduce the risk of fetal
injury, including hypoxia, nerve injury, and death.
Recommended:
•	 Shoulder dystocia is an obstetric emergency where the shoulders fail to deliver
after the head has been born. This can rapidly lead to fetal hypoxia and injury.
Immediate, coordinated action is required to deliver the baby safely.
1.
May present with
•	 Head delivers but shoulders do not follow
•	 “Turtle sign” – retraction of the fetal head against the perineum
•	 Difficulty delivering the shoulders
•	 Prolonged second stage of labour
•	 Failure of normal traction to deliver shoulders
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Call for help immediately
•	 Call doctor/obstetrician urgently if available
•	 Explain situation to the woman
•	 DO NOT pull on the fetal head (risk of severe injury)
•	 Position woman immediately for McRoberts manoeuvre:

–
Move woman to edge of bed

–
Flex both legs tightly onto abdomen

Figure: McRoberts position Source: PSRH, 2015. The Pacific Emergency Maternal and Newborn
Training Manual.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 473

•	 Apply suprapubic pressure:

–
Apply firm downward pressure just above the pubic bone

–
Aim to rotate the baby’s shoulder under the symphysis pubis

Figure: Suprapubic pressure (Figure 9.3.5) Source: PSRH, 2015. The Pacific Emergency Maternal
and Newborn Training Manual.
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Confirm diagnosis (head delivered, shoulders stuck)
•	 Note time of head delivery
•	 Assess fetal condition
•	 Identify risk factors if known (macrosomia, diabetes, prolonged labour)
4.	 Management
Follow HELPER mnemonic in sequence:
•	 H – Help

–
Call for assistance immediately
•	 E – Episiotomy

–
Consider if additional space required
•	 L – Legs (McRoberts manoeuvre)

–
Hyperflex hips tightly to abdomen

–
Two assistants may be needed
•	 P – Suprapubic pressure

–
Third assistant applies pressure above pubic bone

–
Do NOT apply fundal pressure
•	 E – Enter manoeuvres (internal rotation)

–
If trained, insert hand into vagina

–
Rotate shoulders into oblique diameter
474 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

•	 R – Remove posterior arm

–
Enter vagina posteriorly

–
Grasp posterior arm at axilla

–
Sweep arm across chest and deliver
•	 R – Roll the woman

–
Turn onto all fours (if possible)
Additional manoeuvres (if needed)
•	 Apply gentle lateral traction only

–
Do NOT jerk or overstretch the neck
•	 If bilateral shoulder dystocia:

–
Push shoulder toward fetal chest

–
Rotate into oblique diameter

–
Deliver as for unilateral dystocia
•	 Alternative:

–
Rotate baby 180 degrees to bring posterior shoulder forward
5.	 Follow up
•	 Assess newborn immediately:

–
Breathing, tone, injuries
•	 Monitor mother closely:

–
Postpartum haemorrhage (common)

–
Perineal trauma
•	 Insert IV line if required
•	 Monitor uterus:

–
Every 15 min for first 2 hours

–
Then hourly for 4 hours
•	 Check temperature 6-hourly for 2–3 days
•	 Document:

–
Time of head delivery

–
Time of full delivery

–
Manoeuvres used
•	 Debrief the woman after delivery

–
Explain what happened

–
Discuss potential complications
6.	 Referral and consultation
•	 Call doctor/obstetrician immediately where available
•	 Prepare for neonatal resuscitation
•	 Refer mother and baby if complications occur
•	 Document and report incident
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 475

Uterine Inversion (Kiribati Protocol)
Acute uterine inversion
Recommended:
•	 Acute uterine inversion is a life-threatening obstetric emergency where the
uterus turns inside out, usually following delivery of the baby. It may occur due to
inappropriate cord traction on a fundal placenta, particularly in a relaxed (flaccid)
uterus without adequate counter-pressure, or it may occur spontaneously.
Background:
•	 Uterine inversion causes severe haemorrhage and profound shock, which is often
greater than expected for the visible blood loss. This is due to both haemorrhage
and neurogenic shock from traction on pelvic structures. Immediate recognition
and rapid correction are essential to restore uterine anatomy, control bleeding,
and prevent maternal death.
1.
May present with
•	 Severe postpartum bleeding
•	 Sudden onset of shock (pallor, hypotension, tachycardia)
•	 Collapse or altered level of consciousness
•	 Severe lower abdominal or pelvic pain
•	 Uterus not palpable in the abdomen
•	 Mass felt or seen in the vagina or protruding from the introitus
•	 Placenta may still be attached
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Call for help immediately
•	 Call hospital and obstetrician urgently
•	 Begin resuscitation immediately (ABC approach):

–
Ensure airway is clear

–
Support breathing

–
Give oxygen if available

–
Establish large-bore IV access

–
Start rapid IV fluid resuscitation
•	 Order blood for transfusion if haemorrhage present
•	 DO NOT remove placenta if still attached
•	 Stop oxytocin infusion immediately (if running)
•	 Prepare for immediate manual reduction
476 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Assess vital signs (shock severity)
•	 Estimate blood loss
•	 Palpate abdomen (uterus absent from normal position)
•	 Identify inverted uterus (vaginal or external mass)
•	 Assess whether placenta is attached
•	 Monitor level of consciousness
4.	 Management
4.1 Manual reduction (FIRST-LINE – perform immediately)
•	 Wear sterile gloves
•	 Place the flat palm of the hand against the inverted uterine fundus
•	 Push the uterus upward through the cervix toward its normal anatomical position
•	 Use the other hand externally to support the uterus if required
•	 Apply steady, continuous upward pressure (do not use forceful or jerking
movements)
Figure: Manual reduction of inverted uterus
•	 Maintain pressure continuously for several minutes
•	 Reduction usually occurs with sustained upward pressure
Figure: Complete reduction
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 477

4.2 After successful manual reduction
•	 Start Oxytocin infusion:

–
40 units in 1 L normal saline

–
Run at approximately 250 mL/hour
•	 Do NOT remove hand from the uterus until a firm uterine contraction is felt
•	 Continue IV fluids
•	 Deliver placenta only when signs of separation are present
•	 If placenta does not deliver spontaneously:

–
Refer for removal in theatre
4.3 If manual reduction fails (SECOND-LINE MANAGEMENT)
•	 Consult obstetrician immediately
•	 Attempt hydrostatic reduction (if trained and equipment available)
Hydrostatic reduction technique
•	 Place the woman in deep Trendelenburg position

–
Lower her head approximately 0.5 metres below
the level of the perineum
•	 Infuse 500–1000 mL of warm normal saline into the
posterior vaginal fornix:

–
Use IV administration set

–
Hold fluid bag 1–2 metres above the patient
•	 Ensure assistant firmly seals the vaginal orifice to
maintain pressure
Figure: Position for hydrostatic reduction / saline infusion setup
Figure: Hand blocking vaginal orifice
•	 The fluid distends the posterior fornix and cervix
•	 This increases the circumference of the cervical ring
•	 This relieves constriction and allows the uterus to return to normal position
478 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.4 If hydrostatic reduction successful
•	 Proceed as per post-reduction management above
•	 Continue monitoring closely
4.5 If reduction unsuccessful
•	 This is a surgical emergency
•	 Requires urgent laparotomy
•	 Refer immediately to hospital
•	 Alert obstetrician urgently
5.	 Follow up
•	 Monitor vital signs closely (shock, bleeding)
•	 Monitor for postpartum haemorrhage
•	 Observe for reinversion
•	 Provide:

–
Antibiotics (if available)

–
Analgesia
•	 Continue uterine monitoring:

–
Frequent checks for tone and bleeding
•	 Document:

–
Time of inversion

–
Time of reduction

–
All interventions

–
Patient response
6.	 Referral and consultation
•	 This is a life-threatening emergency – urgent referral required in ALL cases
•	 Contact hospital and obstetrician immediately
•	 Continue resuscitation during transfer
•	 Ensure skilled escort if available
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 479

Genitourinary emergencies

Renal colic - Adult
Background1
•	 Pain can be caused by kidney stone passage or an obstruction in the urinary tract
1.
May present with2,3
•	 Severe 1 sided flank pain radiating to the groin:

–
usually episodic (colicky), typically lasting 20–60 minutes + may not
completely resolve before next wave4

–
very distressed and unable to find a comfortable position
•	 Nausea + vomiting, sweating, looks unwell
•	 ± haematuria
2.	 Immediate management5
•	 Do vital signs
•	 Give oral ibuprofen + paracetamol or ± antiemetic. See Acute pain, Nausea and
vomiting
•	 If severe pain, may need IV morphine or diclofenac injection with buscopan until
above works
3.	 Clinical assessment5
•	 See Abdominal pain
•	 Get history, including:

–
nausea + vomiting, fever

–
urinary symptoms eg dysuria, frequency, urgency

–
past history of kidney stones, previous episodes, renal impairment
•	 Do physical examination, including:

–
Hydration assessment - adult

–
urinalysis to confirm haematuria + MSU for MCS if nitrites or leucocytes.
Note: no haematuria does not exclude renal colic

–
pregnancy test if reproductive age
4.	 Management5
•	 Contact MO urgently if:

–
a pulsatile abdominal mass and "BP - a ruptured AAA can mimic renal colic

–
fever, pyuria (eg cloudy urine) or significant tenderness - suggests infected
obstructed kidney
•	 Main aim of treatment is analgesia + IV fluids if significantly dehydrated and to
start IV antibiotic (check antibiotic guideline for urinary tract infection).
Note: most stones pass spontaneously &lt; 1 month3
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 481

•	 Consult MO, who may advise:

–
retrieval

–
IV fluids, bloods/i-STAT - check kidney function/not going into renal failure

–
or consider sending home if:
	›
afebrile, passing urine, able to tolerate oral fluids
	›
adequate pain relief with oral analgesia
	›
and with follow up at next MO clinic
•	 If pain persist and patient is showing signs of Obstructed uropathy – need urgent
referral

–
and with follow up at next MA clinic
5.	 Follow up
•	 If not retrieved, advise to:

–
be reviewed if develops fever, vomiting or pain returns - contact MO

–
see MO at next clinic ± urology referral
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 As above
482 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Acute retention of urine - Adult
Background
•	 Predominantly affects men. Most common cause is obstruction eg enlarged
prostate. Can also be caused from infection, constipation, inflammation or injury1
1.
May present with1,2
•	 Inability to urinate or empty bladder effectively ± suprapubic pain, bloating,
urgency, distress
2.	 Immediate management1-3
•	 Do vital signs
•	 Screen for Sepsis
•	 Offer analgesia. See Acute pain
3.	 Clinical assessment1-3
•	 See Abdominal pain
•	 Also ask about:

–
worsening urinary symptoms eg nocturia, frequency, urgency, weak stream,
dribbling

–
systemic symptoms eg T ≥ 38, chills, sweats

–
previous episodes, prostate or bladder problems

–
prolapse of the bladder, rectum or uterus - in females

–
recent pelvic trauma, STI, IDC

–
nausea, vomiting, constipation

–
medications eg anticholinergic, antidepressants

–
recent weight loss
•	 Do physical examination, including:

–
abdomen for distension and palpable bladder

–
check for swollen lymph nodes

–
urinalysis if possible + MSU for MCS or collect from IDC if advised to insert

–
bladder scan if available
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 483

4.	 Management1,3
•	 Contact MO/Surgeon, who may advise:

–
insert IDC:
	›
if likely difficult catheterisation, urgent retrieval for urological review/
suprapubic catheter
	›
DO NOT use force to push IDC through the obstructed urethra

–
If it is still can’t and patient is really in distress – consult surgeon: Need
temporary urine aspiration or cystostomy ( need guidance from the surgeon)

–
Collect urine for urine test – dipstick urine test

–
Start antibiotic – Ampicillin and Gentamycin
•	 Monitor - urine output, for haematuria, transient hypotension, and post-
obstructive diuresis
5.	 Follow up
•	 If not retrieved, always close monitor and update the surgeon concerned.
•	 Advise to see next MO clinic
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 As above
484 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Testicular/scrotal pain - Adult/Child
Testicular torsion
Recommend1
•	 Testicular torsion is an emergency. Surgery is required ≤ 6 hours to avoid
permanent harm, even if the pain has been present > 6 hours
1.
May present with1,2
•	 Pain in the testes/scrotum ± swelling/lump, nausea + vomiting
•	 Abdominal pain only
•	 Note: if recent testicular injury assume testicular torsion until proven otherwise
2.	 Immediate management1
•	 Rapidly assess against table below:

–
the presence or absence of a single sign cannot exclude testicular torsion +

–
keep in mind patient may be reluctant to volunteer symptoms due to
embarrassment and reluctance to be examined3
•	 Contact MO urgently in all cases of testicular/scrotal pain
•	 Offer analgesia ± antiemetic. See Acute pain, Nausea and vomiting
•	 Do vital signs
Assessment of testicular pain1,2
Testicular torsion
Epididymo-orchitis (EDO)
Onset and
location
•	 Sudden onset of severe
unilateral scrotal pain + pain
at rest
•	 May be gradual, mainly in the
iliac fossa ± history of minor
trauma
•	 Intermittent testicular pain -
can result from intermittent
torsion/spontaneous
detorsion
•	 Gradual onset of pain and
swelling over a few days
•	 Usually 1 side
Other
symptoms
•	 Nausea, vomiting, tachycardia,
dizziness ± fever1
•	 Dysuria, frequency, smelly
urine
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 485

Testicular torsion
Epididymo-orchitis (EDO)
Examination
•	 Absent cremasteric reflex*
•	 Testis - abnormal position,
horizontal lie on standing and
high riding
•	 Scrotal skin changes - red or
darkening
•	 Tender to palpate globally
•	 Thickened spermatic cord
•	 Intact cremasteric reflex*
•	 Red, hot, swollen testis in
normal position
•	 Tender epididymis (tubular
structure found at back of
testes, running in a sagittal
plane)
Also check
•	 Urinalysis - leucocytes does
not exclude testicular torsion
•	 Lump in groin of child with
acute abdominal or inguinal
pain can be torsion of
undescended testis but may
be mistaken for swollen lymph
node or abscess
•	 Urinalysis - NAD does not
exclude EDO
•	 Risk factors eg recent
urethral surgery, history of
viral infection eg mumps;
sexually active4
*Cremasteric reflex - pinch or stroke the skin of the upper thigh. The testis on the
same side should elevate via contraction of the muscle
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 See Immediate management
4.	 Management5
•	 Contact MO urgently in all cases who will arrange urgent retrieval + referral to
surgical team:

–
even if the pain has been present > 6 hours
•	 Monitor closely:

–
vital signs + pain

–
re-examine scrotum if patient deteriorates, check for any changes3
•	 Keep nil by mouth
•	 Need urgent doppler scan of the scrotum – cannot be done at the outer island
service setting
•	 Consider Child protection, if scrotal trauma/bruising, especially in infants or
where the cause is unclear2
•	 Need urgent referral so discuss with surgent for urgent advice and opinion.
5.	 Follow up
•	 As per MO/surgical team
6.	 Referral/consultation
486 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Urinary Tract Infection (UTI) - Adult
Cystitis, pyelonephritis
Background
•	 The safety and efficacy of urinary alkalisers eg Ural®, Citravescent® is unknown1
1.
May present with
Cystitis2-4
•	 Urinary symptoms - frequency, urgency, dysuria, haematuria
•	 Low abdominal pain
•	 Nitrites ± leucocytes on urinalysis
Pyelonephritis4
•	 T ≥ 38, flank pain, nausea, vomiting
•	 ± urinary symptoms
2.	 Immediate management
•	 Consider Ectopic pregnancy, in sexually active females with low abdominal pain
•	 Do vital signs
•	 Screen for Sepsis
3.	 Clinical assessment3,4
•	 Ask about:

–
past episode(s) of UTI and STI:
	›
when, treatment, effectiveness

–
kidney stones, prostate problems, urinary tract abnormalities

–
vaginal discharge
•	 Do:

–
urinalysis

–
MSU for MCS if:

–
pregnancy test if female of reproductive age

–
check for any suprapubic, loin tenderness

–
if sexually active offer STI/BBV tests
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 487

4.	 Management
•  If pregnant see UTI in pregnancy
Pyelonephritis4
•	 Contact MO promptly who may advise:

–
IV gentamicin 4-5mg/kg od + ampicillin 2g IV q6h
	›
check previous urine pathology results to ensure no resistance to
antibiotics recommended
	›
may change to oral cotrimoxazole once clinically improving, to complete
total antibiotic course of 14 days.
Cystitis
•	 Diagnosis requires the presence of symptoms (+ve urine culture alone does not
require antibiotics)
•	 Offer paracetamol or ibuprofen. See Acute pain
•	 Advise to drink enough fluids so not thirsty, aim for 6–8 glasses  of water/day3
•	 Male:2

–
contact MO

–
UTIs are uncommon - dysuria in younger males is usually caused by an STI
•	 Female:

–
if low abdominal pain without urine symptoms consider PID

–
if nitrites or leucocytes on urinalysis AND has urinary symptoms give empirical
antibiotics.
•	 Antibiotics if indicated
•	 cotrimoxazole OR nitrofurantoin. If contraindicated, give cefalexin:

–
note: give nitrofurantoin if treated ≤ 3 months ago with cotrimoxazole2

–
check previous urine samples to ensure no resistance to antibiotics
recommended. If resistance, contact MO
COTRIMOXAZOLE
L3
FORM
STRENGTH
ROUTE
DOSE
DURATION
Tablet
480 mg
Oral
960mg BD
Female 3 days
Male 7 days
Offer CMI: Take at night to maximise urinary concentration. May cause fever, itch,
rash or nausea
Note: If renal impairment or on an ACEI, seek MO advice. Elderly may be more
susceptible to adverse effects eg hyperkalaemia
Pregnancy: Avoid in 1st trimester
Contraindication: Megaloblastic anaemia
Management of associated emergency: Consult MO. See Anaphylaxis2,6
488 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

NITROFURANTOIN
L3
FORM
STRENGTH
ROUTE
DOSE
DURATION
Capsule
50 mg
Oral
100 mg
qid
Female 5 days
Offer CMI: Take with food or milk to reduce nausea + improve absorption. May
cause nausea, vomiting, headache, drowsiness or dizziness. Report if develop
difficulty breathing, cough or numbness or tingling. May turn urine a brownish
colour. Do not use with urinary alkalisers (eg Ural®, Citravescent®) as they reduce
the antimicrobial effect
Management of associated emergency: Consult MO. See Anaphylaxis2,7
CEFALEXIN
L3
FORM
STRENGTH
ROUTE
DOSE
DURATION
Capsule
250 mg
Oral
500 mg
bd
Female 5 days
Offer CMI: May  cause rash, diarrhoea, nausea, vomiting, dizziness, headache or
thrush
Note: If renal impairment seek MO advice
Contraindication: Severe or immediate allergic reaction to a cephalosporin or a
penicillin. Be aware of cross-reactivity between penicillins,  cephalosporins and
carbapenems
Management of associated emergency: Consult MO. See Anaphylaxis2,8
5.	 Follow up2
•	 Advise to be reviewed in 2–3 days if still has symptoms, or sooner if concerned:

–
consult MO if symptoms persist or worsen
6.	 Referral/consultation
•	 As above
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 489

Mental health

Acute severe behavioral disturbance (ASBD)
1.
May present with
•	 Violent behaviour or extreme agitation
•	 Self-destructive, physically or verbally aggressive or threatening behaviour
•	 Possession of a weapon with intent to use
•	 Bizarre, disoriented behaviour:
•	 Hallucinations:

–
Delusions:

–
Withdrawn e.g refusing to talk

–
Current suicide attempt, or:

–
Situational crisis

–
Family member seeking help for any of the above.
2.	 Immediate management
•	 DRSABCD
•	 If person has survived an attempted suicide or a self-harm event, manage
accordingly:

–
E.g, hanging see Traumatic injuries, spinal injuries.
•	 Consider injuries consistent with self-harm attempt
Stay safe:
•	 Never approach a patient who has a weapon.
•	 If needed take protective measures e.g lock yourself in your clinic, leave your
facility
•	 Consider risks in the immediate environment e.g access to knives, scissors, IV
poles
•	 Note exits for escape
•	 Remove other people and bystanders
•	 Assess in an open space
•	 Minimise distractions and give full attention to the patient
•	 Use a calm, confident manner, avoid sudden or threatening gestures
•	 Avoid prolonged eye contact, and do not confront, corner or stand over the
patient
•	 Be familiar with duress alarms
 Then
•	 Call police if concerned about safety
•	 Call Mental Health Doctor / DPNO
•	 Ensure at least 2 staff are always on hand
•	 Always remain with patient unless your safety is at risk
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 491

If required attempt de-escalation
•	 Listen. Allow patient to speak without interruption
•	 Be empathic, non-judgemental and respectful
•	 Monitor changes in mood or composure that may lead to aggression
•	 Continually show respect and empathy for patient
•	 Identify any patient needs that have not been met
•	 Use a slow, clear and steady voice, and do not raise your voice
•	 If the patient raises their voice, pause and listen to the patient vent their
frustrations
•	 For children, consider removing people who may be the source of violent or
aggressive behaviour
•	 If restraints is to be used, it should be undertaken in accordance with the KSPEC
Training guidelines and the Mental Health Act 2022.
Never undertake physical restraint
•	 In Outer Islands clinics and health centres lack the resources to undertake this
procedure safety
•	 As a matter of urgency staff are to:

–
Call the police

–
Protect themselves and others – retreat from the situation
For non-consenting or uncooperative patient
•	 Forms are required by law before examination or treatment
•	 If brought in by police or hospital vehicle/ambulance (Mental Health – ACT,
POLICY.SOPS)

–
In South Tarawa,
	›
For a new case, police will bring patient to the Emergency Department for
initial assessment and examination of the patient. If ED Dr finds that the
patient has a mental disorder, patient will be referred to the Mental Health
Doctor for involuntary admission at the Meeria ward.
	›
For a relapse case, police will bring patient to the KTM clinic or Meeria ward
for assessment and treatment.
In Outer Islands, Police with family will bring patient (new case or relapse case) to
the Medical Assistant for assessment and Examination. If MA finds that the patient
has mental disorder and aggressive and can not be manage, MA will contact the
Mental Health Dr for referral for further treatment at MA will contact the Mental
Health Dr for referral for further treatment.
492 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

If patient presents otherwise (e.g with family):
•	 If a new case, the family will to take the patient to the Emergency Department
for Assessment and Examination and if the ED Dr finds that patient has a mental
disorder, patient will be referred to the Mental Health Doctor for admission and
treatment at the Meeria ward.
•	 If a relapse case, the family will take the patient to the KTM clinic or Meeria ward
for assessment, treatment and admission.
•	 In Outer-islands, family bring the patient (both new case and relapse case)
to Health Center to the Medical Assistant for Assessment. Treatment will be
provided and patient aggressive and violent, patient will be detain at the Police
cell for safety and will be transferred to Meeria ward for further assessment and
treatment.
3.	 Clinical assessment
Get history, including
•	 Current mental health management plan and related presentations
•	 Establishing patient’s behaviour plus premorbid personality. Via family and
friends if needed.
•	 Do vital signs

–
BSL + SPO2 to exclude glycaemic plus hypoxic causes of behaviour change.
•	 Do physical examination – consider injuries consistent with self-harm attempt
•	 Do a Mental State Examination (MSE). See following box for observations plus
example questions.
•	 For children, assess for sudden or significant, unexplained changes of behaviour
or emotional state such as

–
Unsual fearfulness or severe distress e.g. inconsolable crying

–
Self-harm or social withdrawal

–
Aggression or running away from home

–
Indiscriminate attention seeking with adults

–
Development of new behaviours e.g. soiling or wetting, thumb sucking

–
Consider alternate cause where child presentation is inconsistent with history.
See child protection
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 493

MENTAL STATE EXAMINATION (MSE) OBSERVATION AND EXAMPLE

Gender, ethnicity, apparent age, clothing grooming, hygiene
and cultural appropriateness

Rate, Rhythm, and volume of speech. Is it spontaneous, is it

The outward appearance of the patient’s emotional state eg

Are the patient’s ideas or thoughts connected in a strange or

is the patient incoherent, use words that rhymes or have

Thought Content
Explore anxieties, obsessions, preoccupations and delusions,

Impression of current abilities, awareness to confusion of self
494 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management
•	 Once imminent risk of harm to themselves or others has been addressed refer to
relevant presentation:

–
Suicidal behaviour

–
Psychosis

–
Mood disorder

–
Panic attack

–
Dementia

–
Delirium
•	 Manage in consultation with Medical Assistant/Medical Officer + Psychiatrist or
Mental Health Team
•	 Only consider sedation (see flowchart on next page)

–
If attempts at de-escalation have been exhausted

–
To control severe behaviour disturbance for patient’s safety and safety of
others

–
To allow diagnostic assessment + management

–
To relieve patient distress

–
If child, adolescent or medically frail patient – after consultation with MA/MO
or Psychiatrist

–
For eligible adult only.
For family members and relative of patients, including children:
This may be a very frightening experience for them. Provide support
•	 Consider immediate safety needs of vulnerable people for whom the patient has
care responsibilities i.e. children
•	 Provide a copy of any management plan
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 495

Referral/consultation
•	 Consult MA/Mental Health Team as above
•	 Refer for further mental health assessment by psychiatrist or Mental Health Team
•	 Always consider referral to child protection agencies for:

–
A child presentation

–
A child in care for the patient

–
See child protection
502 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Suicidal behaviour – Adult/Child
Recommend
•	 Identify and respond to suicidal thoughts or behaviours early
•	 Assess risk and implement appropriate safety and management plans
•	 Consult MO for assessment, planning and ongoing care
•	 Refer all cases of suicide attempt to MO for review
Background
•	 Suicidal behaviour includes thoughts, plans, or actions intended to cause self-
harm or death
•	 It is often associated with recent stressors, mental illness, or limited psychosocial
supports
•	 Both adults and children may present with suicidal ideation or actions
•	 Timely assessment and appropriate safety planning are essential to prevent
further harm
1.
May present with¹ ²
•	 Verbalises suicidal ideas or suicidal intent
•	 Previous presentations for suicidal thoughts or deliberate self-harm
•	 A recent psychosocial stressor or loss e.g. grief, relationship breakdown, loss of
job, pregnancy or new birth
•	 Feelings of helplessness or hopelessness
2.	 Immediate management¹
•	 DRSABCD
•	 Do not leave the patient alone
•	 If the person has survived an attempted suicide or self-harm event, manage
accordingly
•	 Consider injuries consistent with self-harm attempt, e.g. for hanging, see
Traumatic injuries, Spinal injuries
•	 If the patient is highly agitated, expressing intent to suicide, aggressive or violent,
see Mental health emergency
•	 It is important to ask the person directly about feelings or thoughts of suicide
•	 Asking about self-harm does not provoke acts of self-harm
•	 Discuss the balance of confidentiality versus notifying others to ensure safety
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 503

3.	 Clinical assessment¹
•	 Ensure the patient has an opportunity to discuss their suicidality alone
•	 With consent, seek information from family, friends, support persons or others
Communication skills¹
•	 Listen. Allow the patient to speak without interruption
•	 Develop and maintain rapport
•	 Be mindful of your own values and beliefs in relation to suicide
•	 Convey warmth, non-judgemental acceptance, and interest in the patient’s
distress
•	 Be respectful and empathic
•	 Avoid minimising the seriousness of suicide risk
Ask Questions about suicidal thoughts:² ⁴
•	 Ask the person directly about suicide
•	 Asking about self-harm does not provoke self-harm⁴
•	 Discuss confidentiality versus the need to notify others for safety
504 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Questions about suicidal thoughts¹-⁴
Suicide risk
•	 When people feel like you have been feeling, they sometimes think life is not
worth living. Have you been thinking like that?
•	 Have you been thinking of harming yourself?
•	 Are you thinking of suicide?
•	 If yes, how often?

–
Have you thought about how you would act? Is there a plan? Is it feasible and
likely to be lethal?

–
Have you thought about when you might act?

–
Are there any reasons that stop you from acting on these thoughts?
•	 Have you tried to harm yourself in the past?

–
If yes, how many times?

–
When was the most recent time?
•	 Do you know anyone who has recently attempted suicide?
•	 Do you feel safe at the moment?
If a suicide attempt has been made
•	 What did you hope would happen as a result?
•	 Did you want to die, or to end your pain?
•	 Do you regret not succeeding?
•	 Do you still have access to the method used?
•	 Did you use alcohol or drugs before the attempt? What did you use?
•	 Do you have easy access to a weapon?
Risk of harm to others
•	 Have you thought of hurting anyone else?

–
If yes, have you acted on these thoughts?
•	 Have you been in any recent fights?

–
If yes, were drugs or alcohol involved?
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 505

Determining suicidal intent
 Patientstrengths
•	 Therapeutic relationship between
clinician and patient
•	 Family warmth, support and
acceptance
•	 Willingness to access and engage
with professional help
•	 Activities/hobbies that an
individual finds meaningful
•	 Community support and cultural
identity
•	 Having children and child rearing
responsibilities •
•	 Sense of belonging and connection
•	 Skills in coping and problem
solving, conflict resolution and non-
violent ways of handling disputes
 Risk factors
•	 Mental health issues eg depression
•	 Gender (male)
•	 Relationship or family problems,
violence, abuse or custody issues,
pregnancy
•	 Substance misuse
•	 Social or geographical isolation
•	 Financial stress, unemployment,
impending court case
•	 Recent bereavement
•	 Prior suicide attempt
•	 Bullying, sexual assault, torture, or
refugee status
•	 Losing a friend or family member to
suicide
 Warning signs
•	 Hopelessness
•	 Feeling trapped
•	 Escalating substance misuse
•	 Giving away possessions
•	 Seeking ways to kill oneself ie
searching online or buying a means
•	 Withdrawing from friends, family or
society
•	 Voicing no reason for living, no
sense of purpose in life, being a
burden to others •
•	 Uncharacteristic or impaired
judgement or behaviour
•	 Verbalising or writing about
wanting to die
 Tipping points
•	 Relationship separation
•	 Loss of status or respect
•	 Recent death or suicide of relative
or friend
•	 Recent argumentat home
•	 Current abuse or bullying
•	 Debilitating physical illness,
accident or pain
Imminent risk
•	 Expressed intent to die
•	 Has plan in mind
•	 Impulsive, aggressive or anti-social
behaviour
•	 Has access to let hal means
506 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Management
•	 Consult MO who will plan:
– Immediate and ongoing management in the community, or

–
ransfer/evacuation for comprehensive care
•	 If the patient is the primary carer for children or vulnerable people, consider
alternative care. See Child protection1,⁴
•	 Discuss the patient’s behaviour with family or friends to reduce their anxiety or
anger
•	 In consultation with MO, assess whether the patient is safe to be managed at
home⁴ ⁵

–
Acute problems are being addresse

–
Patient knows how to seek help if suicidal thoughts return

–
Patient is not intoxicated, sedated, delirious or psychotic

–
‘Lethal means’ counselling is completed:
	›
Identify who will manage access to means of suicide (e.g. guns, knives,
ropes, drugs, poisons, high places)
	›
Discuss if occupation allows access to lethal means (e.g. pesticides,
firearms, medications)

–
Identify who manages medications in the home

–
Reduce quantities of prescription medications

–
If the patient has access to a firearm, notify police at:
www.police.qld.gov.au/weapon-licensing/mental-and-physical-health
•	 Ensure the following are in place:

–
Follow-up arrangements documented and shared with patient and carer

–
Treatment plan arranged for mental health and medical needs

–
Written Safety Plan given to patient and support person
•	 Develop a Safety Plan with the patient. It involves² ⁴:

–
Recognising warning signs and triggers

–
Making surroundings safe

–
Listing reminders of reasons to live

–
Identifying what helps them feel strong

–
Naming people and places to connect with

–
Listing family/friends they can talk to

–
Including professional supports

–
See: www.beyondblue.org.au/get-support/beyondnow-suicide-safety-planning
4.	 Follow up
•	 If referred to the Mental Health Team: See Mental Health Team response time:
table below for expected response + action times
5.	 Referral / Consultation
•	 If referred to the Mental Health Team: See Mental Health Team response time:
table below for expected response + action times
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual | 507

Dementia – Adult
Severe behavioral and psychological symptoms
1.
May present with
•	 A patient with known dementia plus
•	 Psychosis, agitation or aggression
2.	 Immediate management
•	 DRSABCD
•	 If a person is at risk of harm to themselves, others or their behaviour is
associated with perceptual or thought disturbance, delirium or impaired impulse
control, see Mental health emergency,
3.	 Clinical assessment
•	 Assess + manage calmly in a quiet area, with a familiar person present
•	 Get history, including:

–
current mental health management plan + related presentations²

–
patient’s behaviour + personality prior to the current presentation

–
alcohol + drug use
•	 Identify the trigger (antecedent)¹

–
side effects or toxicity from medicines

–
signs of infection or presence of wounds e.g. acute febrile illness + altered
mental status

–
herpes simplex virus leading to encephalitis e.g. headache, seizures, focal
neurological signs¹ ²

–
acute thiamine deficiency e.g. ocular abnormalities, gait ataxia, inability to
concentrate, apathy, impaired awareness of the immediate situation, spatial
disorientation, confusion, psychosis + coma¹ ³

–
environmental stimuli e.g. temperature extremes, noise
	›
unmet needs e.g. hunger, thirst, warmth
	›
patient-carer conflict
	›
pain, constipation
	›
vision or hearing difficulties
	›
separation from family
•	 What behaviour occurred:

–
does behaviour cause significant distress to themselves or others e.g. verbal
aggression, use of a weapon⁴
•	 What was consequences of the behaviour e.g. injury, fall
•	 Consider elder abuse where presentation for non-accidental cause is inconsistent
with history or is unexpected in older or vulnerable people
508 | Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

•	 Do physical examination¹ +

–
vital signs

–
BGL + SpO₂ to exclude glycaemic or hypoxic causes of behaviour change

–
bloods – FBC, TFT, PTH, UE, calcium, vitamin B12, folate, niacin, CHEM20,
thiamine

–
urine for MCS + drug screen
Use the table below to distinguishing signs and symptoms of delirium, dementia
and depression⁸
